**9.1.1**  Janamejaya said: When Karṇa had thus been struck down in battle by Savyasācin, and the Kurus were left but few, what then did they do, O brāhmaṇa?

**9.1.2**  Seeing the host of the Pāṇḍavas surging upward and the hour come upon him, what course did King Suyodhana, the Kaurava, resolve upon?

**9.1.3**  This I wish to hear; tell it to me, O best of the twice-born, for I am never sated hearing of the mighty deeds of the ancients.

**9.1.4**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Then, when Karṇa had been slain, O king, Suyodhana the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra sank deep in an ocean of grief and grew hopeless on every side.

**9.1.5**  Crying again and again "Alas, Karṇa! Alas, Karṇa!" in his sorrow, he made his way with difficulty to his own camp, together with the kings who survived the slaughter.

**9.1.6**  Though consoled by the kings with reasonings settled in the śāstras, he found no peace, remembering the slaying of the Sūta's son.

**9.1.7**  Deeming fate all-powerful and what must be inevitable, that prince made his resolve for battle and went forth once more to fight.

**9.1.8**  Having made Śalya commander of his army with due rites, that bull among kings went forth to war, together with the kings who survived the slaughter.

**9.1.9**  Then a most tumultuous battle arose between the armies of the Kurus and the Pāṇḍavas, O best of the Bhāratas, like the war of gods and asuras.

**9.1.10**  Then, O great king, Śalya, after wreaking havoc in battle among the Pāṇḍava host, was struck down at midday by the King of Righteousness.

**9.1.11**  Then King Duryodhana, his kinsmen slain, withdrew from the field of battle and entered a dreadful lake out of fear born of his enemies.

**9.1.12**  Then in the afternoon of that day, surrounded by the great car-warriors and called forth from the lake by a stratagem, he was struck down by Bhīmasena.

**9.1.13**  When that mighty bowman had been slain, the three car-warriors who remained, in their fury, slew the Pāñcāla soldiers by night, O lord of kings.

**9.1.14**  Then in the forenoon Saṃjaya, coming from the camp, entered the city wretched, filled with sorrow and grief.

**9.1.15**  Having swiftly entered the city, grief-stricken, raising up his arms and trembling, he then entered the dwelling of the king.

**9.1.16**  And he wept aloud, O tiger among men, crying "Alas, O king!" in his grief: "Ah, woe, we are stricken with anguish by the destruction of the great-souled ones."

**9.1.17**  "Ah, how mighty is Time, and how supreme its course, where all those kings, equal in strength to Śakra, were slain!"

**9.1.18**  And the moment they saw Saṃjaya before them, O king, all the people, sorely agitated, wailed loudly, crying "Alas, O king!"

**9.1.19**  That whole city, O tiger among men, down to its children, raised on every side a great cry of anguish when it heard the king had been slain.

**9.1.20**  And he saw there three bulls among men running about, like men robbed of their senses, like madmen, sorely afflicted with grief.

**9.1.21**  Then that overwhelmed sūta, entering the dwelling of the king, beheld the best of kings, the lord whose eye was wisdom.

**9.1.22**  And he saw the sinless one seated, surrounded on every side by his daughters-in-law, by Gāndhārī, and by Vidura, O best of the Bhāratas,

**9.1.23**  and by other friends and kinsmen who wished him well, while he brooded upon that very matter — the death of Karṇa.

**9.1.24**  Weeping still, the sūta, his mind far from glad, addressed these words to the king, O Janamejaya, in a voice choked with tears:

**9.1.25**  "I am Saṃjaya, O tiger among men; homage to you, O bull of the Bhāratas. Śalya, lord of the Madras, is slain, and Śakuni, son of Subala, and Ulūka, O tiger among men, the son of the gambler, of steadfast valor."

**9.1.26**  "The saṃśaptakas are all slain, and the Kāmbojas together with the Śakas; the mlecchas too, the mountain-dwellers, and the Yavanas have been struck down."

**9.1.27**  "The men of the east are slain, O great king, and those of the south entirely; all those of the north are killed, and those of the west, O lord of men; kings and princes have been slain on every side, O king."

**9.1.28**  "Duryodhana is slain, O king, as foretold by the Pāṇḍava; his thighs broken, O great king, he lies in the dust, smeared over with it."

**9.1.29**  "Dhṛṣṭadyumna is slain, O king, and the unconquered Śikhaṇḍin, Uttamaujas and Yudhāmanyu too, O king, and the Prabhadrakas."

**9.1.30**  "The Pāñcālas, those tigers among men, and the Cedis are destroyed; all your sons are slain, and the sons of Draupadī, O Bhārata; the heroic son of Karṇa, the mighty Vṛṣasena, is slain."

**9.1.31**  "All the foot-soldiers are slain and the elephants struck down; the car-warriors too, O tiger among men, and the horses have been killed in the battle."

**9.1.32**  "Little is left of the camp of your men, O lord, nor of the heroes of the Pāṇḍavas, after they clashed with one another."

**9.1.33**  "The world, deluded by Time, has come down well-nigh to women alone; seven remain on the Pāṇḍava side, and of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's, three."

**9.1.34**  "Those are the five brothers, then Vāsudeva and Sātyaki; and Kṛpa, Kṛtavarman, and Droṇa's son, best of the victorious."

**9.1.35**  "These car-warriors are yours, O great king, O best of kings; of all the akṣauhiṇīs gathered together, O lord of men, these alone remain, O great king; all the others have gone to destruction."

**9.1.36**  "By Time the whole world has been slain, O bull of the Bhāratas, with Duryodhana set foremost in the enmity, O Bhārata."

**9.1.37**  Hearing these cruel words, Dhṛtarāṣṭra, lord of men, fell to the ground, O great king, his spirit gone.

**9.1.38**  When he had fallen to the earth, Vidura too, of great renown, fell down, O great king, worn with grief at the king's calamity.

**9.1.39**  And Gāndhārī, O best of kings, and all the women of the Kurus fell suddenly to the earth on hearing those cruel words.

**9.1.40**  That whole royal circle then lay senseless, fallen to the earth, like a great tale full of lamentation set down upon a painted scroll.

**9.1.41**  Then with much difficulty King Dhṛtarāṣṭra, lord of the earth, slowly regained his breath, worn with grief at the loss of his sons.

**9.1.42**  Having regained his senses, that king, trembling and sorely grieved, looked toward all the quarters and spoke these words to the Steward:

**9.1.43**  "O learned Steward, of great wisdom, you are my refuge, O bull of the Bhāratas — mine, who am forlorn, utterly bereft of all my sons." Having spoken thus, he again fell down senseless.

**9.1.44**  Seeing him thus fallen, such kinsmen of his as were near sprinkled him with cool waters and fanned him with fans.

**9.1.45**  After a long while that lord of the earth, regaining his composure, brooded in silence, the king worn with grief at the loss of his sons, hissing like a serpent thrust into a jar, O lord of peoples.

**9.1.46**  Saṃjaya too wept there, seeing the king so stricken, as did all the women, and the illustrious Gāndhārī.

**9.1.47**  Then after a long while Dhṛtarāṣṭra, that tiger among men, swooning again and again, spoke these words to Vidura:

**9.1.48**  "Let all the women go, and the illustrious Gāndhārī, and likewise all these friends; my mind is utterly giving way."

**9.1.49**  Thus addressed, the Steward, O bull of the Bhāratas, gently dismissed those women, himself trembling again and again.

**9.1.50**  Then all those women went out, O bull of the Bhāratas, and all the friends as well, having seen the king so stricken.

**9.1.51**  Then, O scorcher of foes, Saṃjaya, wretched, looking upon that lord of men who had regained his senses there, weeping and sorely afflicted,

**9.1.52**  with joined palms — and the Steward too — comforted with gentle words that lord of men, who sighed again and again.

**9.2.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: When the women had been dismissed, Dhṛtarāṣṭra, son of Ambikā, lamented, O great king, passing from grief to deeper grief.

**9.2.2**  Sighing as if with smoke, wringing his hands again and again, and reflecting, O great king, he then spoke these words:

**9.2.3**  "Ah, woe, this is great sorrow, that I hear from you, O sūta, that the Pāṇḍavas are safe and unscathed in the battle."

**9.2.4**  "Surely my heart is most hard, made of the very stuff of adamant, since on hearing my sons slain it does not burst into a thousand pieces."

**9.2.5**  "Recalling their speech and their childhood play, O Saṃjaya, now on hearing my sons slain, my mind is utterly torn asunder."

**9.2.6**  "Though through my blindness I had no sight of their forms, yet the affection born of a father's love for them I held ever in my heart."

**9.2.7**  "As they passed beyond childhood, and stood in youth, and came to the prime of life, on hearing of it I rejoiced, O sinless one."

**9.2.8**  "Hearing them now slain, their sovereignty stripped away, their splendor stripped away, I find no peace anywhere, overwhelmed by sorrow for my sons."

**9.2.9**  "Come, come, my son, O lord of kings, to me who am now forlorn; bereft of you, O mighty-armed one, to what refuge shall I go?"

**9.2.10**  "Having been the refuge, O great king, of kinsmen and friends alike, where do you go, O hero, abandoning me, blind and old?"

**9.2.11**  "That tenderness of yours, that affection, and that high honor you bore, O king — how were you, unconquered in battles, slain by the Pārthas?"

**9.2.12**  "How is it that you, having enjoyed the homage of the assembled lords of the earth, my child, now lie slain upon the ground like some common, paltry king?"

**9.2.13**  "Who now, when I rise at dawn, will say to me 'Father, father,' and ever 'Great king,' and again and again 'Lord of the world'?"

**9.2.14**  "Embracing me about the neck, his eyes moist with affection, who will say 'Command me,' O Kaurava — speak to me that good word!"

**9.2.15**  "Did I not surely hear your word, my dear son: 'This earth of mine is greater than the Pārtha's, not theirs'?"

**9.2.16**  "Bhagadatta, Kṛpa, Śalya, the lord of the Avantis, and Jayadratha; Bhūriśravas, Somadatta, and the great king Bāhlika;"

**9.2.17**  "Aśvatthāman, and the Bhoja, and the mighty king of Magadha; Bṛhadbala, the lord of Kāśi, and Śakuni the son of Subala;"

**9.2.18**  "The mlecchas in their many thousands, the Śakas together with the Yavanas, and Sudakṣiṇa the Kāmboja, and the lord of the Trigartas;"

**9.2.19**  "Bhīṣma the grandsire, and Bhāradvāja, and the Gautama; Śrutāyus and Acyutāyus, and the valiant Śatāyus;"

**9.2.20**  "Jalasaṃdha, then Ārśyaśṛṅgi, and the rākṣasa Alāyudha; the mighty-armed Alambusa, and Subāhu the great car-warrior —"

**9.2.21**  "These and many other kings, O best of kings, all stood ready for my sake, giving up their lives in battle, O lord."

**9.2.22**  "'Standing in their midst in the war, surrounded by my brothers, I shall make battle against the Pārthas and against all the Pāñcālas,'"

**9.2.23**  "'and the Cedis, O tiger among kings, and the sons of Draupadī in the encounter, Sātyaki and Kuntibhoja, and the rākṣasa Ghaṭotkaca' —

**9.2.24**  "'Even one of these, O great king, is able to ward off in battle the Pāṇḍava host charging in fury; how much more these heroes together, sworn in enmity to the Pāṇḍavas!'"

**9.2.25**  "'Or else all of these will fight against the followers of the Pāṇḍava, O lord of kings, and will slay them in the fray.'"

**9.2.26**  "'Karṇa alone, together with me, will slay the Pāṇḍavas; then the heroic kings will abide under my command.'"

**9.2.27**  "'And he who is their leader, the mighty Vāsudeva, does not gird himself for arms, O king' — such was the word he spoke to me."

**9.2.28**  "As he spoke thus to me many times in my presence, O sūta, by his reasoning I did indeed foresee the Pāṇḍavas slain in the fray."

**9.2.29**  When my dear sons, standing in their very midst and struggling hard in battle, are slain there—what is this but the working of fate?

**9.2.30**  When Bhīṣma too, lord of the world and full of valor, was struck down there—meeting Śikhaṇḍin as a lion might meet a jackal—what is this but fate?

**9.2.31**  When Droṇa too, the brāhmaṇa, master of every weapon and missile, was slain by the Pāṇḍavas in the fray—what is this but the working of fate?

**9.2.32**  When Bhūriśravas was slain there, and Somadatta in the combat, and Bāhlika too, O great king—what is this but the working of fate?

**9.2.33**  When Sudakṣiṇa was slain there, and Jalasaṃdha the Kaurava, and Śrutāyus and Acyutāyus—what is this but the working of fate?

**9.2.34**  When Bṛhadbala was slain there, and the mighty king of Magadha, when the lord of Avanti fell, and the Trigarta ruler of men, and the many Saṃśaptakas—what is this but the working of fate?

**9.2.35**  And Alaṃbusa likewise, O king, and the rākṣasa Alāyudha, and Ārśyaśṛṅga slain—what is this but the working of fate?

**9.2.36**  When the Nārāyaṇa cowherds, frenzied for battle, were slain there, and the mlecchas in their many thousands—what is this but the working of fate?

**9.2.37**  When Śakuni, Subala's son, and the mighty hero, son of the gambler, were slain there together with their host—what is this but the working of fate?

**9.2.38**  When kings and princes, valiant men with arms like iron bars, were slain there in their numbers—what is this but the working of fate?

**9.2.39**  When the kṣatriyas gathered from many lands, O Saṃjaya, were all slain there in the battle—what is this but the working of fate?

**9.2.40**  When my sons have been struck down, and my mighty grandsons too, and my companions and brothers—what is this but the working of fate?

**9.2.41**  Surely a man is born endowed with his own portion of fate; and the man who is blessed with good fortune attains what is auspicious.

**9.2.42**  But I am sundered from my own good fortune and from my sons, O Saṃjaya. How shall I live on now, an old man fallen into the power of my foes?

**9.2.43**  I can think of nothing better in this, my lord, than to dwell in the forest. So I shall go to the forest, kinless now that my kinsmen are destroyed.

**9.2.44**  For nothing else could be better for me than to retire to the forest, fallen as I am into this plight, my wings shorn away, O Saṃjaya.

**9.2.45**  When Duryodhana has been slain there, and Śalya struck down in the fight, and Duḥśāsana hacked apart, and the mighty Vikarṇa as well—

**9.2.46**  how shall I bear to hear the boastful cry of Bhīmasena, by whom alone in battle my hundred sons were slain?

**9.2.47**  As he proclaims it again and again, with the slaying of Duryodhana too, scorched by grief and sorrow I shall not be able to endure his harsh words.

**9.2.48**  Thus the king, scorched by sorrow, his kinsmen slain, swooning again and again, overwhelmed by anguish for his sons,

**9.2.49**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra, the son of Ambikā, having lamented for a long while, drawing long and burning sighs and brooding upon his defeat,

**9.2.50**  the king, scorched by great sorrow, O bull of the Bhāratas, once more questioned in due order Saṃjaya, the son of Gavalgaṇa, the bard:

**9.2.51**  "Having heard that Bhīṣma and Droṇa were slain, and that the charioteer's son was brought down, what commander, what leader did my men then appoint?

**9.2.52**  Whomever my men make leader of the host in battle, him in but a little while the Pāṇḍavas strike down.

**9.2.53**  At the head of the battle Bhīṣma was slain by the Diademed One before your very eyes; in just the same way Droṇa too was slain before the eyes of all.

**9.2.54**  And in just the same way Karṇa, the valiant son of the charioteer, was slain by the Diademed One before the eyes of all you princes.

**9.2.55**  Long ago I was told by the great-souled Vidura: through the wrongdoing of Duryodhana this people shall be destroyed.

**9.2.56**  Some, being deluded, do not see rightly, while others see rightly; and for me, deluded as I was, that very word of his has come to pass just so.

**9.2.57**  What the righteous, far-seeing Vidura spoke to me—that word of the truth-teller has now come to pass exactly so.

**9.2.58**  Of the wrong I did of old, my mind undone by fate—of that misrule tell me the fruit once more, O son of Gavalgaṇa.

**9.2.59**  Who stood at the forefront of the armies when Karṇa had fallen? And what warrior went forth to meet Arjuna and Vāsudeva?

**9.2.60**  Who guarded the right wheel of the king of Madra in the combat, and who the left, and who stood at the back of that hero eager to fight?

**9.2.61**  And how, when you were all gathered together, was the mighty king of Madra slain by the Pāṇḍavas in the fray—and how my son, O Saṃjaya?

**9.2.62**  Tell me all according to the truth, the great ruin of the Bhāratas, and how my son Duryodhana was slain in the battle;

**9.2.63**  and how all the Pāñcālas were slain together with their followers, and Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Śikhaṇḍin and the five sons of Draupadī;

**9.2.64**  and how the Pāṇḍavas escaped, and likewise the two Sātvatas in the fight, and Kṛpa and Kṛtavarman and the son of Bharadvāja.

**9.2.65**  What befell, how, and of what kind the recent battle was—I wish to hear it all in full, for you are skilled in the telling, O Saṃjaya."

**9.3.1**  Saṃjaya said: Hear attentively, O king, how the great destruction came to pass when the Kurus and the Pāṇḍavas met one another in battle.

**9.3.2**  When the charioteer's son had been slain by the great-souled Pāṇḍava, and the armies, scattered, had been rallied again and again,

**9.3.3**  and your son had turned away, his mind stricken with grief, and the troops were sorely shaken at the sight of Pārtha's prowess,

**9.3.4**  the troops brooding and sunk in misery, O Bhārata, and hearing the dreadful uproar of the hosts being churned to ruin,

**9.3.5**  beholding the ensigns of the kings ravaged in the combat, and the fallen chariot-frames and chariots of the great-souled ones,

**9.3.6**  seeing the elephants slain in the fray and the foot-soldiers too, O venerable one, and the battlefield most terrible, like the playground of Rudra,

**9.3.7**  as kings by the hundreds of thousands passed into oblivion—then Kṛpa, O king, filled with compassion, endowed with age and good character,

**9.3.8**  that man of splendor, skilled in speech, approaching the lord of men, spoke these words to Duryodhana, moved by sorrow:

**9.3.9**  "Duryodhana, mark this that I shall say to you, O Kaurava; having heard it, act upon it, O great king, if it pleases you, O blameless one.

**9.3.10**  There is no path better than the law of battle, O lord of kings—the path upon which kṣatriyas rely when they fight, O bull among kṣatriyas.

**9.3.11**  Son, brother, even father, sister's son and maternal uncle alike, kinsmen and relatives all—these must be fought by one who lives by the warrior's calling.

**9.3.12**  In slaying lies the highest duty, and in flight the gravest wrong; we who long to live have entered upon a grim way of life.

**9.3.13**  And so I shall offer you a word of counsel for your good: with Bhīṣma slain, and Droṇa, and Karṇa too, that great chariot-warrior,

**9.3.14**  and Jayadratha struck down, and your brothers, O blameless one, and Lakṣmaṇa your son—what remnant is left to us to rely upon?

**9.3.15**  Those upon whom we laid our burden and set our hopes of sovereignty—those heroes, casting off their bodies, have gone to the goal of the knowers of Brahman.

**9.3.16**  But we, here bereft of those gifted great chariot-warriors, shall lead a wretched existence, having brought down many kings.

**9.3.17**  Even were they all yet living, Bībhatsu remains unconquered—dark-eyed, mighty-armed, hard to assail even by the gods—

**9.3.18**  and confronting that ape-bannered standard, raised on high like Indra's banner and gleaming like Indra's bow and thunderbolt, the great host has trembled.

**9.3.19**  At the lion's roar of Bhīma, at the blast of the Pāñcajanya, and at the thunder of the Gāṇḍīva, our hearts stand on end with dread.

**9.3.20**  The Gāṇḍīva, like a great lightning ranging the sky, robs the eyes of their light, and is seen whirling like a brandished firebrand.

**9.3.21**  That great bow, adorned with gold and shaken in his grip, is seen in every quarter like lightning amid massed clouds.

**9.3.22**  Borne along by Kṛṣṇa as a cloud is borne by the wind, Arjuna, O king, the foremost of those who know weapons, consumed that army of yours as a fire risen up devours dense, dry brushwood in the cold season.

**9.3.23**  We beheld Dhanaṃjaya, splendid as great Indra, plunging through the ranks like a four-tusked elephant.

**9.3.24**  We beheld Dhanaṃjaya throwing your army into turmoil and terrifying the kings, like an elephant ravaging a lotus-pond.

**9.3.25**  Again we beheld that Pāṇḍava terrifying the warriors with the twang of his bow, as herds of deer behold a lion.

**9.3.26**  Those two great archers of all the world, those bulls among all bowmen, the two dark heroes clad in their armor, shone forth in the midst of the host.

**9.3.27**  This is now the seventeenth day, O Bhārata, of this most terrible battle that wears on, as men are slain on every side in the fighting.

**9.3.28**  Your armies, scattered on every hand as if by the wind, have been broken apart all around like a web of autumn clouds.

**9.3.29**  Like a ship overturned and storm-tossed upon the great sea, the Ambidextrous One set your army shuddering, O great king.

**9.3.30**  Where then was the charioteer's son for you, and where Droṇa with his followers? Where was I, and where your own self, and where the son of Hṛdika? And where Duḥśāsana your brother, together with your brothers—where were they all?

**9.3.31**  For seeing Jayadratha come within range of his arrows, and your kinsmen and brothers, your allies and your maternal uncles,

**9.3.32**  overpowering them all and treading upon the worlds before our very eyes, Jayadratha was slain, O king—what remnant then do we rely upon?

**9.3.33**  What man is there here who shall vanquish that Pāṇḍava? The manifold divine weapons of that great-souled one, and the thunder of the Gāṇḍīva, rob us of our valor.

**9.3.34**  Like a night whose moon has vanished is this army, its leaders slain; like a dried-up river whose trees are broken by elephants, it has fallen into disarray.

**9.3.35**  With our banner-host bereft of its leaders, the mighty-armed warrior of the white steeds will range at will, like a fire blazing through the brushwood.

**9.3.36**  The onrush of both Sātyaki and Bhīmasena could rend asunder all the mountains and dry up the very seas.

**9.3.37**  The vow that Bhīma uttered in the midst of the assembly, O lord of peoples—the whole of it he has fulfilled, and yet more will he do.

**9.3.38**  With Karṇa once stationed in the van, the army the Pāṇḍavas guarded was unassailable—warded and hidden as it was by the wielder of the Gāṇḍīva.

**9.3.39**  Those evil deeds that you wrought against the good, done without cause—the fruit of them has now come upon you.

**9.3.40**  For your own sake you gathered the whole world by every effort; yet now that world, and your own life as well, are in peril, my son, O bull of the Bhāratas.

**9.3.41**  Guard your own self, Duryodhana, for the self is the vessel of all; for once the vessel is broken, my son, that which it held flows away to every quarter.

**9.3.42**  He who is waning, or equal in strength, should seek a treaty; he who is growing in power may make war—such is the policy of Bṛhaspati.

**9.3.43**  We, then, are inferior to the sons of Pāṇḍu in the strength of our own forces; herein I judge a treaty with the Pāṇḍavas to be the fitting course, my lord."

**9.3.44**  The man who does not know where his good lies, and who scorns it even when it is shown to him, swiftly falls from his kingdom and never attains the good.

**9.3.45**  If by bowing before the king we might regain the kingdom, that would be the better course, O king; to go down to ruin through sheer folly is not.

**9.3.46**  At a word from the son of Vicitravīrya, and at a word from Govinda, the compassionate Yudhiṣṭhira would install you in your kingdom.

**9.3.47**  Whatever Hṛṣīkeśa should bid the unvanquished king, and Arjuna, and Bhīmasena—all of it they would do, without a doubt.

**9.3.48**  Kṛṣṇa, I think, will not transgress the word of the Kaurava Dhṛtarāṣṭra; nor will the son of Pāṇḍu transgress the word of Kṛṣṇa.

**9.3.49**  I hold a peace with the sons of Pṛthā to be the fitting course for you. I do not speak to you out of weakness, nor to save my own life; I speak to you what is wholesome, O king—and when your breath has fled, you will remember it.

**9.3.50**  Having lamented thus, the aged Kṛpa, son of Śaradvat, drew a long, hot sigh, and grieved, and swooned away.

**9.4.1**  Saṃjaya said: Thus addressed by the illustrious son of Gautama, the king, O lord of peoples, drew a long, hot sigh and fell silent.

**9.4.2**  Then, having pondered a while, the high-minded son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, that scorcher of foes, spoke these words to Kṛpa, son of Śaradvat.

**9.4.3**  All that a friend should say, I have indeed been made to hear; and all has been done by you, who fought while ready to lay down your very life.

**9.4.4**  The world has beheld you plunging into the enemy hosts, doing battle with the great chariot-warriors, the Pāṇḍavas of surpassing might.

**9.4.5**  This counsel, which a friend should give, you have made me hear; yet none of it pleases me, as medicine pleases not a man about to die.

**9.4.6**  This excellent and salutary speech, furnished with reasons and grounds, though it is spoken, O mighty-armed, foremost of brahmins, finds no favor with me.

**9.4.7**  Cheated by us of his kingdom, how should that king trust us? At the game of dice that king of great wealth was beaten by us; how, then, should he ever again put faith in my words?

**9.4.8**  And Kṛṣṇa, who came as an envoy, intent on the welfare of the sons of Pṛthā—Hṛṣīkeśa too was deceived, and that deed of mine bred enmity; how then, O brahmin, will he ever heed a word of mine?

**9.4.9**  What Kṛṣṇā lamented when she came into the midst of the assembly hall, that Kṛṣṇa does not forgive, nor likewise the seizure of the kingdom.

**9.4.10**  The two Kṛṣṇas are bound to each other as a single life, joined together as one; what once I had only heard, that, O lord, I now behold.

**9.4.11**  Hearing that his sister's son was slain, Keśava sleeps in sorrow; we have done him grievous wrong—how should he forgive it for my sake?

**9.4.12**  Through the destruction of Abhimanyu, Arjuna finds no peace; how, though entreated, should he exert himself for my welfare?

**9.4.13**  The middle son of Pāṇḍu, the fierce Bhīmasena of mighty strength, has sworn a dreadful oath; he would break sooner than he would bend.

**9.4.14**  Both have girded on their swords, both have buckled on their mail; both heroes have made themselves my foes—the twins too, who are like unto Yama.

**9.4.15**  Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Śikhaṇḍin have both made themselves my enemies; how, O best of the twice-born, should those two exert themselves for my good?

**9.4.16**  How Kṛṣṇā, clad in a single garment, in her courses, was tormented by Duḥśāsana in the midst of the assembly, before the eyes of all the people—

**9.4.17**  remembering her thus, half-stripped and wretched, even now the Pāṇḍavas, those scorchers of foes, cannot be turned back from battle.

**9.4.18**  And when Draupadī Kṛṣṇā, grieving, performed fierce austerities for my undoing—Kṛṣṇā, for the fulfillment of her husbands' cause, lies ever upon the bare ground, so long as the requital of the feud endures.

**9.4.19**  Casting aside pride and arrogance, the sister of Vāsudeva, become as a serving-maid, ever waits in attendance upon Kṛṣṇā.

**9.4.20**  Thus all is inflamed, and in no wise can it be quenched; through the slaying of Abhimanyu, how is peace to be made by me with him?

**9.4.21**  And how, having enjoyed this earth girdled by the seas, should I enjoy a petty kingdom by the grace of the Pāṇḍavas?

**9.4.22**  I who blazed above all kings, one above another, like the radiant sun—how should I now follow behind Yudhiṣṭhira like a slave?

**9.4.23**  Having myself enjoyed all delights and bestowed lavish gifts, how should I lead a beggarly livelihood in the company of the wretched?

**9.4.24**  I find no fault with your words, spoken in affection and for my good; yet in no wise do I deem this peace to be timely.

**9.4.25**  By a good fight I foresee good policy, O scorcher of foes; this is no time to play the coward—now is our time for battle.

**9.4.26**  I have sacrificed with many sacrifices, bestowed largesse upon brahmins, gained the Vedas heard in due succession, and trodden upon the heads of my foes.

**9.4.27**  My dependents have been well maintained, dear sir, and the wretched folk uplifted; the realms of enemies have been overrun, and my own realm protected.

**9.4.28**  Manifold pleasures have been enjoyed, and the threefold aim of life pursued by me; the debt to the ancestors is discharged, and likewise the duty of the warrior—both.

**9.4.29**  No happiness is lasting here below—whence then a kingdom, whence renown? Fame is to be won in this world, and that by battle and no otherwise.

**9.4.30**  Even for a warrior, death within his house is a thing condemned; a most grievous unrighteousness is this—to die in bed at home.

**9.4.31**  The man who lays down his body in the wilderness or in battle, having offered great sacrifices, attains to glory.

**9.4.32**  He who, wailing piteously and afflicted, overwhelmed by old age, dies amid weeping kinsmen—that man is no man at all.

**9.4.33**  Having forsaken manifold delights, may I even now, by a good fight, attain that supreme goal which they have reached—the realm of the righteous.

**9.4.34**  For heroes of noble conduct, who never turn back in battle, wise men true to their word, all of them performers of sacrifice—

**9.4.35**  for those who have attained the final ablution of weapons, a sure dwelling is in heaven; surely the radiant troops of apsarases behold them with joy.

**9.4.36**  Surely the ancestors behold them honored in the assembly of Śakra, encircled by apsarases, rejoicing in heaven.

**9.4.37**  The path trodden by the immortals and by heroes who never turn back—that road which they have joined, may we too ascend.

**9.4.38**  By the aged grandsire, and by the wise preceptor, by Jayadratha and by Karṇa, and likewise by Duḥśāsana—

**9.4.39**  those heroes, those lords of men, striving in this cause of mine, lie slain, their limbs smeared with blood, mangled by arrows upon the earth.

**9.4.40**  Those heroes, masters of the highest weapons, who sacrificed with rites as enjoined, having laid down their lives according to the law, are stationed in the halls of Indra.

**9.4.41**  By them has this path been laid out—a path that would be hard to tread again—by those who fell with mighty speed and went hence to the goal of the good.

**9.4.42**  Remembering the deeds of those heroes who were slain in my cause, and seeking to discharge that debt to them, I set not my mind upon the kingdom.

**9.4.43**  Having brought down my comrades, my brothers, and my grandsires, were I to guard my own life, the world would surely revile me.

**9.4.44**  And what manner of kingdom would it be for me, bereft of kinsmen, of companions and of friends, and won by bowing before the son of Pāṇḍu?

**9.4.45**  I, then, having wrought such ruin upon this world, shall thereafter by a good fight attain to heaven, and not otherwise.

**9.4.46**  When Duryodhana had spoken thus, all the warriors honored that speech and addressed the king, crying, "Well said, well said!"

**9.4.47**  Grieving not over their defeat, and resolved upon valor, all were firmly determined to fight, and grew exalted in spirit.

**9.4.48**  Then, having refreshed their mounts, all the Kauravas, rejoicing in the prospect of battle, having gone a little less than two yojanas, halted and took their stand.

**9.4.49**  On a clear and holy, coral-hued tableland of Himavat, fair to behold, reaching the red Sarasvatī, they drank and bathed in its waters.

**9.4.50**  Your sons, their resolve renewed, then turned about; and steadying themselves once more, the one through the other, all the warriors, O king, urged on by fate, returned again.

**9.5.1**  Saṃjaya said: Now, halting upon that tableland of Himavat, all the warriors, rejoicing in battle, O great king, gathered together there.

**9.5.2**  Śalya and Citrasena, and Śakuni the great chariot-warrior, Aśvatthāman too, and Kṛpa, and Kṛtavarman of the Sātvatas—

**9.5.3**  Suṣeṇa and Ariṣṭasena, and the valiant Dhṛtasena, and king Jayatsena—these spent the night there.

**9.5.4**  When the hero Karṇa had been slain in battle, terrified by the foes who bore the marks of victory, your sons found no refuge save the mountain Himavat.

**9.5.5**  Gathered together there, they spoke to the king before the army; having striven in battle, O king, and having honored him then with due ceremony, they said:

**9.5.6**  "Having appointed a leader of the host, you should do battle with the foe, under whose protection in the fray we may conquer our enemies."

**9.5.7**  Then Duryodhana, standing upon his chariot, looked upon that foremost of chariot-warriors, versed in every division of warfare, the very likeness of Death in battle—

**9.5.8**  fair of limb, his head well-covered, his neck shapely as a conch, gracious in speech, his face like an opened lotus, tiger-mouthed, weighty as Meru—

**9.5.9**  in shoulders, eyes, gait, and voice like the bull of the Steadfast One, his arms full, firm, and long, his chest broad and massive—

**9.5.10**  in speed and in strength like the younger brother of Aruṇa and like the Wind, in radiance equal to the Sun, and in wisdom the peer of Uśanas—

**9.5.11**  in loveliness, beauty, and the majesty of his countenance—these three—comparable to the moon, like a mass of golden stone, his joints well-knit—

**9.5.12**  with shapely thighs, hips, and legs, fair feet, and comely fingers and nails, fashioned with care by the Creator, who called to mind virtue upon virtue—

**9.5.13**  endowed with every auspicious mark, skilled, an ocean of sacred lore, a conqueror of foes by his swiftness, himself unconquerable by enemies through force—

**9.5.14**  him who knows in truth the science of archery with its ten branches and four feet, and the four Vedas with their auxiliaries, with the sacred narratives as a fifth—

**9.5.15**  the great ascetic who, having earnestly propitiated the Three-Eyed One with fierce vows, was begotten by Droṇa, himself not born of a womb, upon a woman not born of a womb—

**9.5.16**  him of matchless deeds, in beauty unequaled upon earth, master of all the sciences, an ocean of virtues, the blameless one—approaching that Aśvatthāman, O king, your son spoke to him:

**9.5.17**  "With whom set at our head, united, we may vanquish the Pāṇḍavas in battle—the preceptor's son this day is the supreme refuge of us all. By your appointment, then, who shall be commander of my host?"

**9.5.18**  The son of Droṇa said: "This Śalya, endowed in birth, in valor, in energy, in renown, in splendor, in every virtue—let him be the leader of our army."

**9.5.19**  "Forsaking his own sisters' sons, in gratitude he came over to us—lord of a great army, mighty-armed, like a second Mahāsena."

**9.5.20**  "Having made this king commander of the host, O best of kings, victory may be won by us, as the gods won it through the unconquered Skanda."

**9.5.21**  When the son of Droṇa had spoken thus, all the lords of men stood encircling Śalya and raised shouts of victory; they set their minds upon battle and passed into the highest fervor.

**9.5.22**  Then Duryodhana, standing upon the ground, with joined palms addressed Śalya, who stood upon his chariot, the equal in battle of Rāma and of Bhīṣma:

**9.5.23**  "This is that hour come round, O lover of friends, the hour of friends, wherein wise men put to the test whether one be a friend or no friend."

**9.5.24**  Be you then our hero, our leader at the head of the host; and when you have entered the battle, the Pāṇḍavas will grow faint of heart, and the Pāñcālas too, with their counsellors, will be robbed of their resolve.

**9.5.25**  Śalya said: Whatever you would have of me, O king of the Kurus, that I will do; for your pleasure all that is mine — my life, my kingdom, my wealth — is yours.

**9.5.26**  Duryodhana said: I choose you, my peerless uncle, for the command of the army. Guard us, O best of warriors, as Skanda guards the gods in battle.

**9.5.27**  Be consecrated, O lord of kings, as the son of Fire was consecrated by the gods; slay the foes in battle, O hero, as great Indra slew the Dānavas.

**9.6.1**  Saṃjaya said: Having heard these words of the king, the mighty lord of the Madras then, O king, spoke this speech to Duryodhana.

**9.6.2**  O mighty-armed Duryodhana, hear, best of those skilled in speech: these two Kṛṣṇas whom you reckon, the foremost of chariot-warriors mounted on their car — neither of them is in any way my equal in the prowess of arm.

**9.6.3**  Roused to wrath, I would do battle at the front of the fray with the whole earth in arms, with its gods and Asuras and men — how much more the Pāṇḍavas! I shall conquer in the field the sons of Pṛthā and the Somakas gathered together.

**9.6.4**  I will be the leader of your army, of that there is no doubt; and I will array such a battle-formation as the enemy shall not cross. This I tell you truly, Duryodhana, beyond all doubt.

**9.6.5**  Thus addressed, the king straightway consecrated the lord of the Madras in the midst of the army, O best of the Bhāratas, by the rite ordained in scripture, his countenance full of joy, O lord of peoples.

**9.6.6**  When he had been consecrated, a great lion-roar arose throughout your forces, O Bhārata, and the instruments of music were sounded.

**9.6.7**  The warriors then were filled with gladness, and the mighty car-warriors of the Madras, and they praised king Śalya, the splendour of the battlefield.

**9.6.8**  Conquer, O king! Live long! Slay the foes assembled! Having gained the strength of your arms, may the mighty sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, their enemies slain, rule over the whole of this entire earth.

**9.6.9**  For you are able to vanquish in battle the gods, Asuras, and men together; how much more these Somakas and Sṛñjayas, who are but mortal in their nature.

**9.6.10**  Thus extolled, the mighty lord of the Madras, that hero, felt then a joy hard for the unrestrained of soul to attain.

**9.6.11**  Śalya said: This very day, O lord of kings, I shall slay in battle all the Pāñcālas together with the Pāṇḍavas, or, slain myself, shall go to heaven.

**9.6.12**  This day let all the worlds behold me roaming fearless; this day let all the sons of Pāṇḍu, and Vāsudeva with Sātyaki,

**9.6.13**  the Pāñcālas and the Cedis, and the sons of Draupadī one and all, Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Śikhaṇḍin and all the Prabhadrakas as well —

**9.6.14**  let them behold my valour, the great power of my bow, my swiftness of hand, the force of my weapons, and the strength of my arms in the fray.

**9.6.15**  This day let the sons of Pṛthā behold, and the Siddhas with the Cāraṇas, what is the strength of my arms and what mastery is mine in weapons.

**9.6.16**  This day, beholding my valour, let the mighty car-warriors of the Pāṇḍavas, bent on resistance, attempt their manifold counter-measures.

**9.6.17**  This day I shall scatter the armies of the Pāṇḍus on every side, O lord, surpassing Droṇa and Bhīṣma and the son of the charioteer in the encounter; fighting in battle I shall range about for your pleasure, O Kaurava.

**9.6.18**  Saṃjaya said: When Śalya had thus been consecrated, O giver of honour, your forces no longer reckoned the loss of Karṇa as any calamity at all, O Bhārata.

**9.6.19**  Glad and good of heart the soldiers there became; they reckoned the sons of Pṛthā as already slain, fallen into the power of the king of the Madras.

**9.6.20**  Having gained delight, your army, O bull of the Bhāratas, slept happily through that night, and became as one whose mind was at ease.

**9.6.21**  Hearing that clamour of your host, king Yudhiṣṭhira spoke these words to the scion of the Vṛṣṇis, within hearing of all the warrior-kind.

**9.6.22**  Śalya, king of the Madras, has been made commander by the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, O Mādhava — that mighty archer, honoured throughout all the forces.

**9.6.23**  Having heard this as it truly is, do, O Mādhava, what is fitting. You are our guide and our protector; ordain what should next be done.

**9.6.24**  To him, O great king, spoke Vāsudeva, the lord of men: I know the son of Ṛtāyana truly as he is, O Bhārata.

**9.6.25**  He is valiant and of great splendour, and high-souled above the rest; accomplished and varied in the modes of combat, and endowed with swiftness of hand.

**9.6.26**  Such as Bhīṣma was, and Droṇa, and such as Karṇa was in the encounter — such, or even greater, do I deem the king of the Madras to be.

**9.6.27**  Pondering on him as he fights in the field, O Bhārata, I can find no warrior of equal stature to oppose him, O lord of men.

**9.6.28**  In strength he surpasses, in battle, Śikhaṇḍin, Arjuna, and Bhīma, and the Sātvata too, O Bhārata, and likewise Dhṛṣṭadyumna.

**9.6.29**  The king of the Madras, O great king, with the prowess of a lion and an elephant, will range about fearless when the hour comes, like wrathful Death among living creatures.

**9.6.30**  For him this day I see no fit antagonist in battle save you, O tiger among men, you whose valour is like a tiger's.

**9.6.31**  In all this world of the gods there is no man but you, O joy of the Kurus, who could slay in battle the king of the Madras when roused — him who, fighting day after day, throws your host into turmoil.

**9.6.32**  Therefore slay Śalya in battle as Maghavat slew Śambara. That hero has, at this last, been honoured by the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra.

**9.6.33**  Surely the victory is yours alone, once the lord of the Madras is slain in the fight; when he is slain, the whole great host of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son is slain.

**9.6.34**  Having heard, O great king, this my timely counsel, go forth in battle, O son of Pṛthā, against the mighty king of the Madras, and slay him, O mighty-armed one, as Vāsava slew Namuci.

**9.6.35**  And here let no pity be shown, with the thought 'this is my uncle'; setting the law of the warrior foremost, slay the lord of the Madra folk.

**9.6.36**  Having crossed the ocean of Bhīṣma and Droṇa, with the abyss that was Karṇa, do not, with your followers, sink in coming upon Śalya, who is but a puddle in a cow's hoof-print.

**9.6.37**  Whatever is the power of your austerity, and whatever your warrior strength, show it all forth in the battle, and slay that mighty car-warrior.

**9.6.38**  Having spoken these words, Keśava, the slayer of hostile heroes, went at evening to his camp, honoured then by the Pāṇḍavas.

**9.6.39**  When Keśava had departed, Yudhiṣṭhira the king of righteousness dismissed all his brothers and the Pāñcālas and the Somakas, and slept through that night like an elephant from whom the dart has been drawn.

**9.6.40**  And all those mighty archers, the Pāñcālas and the Pāṇḍavas alike, rejoicing in the death of Karṇa, slept through that night.

**9.6.41**  Its fever gone, having reached the farther shore — that host of the Pāṇḍavas, of mighty archers and great car-warriors, was filled with delight in the night, having won victory in the slaying of the charioteer's son, O sire.

**9.7.1**  Saṃjaya said: When the night had passed, king Duryodhana then said to all your men: Let the mighty car-warriors arm themselves.

**9.7.2**  Knowing the king's intent, that army armed itself; some yoked the chariots in haste, while others ran about on every side.

**9.7.3**  The elephants were caparisoned, the foot-soldiers donned their armour, and others by the thousand made ready the horses with their housings.

**9.7.4**  The din of musical instruments arose, O lord of peoples, to rouse the warriors and to stir up the troops as they marshalled.

**9.7.5**  Then all the forces, what remained of the army, O Bhārata, fully armed, were seen having made death their place of return.

**9.7.6**  Having made Śalya, king of the Madras, their commander, the great car-warriors apportioned the whole host and took their stations in the divisions.

**9.7.7**  Then all the warriors, gathering together with your son — Kṛpa and Kṛtavarman, the son of Droṇa, Śalya, and the son of Subala —

**9.7.8**  and the other kings who remained, then made this compact: that not one of them should ever fight singly against the Pāṇḍavas in any way.

**9.7.9**  For whoever should fight singly against the Pāṇḍavas, or should abandon a comrade who was fighting, he would be joined to the five great sins together with the lesser sins; we must fight together, guarding one another.

**9.7.10**  Having thus made their compact, all those great car-warriors there, placing the king of the Madras at their head, swiftly rushed against the foe.

**9.7.11**  Even so the Pāṇḍavas, O king, having arrayed their host for the great battle, advanced against all the Kauravas, ready to do combat on every side.

**9.7.12**  That host, O best of the Bhāratas, roared like a tossing sea; with its uplifted chariots and elephants it had the very semblance of a heaving ocean.

**9.7.13**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Of the fall of Droṇa and of Bhīṣma and of the son of Rādhā I have heard; tell me now further of the fall of Śalya, and of my son.

**9.7.14**  How was Śalya slain in battle by the king of righteousness, O Saṃjaya? And how was my mighty-armed son Duryodhana slain by Bhīma?

**9.7.15**  Saṃjaya said: The destruction of human bodies, the ruin of chariots, elephants, and horses — hear it, O king, with steady mind, as I recount the battle.

**9.7.16**  A strong hope then arose, O king, in your sons, even when Bhīṣma was slain and Droṇa and the charioteer's son struck down: that Śalya would slay all the sons of Pṛthā in battle, O sire.

**9.7.17**  Holding that hope in his heart, and taking comfort, O Bhārata, and leaning in the fray upon the king of the Madras, that great car-warrior, your son deemed himself to have a protector.

**9.7.18**  When, Karṇa being slain, the sons of Pṛthā raised their lion-roar, then, O king, a great fear entered the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra.

**9.7.19**  But the valiant king of the Madras, having reassured them, then arrayed, O great king, the prosperous Sarvatobhadra formation.

**9.7.20**  The valiant king of the Madras went forth in battle against the sons of Pṛthā, brandishing his bright bow, crushing of burdens and exceedingly swift.

**9.7.21**  That great car-warrior, mounting his foremost chariot drawn by horses of the Sindhu, had its banner-pole, O great king, set upon the car, adorning it.

**9.7.22**  Encompassed by that chariot, the hero, scourge of foes, stood firm, O great king — that warrior who dispelled the fear of your sons.

**9.7.23**  On the march the king of the Madras, armoured, formed the front of the formation, together with the heroic Madrakas and the invincible sons of Karṇa.

**9.7.24**  On the left was Kṛtavarman, surrounded by the Trigartas; the son of Gautama held the right flank, with the Śakas and the Yavanas.

**9.7.25**  Aśvatthāman was at the rear, surrounded by the Kāmbojas; Duryodhana was in the centre, guarded by the bulls of the Kurus.

**9.7.26**  With a great body of horse the son of Subala too, surrounded, marched forth with all his troops, and the prince of the Kaitavyas, that great car-warrior.

**9.7.27**  And the Pāṇḍavas, those mighty archers and tamers of foes, having arrayed their host and divided it into three, O great king, fell upon your army.

**9.7.28**  Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Śikhaṇḍin and Sātyaki, that great car-warrior, swiftly charged against the host of Śalya in the battle.

**9.7.29**  Then king Yudhiṣṭhira, surrounded by his own division, charged straight at Śalya, seeking to slay him, O bull of the Bhāratas.

**9.7.30**  But Arjuna, slayer of hosts of foes, swiftly charged against Hārdikya, that mighty archer, and against the bands of the Saṃśaptakas.

**9.7.31**  Bhīmasena and the Somaka great car-warriors charged against the son of Gautama, O lord of kings, seeking to slay the foe in the fight.

**9.7.32**  And the two sons of Mādrī, those great car-warriors, with their troops and forces, stood up in battle against Śakuni and Ulūka.

**9.7.33**  Even so your warriors by tens of thousands rushed against the Pāṇḍavas in the battle, full of wrath, with manifold weapons in their hands.

**9.7.34**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: When Bhīṣma the mighty archer was slain, and Droṇa, and Karṇa the great car-warrior, when the Kurus were left but few, and the Pāṇḍavas too, in the war —

**9.7.35**  when the sons of Pṛthā were sorely roused and pressing on, O Saṃjaya, what strength remained, of mine and of the foe?

**9.7.36**  Saṃjaya said: How we and the foe were arrayed for battle, O king, and what strength yet remained to us in the fray — learn that from me.

**9.7.37**  Eleven thousand chariots, O bull of the Bhāratas, and ten thousand and seven hundred elephants.

**9.7.38**  Two full hundred thousand horses, O bull of the Bhāratas, and three crores of foot-soldiers — such was the force that remained to you.

**9.7.39**  Six thousand chariots, six thousand elephants, ten thousand horses, and one crore of foot, O Bhārata —

**9.7.40**  this was the strength of the Pāṇḍavas that remained in the battle; these very ones came together for the fight, O bull of the Bhāratas.

**9.7.41**  Having thus divided our forces, O lord of kings, and standing by the counsel of the king of the Madras, we advanced against the Pāṇḍavas, greedy for victory and filled with wrath.

**9.7.42**  In like manner the valiant Pāṇḍavas, those tigers among men wearing the look of victors in battle, advanced, and with them the glorious Pāñcālas.

**9.7.43**  Thus these tigers among men, each seeking the other's death, advanced with their flood of forces toward the eastern twilight, O lord.

**9.7.44**  Then began a battle of dreadful aspect and terrifying, as your men and the enemy slew one another.

**9.8.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then began a battle that swelled the terror of the Kurus, fought against the Sṛñjayas, O lord of kings — a dreadful one, like that of the gods and the asuras.

**9.8.2**  Men, chariots, droves of elephants, horsemen by the thousands, and valiant steeds came together against one another.

**9.8.3**  The great roar of the charging elephants of dreadful form was heard, like that of rain-clouds in the sky at the appointed season.

**9.8.4**  Struck by the elephants, some chariot-warriors fell together with their cars, and heroes fled in the battle, driven before the rut-maddened beasts.

**9.8.5**  There the trained chariot-warriors with their arrows dispatched whole troops of horses and the foot-guards to the other world, O Bhārata.

**9.8.6**  The skilled horsemen, O king, surrounding the great chariot-warriors and ranging through the battle, struck them with lances, spears, and double-edged swords.

**9.8.7**  Certain bowmen, beating back the great chariot-warriors, many of them closing upon a single foe, would send him to the abode of Yama.

**9.8.8**  Other great chariot-warriors, surrounding an elephant with its fighters above, slew it as it charged with a mighty trumpeting.

**9.8.9**  And likewise the elephants, O great king, encircling on every side an enraged chariot-warrior who scattered many shafts, slew him.

**9.8.10**  Elephant rushing against elephant, and chariot-warrior against chariot-warrior in the battle, they slew one another here and there with darts, javelins, and iron shafts.

**9.8.11**  Chariots, elephants, and horses were seen in the midst of the battle, trampling down the foot-soldiers and wreaking great confusion.

**9.8.12**  The horses, adorned with yak-tail whisks, raced about as though they were swans drinking up the earth on a tableland of the Himālaya.

**9.8.13**  The ground, dappled by the hooves of those steeds, O lord of peoples, gleamed like a woman scored and marked by the scratches of nails.

**9.8.14**  With the clatter of the horses' hooves, the rumble of the chariot-wheels, the shouting of the foot-soldiers, and the trumpeting of the elephants —

**9.8.15**  with the din of the war-drums and the blare of the conches, the earth was made to resound as if by thunderclaps, O Bhārata.

**9.8.16**  From the twanging of the bows, the flashing of the swords, and the glare of the armor, nothing at all could be discerned.

**9.8.17**  Many severed arms, like the trunks of lordly elephants, writhed and twisted and jerked with a dreadful violence.

**9.8.18**  And the sound of the heads falling to the earth, O great king, was heard like that of fruits dropping from palm-trees.

**9.8.19**  With the fallen heads, drenched in blood and gleaming like burnished gold, the earth shone as though strewn with lotuses in their season, O Bhārata.

**9.8.20**  With those heads, their eyes upturned, their life gone, sorely gashed, the earth blazed forth, O great king, as if covered with white lotuses.

**9.8.21**  With the arms anointed with sandal-paste, decked with armlets and rich ornaments, fallen there, the earth shone, O lord of kings, as though set with the banners of Indra.

**9.8.22**  And with the thighs of the lords of men, hewn off in that great battle, and with others like the trunks of elephants, that battlefield was strewn over.

**9.8.23**  Thronged with hundreds of headless trunks, adorned with parasols and whisks, that forest of armies shone like a wood heaped with blossoms.

**9.8.24**  There the warriors, O great king, ranging about fearlessly, their limbs smeared with blood, were seen like kiṃśuka trees in full bloom.

**9.8.25**  The elephants too were seen, tormented by arrows and javelins, falling here and there in the battle like sundered clouds.

**9.8.26**  The host of elephants, O great king, slaughtered by the great-souled warriors, broke and scattered to every quarter like clouds driven by the wind.

**9.8.27**  Those elephants, resembling clouds, fell upon the earth on every side, looking like mountains shattered by the thunderbolt at the dissolution of an age.

**9.8.28**  Heaps of horses fallen upon the ground together with their riders were seen here and there, each great as a hill.

**9.8.29**  On the field of battle there arose a river bearing men to the other world: its water was blood, its whirlpools were chariots, its trees were banners, its gravel was bones.

**9.8.30**  Its crocodiles were arms, its current was bows, its rocks were elephants, its pebbles were horses; its mire was marrow and fat, its swans were parasols, its rafts were maces.

**9.8.31**  It was covered over with armor and turbans, its lovely trees were the banners, it teemed with rings of chariot-wheels, and it was strewn with the three-fold poles and shafts of the cars.

**9.8.32**  Gladdening to the brave and swelling the terror of cowards, that fearsome river flowed on, crowded with Kurus and Sṛñjayas.

**9.8.33**  That most terrible river, bearing men to the realm of the fathers, those heroes with arms like iron bars crossed by the boats of their mounts.

**9.8.34**  As that battle without bounds raged on, O lord of peoples, in the dreadful destruction of the four arms of the host, like the war of old between the gods and the asuras —

**9.8.35**  some there cried out to their kinsmen here and there, O scorcher of foes, while others, called to by their kinsmen, were so stricken with fear that they did not turn back.

**9.8.36**  As that terrible battle without bounds raged on, Arjuna and Bhīmasena threw the enemy into bewilderment.

**9.8.37**  That great host of yours, being slaughtered, O lord of men, swooned away here and there, like a woman overcome by drink.

**9.8.38**  And having confounded that army, Bhīmasena and Dhanaṃjaya blew their conches there and roared their lion-roars.

**9.8.39**  No sooner had they heard that mighty sound than Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Śikhaṇḍin, placing the King of Righteousness at their head, rushed upon the king of the Madras.

**9.8.40**  There we beheld a wonder of dreadful aspect, O lord of peoples — how those heroes, met with Śalya, fought him each in his own division.

**9.8.41**  The two sons of Mādrī, impetuous, accomplished in arms, drunk with the lust of war, advanced in haste, eager to overcome your force.

**9.8.42**  Then your army turned back, O bull of the Bhāratas, driven in many directions by the arrows of the Pāṇḍavas who wore the look of victors.

**9.8.43**  That host, cut down while your sons looked on, fled to every quarter, O great king, driven before the stout bowmen; and a great cry of woe arose among your warriors, O Bhārata.

**9.8.44**  "Stand, stand!" was the cry of the great-souled kṣatriyas who were put to flight, each desiring victory over the other in the fray; yet your soldiers, broken by the Pāṇḍavas, only fled the faster.

**9.8.45**  Abandoning in the battle their dear sons, brothers, grandsires, maternal uncles, sisters' sons, and kinsmen and friends as well,

**9.8.46**  your warriors fled in every direction, urging on their horses and elephants, bent only upon the saving of their own lives, O bull of the Bhāratas.

**9.9.1**  Saṃjaya said: Seeing that force shattered, the valorous king of the Madras swiftly said to his charioteer: "Urge on the horses of great speed!

**9.9.2**  Yonder stands the king Yudhiṣṭhira, son of Pāṇḍu, resplendent beneath a white parasol held aloft over him.

**9.9.3**  Bear me thither at once; behold my strength, O charioteer — the sons of Pṛthā are not able to stand before me in battle this day."

**9.9.4**  Thus addressed, the charioteer of the king of the Madras set forth toward where the truthful King of Righteousness, Yudhiṣṭhira, stood.

**9.9.5**  And the great host of the Pāṇḍavas rushing suddenly on, Śalya alone in the battle held back, as a shore holds back the heaving ocean.

**9.9.6**  The flood of the Pāṇḍava force, coming upon Śalya, O honored sir, was stayed in the battle, like the current of the Indus against a mountain.

**9.9.7**  Beholding the king of the Madras stationed for battle in the fray, the Kurus rallied back, making of death itself their turning-point.

**9.9.8**  When they had rallied, O king, their ranks arrayed in their several divisions, a most dreadful combat began, its water blood; and Nakula, drunk with the frenzy of war, closed with Citrasena.

**9.9.9**  Those two, closing upon each other, each bearing a gleaming bow, were like two upreared clouds pouring rain from the south and the north.

**9.9.10**  With showers of arrows for their rain they drenched each other in the battle; I could mark no difference there between the Pāṇḍava and the other.

**9.9.11**  Both were accomplished in arms, both mighty, both skilled in the maneuvering of chariots, both intent upon slaying the other and eager to spy out his opening.

**9.9.12**  But Citrasena, with a yellow, whetted, broad-headed shaft, severed Nakula's bow, O great king, at the place of the grip.

**9.9.13**  Then, his bow cut away, unflinching Citrasena planted three arrows with golden shafts, sharpened on stone, full upon his brow.

**9.9.14**  And with sharp arrows he sent his horses to their death, and with three shafts each he brought down his banner and his charioteer.

**9.9.15**  With those three arrows loosed from the enemy's arm and lodged in his brow, Nakula shone, O king, like a three-peaked mountain.

**9.9.16**  His bow cut, his chariot lost, the hero took up sword and shield and leapt down from his car, like a lion from a mountain-peak.

**9.9.17**  As he came on afoot, Citrasena loosed a rain of arrows upon him; but Nakula, swift in his prowess, swallowed it up with his shield.

**9.9.18**  Reaching Citrasena's chariot, that wondrous fighter, mighty-armed and unwearied, mounted it as the whole army looked on.

**9.9.19**  With its earrings, its diadem, its shapely nose, and its long fine eyes, the Pāṇḍava struck Citrasena's head from his body; and he fell from the floor of the car, radiant as the sun.

**9.9.20**  Seeing Citrasena slain there, the great chariot-warriors raised shouts of approval and many a lion-roar.

**9.9.21**  Seeing their brother slain, the two sons of Karṇa, those great chariot-warriors Suṣeṇa and Satyasena, loosing whetted arrows,

**9.9.22**  thereupon rushed swiftly against the Pāṇḍava, that best of chariot-warriors, longing to slay him, as two tigers fall upon an elephant, O king, in a great forest.

**9.9.23**  Those two fierce ones, both of them, charged that great chariot-warrior, pouring forth floods of arrows aright, as two storm-clouds pour down rain.

**9.9.24**  Pierced on every side by their arrows, the Pāṇḍava, as though delighted, took up another bow, and the valiant hero, mounting his chariot, stood in the battle like Death himself in the form of wrath.

**9.9.25**  The two brothers, O king, with arrows whose joints were well-knit, set about to break his chariot to pieces, O lord of peoples.

**9.9.26**  Then Nakula, laughing, slew with four keen, sharp arrows the four horses of Satyasena in the battle.

**9.9.27**  Then, fitting to his bow an iron shaft, gold-feathered and whetted on stone, the son of Pāṇḍu, O lord of kings, cut asunder the bow of Satyasena.

**9.9.28**  Then mounting another chariot and taking up another bow, Satyasena and Suṣeṇa together rushed upon the son of Pāṇḍu.

**9.9.29**  Unflinching, the valiant son of Mādrī pierced them both, O great king, with two arrows each at the head of the battle.

**9.9.30**  Then Suṣeṇa, enraged, that mighty car-warrior, laughing, cut the great bow of the son of Pāṇḍu in the fight with a razor-edged arrow.

**9.9.31**  Then, taking up another bow, Nakula, beside himself with rage, pierced Suṣeṇa with five arrows and cut down his banner with one.

**9.9.32**  And the bow of Satyasena and his leather hand-guard, O sire, he swiftly cut through in the fight; thereupon the people raised a shout.

**9.9.33**  Then, taking up another bow that could break the onrush and bear a heavy strain, he covered the son of Pāṇḍu with arrows on every side.

**9.9.34**  But Nakula, slayer of hostile heroes, having warded off those arrows, pierced Satyasena and Suṣeṇa with two shafts each.

**9.9.35**  Those two pierced him in return, each separately, with straight-flying shafts, and they wounded his charioteer, O lord of kings, with whetted arrows.

**9.9.36**  And Satyasena, valiant and deft of hand, cut with two separate arrows the chariot-pole and the bow of Nakula.

**9.9.37**  That foremost of car-warriors, standing in his chariot, laid hold of a chariot-lance with a golden shaft, of unblunted point, oil-washed and gleaming clean.

**9.9.38**  Resembling, O lord, a serpent-maiden of deadly venom flickering her tongue, he raised it high and hurled it at Satyasena in the encounter.

**9.9.39**  It split his heart asunder a hundredfold in the battle, O king; bereft of life and barely conscious, he fell from his chariot to the ground.

**9.9.40**  Seeing his brother slain, Suṣeṇa, beside himself with rage, swiftly rained arrows upon the son of Pāṇḍu, who was now on foot.

**9.9.41**  Seeing Nakula deprived of his chariot, the mighty Sutasoma, son of Draupadī, rushed forward, eager to save his father in the battle.

**9.9.42**  Then mounting that chariot of Sutasoma, Nakula, foremost of the Bhāratas, shone like a lion upon a mountain; taking up another bow, he gave battle to Suṣeṇa.

**9.9.43**  Those two mighty car-warriors, closing upon each other with showers of arrows, each strove hard to slay the other.

**9.9.44**  Then Suṣeṇa, enraged, planted three shafts in the son of Pāṇḍu and twenty in the arms and chest of Sutasoma.

**9.9.45**  Then, enraged, O great king, the valiant Nakula, slayer of hostile heroes, covered all the quarters about him with arrows.

**9.9.46**  Then, taking up a keen-pointed, well-sharpened crescent-headed arrow, he hurled it with great force at the son of Karṇa in the encounter.

**9.9.47**  With it he severed his head from his body, O best of kings, before the eyes of all the troops; that was as a thing of wonder.

**9.9.48**  Slain by the great-souled Nakula, O king, he fell down like a great tree growing on a bank, uprooted by the river's flood.

**9.9.49**  Seeing the slaying of Karṇa's son and the prowess of Nakula, your army fled in terror, O bull of the Bhāratas.

**9.9.50**  But that army, O great king, the valiant king of the Madras, the heroic commander and tamer of foes, protected in the battle.

**9.9.51**  Fearless he stood, O great king, having rallied the host, raising a great lion's roar and a terrible twang of his bow.

**9.9.52**  Your men in the battle, O king, guarded by that firm-bowed warrior, advanced against their foes on every side, their anguish gone.

**9.9.53**  Surrounding the great archer, the king of the Madras, on every side, the mighty host stood, O king, eager for battle all around.

**9.9.54**  Sātyaki and Bhīmasena, and the two sons of Mādrī, the Pāṇḍavas, placing at their head Yudhiṣṭhira, the tamer of foes who shrank from no shameful deed—

**9.9.55**  those heroes, ranging themselves about him in the battle, raised a lion's roar and uttered fierce twangs of bowstrings and shouts of many kinds.

**9.9.56**  Even so all your men, gathering swiftly about the lord of the Madras, full of fury, longed once more for the fight.

**9.9.57**  Then began a battle that swelled the terror of the timid, in which both your men and the foe made death their only turning-back.

**9.9.58**  As of old, O lord of peoples, was the battle of gods and asuras, so was this fight of fearless men, O king, that swelled the realm of Yama.

**9.9.59**  Then the ape-bannered son of Pāṇḍu, O king, having slain the saṃśaptakas in the battle, charged against that Kaurava army.

**9.9.60**  Even so the remaining Pāṇḍavas, with Dhṛṣṭadyumna at their head, rushed against that army, loosing whetted arrows.

**9.9.61**  Among those scattered by the Pāṇḍavas a great bewilderment arose; they could no longer tell their own divisions, nor the cardinal points, nor the quarters between.

**9.9.62**  Filled with whetted arrows sped by the Pāṇḍavas, its foremost warriors slain, shattered and scattered on every side, the Kaurava host was slaughtered by the mighty car-warriors, the sons of Pāṇḍu.

**9.9.63**  Even so the Pāṇḍava army, O king, was struck with arrows on every side in the battle by your sons, by hundreds and by thousands.

**9.9.64**  Those two armies, sorely afflicted and slaughtering each other, fell into confusion, like two rivers in the rainy season.

**9.9.65**  Then a sharp and mighty terror entered both your men and the Pāṇḍavas, O lord of kings, in that great battle so engaged.

**9.10.1**  Saṃjaya said: When that host was thrown into disorder and the warriors slew one another, the fighters fleeing and the elephants trumpeting—

**9.10.2**  the foot-soldiers wailing and groaning in that great battle, and the horses, O great king, scattering in all directions then—

**9.10.3**  a terrible destruction having arisen, a slaughter of all embodied beings, with a clash of weapons of every kind, the chariots and elephants entangled together—

**9.10.4**  a thing of joy to those drunk with war, and of swelling terror to the timid, as the warriors plunged in, each seeking to slay the other—

**9.10.5**  while that most dreadful taking of life went on, like a desperate game of dice, in a battle of terrible aspect that swelled the realm of Yama—

**9.10.6**  the Pāṇḍavas scattered your host with whetted arrows, and even so your warriors slew the soldiers of the Pāṇḍavas.

**9.10.7**  While that battle, striking terror into the fearful, was thus being waged, and the forenoon had come, near the rising of the sun—

**9.10.8**  the foes, sure of their aim and guarded by that great-souled one, gave battle to your forces, making death their only turning-back.

**9.10.9**  Before the strong, proud, sure-aiming, hard-striking Pāṇḍavas, the Kaurava army sank down, distraught like a doe in a forest fire.

**9.10.10**  Seeing that army sinking like a feeble cow in the mire, Śalya, wishing to rescue it, then advanced against the host of the Pāṇḍavas.

**9.10.11**  And the king of the Madras, greatly enraged, taking up his excellent bow, rushed in the battle against the Pāṇḍavas with bows drawn for the kill.

**9.10.12**  And the Pāṇḍavas, O great king, wearing the look of conquerors in the battle, closing upon the king of the Madras, pierced him with whetted arrows.

**9.10.13**  Then with hundreds of keen arrows the mighty king of the Madras tormented that army, while the King of Righteousness looked on.

**9.10.14**  Then, O king, portents of many kinds appeared in their multitudes, and the earth with its mountains trembled, giving forth a roar.

**9.10.15**  With trailing shafts like spears and blazing tips, breaking apart on every side, meteors fell from the sky to the earth, striking the disk of the sun.

**9.10.16**  And deer and buffaloes and birds, O lord of peoples, then circled the army, keeping it on their left, again and again, O king.

**9.10.17**  Then most fierce grew the battle of those who moved in massed array; and all the divisions joining together, O lord of men, the Kauravas, O king, advanced against the host of the Pāṇḍavas.

**9.10.18**  And Śalya, of undaunted soul, raining a shower of arrows like the thousand-eyed Indra pouring rain, deluged Yudhiṣṭhira, the son of Kuntī.

**9.10.19**  And with arrows gold-feathered and whetted on stone he struck Bhīmasena, and all the sons of Draupadī, and the two sons of Mādrī, the Pāṇḍavas.

**9.10.20**  And Dhṛṣṭadyumna, and the grandson of Śini, and Śikhaṇḍin too—each one the mighty warrior pierced with ten arrows; then he loosed a shower of arrows, as Maghavan pours rain at the end of the hot season.

**9.10.21**  Then the Prabhadrakas, O king, and the Somakas by the thousand were seen fallen and falling beneath the shafts of Śalya.

**9.10.22**  Like swarms of bees, like flights of locusts, like lightnings from the clouds, the arrows of Śalya fell down.

**9.10.23**  Elephants and horses in distress, and foot-soldiers and car-warriors alike, fell beneath the arrows of Śalya, reeled, and cried aloud.

**9.10.24**  As if possessed, the lord of the Madras, with wrath and manly might, covered his foes in the battle like the Ender sent forth by Time; roaring like a thunder-cloud, mighty was the lord of the Madras.

**9.10.25**  That host of the Pāṇḍavas, slaughtered by Śalya, ran toward Yudhiṣṭhira, the son of Kuntī, whose foe was yet unborn.

**9.10.26**  Having flung that host upon him in the battle, the deft-handed one with whetted arrows, with a great shower of shafts, afflicted Yudhiṣṭhira.

**9.10.27**  As he bore down with foot and horse, the enraged king Yudhiṣṭhira held him back with keen arrows, as one checks a rutting elephant with goads.

**9.10.28**  At him Śalya loosed a dreadful arrow like a venomous serpent; it pierced the great-souled one and then with force buried itself in the earth.

**9.10.29**  Then Vṛkodara, enraged, pierced Śalya with seven arrows, and Sahadeva with five, and Nakula with ten.

**9.10.30**  And the sons of Draupadī rained arrows upon the foe-slaying hero, the illustrious son of Ṛtāyana, as clouds upon a mountain.

**9.10.31**  Then, seeing Śalya being struck by the sons of Pṛthā on every side, Kṛtavarman and Kṛpa, both greatly enraged, rushed forward.

**9.10.32**  And Ulūka and Patatrin, and Śakuni the son of Subala, and Aśvatthāman the mighty car-warrior, smiling softly, and your sons in their entirety, guarded Śalya in the battle.

**9.10.33**  Kṛtavarman, having pierced Bhīmasena with three sharp arrows, held him back, wrath-faced as he was, with a great shower of shafts.

**9.10.34**  Kṛpa, enraged, afflicted Dhṛṣṭadyumna with showers of arrows; Śakuni assailed the sons of Draupadī, and the son of Droṇa fell upon the twins.

**9.10.35**  Duryodhana of fierce energy advanced against those two foremost of warriors in the battle, Keśava and Arjuna, and the mighty one struck them with arrows.

**9.10.36**  Thus there were hundreds of single combats between your men and the foe, of terrible aspect and wondrous to behold, here and there, O lord of peoples.

**9.10.37**  The Bhoja slew the antelope-colored horses of Bhīma in the encounter; and the son of Pāṇḍu, his horses slain, descending from the floor of his chariot, mace in hand, raising it like Death lifting his rod, fought on.

**9.10.38**  Before the face of Sahadeva the king of the Madras slew his horses; thereupon Sahadeva slew the son of Śalya with his sword.

**9.10.39**  The preceptor of Gautama's line again gave battle to Dhṛṣṭadyumna—the unflinching against the unflinching, the diligent against the more diligent still.

**9.10.40**  And the heroic sons of Draupadī—each one with ten arrows the son of the preceptor pierced, not greatly enraged, as though with a smile.

**9.10.41**  Śalya too, O king, in fury slaying the Somakas and Pāṇḍavas, once more harried Yudhiṣṭhira with whetted arrows.

**9.10.42**  At this the valiant Bhīma, enraged in battle, biting his lip, resolved upon his destruction and took up his mace.

**9.10.43**  It was like the rod of Yama, raised aloft like the Night of Doom, the bringer of death to elephants, horses, and men.

**9.10.44**  Wound about with bands of gold, like a blazing meteor, like a most fierce serpent slung in a cord, hard as the thunderbolt and wrought of iron.

**9.10.45**  Smeared with paste of sandal and aloe like a desired young woman, yet daubed with fat, marrow, and blood like the tongue of Yama, son of the Sun.

**9.10.46**  Ringing with a hundred shrill bells like the thunderbolt of Vāsava, shaped like a venomous serpent freed from its skin, and slick with the ichor of elephants.

**9.10.47**  The terror of the enemy's hosts, the delight of his own troops, renowned in the world of men, the shatterer of mountain peaks.

**9.10.48**  With which the mighty son of Kuntī, in the dwelling on Kailāsa, had challenged in his wrath the lord of Alakā, the friend of Maheśvara.

**9.10.49**  With which the mighty one, enraged, slew in the abode of the Lord of Wealth many haughty Guhyakas skilled in illusion, for the sake of the mandāra blossoms, though warned off by many, intent upon Draupadī's pleasure.

**9.10.50**  Raising that mace, set with masses of diamonds and gems, eight-sided and heavy as the thunderbolt, the mighty-armed one rushed upon Śalya in battle.

**9.10.51**  Skilled in combat, with that mace of terrible roar he crushed the four swift-coursing horses of Śalya.

**9.10.52**  Then Śalya, enraged in battle, with a roar drove a lance into Bhīma's broad chest, and the hero's weapon pierced through the armor and entered deep.

**9.10.53**  But Vṛkodara, unshaken, plucked out that very lance and with it pierced the charioteer of the king of the Madras through the heart.

**9.10.54**  His armor cleft, vomiting blood, his spirit unnerved, the charioteer fell forward in his misery, and the king of the Madras drew back.

**9.10.55**  Seeing the blow returned for blow, Śalya, his mind amazed, the steadfast one, leaning on his mace, looked upon his foe.

**9.10.56**  Then the sons of Pṛthā, glad at heart, honored Bhīmasena, beholding that terrible deed in battle wrought by him whose works are tireless.

**9.11.1**  Saṃjaya said: Seeing his charioteer fallen, Śalya, O king, swiftly took up his mace all of iron and stood firm as an unmoving mountain.

**9.11.2**  Upon him, who was like the blazing fire of Doom, like Death with noose in hand, like Kailāsa with its peak, like Vāsava with his thunderbolt—

**9.11.3**  like the three-eyed Hari with his trident, like a rutting elephant in the forest—Bhīma rushed at speed, gripping his great mace.

**9.11.4**  Then arose the blare of conches and of trumpets in their thousands, and the lions' roar of the heroes, swelling their joy.

**9.11.5**  Watching from every side those two warriors, the great war-elephants among fighters, your men and the foe alike cried, 'Well done! Well done!'

**9.11.6**  For none save the lord of the Madras, or Rāma the delight of the Yadus, could endure in combat the onset of Bhīmasena.

**9.11.7**  And likewise no other warrior in battle save Vṛkodara could endure the force of the great-souled lord of the Madras's mace.

**9.11.8**  Bellowing like two bulls, the king of the Madras and Vṛkodara, maces in hand, ranged in wheeling circles, leaping and bounding.

**9.11.9**  In the wheeling, turning paths and in the flourishing of maces, the combat of those two lions among men was without distinction between them.

**9.11.10**  Wound about with bright bands of refined gold, that mace of Śalya grew terror-spreading, as though girt with tongues of fire.

**9.11.11**  And likewise, as the great-souled Bhīma ranged his paths in circles, his mace shone forth like lightning against a cloud.

**9.11.12**  Struck by the king of the Madras, Bhīma's mace, as if ablaze, O king, gave off flashes of fire.

**9.11.13**  And likewise Śalya's mace, struck by Bhīma's mace, scattered a rain of embers, and it seemed a marvel.

**9.11.14**  As two great elephants with their tusks, as two mighty bulls with their horns, as with goads, so then did they smite each other with the heads of their maces.

**9.11.15**  Their limbs battered by the maces and in a moment drenched with blood, the two grew yet fairer to behold, like two kiṃśuka trees in flower.

**9.11.16**  Struck on left and right by the king of the Madras's mace, the mighty-armed Bhīmasena did not waver, like a mountain unmoved.

**9.11.17**  And likewise Śalya, battered again and again by the force of Bhīma's mace, did not flinch, O king, like a hill struck by a tusker.

**9.11.18**  In all directions was heard the crash of the falling maces of those two lions among men, like the clap of two thunderbolts.

**9.11.19**  Then those two of mighty valor, drawing back, both with maces uplifted, taking up positions on inner paths, ranged once more in wheeling circles.

**9.11.20**  Then, advancing eight paces, those two of superhuman deeds came together in collision, raising their iron clubs.

**9.11.21**  Each seeking the other's life, they ranged in circles, and those two adepts then displayed their special skill.

**9.11.22**  Then, raising their terrible maces, like two mountains with their peaks, they smote each other as two hills in an earthquake.

**9.11.23**  By each other's force and grievously struck by the maces, both heroes fell together at once, like two banners of Indra.

**9.11.24**  The heroes of both armies then raised a cry of dismay; both, struck hard in their vitals, lay sorely stricken.

**9.11.25**  Then Kṛpa, lifting onto his chariot, mace and all, that bull of the Madras, swiftly bore Śalya away from the battle.

**9.11.26**  But, swaying as if drunk in his daze, Bhīmasena rose again in a twinkling and, mace in hand, summoned the lord of the Madras.

**9.11.27**  Then your heroes, furnished with weapons of every kind, with the din of many instruments did battle against the host of the Pāṇḍavas.

**9.11.28**  Raising their arms and weapons, with a mighty shout, O great king, they charged, with Duryodhana at their head.
