**9.11.29**  Beholding that host, the sons of Pāṇḍu then advanced with a lion's roar, eager to slay Duryodhana.

**9.11.30**  As they rushed swiftly on, your son, O bull of the Bhāratas, with a barbed dart pierced Cekitāna sorely in the heart.

**9.11.31**  Struck by your son, he fell upon the floor of his chariot, drenched in a flood of blood, sinking into deep darkness.

**9.11.32**  Seeing Cekitāna slain, the great car-warriors of the Pāṇḍavas poured down, each in his quarter, unbroken showers of arrows.

**9.11.33**  Amid the ranks of your men the Pāṇḍavas, flushed with victory, ranged about, O great king, a sight to behold on every side.

**9.11.34**  Kṛpa, and Kṛtavarman, and the mighty son of Subala did battle against the King of Righteousness, with the king of the Madras at their fore.

**9.11.35**  Duryodhana, O great king, did battle against Dhṛṣṭadyumna, slayer of the son of Bharadvāja, a man of abounding valor and prowess.

**9.11.36**  Three thousand chariots, O king, urged on by your son and led by Droṇa's son, did battle against the Conqueror.

**9.11.37**  Fixed in their resolve for victory, having wholly cast away their lives, your men plunged in, O king, like swans into a great lake.

**9.11.38**  Then there was a terrible battle between those who craved each other's death, a slaughter of one another that yet swelled their mutual delight.

**9.11.39**  When that battle was joined, O king, that destroyer of the best of heroes, a terrible dust of the earth, stirred by the wind, arose.

**9.11.40**  By the hearing of their names and by the proclaiming of the Pāṇḍavas' names we knew one another, and those who fought on without fear.

**9.11.41**  That dust, O tiger among men, was laid by blood, and when the dust was stilled the quarters of the sky grew clear.

**9.11.42**  As that battle raged on, of terrible form and dreadful, among your men and the foe there was not one who turned his back.

**9.11.43**  Setting Brahmā's world above all, seeking victory in battle, men strove valiantly by fair fighting, longing for heaven.

**9.11.44**  To discharge the debt of their lord's bread, resolved upon their lord's cause, their minds fixed on heaven, the warriors fought on then.

**9.11.45**  Loosing weapons of many forms, the great car-warriors roared at one another and struck each other.

**9.11.46**  'Strike! Pierce! Seize! Smite! Cut him down!'—such cries were heard in your host and in theirs.

**9.11.47**  Then Śalya, O great king, longing to slay him, pierced the King of Righteousness Yudhiṣṭhira, that great car-warrior, with whetted arrows.

**9.11.48**  At him the son of Pṛthā, O great king, knowing the vital points, drove fourteen iron shafts, aiming at those points, as if with a smile.

**9.11.49**  Checking the Pāṇḍava with arrows and longing to slay him, the man of great renown, enraged, pierced him in battle with many heron-feathered shafts.

**9.11.50**  Then again, O great king, with a smooth-jointed arrow he struck Yudhiṣṭhira hard, before the eyes of all the host.

**9.11.51**  The King of Righteousness too, of great renown, in fury pierced the king of the Madras with whetted arrows fledged with heron and peacock feathers.

**9.11.52**  That great car-warrior struck down Candrasena with seventy arrows, the charioteer with nine, and Drumasena with sixty-four.

**9.11.53**  His wheel-guards slain by the great-souled Pāṇḍava, Śalya then, O king, struck down five-and-twenty of the Cedis.

**9.11.54**  He pierced Sātyaki with five-and-twenty, Bhīmasena with five, and the two sons of Mādrī in battle with a hundred whetted arrows.

**9.11.55**  As he ranged thus in the battle, O best of kings, the son of Pṛthā sent forth whetted arrows like venomous serpents.

**9.11.56**  And the son of Kuntī, Yudhiṣṭhira, with a broad-headed shaft struck the crest of his banner from his chariot as he stood at the forefront of battle.

**9.11.57**  We saw his standard, cut down by the great-souled son of Pāṇḍu, falling like a mountain peak struck asunder.

**9.11.58**  Seeing his banner fallen and the Pāṇḍava holding firm, the king of the Madras grew enraged and loosed a rain of arrows.

**9.11.59**  Śalya, that bull among Kṣatriyas of measureless soul, rained down with a shower of arrows upon that Kṣatriya, as the rain-cloud pours its showers.

**9.11.60**  Piercing Sātyaki, Bhīmasena, and the two sons of Mādrī, the Pāṇḍavas, each with five arrows, he harried Yudhiṣṭhira.

**9.11.61**  Then we saw a net of arrows spread over the breast of the Pāṇḍava, O great king, like a mass of clouds risen up.

**9.11.62**  Śalya, that great car-warrior, enraged in battle, with his smooth-jointed arrows covered over the quarters and the intermediate quarters of the sky.

**9.11.63**  Then King Yudhiṣṭhira, oppressed by the net of arrows, was robbed of his valor, as Jambha was by the slayer of Vṛtra.

**9.12.1**  Saṃjaya said: When the King of Righteousness was hard-pressed by the king of the Madras, O honored one, Sātyaki and Bhīmasena and the two sons of Mādrī, the Pāṇḍavas, surrounded Śalya with their chariots and beset him in the battle.

**9.12.2**  Seeing him, one man, beset by many great chariot-warriors, a vast cry of approval arose, and the Siddhas were thrilled; the sages too, gathered there, exclaimed, "A wonder!"

**9.12.3**  In the battle Bhīmasena, having pierced Śalya—who was like a barb in his valor—with a single arrow, struck him again with seven.

**9.12.4**  And Sātyaki, longing to protect the son of Dharma, overwhelmed the lord of the Madras with a hundred shafts and then let loose a lion's roar.

**9.12.5**  Nakula pierced him with five and Sahadeva with seven, and then swiftly struck him once more with seven.

**9.12.6**  But that hero, intent in the fray and hard-pressed by those great chariot-warriors, drew his dreadful bow—deadly in its speed, equal to its heavy task.

**9.12.7**  Śalya pierced Sātyaki with twenty-five arrows, O lord, Bhīmasena with seventy-three, and Nakula with seven.

**9.12.8**  Then, cutting the bow of the archer Sahadeva together with its nocked arrow by a broad-headed shaft in the battle, he pierced him with twenty-one.

**9.12.9**  But Sahadeva in the fray, stringing another bow, struck his uncle of abundant splendor with five arrows shaped like venomous serpents, blazing like flaming fire.

**9.12.10**  And in the battle, exceedingly enraged, he pierced his charioteer with a smooth-jointed arrow, and the man again with three more shafts.

**9.12.11**  Bhīmasena with seventy-three, Sātyaki with nine arrows, and the king of righteousness with sixty planted their shafts in Śalya's body.

**9.12.12**  Then, O great king, Śalya, pierced through by those great chariot-warriors, streamed blood from his limbs as a mountain streams red ochre.

**9.12.13**  And he pierced all those mighty archers, five arrows to each, with such force, O king, that it seemed a marvel.

**9.12.14**  Then with another broad-headed shaft, O lord, that mighty chariot-warrior cut apart the strung bow of the son of Dharma in the battle.

**9.12.15**  Then, taking up another bow, the son of Dharma, that great chariot-warrior, covered Śalya over with arrows—horses, charioteer, banner, and car alike.

**9.12.16**  Being so covered in the fray by the shafts of Dharma's son, he in turn pierced Yudhiṣṭhira with ten sharp arrows.

**9.12.17**  Then Sātyaki, enraged when the son of Dharma was sore wounded by arrows, held off the valiant lord of the Madras with floods of shafts.

**9.12.18**  He cut apart Sātyaki's great bow with a razor-tipped shaft, and struck those warriors led by Bhīmasena, three arrows to each.

**9.12.19**  Enraged at him, O great king, Sātyaki of true valor hurled a lance, gold-shafted and of great worth.

**9.12.20**  Bhīmasena then an iron shaft blazing like a serpent, Nakula a spear in the fray, Sahadeva a fair mace, and the king of righteousness a hundred-slayer—each bent on slaying Śalya in the battle.

**9.12.21**  As these missiles, loosed from the arms of the five, came hurtling swiftly down, Śalya cut the lance flung by Sātyaki to pieces with his broad-headed shafts.

**9.12.22**  And the gold-adorned arrow sped by Bhīma, that deft-handed and glorious warrior cut in two within the battle.

**9.12.23**  The dreadful, gold-shafted spear sent by Nakula, and the mace by Sahadeva, he warded off with floods of arrows.

**9.12.24**  And with two arrows he cut apart that hundred-slayer of the king, O Bhārata, before the eyes of Pāṇḍu's sons, and roared a lion's roar; but the grandson of Śini could not endure the foe's triumph in the battle.

**9.12.25**  Then, seizing another bow, Sātyaki, swooning with rage, pierced the lord of the Madras with two arrows and his charioteer with three.

**9.12.26**  Then Śalya, O great king, exceedingly wrathful, pierced them all with ten arrows each, as one goads great elephants with prods.

**9.12.27**  Held back in the fray by the king of the Madras, those great chariot-warriors, slayers of foes though they were, could not stand before his face.

**9.12.28**  Then King Duryodhana, beholding Śalya's prowess, deemed the Pāṇḍavas, the Pāñcālas, and the Sṛñjayas already slain.

**9.12.29**  Then, O king, the mighty-armed and glorious Bhīmasena, resigning his very life in his heart, gave battle to the lord of the Madras.

**9.12.30**  Nakula and Sahadeva, and Sātyaki the great chariot-warrior, surrounding Śalya then, covered him on every side with arrows.

**9.12.31**  Encircled by those four mighty archers, the great chariot-warriors of the Pāṇḍavas, the glorious king of the Madras gave them battle.

**9.12.32**  In that great battle, O king, the son of Dharma with a razor-tipped shaft swiftly slew the wheel-guard of the king of the Madras, O lord of the earth.

**9.12.33**  But when that valiant wheel-guard, a great chariot-warrior, was slain, the exceedingly mighty king of the Madras strewed down the soldiers with his arrows.

**9.12.34**  Then, O king, beholding those soldiers overwhelmed in the fray, the king of righteousness, Yudhiṣṭhira, fell to pondering.

**9.12.35**  "How shall that great word of Mādhava not prove true? Surely this king, enraged in battle, will not consume my host!"

**9.12.36**  Then, O elder-born of Pāṇḍu's line, the Pāṇḍavas with their chariots, elephants, and horses closed upon the lord of the Madras, beleaguering him on every side.

**9.12.37**  The rain of weapons, swollen with floods of manifold arms, that rose against him, he scattered in the fray, O king, as the wind scatters great clouds.

**9.12.38**  Then we beheld that rain of gold-feathered arrows, loosed by Śalya and gone aloft into the sky, like an extending swarm of locusts.

**9.12.39**  Those arrows sped by the king of the Madras at the battle's front were seen falling together like flocks of locusts.

**9.12.40**  By the gold-adorned arrows loosed from the bow of the king of the Madras, the very sky became, as it were, without a gap, O ruler of men.

**9.12.41**  Neither of the Pāṇḍavas nor of our own host could anyone there be seen, so great and dreadful was the darkness of arrows wrought in that place of terror.

**9.12.42**  Beholding the ocean-like host of the Pāṇḍavas churned thus by the mighty king of the Madras with his swift rains of arrows, the gods, gandharvas, and dānavas fell into the utmost wonder.

**9.12.43**  But he, beleaguering with arrows those who pressed from every side, O lord, and covering over the king of righteousness, roared again and again like a lion.

**9.12.44**  Covered over by him in the fray, those great chariot-warriors of the Pāṇḍavas could not then come forth against that great car-warrior in the battle.

**9.12.45**  Yet those car-warriors, led by the king of righteousness and headed by Bhīmasena, did not forsake the fray against the valiant Śalya, the splendor of the battle.

**9.13.1**  Saṃjaya said: Arjuna, pierced in the fight by Droṇa's son with many iron shafts, and by his valiant followers, the great chariot-warriors of the Trigartas, pierced Droṇa's son in the battle with but three sharp-pointed arrows.

**9.13.2**  And the other mighty archers Dhanaṃjaya pierced, two arrows to each, and the mighty-armed one moreover overwhelmed them with showers of shafts.

**9.13.3**  But your men, O bull of the Bhāratas, bristling with arrows like thorns, did not forsake Pārtha in the fray, though slain by his keen shafts.

**9.13.4**  With a line of chariots, led by the son of Droṇa, those great car-warriors surrounded Arjuna and gave him battle in the fray.

**9.13.5**  The arrows hurled by them, O king, adorned with gold, straightway filled the floor of Arjuna's chariot.

**9.13.6**  And the two Kṛṣṇas, those mighty archers, bulls among all bowmen, beholding their limbs pierced through with arrows, grew exultant, irresistible in battle.

**9.13.7**  The pole, the chariot-wheels, the shaft, the harness-cords, O lord, the yoke and the under-pole—all then became, as it were, made of arrows.

**9.13.8**  Never before, O king, had we seen or heard such a feat as your men there wrought upon Pārtha.

**9.13.9**  That chariot shone on every side with keen, bright-feathered arrows, like a celestial car ablaze upon the earth with a hundred meteors.

**9.13.10**  Then Arjuna, O great king, overwhelmed that host with smooth-jointed arrows, as a cloud overwhelms a mountain with rain.

**9.13.11**  Slain in the fray by arrows marked with Pārtha's name, they, seeing him on every side, deemed all the world become Pārtha.

**9.13.12**  Then the fire that was Pārtha, with a wondrous flame of arrows and a great wind that was the twang of his bow, swiftly burned up your army as its fuel.

**9.13.13**  Of wheels and yokes falling to the earth, of quivers, pennons, and banners together with the chariots,

**9.13.14**  of shafts and under-poles and triple bamboo frames, O Bhārata, and of axles, harness-cords, and goads on every side,

**9.13.15**  and of falling heads still wearing their earrings and turbans, and of arms and shoulders, O great king, on every side—

**9.13.16**  heaps of parasols together with fans, and of crowns, were seen along the path of Pārtha's chariot, O Bhārata.

**9.13.17**  The earth became impassable, a mire of flesh and blood, O best of the Bhāratas, like the playground of Rudra—begetting terror in the timid, swelling the joy of the brave.

**9.13.18**  Having slain in the fray, O scorcher of foes, two thousand car-warriors together with their chariot-guards, Pārtha blazed like a smokeless fire.

**9.13.19**  For as the blessed Fire, having burned up the world of things moving and unmoving, is seen smokeless, O king, so was Pārtha, that great chariot-warrior.

**9.13.20**  But the son of Droṇa, beholding the Pāṇḍava's prowess in the fray, withstood the Pāṇḍava with his chariot of towering banners.

**9.13.21**  Those two tigers among men, both with white horses, foremost of bowmen, then closed swiftly together, each seeking the other's death.

**9.13.22**  Between them, O great king, there fell a most dreadful rain of arrows, like the downpour of rain-clouds at the close of the hot season, O bull of the Bhāratas.

**9.13.23**  Contending each with the other, the two gashed one another in the fray with smooth-jointed arrows, as two bulls gore each other with their horns.

**9.13.24**  Their battle, O great king, for a long while was as if evenly matched, and there was a clash of weapons there, dreadful and great.

**9.13.25**  Then the son of Droṇa pierced Arjuna with twelve well-sharpened, gold-feathered shafts, and Vāsudeva with ten, O Bhārata.

**9.13.26**  Then Bībhatsu, laughing, drew his Gāṇḍīva bow, having for a moment honored the teacher's son in that great battle.

**9.13.27**  Savyasācin, the great car-warrior, made him bereft of horses, charioteer, and chariot, and then, after first sparing him, pierced him with three arrows.

**9.13.28**  Standing in his chariot whose horses were slain, the son of Droṇa hurled at Pāṇḍu's son an iron pestle, like an iron bludgeon.

**9.13.29**  As it came hurtling suddenly down, adorned with bands of gold, the hero Pārtha, crusher of foes, cut it into seven pieces.

**9.13.30**  Seeing the pestle cut apart, the son of Droṇa, in extreme wrath, took up a dreadful iron bludgeon like the peak of a lordly mountain; and Droṇa's son, skilled in war, hurled it at Pārtha.

**9.13.31**  Beholding that bludgeon, wrathful as Death itself, the Pāṇḍava Arjuna swiftly struck it down with five excellent arrows.

**9.13.32**  Severed by Pārtha's arrows, it fell to the earth in that great battle, rending the hearts of the kings of the earth with its crash, O Bhārata.

**9.13.33**  Then the Pāṇḍava pierced the son of Droṇa with three more arrows; yet, though sore wounded by the mighty Pārtha, the exceedingly powerful son of Droṇa was not dismayed, but stood firm in his own manliness.

**9.13.34**  Then Sudharman, O king, overwhelmed the great chariot-warrior, son of Bharadvāja, with clusters of arrows, before the eyes of all the warriors.

**9.13.35**  Then Suratha too, a great chariot-warrior of the Pāñcālas, charged in the fight upon the son of Droṇa with his thunder-roaring chariot.

**9.13.36**  Drawing his finest bow, firm and able to bear every strain, he overwhelmed him with arrows like blazing venomous serpents.

**9.13.37**  Then, as the wrathful Suratha, that mighty car-warrior, came rushing upon him, the son of Droṇa blazed with rage in the battle, like a serpent struck with a staff.

**9.13.38**  Knitting his brow into a triple furrow and licking the corners of his mouth, glaring at Suratha in his fury and drawing back his bowstring, he loosed a keen iron shaft that shone like the rod of Yama.

**9.13.39**  Cleaving through his heart, it sank in with tremendous speed, like the thunderbolt of Śakra hurled down to rend the surface of the earth.

**9.13.40**  Then he fell to the ground, smitten by that iron shaft, like the precious peak of a mountain struck down by the thunderbolt.

**9.13.41**  When that hero had been slain, the valiant son of Droṇa, foremost of car-warriors, swiftly mounted that very chariot.

**9.13.42**  Then, made ready, O great king, the son of Droṇa, frenzied in battle, fought against Arjuna, surrounded by the saṃśaptakas in the fray.

**9.13.43**  There a great battle was fought between Arjuna and his foes, while the sun stood at midday—a battle that swelled the realm of Yama.

**9.13.44**  There we beheld a marvel, witnessing their prowess: that Arjuna alone fought against those heroes all at once.

**9.13.45**  And great was the clash between Arjuna and his foes, as of old between Śatakratu and the vast host of the Daityas.

**9.14.1**  Saṃjaya said: Duryodhana, O great king, and Dhṛṣṭadyumna the son of Pṛṣata waged a tremendous battle, thick with arrows and spears.

**9.14.2**  Between the two, O great king, the streams of arrows flowed by thousands on every side, as the showers of rain from the clouds in their season.

**9.14.3**  The king, piercing the son of Pṛṣata, the slayer of Droṇa, with five iron arrows, that fierce archer then struck him again with seven.

**9.14.4**  But Dhṛṣṭadyumna, mighty and firm in valor, sorely afflicted Duryodhana in battle with seventy shafts.

**9.14.5**  Seeing the king hard pressed, his own brothers, O bull of the Bhāratas, together with a great host surrounded the son of Pṛṣata.

**9.14.6**  Hemmed in on all sides by those heroes, those mighty car-warriors, he ranged through the battle, O king, displaying the lightness of his hand.

**9.14.7**  Śikhaṇḍin, joined by the Prabhadrakas, fought against Kṛtavarman and the descendant of Gotama, that mighty car-warrior—both of them archers.

**9.14.8**  There too, O lord of the people, a tremendous and terrible battle was fought by men casting away their lives, as in a gambling-match with life itself for the stake.

**9.14.9**  And Śalya, loosing showers of arrows in every direction, sorely pressed the Pāṇḍavas, together with Sātyaki and Vṛkodara.

**9.14.10**  And the two twins as well, whose prowess equalled Yama's, he fought in battle, O lord of kings, with valor and with might.

**9.14.11**  For the Pāṇḍavas, driven back by Śalya's arrows in that great fray, certain of their mighty car-warriors could find no protector there.

**9.14.12**  Then the heroic Nakula, the son of Mādrī, when the King of Righteousness was hard pressed, rushed with speed against his maternal uncle.

**9.14.13**  Veiling Śalya in battle, Nakula, slayer of hostile heroes, smiling as he did so, pierced him with ten shafts in the breast.

**9.14.14**  They were arrows all of iron, burnished by the smith, golden-feathered, whetted on stone, sped by the engine of his bow.

**9.14.15**  But Śalya, sorely pressed by that high-souled son of his sister, in turn afflicted Nakula with feathered shafts smoothed at the joints.

**9.14.16**  Then King Yudhiṣṭhira, and Bhīmasena, and Sātyaki, and Sahadeva the son of Mādrī rushed upon the King of the Madras.

**9.14.17**  As they came swiftly on, filling the quarters and the intermediate quarters with the rattle of their chariots and making the earth tremble, the commander, conqueror of his foes, received them in battle.

**9.14.18**  Piercing Yudhiṣṭhira with three shafts, and Bhīmasena with seven, and Sātyaki with a hundred in the fight, and Sahadeva with three arrows—

**9.14.19**  Then the lord of the Madras, with a razor-edged arrow, cut through the bow of the high-souled Nakula together with the shaft set to it, O sire; and so, severed by Śalya's arrows, that bow fell to pieces.

**9.14.20**  Then, taking up another bow, the son of Mādrī, that mighty car-warrior, swiftly covered the chariot of the Madra king with feathered shafts.

**9.14.21**  And Yudhiṣṭhira and Sahadeva, O sire, each pierced the lord of the Madras in the breast with ten arrows apiece.

**9.14.22**  Then Bhīmasena with sixty and Sātyaki with nine arrows, rushing upon the Madra king, struck him with heron-feathered shafts.

**9.14.23**  Then the Madra king in wrath pierced Sātyaki with nine arrows, and again with seventy shafts smoothed at the joints.

**9.14.24**  Then he cut through his bow, shaft and all, at the grip, O sire, and dispatched his four horses to death in the encounter.

**9.14.25**  Having made Sātyaki carless, the mighty Madra king smote him on every side with a hundred shafts.

**9.14.26**  And the two enraged sons of Mādrī, and Bhīmasena the Pāṇḍava, and Yudhiṣṭhira, O Kaurava, he pierced each with ten arrows.

**9.14.27**  There we beheld the wondrous manhood of the Madra king, in that the sons of Pṛthā, even united, could not overcome him in the encounter.

**9.14.28**  Then, mounting another chariot, Sātyaki of true valor, seeing the Pāṇḍavas hard pressed and fallen into the power of the Madra king, rushed with speed, mighty as he was, upon the lord of the Madras.

**9.14.29**  As his chariot came rushing on, Śalya, the splendor of assemblies, advanced against it with his own chariot, as one furious elephant against another.

**9.14.30**  That tumultuous collision between the heroic Sātyaki and the lord of the Madras was a wondrous sight to behold, even such as once befell between Śambara and the king of the immortals.

**9.14.31**  Sātyaki, beholding the Madra king arrayed against him in battle, pierced him with ten arrows and cried, 'Stand, stand!'

**9.14.32**  And the Madra king, sorely wounded by that high-souled one, in turn pierced Sātyaki with sharp shafts of variegated feathering.

**9.14.33**  Then the sons of Pṛthā, those great archers, swiftly rushed in their chariots upon the king beset by the Sātvata—their uncle—eager to slay him.

**9.14.34**  Then there arose a tumultuous clash, its waters of blood, among those battling heroes, like lions roaring in their rage.

**9.14.35**  Their hurling of weapons against one another, O great king, was like the snarling of lions that crave the flesh of prey in the fray.

**9.14.36**  With the flooding thousands of their arrows the earth grew thickly strewn, and the very sky became, all at once, a thing made wholly of arrows.

**9.14.37**  A darkness of arrows was wrought there manifoldly on every side; like the shadow of a cloud it gathered from the shafts loosed by those high-souled ones.

**9.14.38**  There, O king, with the loosed arrows—golden-feathered and gleaming, like serpents sloughed of their skins—the quarters of the sky were made to shine.

**9.14.39**  There Śalya, crusher of foes, performed a surpassing marvel, in that that hero alone fought against many in the battle.

**9.14.40**  With the dreadful arrows let fly by the arm of the Madra king, fledged with heron and peacock feathers, raining down, the earth was overspread.

**9.14.41**  There we beheld the chariot of Śalya, O king, ranging through that great battle, even as of old that of Śakra in the destruction of the Asuras.

**9.15.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then your troops, O mighty lord, with the Madra king at their head, again rushed upon the sons of Pṛthā with great speed in the battle.

**9.15.2**  All your men, though hard pressed, charging on, frenzied for battle, in but a moment routed the sons of Pṛthā by their numbers.

**9.15.3**  The Pāṇḍavas, cut down by the Kurus, could not hold their ground, though restrained by Bhīma, while Kṛṣṇa and Pārtha looked on.

**9.15.4**  Then Dhanaṃjaya in wrath overwhelmed Kṛpa, together with his foot-followers, and Kṛtavarman as well, with a flood of arrows.

**9.15.5**  Sahadeva held back Śakuni together with his host, while Nakula, standing at his side, kept his eye upon the Madra king.

**9.15.6**  The sons of Draupadī held back most of the kings, and Śikhaṇḍin the Pāñcāla held back the son of Droṇa.

**9.15.7**  Bhīmasena, mace in hand, held back the king, while Yudhiṣṭhira the son of Kuntī held back Śalya together with his host.

**9.15.8**  Then a battle close-locked arose, here and there, between your men and the foe, who never turned back from the fight.

**9.15.9**  There we beheld the surpassingly mighty deed of Śalya in the battle, in that he alone fought against all the hosts of the Pāṇḍavas.

**9.15.10**  Śalya then appeared close beside Yudhiṣṭhira in the battle, like the planet Śanaiścara near the moon.

**9.15.11**  Having afflicted the king with arrows like venomous serpents, he rushed again upon Bhīma and covered him with showers of shafts.

**9.15.12**  Beholding that lightness of his hand and his mastery of weapons, the ranks of both the foe and your own men honored him.

**9.15.13**  And the Pāṇḍavas, hard pressed by Śalya and sorely wounded, fled, abandoning the battle, while Yudhiṣṭhira cried out to them.

**9.15.14**  As his ranks were being cut down by the Madra king, the Pāṇḍava Yudhiṣṭhira, the King of Righteousness, gave way to indignation, and then, summoning his manhood, sorely pressed the Madra king.

**9.15.15**  Resolved in mind that it should be either victory or death, that mighty car-warrior summoned all his brothers and Kṛṣṇa the Mādhava, and spoke.

**9.15.16**  Bhīṣma and Droṇa and Karṇa, and whatever other rulers of the earth strove for the cause of the Kauravas, have met their death in battle.

**9.15.17**  Each according to his portion and his zeal, you have shown your manhood; one portion alone remains for me—Śalya, the mighty car-warrior.

**9.15.18**  I therefore hope this day to conquer the lord of the Madrakas in combat; and all that is in my mind concerning this I now declare to you.

**9.15.19**  Let these two heroes, the sons of Mādravatī, be the guards of my chariot-wheels—men unconquerable even by Vāsava in battle, honored among the brave.

**9.15.20**  Worthily let these two, holding the warrior's law foremost, deserving of honor and true to their plighted word, fight against their uncle in battle for my sake.

**9.15.21**  Either Śalya shall slay me in battle, or I shall slay him—good fortune be yours! Mark, O heroes of the world, this true word of mine.

**9.15.22**  This day, O princes, I myself, having so resolved, shall fight with my uncle by the law of the warrior—for victory or for the other.

**9.15.23**  Let the yokers of chariots quickly furnish me, for this battle, with surpassing weaponry and all its appurtenances, according to the rule.

**9.15.24**  Let the grandson of Śini guard my right wheel and Dhṛṣṭadyumna the left, and let Pārtha Dhanaṃjaya be the guardian of my rear this day.

**9.15.25**  Let Bhīma, foremost of weapon-bearers, be my vanguard this day; thus shall I prove superior to Śalya in the great fray.

**9.15.26**  Thus addressed, all those who sought the king's good did accordingly; and then, O king, joy arose once more among the troops.

**9.15.27**  Of the Pāñcālas, the Somakas, and the Matsyas especially, fulfilling that vow of the King of Righteousness in the battle—

**9.15.28**  Then the Pāñcālas sounded conches and kettledrums and tabors by the hundred, and roared their lion-roars.

**9.15.29**  Enraged and swift, those bulls among the Kurus charged upon the Madra king with a great cry born of their gladness.

**9.15.30**  With the clangor of the bells of elephants, and with the blare of conches, and with the mighty din of trumpets, they made the earth resound.

**9.15.31**  Your son and the valiant king of the Madras received those onrushing warriors, the two of them standing firm like the mountains of the eastern and western horizons confronting a host of great storm-clouds.

**9.15.32**  Śalya, glorying in battle, rained showers of arrows upon Yudhiṣṭhira, that tamer of foes, even as Maghavan pours down the rain.

**9.15.33**  In like manner the high-souled king of the Kurus too, taking up his splendid bow, displayed the manifold lessons taught him by Droṇa.

**9.15.34**  He poured forth showers of arrows, wondrously, swiftly, and well; and as he ranged across the field of battle none could mark an opening in his guard.

**9.15.35**  With their various shafts the two of them wounded each other, like two valiant tigers in combat, each greedy for the flesh of its prey.

**9.15.36**  Bhīma closed with your son, that drunkard of battle; and the prince of the Pāñcālas, and Sātyaki, and the two Pāṇḍava sons of Mādrī received from every side the heroes led by Śakuni.

**9.15.37**  Then once again, O king, fierce grew the battle between your men and the foe, both eager for victory—the fruit of your evil counsels.

**9.15.38**  Duryodhana, taking deliberate aim, with a smooth-jointed arrow cut down in the fight Bhīma's banner adorned with gold.

**9.15.39**  Fair to behold, with its great net of little bells, the splendid lion that was Bhīmasena's emblem fell roaring to the ground.

**9.15.40**  And again the king severed with a sharp-edged razor-shaft his brilliant bow, which was thick as the trunk of a lordly elephant.

**9.15.41**  His bow thus cut, the fiery Bhīma, advancing with a car-borne lance, pierced your son in the breast, and Duryodhana sank down upon the floor of his chariot.

**9.15.42**  While your son lay swooning, Vṛkodara then with a razor-headed shaft struck the head of his driver from his body.

**9.15.43**  Their charioteer slain, his horses, O Bhārata, bearing the car away, fled in all directions, O king, and a cry of dismay arose.

**9.15.44**  To his rescue rushed the mighty car-warrior, the son of Droṇa, and Kṛpa, and Kṛtavarman, all bent on saving your son.

**9.15.45**  As that host was thrown into confusion, his foot-soldiers fled in terror; but the wielder of the Gāṇḍīva, twanging his bow, struck them down with his shafts.

**9.15.46**  And Yudhiṣṭhira, filled with wrath, charged upon the lord of the Madras, himself urging on his horses, ivory-white and swift as thought.

**9.15.47**  There we beheld a marvel in Kuntī's son Yudhiṣṭhira: he who before had been gentle and self-controlled had now grown terrible.

**9.15.48**  With eyes wide open and trembling with fury, the son of Kuntī cut down warriors by hundreds of thousands with his whetted shafts.

**9.15.49**  Whatever host advanced to meet him, that host the eldest of the Pāṇḍavas brought down with his arrows, O king, as great mountains are shattered by thunderbolts.

**9.15.50**  Felling many car-warriors with their horses, drivers, banners, and cars, the mighty one sported there alone, like the wind scattering the clouds.

**9.15.51**  Horses with their riders, and foot-soldiers by the thousand, he crushed in the battle, as Rudra in his wrath crushes the beasts.

**9.15.52**  Having swept the field empty on every side with his showers of arrows, he charged upon the lord of the Madras and cried, "Stand, Śalya!"

**9.15.53**  Beholding this exploit of him whose deeds were dreadful in battle, all your men were seized with terror; but Śalya advanced to meet him.

**9.15.54**  Then the two of them, swollen with fury, blew their conches born of the waters, and, challenging and defying one another, closed in combat.

**9.15.55**  Śalya overwhelmed Yudhiṣṭhira with a shower of arrows, and the son of Kuntī in turn overwhelmed the king of the Madras with showers of arrows.

**9.15.56**  Then, O king, the two heroes, the king of the Madras and Yudhiṣṭhira, were seen in the fight bristling with heron-feathered shafts, their blood gushing forth.

**9.15.57**  The two high-souled warriors shone like a flowering śalmali and a kiṃśuka tree in the forest, ablaze with splendor, frenzied in their battle for life itself.

**9.15.58**  Beholding them, none of all the armies could foretell which of the two would win: "Once he has slain the lord of the Madras, Pārtha shall this day enjoy the earth;

**9.15.59**  "or else Śalya, having slain the Pāṇḍava, shall bestow the earth on Duryodhana"—thus, O Bhārata, no certainty came to the warriors there.

**9.15.60**  Yet for Yudhiṣṭhira, as he fought, every omen turned auspicious.

**9.15.61**  Then Śalya swiftly loosed a hundred arrows at Yudhiṣṭhira, and with a sharp-pointed shaft he cut his bow asunder.

**9.15.62**  Taking up another bow, Yudhiṣṭhira pierced Śalya with three hundred arrows and severed his bow with a razor-shaft.

**9.15.63**  Then with smooth-jointed shafts he slew Śalya's four horses, and with two sharp-pointed ones the two drivers who guarded his flanks.

**9.15.64**  Then, as Śalya stood before him, with a blazing, golden, whetted broad-headed shaft he struck down his banner; and thereupon, O tamer of foes, that host of Duryodhana was broken.

**9.15.65**  Then the son of Droṇa rushed to the lord of the Madras, brought thus to such a pass, and lifting him into his own chariot bore him swiftly away.

**9.15.66**  Having gone but a little way, while Yudhiṣṭhira roared aloud, the lord of the Madras halted and mounted another chariot,

**9.15.67**  a gleaming car duly equipped, that rumbled like a great rain-cloud, furnished with all its gear in readiness—a thing to make the foe's hair stand on end.

**9.16.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then, taking up another bow, mightier and swifter still, the lord of the Madras pierced Yudhiṣṭhira and roared like a lion.

**9.16.2**  Then that bull among warriors, immeasurable of soul, rained down a shower of arrows upon the kṣatriyas, like Parjanya pouring forth the rain.

**9.16.3**  Piercing Sātyaki with ten shafts, Bhīmasena with three, and Sahadeva with three, he then pressed hard upon Yudhiṣṭhira.

**9.16.4**  That foremost of car-warriors slew sundry other mighty bowmen together with their horses, chariots, and elephants—elephants with their riders, horses with their cavalrymen, and chariots together with their warriors.

**9.16.5**  He severed arms still grasping their weapons, and banners as well, and made the earth thickly strewn with warriors as an altar is strewn with kuśa grass.

**9.16.6**  As he slaughtered the hostile hosts like Death the all-ender himself, the Pāṇḍavas, Pāñcālas, and Somakas, exceedingly enraged, surrounded him on every side.

**9.16.7**  Bhīmasena, and the grandson of Śini, and the two sons of Mādrī, foremost of men, came upon him as he closed with their king of dreadful might; and, calling upon one another, they hemmed him in.

**9.16.8**  Then those heroes, having reached that king, the lord of the Madras, best of fighters, and having hemmed him about in the battle, those manly warriors struck him with feathered shafts of terrible speed.

**9.16.9**  Guarded by Bhīmasena, by the two sons of Mādrī, and by Mādhava, the son of Dharma struck the lord of the Madras between the breasts with feathered shafts of terrible speed.

**9.16.10**  Then in the battle the throngs of your car-warriors, the foremost among them, seeing the lord of the Madras sore beset with arrows, surrounded him on all sides, with Duryodhana's consent.

**9.16.11**  Then swiftly the king of the Madra folk pierced Yudhiṣṭhira in the fight with seven shafts; and the high-souled Pārtha in turn pierced him with nine arrows, O king, amid that tumult.

**9.16.12**  With arrows drawn back to the ear, oil-washed and well-aimed, the two great car-warriors—the lord of the Madras and Yudhiṣṭhira—then veiled each other from sight in the battle.

**9.16.13**  Then swiftly those two great car-warriors, those best of kings, mighty in strength and unassailable by their foes, watching for each other's opening, pierced one another sorely with their shafts.

**9.16.14**  As those two high-souled heroes, the Madra warrior and the son of Pāṇḍu, rained masses of arrows upon each other, the great twang of their bowstrings against their wrist-guards rang out like the crash of great Indra's thunderbolt.

**9.16.15**  The two ranged the field, bright as tiger-cubs greedy for flesh in the great forest, and like two tusked lordly elephants they gashed each other, their pride born of the combat.

**9.16.16**  Then the high-souled lord of the Madras, overpowering Yudhiṣṭhira of terrible might, pierced that hero with great force in the heart, with a shaft bright as the sun and as fire.

**9.16.17**  Then, sorely wounded, the high-souled Yudhiṣṭhira too, O king, struck the lord of the Madras with a well-aimed shaft, and that bull of the Kurus took joy thereat.

**9.16.18**  Then, after a moment, that lord of kings, recovering his senses, his eyes red with wrath, his might like that of the thousand-eyed one, swiftly struck Pārtha with a hundred shafts.

**9.16.19**  Then the high-souled son of Dharma in haste and wrath, with nine shafts piercing Śalya's breast and his golden mail, smote him again with six other arrows.

**9.16.20**  Then the lord of the Madras, full of joy, drawing his bow, let fly his shafts, and with two razor-headed arrows cut the bow of the king, that bull among the Kurus.

**9.16.21**  Then in the battle the high-souled king, taking up another bow, yet more terrible, pierced Śalya on every side with whetted shafts, as great Indra pierced Namuci with his sharp-pointed bolts.

**9.16.22**  Then the high-souled Śalya, with nine shafts, having cut through the stout golden corslets of both Bhīma and king Yudhiṣṭhira, tore open their arms.

**9.16.23**  Then with another razor-shaft, blazing with the sun's splendor, he shattered the king's bow; and Kṛpa with six arrows slew the king's charioteer, who fell forward upon his face.

**9.16.24**  And the lord of the Madras with four arrows slew the horses of Yudhiṣṭhira; and having slain them, that high-souled one wrought havoc among the warriors of king Yudhiṣṭhira, the son of Dharma.

**9.16.25**  When the king was thus served, the high-souled Bhīmasena swiftly cut the bow of the lord of the Madras with a fast-flying shaft and pierced that king sorely with two arrows.

**9.16.26**  Then with another shaft he struck the head of his driver from his body, from the midst of his armor; and the sorely enraged Bhīmasena swiftly slew his four horses as well.

**9.16.27**  As that foremost of all bowmen ranged the battle alone with great swiftness, Bhīma covered him with a hundred arrows, and so too did Sahadeva, the son of Mādrī.

**9.16.28**  Seeing Śalya dazed by those shafts, Bhīma with his arrows cut through his armor; and his corslet thus rent by Bhīmasena, the lord of the Madras seized a shield set with a thousand stars.

**9.16.29**  And taking up a sword, the high-souled one leapt down from his chariot and rushed upon the son of Kuntī; having cut the chariot-pole of Nakula, that one of dreadful might bore down upon Yudhiṣṭhira.

**9.16.30**  And as that king sprang forward, wrathful, swooping like Death the all-ender, Dhṛṣṭadyumna, the sons of Draupadī, Śikhaṇḍin, and the grandson of Śini suddenly surrounded him.

**9.16.31**  Then the high-souled Bhīma cut his peerless shield to pieces with ten shafts, and with broad-headed arrows severed his sword at the hilt, roaring with joy in the midst of your host.

**9.16.32**  Beholding that deed of Bhīma, the foremost throngs of the Pāṇḍavas' car-warriors, rejoicing, raised a mighty shout, laughing aloud, and blew their conches white as the moon.

**9.16.33**  At that terrible din your army was sore afflicted and joyless, overcome with sweat, its limbs drenched in blood, half-senseless and downcast.

**9.16.34**  That king of the Madras, suddenly hemmed in by the chief Pāṇḍava warriors led by Bhīma, sped swiftly straight toward Yudhiṣṭhira, as a lion makes for its prey.

**9.16.35**  And Yudhiṣṭhira, beholding the lord of the Madras, his horses and driver slain, blazing with wrath like a kindled fire, swiftly rushed upon that foe with all his might.

**9.16.36**  Calling swiftly to mind the words of Govinda, he set his heart upon the slaying of Śalya; and Yudhiṣṭhira, standing in his chariot whose horses and driver were slain, longed for his spear.

**9.16.37**  And, marking that deed of the high-souled Śalya, and remembering the portion of doom that yet remained for him, the self-controlled king bent his mind upon Śalya's slaying, even as Viṣṇu, the younger brother of Indra, had bidden him.

**9.16.38**  Then Yudhiṣṭhira seized a spear, golden of sheen, its shaft set with gems and gold; and opening wide his blazing eyes, with mind enraged he gazed upon the lord of the Madras.

**9.16.39**  Gazed upon, O king of men, by that pure-souled monarch whose sins were purged away, that the king of the Madras was not reduced to ashes—this seems to me a marvel, O king.

**9.16.40**  Then that high-souled and foremost of the Kurus hurled with great force and swiftness at the lord of the Madras the blazing spear, its splendid fierce shaft alight with gems and coral.

**9.16.41**  As it sped down blazing, hurled with mighty strength, scattering sparks, all the assembled Kurus gazed upon it as upon a great meteor at the ending of an age.

**9.16.42**  That spear, like the Night of Doom with her noose in hand, like the dread nurse of Yama, terrible of aspect, unfailing, the very likeness of Brahmā's rod, Yudhiṣṭhira, intent, let fly in the battle.

**9.16.43**  That spear, worshipped with diligence by the sons of Pāṇḍu with perfumes and garlands, with the choicest seats, drink, and food, blazed like the fire of universal dissolution, terrible as a destroying spell of the Atharvan and Aṅgiras seers.

**9.16.44**  That spear had been forged by Tvaṣṭṛ for Īśāna's sake, a devourer of the lives and bodies of foes, with power to slay by force the beings that dwell in earth, in the sky, in the waters, and elsewhere.

**9.16.45**  It was hung with bells and banners, set with gems and adamant, gleaming with cat's-eye, its shaft of refined gold; fashioned by Tvaṣṭṛ with effort and due rite, it was unfailing, the bringer of death to the haters of Brahman.

**9.16.46**  Charged with momentum by force and effort, hallowed with terrible mantras, he then loosed it along that surpassing course for the slaying of the lord of the Madras.

**9.16.47**  "You are slain!" the King of Righteousness roared aloud, as Rudra lets fly the death-dealing shaft against Death himself, stretching forth his firm, fair-handed arm, all but dancing in his wrath.

**9.16.48**  That spear, hurled by Yudhiṣṭhira with all his might, its force not to be turned aside, Śalya roared out to receive, as fire roars to welcome a stream of butter rightly poured in oblation.

**9.16.49**  Cleaving his vital parts and his broad bright chest, and likewise his armor, it sank in as water sinks unhindered into the earth, consuming the king's wide-spread glory.

**9.16.50**  With the blood that streamed from his nose, eyes, ears, and mouth, and that welled forth from the wound, his limbs were drenched—like the great mountain Krauñca smitten by Skanda.

**9.16.51**  His armor cleft asunder by the joy of the Kurus, the great-souled one, who matched the mount of mighty Indra, flung wide his arms and fell from his chariot to the earth, like the peak of a mountain struck by the thunderbolt.

**9.16.52**  Stretching out his arms, the king of the Madras, facing the King of Righteousness, then fell to the ground like an upraised banner of Indra.

**9.16.53**  With every limb thus rent and bathed in blood, that bull among men seemed as if the earth herself had risen up in love to meet him.

**9.16.54**  Like a lover sinking upon the breast of his cherished beloved, having long enjoyed the bountiful earth as a man enjoys his dear and lovely wife, that lord, embracing her with all his limbs, seemed as one fallen asleep.

**9.16.55**  Slain in righteous battle by the righteous-souled son of Dharma, he was like a fire well-fed with rightful oblation, grown calm at the close of the sacrifice.

**9.16.56**  Though his heart was riven by the spear, his weapons and banner cast away, and though the lord of the Madras lay stilled in death, splendor would not forsake him.

**9.16.57**  Then Yudhiṣṭhira, taking up his bow bright as Indra's rainbow, scattered his foes in the fight as the king of birds scatters serpents, in an instant destroying with his keen broad-headed shafts the bodies and the lives of his enemies.

**9.16.58**  Then your soldiers, enveloped by the floods of the Pārtha's arrows, their eyes shut fast, sorely afflicted, wasting one another grievously, cast off their mail, their bodies stripped of weapons, of armor, and of life.

**9.16.59**  Then, when Śalya had fallen, the young brother of the Madra king, a charioteer the equal of his brother in every quality, rushed upon the Pāṇḍava.

**9.16.60**  And that best of men, frenzied with battle, eager to avenge his slain brother, swiftly pierced him with many iron shafts.

**9.16.61**  The King of Righteousness, moving as if in haste, pierced him with six swift arrows and, with two razor-edged shafts, cut apart his bow and his banner too.

**9.16.62**  Then, with a blazing, firm, and whetted broad-headed shaft, he struck off the head of that warrior who stood there before him.

**9.16.63**  That head, still wearing its earrings, was seen to fall from the chariot, like a dweller in heaven plummeting down upon the exhaustion of his merit.

**9.16.64**  Seeing his headless body, fallen from the chariot, its limbs drenched with blood, the army broke and fled.

**9.16.65**  When that brother of the Madra king, clad in many-hued armor, was slain, the Kurus, raising cries of woe, scattered in flight.

**9.16.66**  Seeing Śalya's younger brother slain, your men, ready to lay down their lives, covered thickly with dust, trembled exceedingly in their dread of the Pāṇḍavas.

**9.16.67**  Upon those Kauravas thus broken and terrified, O bull of the Bhāratas, Sātyaki, grandson of Śini, advanced, showering them with arrows.

**9.16.68**  As that mighty bowman came on, irresistible and hard to withstand, Hārdikya, O king, swiftly and without fear received him.

**9.16.69**  Those two great-souled and unconquered scions of the Vṛṣṇis came together, Hārdikya and Sātyaki, like two lions maddened with rut.

**9.16.70**  With arrows of spotless luster they veiled each other, bright as the sun's own beams, each rivaling the day-maker in radiance.

**9.16.71**  The shafts of those two Vṛṣṇi lions, sped by the force of their bowstrings' course, we beheld in the sky moving swift as flights of birds.

**9.16.72**  Hārdikya pierced Sātyaki with ten arrows and his horses with three, and with a single keen-jointed shaft cut asunder his bow.

**9.16.73**  Casting aside that fine bow now severed, the bull of the Śinis swiftly took up another weapon, mightier still in force.

**9.16.74**  Taking up that excellent bow, the foremost of all archers struck Hārdikya in return, full upon the breast, with ten arrows.

**9.16.75**  Then, cutting apart his chariot, yoke, and pole with well-aimed broad-headed shafts, he swiftly slew his horses and both his flanking charioteers.

**9.16.76**  With the king of the Madras slain, O king, and Kṛtavarman robbed of his chariot, the whole host of Duryodhana once again turned its back in flight.

**9.16.77**  The enemy did not perceive it, the field being shrouded by the army's dust; yet that force, the greater part of it slain, had then turned away in retreat.

**9.16.78**  Then in a moment they saw the dust that had risen from the earth laid to rest by the manifold streams of blood, O bull among men.

**9.16.79**  Then Duryodhana, seeing his own force shattered close at hand, alone held back all the Pārthas as they swiftly bore down upon him.

**9.16.80**  Seeing the Pāṇḍavas with their chariots, and Dhṛṣṭadyumna son of Pṛṣata, and the unassailable man of Ānarta, he overwhelmed them with whetted arrows.

**9.16.81**  The foe did not close with him, as mortals shun Death come near; and Hārdikya too, mounting another chariot, returned to the fight.

**9.16.82**  Then King Yudhiṣṭhira, that great chariot-warrior, in haste slew Kṛtavarman's horses with four feathered shafts, and pierced Gautama too with six keen broad-headed arrows.

**9.16.83**  Then Aśvatthāman bore Hārdikya away from Yudhiṣṭhira upon his own chariot, since the king had killed his horses and left him without a car.

**9.16.84**  Then the son of Śaradvat struck Yudhiṣṭhira in return with eight arrows, and pierced his horses with eight keen shafts.

**9.16.85**  Thus it was, O great king, that this remnant of the battle came to pass, through your evil counsel, O Bhārata, yours and your son's together.

**9.16.86**  When that foremost of mighty bowmen had been laid low in the midst of battle by the bull of the Kurus, the Pārthas, gathered together and filled with utmost joy, blew their conches at the sight of Śalya slain.

**9.16.87**  And they praised Yudhiṣṭhira in the fray as of old the gods praised Indra at the slaying of Vṛtra, and they raised the sounds of instruments of every kind, making the earth resound on every side.

**9.17.1**  Saṃjaya said: When Śalya had been slain, O king, the followers of the Madra king, seven hundred heroic chariot-warriors, sallied forth from the great host.

**9.17.2**  But Duryodhana, mounted upon an elephant like a mountain, the parasol held above him and the yak-tail whisks fanning him, restrained the Madras, crying, "You must not go! You must not go!"

**9.17.3**  Though held back again and again by Duryodhana, those heroes, longing to slay Yudhiṣṭhira, plunged into the host of the Pāṇḍavas.

**9.17.4**  Those heroes, O great king, their minds resolved upon battle, raising a mighty twang of bowstrings, fought all together against the Pāṇḍavas.

**9.17.5**  But hearing that Śalya was slain and that the son of Dharma was hard pressed by the great chariot-warriors of the Madras, who were intent upon their king's pleasure—

**9.17.6**  —the Pārtha then came on, drawing his bow Gāṇḍīva, that great chariot-warrior filling all the quarters with the thunder of his car.

**9.17.7**  Then Arjuna and Bhīma, and the two sons of Mādrī, the Pāṇḍavas, and Sātyaki, that tiger among men, and the sons of Draupadī, every one—

**9.17.8**  —Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Śikhaṇḍin, and the Pāñcālas together with the Somakas, seeking to protect Yudhiṣṭhira, ringed him about on every side.

**9.17.9**  Those bulls among men, surrounded on all sides by the Pāṇḍavas, set that army churning as sea-monsters churn the ocean.

**9.17.10**  Like the great river Gaṅgā lashed into turmoil by a head-on gale, the banner-host of the Pāṇḍavas was once again, O king, thrown into uproar.

**9.17.11**  Storming into that great army, the great chariot-warriors who held their lives as nothing made your men quake as mighty winds shake the trees.

**9.17.12**  Many cried out there, "Where is that king Yudhiṣṭhira? Nor are any of his heroic brothers to be seen here at all."

**9.17.13**  "The mighty warriors of the Pāñcālas, and Śikhaṇḍin that great chariot-fighter, and Dhṛṣṭadyumna, and the grandson of Śini, and the sons of Draupadī, all of them—"

**9.17.14**  As they spoke thus, the heroic sons of Draupadī, great chariot-warriors, and Yuyudhāna, smote those followers of the Madra king.

**9.17.15**  Some with their wheels crushed, some with their tall banners cut down, your men were seen in the battle, slain by the foe.

**9.17.16**  Beholding the Pāṇḍavas in the battle, your warriors, O king, though restrained by your son, rushed forth with violence on every side, O Bhārata.

**9.17.17**  Duryodhana sought to restrain those heroes, soothing them with gentle words, yet not one of those great chariot-warriors there obeyed his command.

**9.17.18**  Then the son of the Gāndhāra king, Śakuni, skilled in speech, spoke these words to Duryodhana, O great king:

**9.17.19**  "Why is the force of the Madras being cut down before our very eyes? This is not fitting, O Bhārata, while you stand fast in the battle.

**9.17.20**  "That we should fight in concert—such a compact was made; then why, O king, do you suffer the foe to go on slaying?"

**9.17.21**  Duryodhana said: "Restrained by me before, these men would not heed my word; for all these who have been slain plunged headlong into the host of the Pāṇḍavas."

**9.17.22**  Śakuni said: "Heroes in their wrath heed not their lord's command in battle. Enough of being angered at them; this is no time to stand aloof.

**9.17.23**  "Let us all here join together, with our horse and chariots and elephants, and go to rescue those mighty bowmen, the followers of the Madra king.

**9.17.24**  "Let us guard one another with great effort, O king." So, all taking thought together, they set out to where the soldiers were.

**9.17.25**  Saṃjaya said: Thus addressed, the king, encircled by a great host, advanced, making the earth tremble with his lion's roar.

**9.17.26**  "Strike! Pierce! Seize! Smite! Cut them down!"—such was the tumultuous cry of your army, O Bhārata.

**9.17.27**  But the Pāṇḍavas, seeing in the battle the followers of the Madra king joined together, advanced upon them, taking up a central formation.

**9.17.28**  In a moment, O lord of peoples, those heroes, the followers of the Madra king, were seen slain in the battle, hand to hand.

**9.17.29**  Then, as we pressed forward, the swift foe, their enemies slain, exultant and gathered together, raised shouts of triumph.

**9.17.30**  Then headless trunks were seen rising up on every side, and a great meteor fell straight through the disk of the sun.

**9.17.31**  With chariots shattered, with their yokes and axles broken, with great warriors slain, and with horses fallen, the earth was covered over.

**9.17.32**  Borne along by horses swift as the wind, by steeds still fastened to their yokes, warriors were seen there, O great king, upon that field of battle.

**9.17.33**  In the battle some horses dragged chariots with broken wheels; some, bearing off half a car, wandered to the ten quarters; and here and there were seen steeds still tangled in their harness.

**9.17.34**  And chariot-warriors were seen falling, O best of men, like Siddhas cast down from heaven at the exhaustion of their merits.

**9.17.35**  And when the heroes, the followers of the Madra king, had been slain, and the Pārthas, those great chariot-warriors, saw us bearing down upon them as well—

**9.17.36**  —those smiters, greedy for victory, advanced with violence, raising the din of their arrows mingled with the blare of conches.

**9.17.37**  But falling upon us once more, those striking warriors, sure of their aim, brandished their bows and raised great lion-roars.

**9.17.38**  Then, beholding the great host of the king of the Madras struck down, and seeing the valiant king of the Madras laid low in battle, the whole army of Duryodhana once again turned its face away.

**9.17.39**  Cut down, O great king, by the Pāṇḍavas flushed with victory, terrified and bewildered, it scattered to all directions, driven in panic by those firm-bowed warriors.

**9.18.1**  Saṃjaya said: When the unassailable king of the Madras, that great chariot-warrior, was felled in battle, your men and your sons for the most part turned their backs.

**9.18.2**  With that great-souled hero slain, they were like merchants whose ship has broken apart upon a bottomless, boatless, shoreless sea, longing for the farther bank.

**9.18.3**  When the king of the Madras fell, O great king, terrified and torn by arrows, defenseless and longing for a protector, they were like deer harried by a lion.

**9.18.4**  Like bulls with broken horns, like elephants with shattered tusks, at midday we withdrew, vanquished by the son of Dharma.

**9.18.5**  When Śalya was slain, O king, not one of your warriors had the mind either to rally the ranks or to display valor.

**9.18.6**  O Bhārata, the grief and the fear that came upon your warriors when Bhīṣma and Droṇa and the son of the sūta were slain, O lord of peoples — that fear and that sorrow now returned upon us even more greatly.

**9.18.7**  Despairing now of victory, with Śalya the great chariot-warrior slain, their champions dead, scattered and gashed by sharp arrows — when the king of the Madras fell, O king, your warriors fled in fear.

**9.18.8**  Some mounting horses, some elephants, some chariots, those great warriors, and the foot-soldiers too, swift of foot, fled in fear.

**9.18.9**  And two thousand war-elephants, mountainous and battle-ready, fled together when Śalya fell, goaded by the hook and the thumb.

**9.18.10**  O best of the Bharatas, your men fled from the battlefield in every direction, and even as they ran they were seen gasping, tormented by arrows.

**9.18.11**  Seeing them broken, fleeing, their spirit gone, defeated, the Pāñcālas and the Pāṇḍavas, eager for victory, rushed upon them.

**9.18.12**  And there arose a fearsome din — the twang and whistle of arrows, abundant lion-roars, and the blare of the conches of the heroes.

**9.18.13**  Seeing the Kaurava army fear-stricken and fleeing in all directions, the Pāñcālas, together with the Pāṇḍavas, spoke to one another.

**9.18.14**  Today King Yudhiṣṭhira, steadfast in truth, has conquered his foes; today Duryodhana is stripped of his blazing royal splendor.

**9.18.15**  Today, hearing that his son is slain, may King Dhṛtarāṣṭra, lord of men, fall senseless to the ground and reap the fruit of his sin.

**9.18.16**  Today let him know the son of Kuntī to be the equal of all bowmen; today that evil-minded doer of wrong shall reproach himself; today let him remember the true and salutary words once spoken by Vidura.

**9.18.17**  From this day, reduced to a servant and waiting upon the sons of Pṛthā, let the king know the sorrow that has befallen the joys of Pāṇḍu's sons.

**9.18.18**  Today let that lord of the earth know the greatness of Kṛṣṇa; today let him know the dread thunder of Arjuna's bow in battle.

**9.18.19**  Today he shall know in battle the full power of his weapons and the strength of his arms — the terrible might of the great-souled Bhīma.

**9.18.20**  When Duryodhana is slain in battle — as the demon Maya was slain by Śakra — that deed which Bhīmasena wrought in the killing of Duḥśāsana, no other in the world but Bhīma of mighty strength could have done.

**9.18.21**  Today let them know the valor of the eldest of the Pāṇḍavas, hearing that the king of the Madras — hard to withstand even for the gods — has been slain.

**9.18.22**  Today he shall know in battle the two mighty sons of Mādrī, when the valiant son of Subala is slain and the Gāndhāras are slaughtered to the last.

**9.18.23**  How could victory not be theirs, whose warriors are Dhanaṃjaya, Sātyaki, Bhīmasena, and Dhṛṣṭadyumna the grandson of Pṛṣata?

**9.18.24**  The five sons of Draupadī, the two Pāṇḍava sons of Mādrī, Śikhaṇḍin the great archer, and King Yudhiṣṭhira himself —

**9.18.25**  and whose protector is Kṛṣṇa Janārdana, the lord of the worlds — how could victory not be theirs, whose refuge is righteousness?

**9.18.26**  Bhīṣma and Droṇa and Karṇa, and the king of the Madras too, and other heroic kings besides, by the hundreds and the thousands —

**9.18.27**  who else but Pṛthā's son Yudhiṣṭhira could vanquish them in battle, he whose protector is Hṛṣīkeśa, the everlasting treasury of righteousness and fame?

**9.18.28**  Speaking thus and filled with great joy, the Sṛñjayas, O king, pursued your broken men from behind.

**9.18.29**  The valiant Dhanaṃjaya bore down upon the chariot-host, while the two sons of Mādrī and Sātyaki the great chariot-warrior fell upon Śakuni.

**9.18.30**  Beholding them all fleeing, tormented by fear of Bhīmasena, Duryodhana then spoke to his charioteer, smiling as it were.

**9.18.31**  "The son of Pṛthā shall not pass beyond me while I stand bow in hand. Drive my horses to the rear of all the troops."

**9.18.32**  "With me fighting at the rear, Dhanaṃjaya the son of Kuntī shall no more dare to pass beyond me than the great ocean dares overstep its shore."

**9.18.33**  "Behold, O charioteer, the great army overrun by the Pāṇḍavas; look upon the dust of the host raised up on every side."

**9.18.34**  "Hear the many lion-roars, dreadful and terrifying. Therefore go slowly, O charioteer, and guard the rear."

**9.18.35**  "With me standing firm in battle and the Pāṇḍavas held in check, my host will swiftly rally and return in full force."

**9.18.36**  Hearing those words of your son, worthy of a foremost hero, the charioteer gently urged on the horses adorned with gold.

**9.18.37**  Bereft of elephants, horses, and chariots, having forsaken all care for their lives, twenty-one thousand foot-soldiers stood ready for battle.

**9.18.38**  Sprung from many lands, clad in garments of many hues, those warriors took their stand, seeking great glory.

**9.18.39**  As they rushed upon one another there, exultant, a mighty clash arose, dreadful of form and terrifying.

**9.18.40**  Then, O king, men of many lands held back Bhīmasena and Dhṛṣṭadyumna the grandson of Pṛṣata with a fourfold force.

**9.18.41**  But other foot-soldiers bore down upon Bhīma alone in battle — roaring and slapping their arms, exultant, eager to attain the world of heroes.

**9.18.42**  Falling upon Bhīmasena, furious and maddened with the lust of battle, the followers of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son roared aloud and spoke of nothing else; surrounding Bhīma in the fray, they struck at him from every side.

**9.18.43**  Though assailed in battle, hemmed in by the throng of foot-soldiers, he did not stir from his chariot-seat, like Maināka the mountain.

**9.18.44**  Enraged, O great king, they set themselves to seize that great chariot-warrior of the Pāṇḍavas, and held off the other warriors.

**9.18.45**  Bhīma grew wrathful in battle at those who beset him; quickly descending from his chariot, he took his stand on foot.

**9.18.46**  Grasping his great mace plated with gold, he slew your warriors like Death with his staff in hand.

**9.18.47**  Mighty Bhīma, with his mace, crushed those twenty-one thousand foot-soldiers, bereft of chariots, horses, and elephants.

**9.18.48**  Having destroyed that host of men, Bhīma of true valor, placing Dhṛṣṭadyumna before him, soon came again into view.

**9.18.49**  The slain foot-soldiers lay upon the ground, drenched in blood, like karṇikāra trees in full bloom broken down by the wind.

**9.18.50**  Wearing garlands of varied flowers and earrings of varied kinds, of varied birth and gathered from varied lands, they lay there slain.

**9.18.51**  That great host of foot-soldiers, covered with banners and standards, cut down, appeared there dreadful of aspect and terrifying.

**9.18.52**  Then, with Yudhiṣṭhira at their head, the great chariot-warriors of the whole army charged against your great-souled son Duryodhana.

**9.18.53**  But all of them, seeing your great archers turned away in flight, did not press upon your son, as the shore does not encroach upon the abode of sea-monsters.

**9.18.54**  We beheld that marvel — the manhood of your son — that the sons of Pṛthā united could not overpass him, one though he was.

**9.18.55**  Duryodhana then spoke to his own army — sorely wounded, fled not far off, and bent upon flight —

**9.18.56**  "I see no place on earth or among the mountains where the Pāṇḍavas would not slay you once you fled there. What use is flight to you?"

**9.18.57**  "Small is their force, and the two Kṛṣṇas are sorely wounded. If we all stand firm here, our victory is assured."

**9.18.58**  "But if you flee, scattered and guilty of wrong, the Pāṇḍavas will follow and slay you. Better for us to stand firm in battle."

**9.18.59**  "Hear me, all you Kṣatriyas, as many as are gathered here: since Death ever slays alike the brave and the coward, what fool of a man who calls himself a Kṣatriya would not fight?"

**9.18.60**  "It is better for us to stand before the wrathful Bhīmasena. Death in battle is a blessing for those who fight by the law of the Kṣatriya: victorious, one wins happiness here; slain, one wins a great reward hereafter."

**9.18.61**  "There is no path to heaven better than the law of battle, O Kauravas. Slain in war, a man soon attains the worlds he has won."

**9.18.62**  Hearing his words and honoring them, the kings once more, with bows drawn to slay, turned upon the Pāṇḍavas.

**9.18.63**  As those striking warriors swiftly fell upon them in arrayed ranks, the striking sons of Pṛthā, greedy for victory, then went forth to meet them.

**9.18.64**  The valiant Dhanaṃjaya advanced into the fray upon his chariot, drawing wide the bow Gāṇḍīva, renowned throughout the three worlds.

**9.18.65**  And the two sons of Mādrī and the mighty Sātyaki, exultant, swiftly fell upon Śakuni, there where your host stood.

**9.19.1**  Saṃjaya said: When the flood of the host had turned back, Śālva, lord of the throngs of Mlecchas, in great fury advanced upon the very great army of the Pāṇḍavas.

**9.19.2**  Mounted upon a mighty elephant in rut, like a mountain, proud, resembling Airāvata, a crusher of throngs of foes —

**9.19.3**  that elephant sprung of a great and noble breed, ever honored by the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, well caparisoned by those skilled in the rules of the science, and always ridden into battle, O king —

**9.19.4**  Mounted upon it, that foremost of kings shone like the sun risen at the dawning. Upon that excellent elephant, O king, he advanced against the sons of Pāṇḍu on every side, and tore through them with sharp, dreadful arrows that were like the thunderbolt of great Indra.

**9.19.5**  As he loosed his arrows in that great battle, O king, and led warriors to Yama, neither friend nor foe could find a chink in him — as of old the Daityas could find none in the wielder of the thunderbolt.

**9.19.6**  The Pāṇḍavas, the Somakas, and the Sṛñjayas saw that single elephant on every side, ranging as though it were a thousand, like the elephant of great Indra close at hand.

**9.19.7**  The army of the foe, driven in rout, appeared on every side as if encircled; in their great fear it stood no longer firm in battle, but trampled one upon another.

**9.19.8**  Then that great Pāṇḍava host, suddenly broken by that lord of men, fled at once to the four quarters, unable to withstand the onset of that lord of elephants.

**9.19.9**  And seeing it broken by that swift charge, all your foremost warriors in the battle there honored that lord of men, and blew their conches white as the moon.

**9.19.10**  Then, hearing the shout of the Kauravas let loose in joy together with the blare of conches, the commander of the Pāṇḍavas and Sṛñjayas, the son of Pāñcāla, could not endure it for wrath.

**9.19.11**  Then that great-souled one, hastening for victory, went forth to meet that elephant, as Jambha of old, in his clash with Śakra, met Airāvaṇa, lord of elephants, the mount of Indra.

**9.19.12**  Seeing the king of the Pāñcālas suddenly rushing upon him in battle, that lion among kings swiftly sent forth that elephant, O king, to slay the son of Drupada.

**9.19.13**  As that elephant came rushing suddenly upon him, he pierced it with arrows bright as the sun—three foremost iron shafts, keen, blazing, burnished by the smith, and terrible in their speed.

**9.19.14**  Then the great-souled one loosed five more sharpened iron shafts into the beast's frontal lobes; and that foremost of elephants, sorely struck by them in battle, wheeled about and fled away in haste.

**9.19.15**  But Śālva, seizing that lord of elephants as it was suddenly driven back and put to flight, with goad and hook urged it swiftly forward, directing it against the chariot of the Pāñcāla king.

**9.19.16**  Seeing the elephant suddenly bearing down on him, the hero Dhṛṣṭadyumna swiftly seized his mace and leapt down at once from his own chariot, his limbs trembling with fear, and set foot upon the ground.

**9.19.17**  That mighty elephant crushed in an instant the chariot adorned with gold, together with its horses and driver, and lifting it up with its trunk dashed it down upon the surface of the earth.

**9.19.18**  Seeing the son of the Pāñcāla king thus hard-pressed by that foremost of elephants, Bhīma, Śikhaṇḍin, and the grandson of Śini rushed upon it at once with great speed.

**9.19.19**  Checking with their arrows the onrushing charge of the elephant that bore down upon them, those chariot-warriors held the beast in their grip; and so beset and stayed by them in battle, it staggered.

**9.19.20**  Then the king rained down his shafts on every side, as the sun pours forth its web of rays; and the hosts of chariots, smitten by those swift arrows, fled away in all directions.

**9.19.21**  Beholding that feat of Śālva, O king, all the Pāñcālas, Matsyas, and Sṛñjayas—foremost of men—filling the battle with cries of dismay, hemmed in the elephant on every side.

**9.19.22**  But the valiant king of the Pāñcālas, slayer of foes, swiftly grasping a mace like the peak of a mountain, O descendant of Bharata, pursued the elephant with speed, the hero unshaken and unafraid.

**9.19.23**  Then the swift son of the Pāñcāla king, whirling his mace, struck mightily that elephant which was like a mountain bearing the earth, dark as a rain-cloud, and streaming with rut.

**9.19.24**  Its frontal lobes shattered, the elephant, vast as a mountain, suddenly bellowed and, vomiting a great gush of blood from its mouth, fell to earth like a peak shaken loose by an earthquake.

**9.19.25**  As that lordly elephant was being brought down, and your son's host raised its cry of dismay, the hero of Śini's line struck off, with a keen broad-headed shaft, the head of King Śālva.

**9.19.26**  His head struck off in battle by the Sātvata, he fell to the ground together with the lordly elephant, like a great mountain-peak shattered and hurled down by the thunderbolt sped by the lord of the gods.

**9.20.1**  Saṃjaya said: When that valiant Śālva, the ornament of the assembly of battle, was slain, your host broke apart in headlong flight, as a great tree is shattered by the wind.

**9.20.2**  Seeing that host put to rout, the great chariot-warrior Kṛtavarman, valiant and of mighty strength, held back the army of the foe in battle.

**9.20.3**  Then those warriors turned back, beholding the Sātvata standing firm in the fray like a mountain, O king, though showered over with arrows in the fight.

**9.20.4**  Then the battle was joined afresh between the Kurus and the Pāṇḍavas, O great king—the Kurus who had turned back, having made death itself the price of their return.

**9.20.5**  Wondrous there was the battle of the Sātvata with the foe, in that he alone held back the invincible army of the Pāṇḍavas.

**9.20.6**  When that hard deed was done by him, the lion-roar of those exultant comrades, fond friends each of the other, rose vast enough to touch the heavens.

**9.20.7**  The Pāñcālas, terrified by that sound, O bull of the Bhāratas, the mighty-armed Sātyaki, grandson of Śini, came up in support of.

**9.20.8**  Closing with the mighty king Kṣemadhūrti, he sent him with seven keen arrows to the abode of Yama.

**9.20.9**  As that mighty-armed bull of Śini's line came on, scattering sharp arrows, the sagacious son of Hṛdika fell upon him with speed.

**9.20.10**  Those two bowmen, foremost of chariot-warriors, roaring like lions and bearing the choicest of weapons, rushed each upon the other.

**9.20.11**  The Pāṇḍavas with the Pāñcālas, and the other warriors, those best of kings, became spectators of those two lions among men.

**9.20.12**  With iron shafts and calf-toothed arrows the two great chariot-warriors of the Vṛṣṇis and Andhakas struck at each other, like two rutting elephants in their joy of battle.

**9.20.13**  Ranging through manifold courses of fight, the son of Hṛdika and the bull of Śini's line again and again hid each other from view beneath a rain of arrows.

**9.20.14**  We saw the shafts of those two lions of the Vṛṣṇis, driven up by the force and might of their bows, speeding swift as locusts through the sky.

**9.20.15**  Closing with that lone hero of true deeds, the son of Hṛdika pierced his four horses with four keen arrows.

**9.20.16**  Then that long-armed hero, enraged like an elephant stung by the goad, pierced Kṛtavarman with eight excellent shafts.

**9.20.17**  Then Kṛtavarman, with arrows whetted on stone and loosed from a fully drawn bow, struck Sātyaki with three and with one severed his bow.

**9.20.18**  Casting aside that excellent bow now cut asunder, the bull of Śini's line, the grandson of Śini, swiftly took up another bow with an arrow already fitted.

**9.20.19**  Taking up that excellent bow, the best of all bowmen, of mighty valor, mighty wisdom, and mighty strength, strung it.

**9.20.20**  Unable to brook the cutting of his bow by Kṛtavarman, that surpassing chariot-warrior, in his wrath, swiftly bore down upon Kṛtavarman.

**9.20.21**  Then with ten well-sharpened arrows the bull of Śini's line slew the driver, the horses, and the banner of Kṛtavarman.

**9.20.22**  Then, O king, the great bowman Kṛtavarman, that mighty chariot-warrior, beheld his chariot wrought with gold, its horses and driver slain.

**9.20.23**  Seized by great wrath, O sire, he raised a lance and hurled it with the force of his arm, eager to slay the bull of Śini's line.

**9.20.24**  But the Sātvata in battle, splitting that lance with keen arrows, brought it down shattered to pieces, as though to confound the Mādhava; and then with another broad-headed shaft he struck him upon the breast.

**9.20.25**  His horses slain and his charioteer slain in battle by Yuyudhāna, accomplished in arms, Kṛtavarman sank down upon the earth.

**9.20.26**  When that hero was unhorsed by Sātyaki in their single chariot-duel, a very great fear came upon all the host.

**9.20.27**  And exceeding great was the despondency that came upon your son when Kṛtavarman, his driver slain and his horses slain, was robbed of his chariot.

**9.20.28**  Marking that tamer of foes with his horses slain and his driver slain, Kṛpa rushed forward, O king, eager to slay the bull of Śini's line.

**9.20.29**  Lifting him up into the seat of his chariot, before the eyes of all the bowmen, the mighty-armed one swiftly bore him away even from the field of battle.

**9.20.30**  When the grandson of Śini held the field, O king, and Kṛtavarman was bereft of his chariot, the whole host of Duryodhana once more turned its face away in flight.

**9.20.31**  The foe did not perceive it, the host being veiled in dust; and your men fled away, O king, save only King Duryodhana.

**9.20.32**  But Duryodhana, beholding his own host broken close at hand, dashed forward swiftly with speed and alone held back them all.

**9.20.33**  In wrath he checked all the Pāṇḍavas, and Dhṛṣṭadyumna son of Pṛṣata, and Śikhaṇḍin, and the sons of Draupadī, and the bands of the Pāñcālas,

**9.20.34**  and the Kekayas and the Somakas and the Pāñcālas too, O sire; unflinching and unassailable, he held them back with keen weapons.

**9.20.35**  Your mighty son stood intent in the fray, blazing forth like the great fire of the sacrifice, hallowed by sacred chants.

**9.20.36**  The foe could not stand against him in battle, as mortals cannot stand against death; and meanwhile the son of Hṛdika, mounting another chariot, came up once more.

**9.21.1**  Saṃjaya said: Your son, O great king, foremost of chariot-warriors, standing in his car, shone irresistible in battle like the glowing Rudra.

**9.21.2**  By his thousands of arrows the earth was covered over, and he drenched the foe with shafts as rain-streams drench the mountains.

**9.21.3**  There was no man among the Pāṇḍavas in that great battle, nor horse, nor elephant, nor chariot, that was left unwounded by his arrows.

**9.21.4**  Whatever warrior I beheld in the fight, O lord of peoples, that one was found covered over with arrows by your son, O descendant of Bharata.

**9.21.5**  As an army appears veiled in the dust raised by its own host, so did the host appear shrouded by the arrows of that great-souled one.

**9.21.6**  We saw the earth become all arrows, O lord of the earth, made so by Duryodhana, that bowman of nimble hand.

**9.21.7**  Among those thousands of warriors, your own and the foe's alike, Duryodhana alone was a man indeed—such is my judgment.

**9.21.8**  There we beheld the wondrous prowess of your son, in that the sons of Pṛthā, all united, could not overcome him single-handed, O descendant of Bharata.

**9.21.9**  He pierced Yudhiṣṭhira in battle with a hundred shafts, O bull of the Bhāratas, and Bhīmasena with seventy, and Sahadeva with seven,

**9.21.10**  and Nakula with sixty-four, and Dhṛṣṭadyumna with five, and the sons of Draupadī with seven, and Sātyaki with three; and with a broad-headed shaft he cut asunder the bow of Sahadeva, O sire.

**9.21.11**  Casting aside that broken bow, the valiant son of Mādrī took up another great bow and rushed upon the king; then in the fight he pierced Duryodhana with ten arrows.

**9.21.12**  And then the hero Nakula, that great bowman, pierced the king with nine arrows of dreadful form, and raised a shout.

**9.21.13**  Sātyaki too struck the king with a smooth-jointed shaft, and the sons of Draupadī with seventy-three, and the king of righteousness with seven, and Bhīmasena tormented the king with eighty arrows.

**9.21.14**  Though showered on every side with masses of arrows by those great-souled ones, he did not waver, O great king, while all the host looked on.

**9.21.15**  The deftness, the dexterity, and the valor of that great-souled one—all beings, all men, beheld them surpassing all.

**9.21.16**  But the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, O lord of kings, having gone but a little way, and seeing not their king, turned back, clad once more in their mail.

**9.21.17**  As they came rushing on, a terrible tumult arose, like that of the troubled ocean by night in the season of rains.

**9.21.18**  Rejoining the unconquered king in battle, those great bowmen advanced to meet the Pāṇḍavas, who came on with weapons raised to slay.
