**9.21.19**  The son of Droṇa held back Bhīmasena, enraged in battle; and then, O great king, by the arrows loosed on every side, neither the heroes nor the quarters nor the intermediate points could be discerned in the fray.

**9.21.20**  Those two, both grim in their deeds, both hard to bear, O descendant of Bharata, fought in dreadful fashion, each seeking to repay blow with blow, terrifying the whole world, the skin of their forearms torn by the lashing of their bowstrings.

**9.21.21**  But the hero Śakuni in battle pressed Yudhiṣṭhira hard; and the mighty lord, the son of Subala, having slain his four horses, raised a roar that made all the hosts tremble.

**9.21.22**  In that interval the valiant Sahadeva bore away the unconquered king, the hero, from the battle upon his own chariot.

**9.21.23**  Then Yudhiṣṭhira, king of righteousness, mounting another chariot, pierced Śakuni with nine shafts and again with five, and that foremost of all bowmen sent forth a mighty roar.

**9.21.24**  Wondrous was that battle, and dreadful of form, O sire, delighting the hearts of the onlookers, and graced by the presence of the Siddhas and Cāraṇas.

**9.21.25**  But Ulūka, of measureless spirit, rushed upon the great bowman Nakula, that frenzied warrior, with showers of arrows on every side.

**9.21.26**  And in like manner the valiant Nakula, in battle, hemmed in the son of Subala on every side with a great shower of arrows.

**9.21.27**  Those two heroes there in battle, scions of noble houses and mighty chariot-warriors, were seen fighting on, each having wronged the other.

**9.21.28**  And so too Kṛtavarman, fighting the grandson of Śini, that scorcher of foes, shone resplendent, O king, like Śakra contending with Bala in battle.

**9.21.29**  Duryodhana, having cut the bow of Dhṛṣṭadyumna in the fight, then pierced him, his bow severed, with keen arrows.

**9.21.30**  Dhṛṣṭadyumna too, taking up a supreme weapon in the battle, gave fight to the king before the eyes of all the bowmen.

**9.21.31**  Great was the combat between those two in the fray, O bull of the Bhāratas, like the clash of two rut-maddened lordly elephants with temples streaming.

**9.21.32**  But the descendant of Gautama, enraged in the battle, that hero pierced the mighty sons of Draupadī with many keen-jointed shafts.

**9.21.33**  His battle with them was like that of an embodied soul with its senses—terrible in aspect, irresistible, and utterly without bounds.

**9.21.34**  They harried him as the senses harry a foolish man, while he, roused to fury, fought them back in the encounter.

**9.21.35**  Thus wondrous, O Bhārata, was his battle with them, surging up again and again, O lord, like a man's strife with his senses.

**9.21.36**  Foot soldiers locked with foot soldiers, tuskers with tuskers, horses with horses, and chariot warriors with chariot warriors; and once more, O lord of peoples, the tumult grew terrible in aspect.

**9.21.37**  "This is wondrous, this is terrible, this is fierce!"—thus, O lord, were the combats, O great king, dreadful and many.

**9.21.38**  Those tamers of foes, closing upon one another in the fray, pierced and slew as they met in the great encounter.

**9.21.39**  A fierce dust, raised by their weapons, was seen, O king, whirled up by the gusting wind and by the galloping horsemen.

**9.21.40**  Stirred up by the chariot wheels and by the breathing of the elephants, the dust, tawny as a cloud at twilight, rose to the path of the sun.

**9.21.41**  When the sun was shrouded by that dust and robbed of its brightness, the earth was overspread, and so too those heroes, the mighty chariot-warriors.

**9.21.42**  In but a moment all around grew free of dust, O best of the Bhāratas, for upon the earth, drenched with the blood of heroes, that fierce and dreadful-looking dust then was laid.

**9.21.43**  Then, O great king, I beheld the single combats, O Bhārata, ranged by foremost and by eldest, at that most dreadful noontide; and there, O lord of kings, the blazing gleams of the armor were seen.

**9.21.44**  A roaring tumult arose in the battle from the falling shafts, like that of a great bamboo forest burning on every side.

**9.22.1**  Saṃjaya said: As that battle, terrible in aspect and fearsome, went on, the army of your son was there broken by the Pāṇḍavas.

**9.22.2**  But your son, checking with great effort those mighty chariot-warriors, set them to fight the host of the Pāṇḍavas.

**9.22.3**  Suddenly the warriors who sought your son's welfare turned back to the fight, and when they had thus turned, the battle grew most dreadful.

**9.22.4**  Of your men and of the foe it was like the war of gods and demons; neither among the enemy nor in your host was there a single one who turned his back.

**9.22.5**  They fought one another by guesswork and by signals, and great was the slaughter among them as they battled each against each.

**9.22.6**  Then King Yudhiṣṭhira, filled with mighty wrath, longing to conquer in the fray the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra together with their king—

**9.22.7**  having pierced the son of Śaradvat with three gold-feathered, stone-whetted shafts, with four he struck down the goodly horses of Kṛtavarman.

**9.22.8**  But Aśvatthāman bore away the illustrious son of Hṛdika; then the son of Śaradvat struck Yudhiṣṭhira back with eight shafts.

**9.22.9**  Then King Duryodhana sent forth in the battle seven hundred chariots to where that king, Yudhiṣṭhira the son of Dharma, stood.

**9.22.10**  Those chariots, manned by their warriors and swift as mind and wind, rushed in the fray against the chariot of Kuntī's son.

**9.22.11**  On every side, O great king, surrounding Yudhiṣṭhira, they made him invisible with their arrows, as clouds make the sun.

**9.22.12**  The chariot-warriors led by Śikhaṇḍin, greatly enraged, brooked it not, and with chariots yoked to swiftest steeds and hung about with nets of little bells they came to guard Yudhiṣṭhira, the son of Kuntī.

**9.22.13**  Then there arose a fierce combat between the Pāṇḍavas and the Kurus, its waters of blood, swelling the realm of Yama.

**9.22.14**  Having slain the seven hundred chariots of the assailant Kurus, the Pāṇḍavas together with the Pāñcālas once again held the foe at bay.

**9.22.15**  There was a great battle of your son with the Pāṇḍavas, the like of which we had never seen, nor ever heard tell.

**9.22.16**  As that boundless battle raged on every side, while warriors of your side and of the other were being slain—

**9.22.17**  while the warriors shouted, and the finest conches were blown, and lion-roars were raised on high, and the bowmen thundered—

**9.22.18**  as the battle swelled beyond measure, while vital points were being pierced, and warriors greedy for victory ran to and fro, O sir—

**9.22.19**  when destruction had arisen on every side upon the earth, the spring of grief, and the partings were stripped from the hair of many noble women—

**9.22.20**  as that boundless and most dreadful battle went on, then portents most dreadful appeared, foreboding ruin, and the earth, with its mountains and forests, shook, sounding aloud.

**9.22.21**  Meteors with trails and firebrands, O king, shattering on every side, fell from the sky to the earth, striking the orb of the sun.

**9.22.22**  Winds blew from every quarter, raining gravel low to the ground; the elephants shed tears, and a violent trembling seized them.

**9.22.23**  Heedless of these terrible and most dreadful portents, the warriors, having taken counsel together, stood unshaken for battle once more, on the fair and holy field of Kurukṣetra, eager to attain heaven.

**9.22.24**  Then Śakuni, son of the king of Gāndhāra, said: "Fight you in front, while I strike the Pāṇḍavas from behind."

**9.22.25**  Then, as we set forth, the impetuous warriors of the Madras, exultant, raised a cry of triumph, and so did the others.

**9.22.26**  But coming again upon us, those unassailable ones, sure of their aim, shaking their bows, overwhelmed us with showers of arrows.

**9.22.27**  Then, seeing there the army of the Madra king slain by the foe, the host of Duryodhana once more turned its back.

**9.22.28**  But the mighty king of Gāndhāra then spoke again these words: "Turn back, you who know not righteousness! Fight! What good is flight to you?"

**9.22.29**  A division of ten thousand horse, O bull of the Bhāratas, belonged to the king of Gāndhāra, fighting with gleaming lances.

**9.22.30**  Charging with that force amid the carnage of men, they struck the Pāṇḍava host from behind with whetted shafts.

**9.22.31**  Driven on all sides like a cloud by the wind, the very great army of the Pāṇḍavas was broken, O great king.

**9.22.32**  Then Yudhiṣṭhira, seeing his own army broken close at hand, unflustered, urged on the mighty Sahadeva:

**9.22.33**  "Yonder son of Subala, in his mail, pressing upon our rear, is slaughtering the army. Behold him, O Pāṇḍava, that evil-minded one!

**9.22.34**  Go you and the sons of Draupadī, and slay Śakuni the son of Subala; I, with the Pāñcālas, shall guard the chariot division, O sinless one.

**9.22.35**  Let all the elephants and the horses go with you, and three thousand foot soldiers; slay Śakuni the son of Subala."

**9.22.36**  Then seven hundred elephants, mounted by bow-bearing men, and five thousand horse, and the valiant Sahadeva,

**9.22.37**  and three thousand foot soldiers, and the sons of Draupadī—all of them in the battle rushed upon Śakuni, frenzied for combat.

**9.22.38**  But the son of Subala, O king, pressing past the Pāṇḍavas, the glorious one, greedy for victory, struck the army from behind.

**9.22.39**  But the impetuous horsemen of the swift Pāṇḍavas, in fury, passing those chariots, entered the host of Subala's son.

**9.22.40**  Those heroic horsemen there, taking their stand amid the elephants, overwhelmed with showers of arrows the great host of Subala's son.

**9.22.41**  That great battle, with maces and lances upraised, the resort of no coward, broke out, O king, through your evil counsel.

**9.22.42**  The twang of bowstrings ceased; the chariot-warriors became mere onlookers, for no difference could be seen between one's own and the foe.

**9.22.43**  The Kurus and Pāṇḍavas beheld the lances loosed by heroes' arms, O bull of the Bhāratas, fall like a shower of shooting stars.

**9.22.44**  With bright pikes flying here and there, O lord of peoples, the sky, overspread, shone exceedingly.

**9.22.45**  The aspect of the falling lances, O king, was on every side like that of locusts in the sky, O best of the Bhāratas.

**9.22.46**  Their every limb sprinkled with blood, their riders flung off, the horses fell down by hundreds and by thousands.

**9.22.47**  Crushed against one another as they met, some were seen unwounded yet vomiting blood from their mouths.

**9.22.48**  Then there fell a terrible darkness as the army was shrouded in dust; I saw those tamers of foes withdrawing from that place—the horses, O king, and the men, when all was wrapped in dust.

**9.22.49**  Others, fallen upon the earth, vomiting much blood, locked hair to hair, the men could not so much as stir.

**9.22.50**  Dragging one another from the backs of their horses, the mighty men, closing like wrestlers, slew each other; and many here, their lives fled, were dragged along by the horses.

**9.22.51**  Many others, who had longed for victory, fallen upon the earth, were seen here and there—men who had thought themselves heroes.

**9.22.52**  With men blood-bespattered, their arms hewn off, their hair torn away, the earth was seen strewn by hundreds and by thousands.

**9.22.53**  There it was not possible for any horse to go far, for the floor of the earth was covered with slain horses and their riders.

**9.22.54**  With their armor drenched in blood, their weapons seized and upraised, with diverse and dreadful arms, seeking each other's death, drawn close together in the fray, their soldiery for the most part slain—

**9.22.55**  having fought thus for a while, the son of Subala, O lord of peoples—Śakuni—then withdrew with six thousand horse that remained.

**9.22.56**  In like manner the Pāṇḍava force, bathed in blood, withdrew with six thousand horse that remained, their mounts spent.

**9.22.57**  But the horsemen of the Pāṇḍavas, blood-bespattered, drawn close together in the fray, for the most part with lives cast aside, said:

**9.22.58**  "Here one cannot fight with chariots, how much less with great elephants! Let chariots go against chariots, and elephants against elephants.

**9.22.59**  Śakuni has gone back and taken his stand within his own division; King Subala's son will not come again to battle."

**9.22.60**  Then the sons of Draupadī and those rut-maddened great elephants went to where the Pāñcāla Dhṛṣṭadyumna, the mighty chariot-warrior, was.

**9.22.61**  Sahadeva too, O scion of Kuru, when the cloud of dust had risen, went alone to where King Yudhiṣṭhira was.

**9.22.62**  Then, when they had gone, Śakuni the son of Subala, enraged, once more struck the host of Dhṛṣṭadyumna upon its flank.

**9.22.63**  Then once again a tumultuous battle broke forth, with lives cast aside, between your men and the foe, each seeking the other's death.

**9.22.64**  For they glared at one another in that meeting of heroes, and the warriors fell, O king, by hundreds and by thousands.

**9.22.65**  As heads were severed by swords in that destruction of the world, there arose a great noise, like that of palmyra fruits falling.

**9.22.66**  From bodies sundered and falling to the earth, and from arms still grasping their weapons, and from thighs, O lord of peoples, there arose a great rattling, crackling sound that made the hair stand on end.

**9.22.67**  Striking down brothers, sons, and even friends with their sharpened weapons, the warriors fell upon one another like birds contending over a scrap of flesh.

**9.22.68**  Enraged against each other, closing in man to man, crying "I first! I first!" they slew by the thousand.

**9.22.69**  With their riders flung from the saddle and lifeless, the horses too fell in heaps by the hundred and by the thousand.

**9.22.70**  From the swift-coursing horses, twitching and crushed, and from the armored men groaning aloud, O lord of peoples,

**9.22.71**  there arose a tumultuous din of lances, swords, and javelins, as they pierced one another's vital points—O king, the fruit of your evil counsel.

**9.22.72**  Overcome by exhaustion, frenzied, their mounts worn out, parched with thirst, and torn by sharpened weapons, your men yet pressed on into battle.

**9.22.73**  Maddened by the smell of blood, many here lost their senses and struck down foe and kinsman alike, whoever came next within reach.

**9.22.74**  And many warriors, greedy for victory, their lives gone out, fell to the earth, O king, enveloped in showers of arrows.

**9.22.75**  On that day of riotous feasting for wolves, vultures, and jackals, a fearful destruction of the host came to pass before your son's very eyes.

**9.22.76**  The ground, O lord of peoples, lay strewn with the bodies of men and horses and dappled with streams of blood, deepening the dread of the faint-hearted.

**9.22.77**  Hewn again and again by swords, pikes, and spears, neither your men nor the Pāṇḍavas turned back, O Bhārata.

**9.22.78**  Striking with all their might so long as breath held within them, the warriors fell, vomiting blood from their wounds.

**9.22.79**  A headless trunk was seen, gripping a head by its hair and raising a keen sword drenched in blood.

**9.22.80**  And as many headless trunks rose up, O ruler of men, and from the reek of blood, the warriors sank into a swoon.

**9.22.81**  Then, as the clamor died down, Saubala, with the few horses that remained to him, advanced against the great host of the Pāṇḍavas.

**9.22.82**  Thereupon the Pāṇḍavas, greedy for victory, rushed swiftly upon him—footmen and elephants and horsemen with weapons upraised.

**9.22.83**  Hemming him in within an enclosure and surrounding him on every side, they struck at him with weapons of every kind, eager to reach the farther shore of the war.

**9.22.84**  But your men, seeing them charging in from every quarter, rushed against the Pāṇḍavas with horse, foot, elephants, and chariots.

**9.22.85**  Some footmen, brave men with their weapons spent, struck at one another in the fray with feet and fists, and so fell.

**9.22.86**  Charioteers fell from their chariots and elephant-riders from their elephants, like Siddhas cast down from their celestial cars when their merit is exhausted.

**9.22.87**  Thus, straining against one another in the great battle, the warriors slew—fathers, brothers, comrades, and others their very sons.

**9.22.88**  So lawless was that battle, O best of the Bhāratas, as the dreadful welter of javelins, swords, and arrows raged on.

**9.23.1**  Saṃjaya said: When the clamor had grown faint and the host lay slaughtered by the Pāṇḍavas, Saubala turned about with the seven hundred horses that remained.

**9.23.2**  Riding swiftly to the army, that tamer of foes urged it on, crying again and again to the joyous men, "Fight on!"—and he asked the warriors there, "Where now is the king, the great chariot-hero?"

**9.23.3**  Hearing Śakuni's words, they answered, O bull of the Bhāratas: "Yonder stands the Kaurava, the great chariot-hero, in the midst of the battle—

**9.23.4**  there where that vast parasol shines bright as the full moon, where these armored chariots stand with their guards and finger-shields ready.

**9.23.5**  Where this tumultuous roar resounds like the thundering of a storm-cloud, go thither swiftly, O king, and there you shall behold the Kaurava."

**9.23.6**  Thus addressed by those brave men, Śakuni Saubala then set forth to where your son stood, O ruler of men, encircled on all sides by heroes who never turned back in battle.

**9.23.7**  Then, beholding Duryodhana stationed amid the array of chariots, Śakuni heartened all your chariot-warriors,

**9.23.8**  and with a joyful air, O lord of peoples, deeming his task all but accomplished, spoke this word to King Duryodhana:

**9.23.9**  "Slay the chariot-host, O king! All the horsemen have been vanquished by me. Yet Yudhiṣṭhira cannot be conquered in battle without staking one's very life.

**9.23.10**  When that chariot-host guarded by the Pāṇḍava is destroyed, we shall slay these elephants and the footmen and the rest as well."

**9.23.11**  Hearing his words, your men, greedy for victory, fell joyfully and at speed upon the army of the Pāṇḍavas.

**9.23.12**  All with quivers thrown open and bows seized fast, brandishing their bows, they raised a lion's roar.

**9.23.13**  Then once more, O lord of peoples, arose the din of bowstrings and palm-guards, and a most terrible noise of well-loosed arrows.

**9.23.14**  Seeing them drawn near at speed with bows upraised, Dhanaṃjaya, the son of Kuntī, said to the son of Devakī:

**9.23.15**  "Urge on the horses without fear and plunge into this ocean of armies. Today I shall make an end of my enemies with sharpened arrows.

**9.23.16**  Eighteen days, O Janārdana, has this great war now lasted, as the hosts have closed against each other.

**9.23.17**  The banner-host of these great-souled men, once seeming endless, has come to destruction in the battle this day—behold how fate works its will!

**9.23.18**  The army of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son, vast as the sea, O Mādhava, on meeting us has become like the print of a cow's hoof, O undecaying one.

**9.23.19**  Had he made peace when Bhīṣma fell, well would it have been, O Mādhava; but that fool, the witless son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, did not do so.

**9.23.20**  The word that Bhīṣma spoke, salutary and wholesome, O Mādhava, that too senseless Suyodhana did not heed.

**9.23.21**  When Bhīṣma had fallen, struck down upon the face of the earth, I know not what cause it was for which the war went on.

**9.23.22**  Wholly deluded and utterly foolish I deem the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, who, when the son of Śaṃtanu had fallen, yet renewed the battle.

**9.23.23**  And thereafter, when Droṇa, best of those versed in the Veda, was slain, and Rādheya and Vikarṇa, still the slaughter did not cease.

**9.23.24**  When this army had but little left, and the son of the charioteer, that tiger among men, was struck down together with his son, still the slaughter did not cease.

**9.23.25**  When the hero Śrutāyus was slain, and Jalasaṃdha of the Pauravas, and King Śrutāyudha, still the slaughter did not cease.

**9.23.26**  When Bhūriśravas and Śalya and Śālva too, O Janārdana, and the heroes of the Avantis fell, still the slaughter did not cease.

**9.23.27**  When Jayadratha was slain, and the rākṣasa Alāyudha, and Bāhlika and Somadatta, still the slaughter did not cease.

**9.23.28**  When the brave Bhagadatta was slain, and Sudakṣiṇa of the Kāmbojas, and Duḥśāsana laid low, still the slaughter did not cease.

**9.23.29**  And though he saw brave warriors slain, the kings of many provinces one by one, and the mighty fallen in battle, O Kṛṣṇa, still the slaughter did not cease.

**9.23.30**  Seeing the commanders of whole armies struck down by Bhīmasena, whether from delusion or from greed, still the slaughter did not cease.

**9.23.31**  What other man born in a royal house, a Kaurava above all, would wage so great and so fruitless a feud, save Suyodhana?

**9.23.32**  Knowing his foe the greater in worth, in strength, and in valor, what man not deluded, what wise man who discerns his good from his harm, would still make war?

**9.23.33**  Since his mind would not bend even when you spoke the salutary word of peace with the Pāṇḍavas, how should he hearken to any other?

**9.23.34**  He by whom Bhīṣma the son of Śaṃtanu, and Droṇa, and Vidura too were rebuffed when they pleaded for peace—what cure is there for him today?

**9.23.35**  He who in his folly, O Janārdana, spurned his aged father, and who slighted and rejected his mother as she spoke the salutary word out of love for his welfare—to whose counsel would such a man ever incline?

**9.23.36**  Plainly this man was born to be the ruin of his line, O Janārdana; so do his conduct and his policy appear, O lord of peoples. He will never yield us our kingdom—such is my conviction, O undecaying one.

**9.23.37**  Many a time, my friend, the great-souled Vidura told me: while he lives, the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra will in no wise grant you your portion.

**9.23.38**  "So long as breath stirs within the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, O giver of honor, so long will he work his wickedness against you who are sinless."

**9.23.39**  "He cannot be overcome by any means save war," O Mādhava—thus Vidura, the seer of truth, said to me ever again.

**9.23.40**  All that resolve of the wicked one I now perceive this day to be true, even as it was spoken by the great-souled Vidura.

**9.23.41**  For that fool who, having heard the wholesome and faithful counsel of the son of Jamadagni, made light of it, stands surely at the threshold of his ruin.

**9.23.42**  For it was foretold by many Siddhas at the very birth of Suyodhana: "On this wicked one's coming, the warrior race shall go to its destruction."

**9.23.43**  And that word of theirs has now come true, O Janārdana, for kings in multitudes have gone to their destruction for Duryodhana's sake.

**9.23.44**  This day, O Mādhava, I shall slay all his warriors in battle; and when the warriors are swiftly slain and his camp left empty,

**9.23.45**  he will choose, to his own undoing, to do battle with us; and so, by my reckoning, this feud shall come to its end, O Mādhava.

**9.23.46**  Thus do I see it, O Vārṣṇeya, reflecting with my own understanding, by the word of Vidura and by the conduct of that wicked one.

**9.23.47**  Drive on into the Bhārata host, O hero, that I may slay with sharpened arrows the wicked Duryodhana and his army in the fray.

**9.23.48**  This day I shall secure the welfare of the Dharma King, O Mādhava, by destroying this feeble army of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son before his very eyes."

**9.23.49**  Saṃjaya said: Thus addressed by Savyasācin, the Dāśārha, reins in hand, fearlessly entered amid the flood of the enemy's host in battle.

**9.23.50**  That dreadful army—where the choicest bows were thorny thickets bristling with lances, its pathways maces and iron clubs, its great trees the chariots and elephants—

**9.23.51**  strewn over with creepers of horse and foot—Govinda of great renown, plunging into it, ranged through it there on his banner-decked chariot.

**9.23.52**  Those white horses, O king, bearing Arjuna into battle and urged on by the Dāśārha, were seen ranging in all directions.

**9.23.53**  Then Savyasācin, scorcher of foes, advanced on his chariot into the fight, scattering keen arrows by the hundred like a cloud pouring down its torrents of rain.

**9.23.54**  A great noise arose from the smooth-jointed arrows as the field was covered over with shafts by Savyasācin.

**9.23.55**  Without lodging in the armor, the floods of arrows fell to the ground—shafts loosed from the Gāṇḍīva, whose touch was like the thunderbolt of Indra.

**9.23.56**  Striking down men, elephants, and horses too, O lord of peoples, the arrows fell upon the field like swarms of humming locusts.

**9.23.57**  All was overspread with the shafts loosed from the Gāṇḍīva, and in the battle neither the quarters nor the intermediate quarters could be discerned.

**9.23.58**  The whole world seemed filled with arrows graven with Pārtha's name—gold-feathered, washed in oil, and burnished by the smith.

**9.23.59**  Scorched by Pārtha as elephants are scorched by fire, the Kauravas, struck down by his whetted arrows, sank to the ground.

**9.23.60**  Bearing his bow and arrows, Pārtha, blazing as it were, O Bhārata, burned up the warriors in battle as a flaming fire consumes dry brushwood.

**9.23.61**  As at the forest's edge a roaring blaze, set loose by the woodsmen and leaving a black trail, would burn the thicket thick with trees and canopied with withered creepers—a fierce conflagration grown mightily in strength—

**9.23.62**  even so did he, blazing with his volleys of iron shafts, his arrows for flames and his fierce radiance flaring high and low, in his impetuous fury, brooking nothing, the swift one, burn up the whole army of your sons.

**9.23.63**  His life-taking arrows, well loosed and golden-feathered, were not stayed by any armor; nor did he loose a second shaft upon a man, a horse, or a mighty elephant.

**9.23.64**  For with arrows of many shapes and forms he penetrated the host of great chariot-warriors, and he alone slew the army of your sons as the Wielder of the Thunderbolt slew the Daityas.

**9.24.1**  Saṃjaya said: Of those heroes who hurled their weapons and strove and would not turn back, Dhanaṃjaya with the Gāṇḍīva made every resolve come to nothing.

**9.24.2**  He was seen loosing arrows whose touch was like Indra's thunderbolt, unbearable and of vast power, as a thundercloud pours down its torrents.

**9.24.3**  That army, O best of the Bhāratas, being slaughtered by the Diademed One, fled from the battle before the very eyes of your son.

**9.24.4**  Some chariots had their draught-horses slain, others their charioteers killed, and some, O lord of peoples, had their axles, yokes, wheels, and poles shattered.

**9.24.5**  Others had their arrows spent, and others were tormented by shafts; and some, though unwounded, fled all together, oppressed by fear.

**9.24.6**  Some, taking up their sons, their mounts for the most part slain, cried aloud; others called upon their fathers, and yet others upon their comrades.

**9.24.7**  And some, O tiger among men, abandoning their kinsmen, their brothers and their relations here and there, took to flight, O lord of peoples.

**9.24.8**  Many great chariot-warriors there, grievously pierced and swooning, were seen groaning—men struck down by Pārtha's arrows.

**9.24.9**  Others, mounting them upon a chariot and reviving them for a while, once rested and their thirst quenched, went again to the fight.

**9.24.10**  Some, having set them aside and gone, returned once more eager to fight, frenzied for battle, carrying out the command of your son.

**9.24.11**  Others, having drunk water and refreshed their mounts and donned their armor again, O best of the Bhāratas—

**9.24.12**  and others, having revived their brothers and set them down in the camp—some their sons and some their fathers—once more longed for battle.

**9.24.13**  Some, having made ready their chariots each in due order, O lord of peoples, and charging upon the Pāṇḍava host, once more longed for battle.

**9.24.14**  Those heroes, covered over with nets of little bells, shone forth like the Daityas and Dānavas bent upon the conquest of the three worlds.

**9.24.15**  Some, coming on suddenly in chariots adorned with gold, fought against Dhṛṣṭadyumna amid the ranks of the Pāṇḍavas.

**9.24.16**  And Dhṛṣṭadyumna the Pāñcāla, and Śikhaṇḍin the great chariot-warrior, and Śatānīka the son of Nakula did battle against that host of chariots.

**9.24.17**  Then the Pāñcāla, enraged and surrounded by a great force, charged in fury, eager to slay your men.

**9.24.18**  Then, as he rushed on, your son, O ruler of men, sent forth against him many a volley of arrows, O Bhārata.

**9.24.19**  Then, O king, Dhṛṣṭadyumna was swiftly struck in the arms and the breast by your son the archer with many iron shafts.

**9.24.20**  That mighty archer, deeply pierced like an elephant goaded by the hook, sent his four horses to their death with his arrows, and with a broad-headed shaft swept the head of his charioteer from its body.

**9.24.21**  Then King Duryodhana, his chariot destroyed, mounted upon the back of a horse and withdrew a little way, that subduer of foes.

**9.24.22**  But seeing his own host broken in prowess, your mighty son, O great king, went to where the son of Subala was.

**9.24.23**  Then, when the chariots were broken, three thousand great elephants surrounded on every side the five Pāṇḍava chariot-warriors.

**9.24.24**  Those five, encircled in battle by the host of elephants, O Bhārata, those tigers among men shone like planets veiled in clouds.

**9.24.25**  Then Arjuna, O great king, the mighty-armed, a sure marksman, with his white horses and Kṛṣṇa for his charioteer, sallied forth in his chariot.

**9.24.26**  Surrounded on all sides by those elephants like mountains, he crushed that host of elephants with his bright, sharp iron shafts.

**9.24.27**  There we saw great elephants slain each by a single arrow, fallen and falling and torn asunder by Savyasācin.

**9.24.28**  But Bhīmasena, beholding those elephants—he, the mighty one, like a maddened elephant himself—seized his great mace in his hand and, leaping swiftly down from his chariot, fell upon them like Death with his rod in hand.

**9.24.29**  Seeing that great chariot-warrior of the Pāṇḍavas with his mace upraised, your soldiers trembled and voided their dung and urine; and the whole host was thrown into terror when Vṛkodara held his mace in hand.

**9.24.30**  We saw elephants like mountains, their frontal globes split by Bhīmasena's mace, covered with dust and running wildly.

**9.24.31**  Those elephants, having charged forth and been struck by Bhīmasena's mace, fell with a cry of anguish like mountains whose wings had been clipped.

**9.24.32**  Beholding those many elephants with their temples split, fleeing this way and that and falling, your soldiers were seized with terror.

**9.24.33**  Yudhiṣṭhira too, in great wrath, and the two Pāṇḍava sons of Mādrī slew the elephant-fighters with their whetted, vulture-feathered arrows.

**9.24.34**  But Dhṛṣṭadyumna, having vanquished the king in battle—when your son had withdrawn and taken refuge upon the back of a horse—

**9.24.35**  and seeing all the Pāṇḍavas surrounded by the elephants, Dhṛṣṭadyumna, O great king, the son of the Pāñcāla king, together with all the Prabhadrakas, went forth eager to slay those elephants.

**9.24.36**  But not seeing Duryodhana the subduer of foes among the host of chariots, Aśvatthāman and Kṛpa and Kṛtavarman the Sātvata asked the warriors there: "Where now has Duryodhana gone?"

**9.24.37**  Not seeing the king while the slaughter of men went on, those great chariot-warriors, thinking your son slain there, with downcast faces asked after your son.

**9.24.38**  Some said, "His charioteer being slain, he has gone where the son of Subala is"; but others among the sorely wounded warriors said otherwise.

**9.24.39**  "What need have you of Duryodhana? You shall see him if he lives. Fight, all of you together! What will the king do for you?"

**9.24.40**  Those warriors, their limbs wounded and their mounts for the most part slain, and themselves tormented by arrows, spoke as it were indistinctly.

**9.24.41**  "Let us slay this whole force by which we have been surrounded. These Pāṇḍavas, having slain our elephants, are all advancing upon us."

**9.24.42**  But hearing their words, the mighty Aśvatthāman, leaving behind that irresistible host of the Pāñcāla king—

**9.24.43**  and Kṛpa and Kṛtavarman—those heroes of the firmest bows, abandoning the host of chariots, went to where the son of Subala was.

**9.24.44**  Then, when they had gone, the Pāṇḍavas, with Dhṛṣṭadyumna at their head, came on, O king, slaughtering your men.

**9.24.45**  Seeing those great chariot-warriors rushing on, exultant and bold, those heroes who had then given up all hope of life, your host became for the most part pale of face.

**9.24.46**  Seeing them surrounded, their weapons spent, I, O king, with a force of two divisions, having staked my own life—

**9.24.47**  myself the fifth, fought against the host of the Pāñcāla, taking my stand in that place where the son of Śaradvat stood.

**9.24.48**  We five joined battle, tormented by the arrows of the Diademed One, against Dhṛṣṭadyumna and his great host; there was a mighty struggle there for us. Vanquished by him, we all withdrew thereupon from the fight.

**9.24.49**  Then I saw Sātyaki, that great chariot-warrior, coming on with four hundred chariots, and the hero charged at me in the battle.

**9.24.50**  Hardly escaped from Dhṛṣṭadyumna, my mounts wearied, I fell upon the host of the Mādhava as an evildoer falls into hell. There the battle was dreadful for a while, exceedingly terrible.

**9.24.51**  But the mighty-armed Sātyaki, having slain my retinue, took me captive alive as I lay swooning, fallen upon the ground.

**9.24.52**  Then in but a moment that host of elephants was destroyed—by the mace of Bhīmasena and by the iron shafts of Arjuna.

**9.24.53**  With the great elephants like mountains crushed all around, the passage of the Pāṇḍavas became, as it were, not easily made.

**9.24.54**  Then the mighty Bhīmasena cleared paths for the chariots of the Pāṇḍavas, O great king, by dragging away the great elephants.

**9.24.55**  Aśvatthāman and Kṛpa and Kṛtavarman the Sātvata, not seeing Duryodhana the subduer of foes among the host of chariots, went searching for the king, your son, that great chariot-warrior.

**9.24.56**  And leaving the Pāñcāla behind, they went to where the son of Subala was, distraught at not seeing the king while the slaughter of men went on.

**9.25.1**  Saṃjaya said: When that host of elephants had been destroyed by the son of Pāṇḍu, O Bhārata, and the army was being slaughtered by Bhīmasena in the battle,

**9.25.2**  and seeing Bhīmasena the subduer of foes ranging thus, like wrathful, life-taking Death with his rod in hand—

**9.25.3**  the surviving sons of yours, O king, gathering together in the battle—since the Kaurava, your son Duryodhana, was nowhere to be seen—those brothers of one womb, joining as one, rushed upon Bhīmasena.

**9.25.4**  Durmarṣaṇa, O great king, Jaitra, Bhūribala, and Ravi—these your sons, joining as one, charged upon Bhīmasena from every side and hemmed him in on all quarters.

**9.25.5**  Then Bhīma, O great king, mounting once more upon his own chariot, loosed whetted arrows into the vital parts of your sons.

**9.25.6**  Your sons, overwhelmed by Bhīma in that great battle, sought to drive Bhīmasena back as one might turn an elephant back from a slope.

**9.25.7**  Then Bhīma, enraged in the fight, swiftly struck off the head of Durmarṣaṇa with a razor-edged arrow and brought it down to the ground.

**9.25.8**  Then with another broad-headed shaft that pierced through every armor, Bhīma the great chariot-warrior slew your son Śrutānta.

**9.25.9**  Then, piercing Jayatsena with an iron shaft as if in mirth, the subduer of foes felled that Kaurava from the floor of his chariot; and he fell from the car, O king, to the earth, and swiftly died.

**9.25.10**  Then Śrutarvan, in wrath, pierced Bhīma, O honored one, with a hundred straight-jointed arrows fledged with vulture feathers.

**9.25.11**  Then Bhīma, enraged in the fight, struck these three—Jaitra, Bhūribala, and Ravi—with three arrows like poison and fire.

**9.25.12**  Those great chariot-warriors, slain, fell from their chariots to the ground like kiṃśuka trees in springtime, dappled with blossoms, cut down.

**9.25.13**  Then that scorcher of foes, striking Durvimocana with another sharp iron shaft, sent him to his death.

**9.25.14**  Slain, that foremost of chariot-warriors fell to the ground from his own car, like a tree growing on a mountain peak broken by the wind.

**9.25.15**  Then he slew Duṣpradharṣa and Sujāta, two of your sons, each with two arrows apiece in the battle, at the head of the host; and those two foremost of chariot-warriors fell, their limbs pierced by his shafts.

**9.25.16**  Then, marking another of your sons striving in the fight, Bhīma, hard to withstand in battle, pierced him with a broad-headed shaft; and he fell slain from his mount before the eyes of all the archers.

**9.25.17**  But seeing his many brothers slain by one man in the battle, Śrutarvan, overcome with indignation, advanced upon Bhīma.

**9.25.18**  Drawing his very great bow adorned with gold and loosing many arrows like poison and fire,

**9.25.19**  But he, O king, having cut apart the bow of the Pāṇḍava in that great battle, then covered him, now bowless, with twenty arrows.

**9.25.20**  Then, taking up another bow, Bhīmasena the mighty chariot-warrior overwhelmed your son with shafts and cried, "Stand! Stand!"

**9.25.21**  Great was the combat between those two, wondrous in form and terrible, such as once raged in olden days between Jambha and Vāsava.

**9.25.22**  By the bright arrows loosed by those two, arrows like the rod of Yama, the whole earth was veiled, and the sky, and all the quarters likewise.

**9.25.23**  Then Śrutarvan, swollen with wrath, drawing his bow, struck Bhīmasena in battle, O king, upon the arms and the breast with his shafts.

**9.25.24**  Pierced deep, O great king, by your bow-wielding son, Bhīma, enraged, heaved like the great ocean at the turning of the moon.

**9.25.25**  Then Bhīma, seized by fury, O lord, sent your son's charioteer and his four horses with his arrows to the abode of Yama.

**9.25.26**  Marking him robbed of his chariot, that hero of measureless spirit covered him with feathered shafts, displaying the swiftness of his hand.

**9.25.27**  Śrutarvan, his chariot lost, O king, took up sword and shield; but even as he seized the sword and the gleaming shield bright with a hundred moons, the Pāṇḍava with a razor-edged shaft struck his head from his body.

**9.25.28**  Then, his head severed by the razor-shaft, the body of that great-souled warrior fell from the chariot, making the earth resound.

**9.25.29**  When that hero had fallen, your warriors, bewildered with fear yet eager to fight, rushed upon Bhīmasena in the battle.

**9.25.30**  As they swiftly charged from that ocean of host left over from the slain, the mailed and valiant Bhīmasena received them; and they, closing upon him, encircled him on every side.

**9.25.31**  Then Bhīma, hemmed in by your men with their whetted arrows, harried them all as the thousand-eyed one harried the Asuras.

**9.25.32**  Then, having slain five hundred great chariot-warriors with their car-guards, he struck down in the fight a host of elephants, seven hundred more.

**9.25.33**  Having slain ten thousand foot-soldiers with his supreme shafts, and eight hundred horse, the Pāṇḍava shone resplendent.

**9.25.34**  And Bhīmasena, son of Kuntī, having slain your sons in battle, deemed his purpose fulfilled and his birth made fruitful, O lord.

**9.25.35**  As he thus fought on, cutting down your men, your troops could not bear even to look upon him, O Bhārata.

**9.25.36**  Having routed all the Kurus and slain their foot-followers, he made a din with his two arms, terrifying the great elephants.

**9.25.37**  With the greater part of its warriors slain, your army, O lord of peoples, was reduced to a wretched remnant, O great king.

**9.26.1**  Saṃjaya said: Duryodhana, O great king, and Sudarśana too, your son, the survivors then of the slaughter, stood firm amid their horsemen on the field.

**9.26.2**  Then, beholding Duryodhana posted among his horse, the son of Devakī spoke to Dhanaṃjaya, the son of Kuntī.

**9.26.3**  "The foes are slain for the most part, our kinsmen are kept safe; and yonder bull of Śini's line has turned back, having taken Saṃjaya captive."

**9.26.4**  "And Nakula is worn out, and Sahadeva too, O Bhārata, having fought in battle against the wicked retainers of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son."

**9.26.5**  "Forsaking Suyodhana, these three stand apart: Kṛpa, and Kṛtavarman, and Droṇa's son, the great chariot-warrior."

**9.26.6**  "Yonder stands the prince of Pāñcāla, endowed with surpassing splendor, having slain Duryodhana's host together with all the Prabhadrakas."

**9.26.7**  "Yonder is Duryodhana, O Pārtha, posted amid his horsemen, the parasol held above him, gazing about again and again."

**9.26.8**  "Having marshalled his whole force, he stands in the midst of the field; slay him with your whetted arrows, and you shall have accomplished your end."

**9.26.9**  "Seeing the elephant-host destroyed and you come upon them, O subduer of foes, slay Suyodhana before these others scatter in flight."

**9.26.10**  "Let someone go to the prince of Pāñcāla, bidding him come at once; for with his force worn out, this sinful one shall not escape, my friend."

**9.26.11**  "Having slain your whole force in the battle, the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, deeming the sons of Pāṇḍu vanquished, bears himself with a great show of pride."

**9.26.12**  "Seeing his own host slain and hard-pressed by the Pāṇḍavas, the king will surely come into the battle, even unto his own destruction."

**9.26.13**  Thus addressed, Phalguna spoke this word to Kṛṣṇa: "All the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra have been slain by Bhīma, O giver of honor; and these two who yet stand, O Kṛṣṇa, this day shall be no more."

**9.26.14**  "Bhīṣma is slain, Droṇa is slain, Karṇa son of Vikartana is slain; the king of the Madras is slain, Śalya is slain, and Jayadratha is slain, O Kṛṣṇa."

**9.26.15**  "Five hundred horse remain to Śakuni, son of Subala; and of chariots but two hundred remain, O Janārdana, and a full hundred of tusked elephants, and three thousand foot-soldiers."

**9.26.16**  "Aśvatthāman and Kṛpa, and the lord of the Trigartas likewise, Ulūka and Śakuni, and Kṛtavarman of the Sātvatas—"

**9.26.17**  "—this is the force left to Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son, O Mādhava; for surely from Time there is no deliverance upon the earth for any one."

**9.26.18**  "His army thus destroyed, behold Duryodhana standing firm; this very day shall the great king be left without a friend."

**9.26.19**  "For none of the foe shall escape me, so I resolve; and those fierce in battle, O Kṛṣṇa, who this day shall not abandon the fight—all of them I shall slay, even were they more than human."

**9.26.20**  "This day in battle, sore enraged, I shall take away the long sleeplessness of the king, by felling the Gāndhāra prince with my whetted arrows."

**9.26.21**  "Those jewels which the evil-doing son of Subala, by his cheating, won from us at the dicing in the assembly hall—those I shall this day win back."

**9.26.22**  "This day too shall all the women of the City of the Elephant learn, hearing that their husbands and their sons have been slain by the Pāṇḍavas in the fight."

**9.26.23**  "This day shall all the work be brought to its end, O Kṛṣṇa; this day shall Duryodhana give up his blazing fortune and his life together."

**9.26.24**  "If, O Kṛṣṇa, he does not flee from the battle in fear of me, then know the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, that great fool, as already slain, O Vārṣṇeya."

**9.26.25**  "For this throng of horses cannot bear the twang of my bowstring and the slap of my palm-guard, O subduer of foes; drive on, that I may slay them."

**9.26.26**  Thus addressed by the illustrious Pāṇḍava, the Dāśārha urged on the horses, O king, toward Duryodhana's host.

**9.26.27**  Beholding that array, three great chariot-warriors ready for battle—Bhīmasena and Arjuna and Sahadeva, O lord—advanced with a lion's roar, eager to slay Duryodhana.

**9.26.28**  Seeing them all together, sweeping on at speed with bows uplifted, the son of Subala charged in battle against the Pāṇḍavas, those bringers of doom.

**9.26.29**  Sudarśana, your son, advanced against Bhīmasena; Suśarman and Śakuni did battle with the Diademed One; and your son, mounted upon a horse's back, advanced against Sahadeva.

**9.26.30**  Then swiftly and without effort, your son, O lord of men, smote Sahadeva hard upon the head with a javelin.

**9.26.31**  Struck down by your son, he sank upon the floor of his chariot, all his limbs bathed in blood, hissing like a venomous serpent.

**9.26.32**  Then, regaining his senses, Sahadeva, O lord of peoples, in his wrath covered Duryodhana with sharp arrows.

**9.26.33**  Pārtha too, displaying his prowess in the fight—Dhanaṃjaya, son of Kuntī—struck the heads of the brave warriors from off their horses' backs.

**9.26.34**  Then Pārtha scattered that array with his many arrows, and felling all the horses, drove against the chariots of the Trigartas.

**9.26.35**  Then the great chariot-warriors of the Trigartas, joining together, overwhelmed Arjuna and Vāsudeva with showers of arrows.

**9.26.36**  Assailing Satyakarman, the greatly renowned son of Pāṇḍu cut apart with a razor-shaft the pole of his chariot.

**9.26.37**  And with a razor-shaft honed upon stone, O lord, that greatly renowned one, smiling, severed his head, adorned with burnished earrings.

**9.26.38**  Then, before the very eyes of the warriors, he seized upon Satyeṣu, as a lion in the forest, O king, seizes a deer in its hunger.

**9.26.39**  Having slain him, Pārtha then pierced Suśarman with three arrows, and struck down all those chariots adorned with gold.

**9.26.40**  Then, pressing swiftly on, Pārtha loosed the keen venom of a wrath long gathered up against the lord of Prasthala.

**9.26.41**  Arjuna, O bull of the Bhāratas, having filled him with a hundred shafts, then slew the horses of that bowman.

**9.26.42**  Then, taking up a whetted arrow like the rod of Yama, he aimed at Suśarman and loosed it swiftly, as though in mirth.

**9.26.43**  That arrow, sped by that bowman blazing with wrath, reached Suśarman and clove his heart in the battle.

**9.26.44**  Lifeless, O great king, he fell upon the surface of the earth, gladdening all the Pāṇḍavas and grieving your men.

**9.26.45**  Having slain Suśarman in battle, he brought to destruction with his shafts the sons of that warrior, great chariot-fighters, five and forty in number.

**9.26.46**  Then, having slain all his foot-followers with his whetted arrows, that great chariot-warrior advanced upon the remnant of the Bhārata host.

**9.26.47**  But Bhīma, wrathful in the battle, made your son Sudarśana invisible with his arrows, O lord of men, as though smiling the while.

**9.26.48**  Then, smiling in his wrath, he struck his head from his body with a keen razor-shaft; slain, he fell to the earth.

**9.26.49**  When that hero had been slain, his foot-followers then encircled Bhīma in the battle, scattering their whetted shafts.

**9.26.50**  Then Vṛkodara with his whetted arrows, whose touch was like the thunderbolt of Indra, covered that array on every side; and in a moment, O bull of the Bhāratas, Bhīma cut it down.

**9.26.51**  As these were being destroyed, the mighty captains of the host closed with Bhīmasena and gave battle, O Bhārata; and the Pāṇḍava covered them all with his terrible arrows.

**9.26.52**  In like manner your men, O king, hemmed in on every side the great chariot-warriors of the Pāṇḍavas with a mighty rain of arrows.

**9.26.53**  All was thrown into confusion—the Pāṇḍavas locked with the foe, and your men in the battle striving against the followers of Pāṇḍu.

**9.26.54**  There the warriors then fell, smiting one another down, in both the armies, O king, grieving for their kinsmen.

**9.27.1**  Saṃjaya said: As that battle, the destroyer of men and horses and elephants, raged on, Śakuni, son of Subala, O king, advanced against Sahadeva.

**9.27.2**  Then, as he charged swiftly on, the valiant Sahadeva sent forth floods of arrows, swift-flying like locusts; and Ulūka in the battle pierced Bhīma with ten shafts.

**9.27.3**  And Śakuni, O great king, having pierced Bhīma with three arrows, then covered Sahadeva with ninety shafts.

**9.27.4**  Those heroes, O king, closing with one another in the battle, pierced each other with whetted arrows fledged with heron and peacock feathers, golden-nocked, honed upon stone, and drawn back to the ear before they were loosed.

**9.27.5**  The shower of arrows loosed from their bow-arms, O lord of peoples, veiled all the quarters, as a rain-cloud veils them with its torrents.

**9.27.6**  Then Bhīma, wrathful in the battle, and Sahadeva, O Bhārata, those two of exceeding might, ranged the field, working havoc in the fray.

**9.27.7**  Shrouded by their hundreds of arrows, that host of yours, O Bhārata, became here and there as dark as a sky filled with gloom.

**9.27.8**  By horses galloping wildly in every direction, riddled with arrows, O lord of the people, paths were carved here and there as they dragged along the many slain.

**9.27.9**  With slaughtered horses together with their riders, with shattered coats of mail and broken lances, O venerable one, the earth became so covered that it seemed dappled, as if strewn with blossoms.

**9.27.10**  The warriors there, O great king, closing upon one another, ranged across the battlefield in fury, striking each other down.

**9.27.11**  With faces whose eyes were rolled upward in rage and whose lips were bitten through, still wearing their earrings and resembling the filaments of lotuses, the earth was covered over.

**9.27.12**  With severed arms, O great king, that resembled the trunks of lordly elephants, still bearing their armlets and gauntlets and still grasping sword, lance, and battle-axe,

**9.27.13**  with headless trunks that had risen up though severed, and others dancing on the field, thronged with hosts of carrion-eaters, the earth became a thing of horror, O lord.

**9.27.14**  When the army was reduced to a remnant, the Pāṇḍavas, grown exultant in that great battle, dispatched the Kauravas to the abode of Yama.

**9.27.15**  In that interval the valiant, mighty son of Subala struck Sahadeva hard upon the head with a lance; and he, O great king, reeling and stunned, sank down upon the floor of his chariot.

**9.27.16**  Seeing Sahadeva thus, the mighty Bhīmasena, blazing with wrath, held back all the enemy hosts, O Bhārata.

**9.27.17**  He pierced them through with iron shafts by the hundreds and the thousands, and having pierced them, he sent up a lion's roar, O subduer of foes.

**9.27.18**  Terrified by that sound, the followers of Śakuni, together with their horses and elephants, all at once fled in sudden fear.

**9.27.19**  Seeing them broken in rout, King Duryodhana cried out: "Turn back, you who know not righteousness! Fight! What good is your flight to you?

**9.27.20**  The hero who wins renown here and yields up his life in battle without showing his back attains the higher worlds after death."

**9.27.21**  Thus addressed by the king, the followers of the son of Subala turned again upon the Pāṇḍavas, making death itself their refuge from retreat.

**9.27.22**  By those rushing onward, O lord of kings, a most terrible roar was raised, like the tumult of a churning ocean, and it surged up on every side.

**9.27.23**  Beholding the followers of the son of Subala bearing down upon them, the Pāṇḍavas, intent upon victory, advanced to meet them, O great king.

**9.27.24**  And the unassailable Sahadeva, having regained his breath, O lord of the people, pierced Śakuni with ten shafts and his horses with three, and as if smiling cut through the bow of the son of Subala with his arrows.

**9.27.25**  Then taking up another bow, Śakuni, drunk with the frenzy of battle, pierced Nakula with sixty shafts and Bhīmasena with seven.

**9.27.26**  Ulūka too, O great king, seeking to shield his father in the fight, pierced Bhīma with seven shafts and Sahadeva with seventy.

**9.27.27**  Bhīmasena in that combat pierced him with sharpened shafts, and Śakuni with sixty-four, and those who stood at their flanks with three apiece.

**9.27.28**  Though struck by Bhīma with oil-tempered iron shafts, they in their wrath covered Sahadeva in battle with showers of arrows, as lightning-charged clouds drench a mountain with torrents of rain.

**9.27.29**  Then, as Ulūka bore down upon him, the valiant and mighty Sahadeva struck off his head with a broad-headed shaft, O great king.

**9.27.30**  Cast down by Sahadeva, he fell from his chariot to the ground, his every limb bathed in blood, gladdening the Pāṇḍavas in the battle.

**9.27.31**  But seeing his son slain there, O Bhārata, Śakuni, his throat choked with tears, sighed deeply, recalling the words of Vidura.

**9.27.32**  Having brooded a moment, his eyes brimming with tears and sighing, he closed upon Sahadeva and pierced him with three arrows.

**9.27.33**  Warding off those loosed shafts with volleys of his own arrows, the mighty Sahadeva, O great king, cut through his bow in the encounter.

**9.27.34**  His bow being severed, O lord of kings, Śakuni the son of Subala then seized a broad sword and hurled it at Sahadeva.

**9.27.35**  As that sword of dreadful form came rushing upon him, O lord of the people, he cut the weapon of the son of Subala in two in the fray, as if in mockery.

**9.27.36**  Seeing his sword cleft in two, he took up a great mace and hurled it at Sahadeva; but it fell vainly to the ground.

**9.27.37**  Then the son of Subala, blazing with rage, flung at the Pāṇḍava a most fearful spear, upraised like the Night of Doom.

**9.27.38**  As it came hurtling upon him, Sahadeva, as if smiling, cut it in three with gold-adorned shafts upon the field.

**9.27.39**  Severed in three, that gold-wrought spear fell to the earth, crumbling as it dropped, like a blazing flash of lightning falling from the sky.

**9.27.40**  Seeing the spear struck down and the son of Subala stricken with fear, all your men, the son of Subala among them, fled as terror seized them.

**9.27.41**  Then a great shout went up from the Pāṇḍavas, radiant with the look of victory, and thereupon the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra for the most part turned their faces away.

**9.27.42**  Seeing them downcast, the valiant son of Mādrī held them back in the battle with many thousands of arrows.

**9.27.43**  Then Sahadeva fell upon the son of Subala in the fight as he rode away, guarded by the Gāndhāras and seated upon swift, fine-backed horses, bent upon victory.

**9.27.44**  Recalling that Śakuni remained as the share allotted to him, O king, Sahadeva bore down upon him in his gold-bodied chariot, having strung and drawn back his great bow with main force.

**9.27.45**  Rushing upon the son of Subala, he struck him hard in his wrath with stone-whetted, vulture-feathered shafts, as one goads a great elephant with hooks.

**9.27.46**  And the wise one, checking him as though to remind him, said: "Standing firm in the duty of a warrior, fight! Be a man!

**9.27.47**  That gladness you felt then, you fool, when you cast the dice in the assembly hall — receive now the fruit of that deed, O evil-minded one!

**9.27.48**  Slain are those wicked men who once mocked us; Duryodhana, the firebrand of his line, remains, and you, his maternal uncle.

**9.27.49**  Today I will strike off your head, churned from your shoulders by a razor-shaft, as one plucks down a fruit from a tree with a crushing cudgel."

**9.27.50**  Having spoken thus, O great king, the mighty Sahadeva, that tiger among men, blazing with wrath, advanced upon him with a rush.

**9.27.51**  Closing in, the unassailable Sahadeva, that lord of warriors, drew his bow with might, as if laughing in his anger,

**9.27.52**  and having pierced Śakuni with ten shafts and his horses with four, and cut down his parasol, his banner, and his bow, he roared like a lion.

**9.27.53**  His banner, bow, and parasol cut away by Sahadeva, the son of Subala was then pierced in all his vital points by many arrows.

**9.27.54**  Then once more, O great king, the valiant Sahadeva sent against Śakuni an irresistible shower of arrows.

**9.27.55**  Then the son of Subala, in his fury, single-handed and seeking to slay Sahadeva, the son of Mādrī, swiftly rushed upon him in the press of battle with a gold-decked lance.

**9.27.56**  At the forefront of the fight the son of Mādrī, with three broad-headed shafts at a single stroke, severed that upraised lance together with both his shapely arms, and shouted aloud, sweeping through the middle of the field.

**9.27.57**  Then, acting in an instant, with a well-aimed broad shaft of hard iron, gold-feathered and able to pierce every guard, he wrenched the head from his body.

**9.27.58**  His head struck off in battle by the Pāṇḍava with that gold-adorned, keen-whetted shaft bright as the sun, the son of Subala fell to the earth.

**9.27.59**  That head, which was the very root of the ruin of the Kurus, the son of Pāṇḍu in his wrath sent flying with a swift, gold-feathered, stone-whetted shaft.

**9.27.60**  Beholding Śakuni with his head struck off, lying upon the earth, his limbs drenched with blood, your warriors, their spirit shattered by terror, fled in every direction, still grasping their weapons.

**9.27.61**  Routed, their mouths dry, their senses gone, stunned by the twang of Gāṇḍīva and tormented by fear, their chariots, horses, and elephants broken, the foot-soldiers too, along with the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, fled away.

**9.27.62**  Then, having cast Śakuni down from his chariot, the sons of Pāṇḍu, filled with joy, O Bhārata, blew their conches in the battle in their exultation, gladdening their soldiers, with Keśava among them.

**9.27.63**  And all of them, doing honor to Sahadeva, said to him in their gladness upon the field: "By good fortune, O hero, the deceitful and wicked one, together with his son, has been slain by you in battle."

**9.28.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then, O great king, the followers of the son of Subala, enraged and giving up their lives amid the wailing, hemmed in the Pāṇḍavas on every side.

**9.28.2**  Them Arjuna received in battle, bent upon Sahadeva's victory, and Bhīmasena too, blazing with energy, his aspect like that of an enraged venomous serpent.

**9.28.3**  The resolve of those who, spear, pike, and lance in hand, sought to slay Sahadeva, Dhanaṃjaya rendered vain with Gāṇḍīva.

**9.28.4**  The arms that grasped weapons, of the warriors who rushed forward, Bībhatsu cut away with broad-headed shafts, and their heads as well, and their horses too.

**9.28.5**  Struck down and bereft of life, they sank to the earth, swiftly smitten by Savyasācin, that hero of the world.

**9.28.6**  Then King Duryodhana, beholding the destruction of his host, in his wrath, O lord, gathered together the hundreds of chariots that survived the slaughter,

**9.28.7**  and the elephants and horses and foot-soldiers, O scorcher of foes; and the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra spoke to them all assembled these words:

**9.28.8**  "Closing upon all the Pāṇḍavas in battle, together with their bands of friends, and the Pāñcāla prince too with his force, slay them and swiftly turn back."

**9.28.9**  Receiving his word upon their heads, those drunk with the frenzy of battle advanced to meet the sons of Pṛthā in the fight, at your son's command.

**9.28.10**  As they who had survived the slaughter rushed swiftly forward in that great battle, the Pāṇḍavas covered them over with shafts shaped like venomous serpents.

**9.28.11**  That army, O best of the Bhāratas, was in a moment slain by those great-souled ones once it joined the battle, and found no protector; yet though it sought to stand firm, for fear it could not hold its ground, armored though it was.

**9.28.12**  With horses galloping wildly and the host wrapped in dust, neither the quarters nor the intermediate quarters could be discerned in the battle.

**9.28.13**  Then many men, sallying forth from the Pāṇḍava ranks, struck down your men in the fight in but a moment, O Bhārata; and so that army of yours was left without a remnant, O Bhārata.

**9.28.14**  The eleven full armies assembled for your son, O Bhārata — those, O lord, were slain in battle by the Pāṇḍavas and the Sṛñjayas.

**9.28.15**  Amid those thousands of great-souled kings of your side, Duryodhana alone, O king, was seen, sorely wounded.

**9.28.16**  Then, gazing about him in all directions and seeing the earth made empty, bereft of all his warriors, and beholding the Pāṇḍavas in the field —

**9.28.17**  exultant, all their aims fulfilled, roaring on every side — and hearing too the sound and clamor of the arrows of those great-souled ones,

**9.28.18**  Duryodhana, O great king, overcome by despair, his forces and his mount gone, set his mind upon flight.

**9.28.19**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: My army slain and left without remnant, my camp made desolate — what then, O charioteer, was the strength of the Pāṇḍavas that remained? Tell me this, who ask it of you, for you are skilled, Saṃjaya.

**9.28.20**  And what did my dull-witted son Duryodhana do, that lord of the earth, alone, having beheld the ruin of his host in this way?

**9.28.21**  Saṃjaya said: Two thousand chariots, and seven hundred elephants, and five thousand horse, and ten thousand foot —

**9.28.22**  this was the great force of the Pāṇḍavas that remained, O king, having taken command of which Dhṛṣṭadyumna stood arrayed in the battle.

**9.28.23**  Then King Duryodhana, alone, O best of the Bhāratas, that foremost of chariot-warriors, saw no comrade anywhere in the battle.

**9.28.24**  Beholding the foes roaring and the destruction of his own host, he abandoned his slain horse and fled eastward in fear.

**9.28.25**  Your son Duryodhana, lord of eleven armies, blazing with energy, took up his mace and set out on foot toward the lake.

**9.28.26**  When the king had gone but a little way on foot, he remembered the words of Vidura, that wise man of righteous conduct.

**9.28.27**  "Surely the deeply wise Vidura foresaw this long ago—this great slaughter of ours and of the kṣatriyas in battle."

**9.28.28**  Brooding thus, his heart scorched with grief at the sight of his army's destruction, O king, the lord wished to enter the lake.

**9.28.29**  And the Pāṇḍavas, great king, with Dhṛṣṭadyumna at their head, enraged, rushed against your forces, O king.

**9.28.30**  As the host roared, brandishing spears, swords, and lances in their hands, Dhanaṃjaya with his Gāṇḍīva brought their resolve to nothing.

**9.28.31**  Slaying them with his keen shafts, together with their counselors and kinsmen, Arjuna, standing in his chariot drawn by white horses, shone in great splendor.

**9.28.32**  When Subala's son fell with his horses, chariots, and elephants, your army became like a vast forest hewn down.

**9.28.33**  In the host of Duryodhana, many hundreds of thousands strong, no other great warrior, O king, was seen to be left alive—

**9.28.34**  save the heroic son of Droṇa, and likewise Kṛtavarman, and Kṛpa of Gautama's line, O king, and your son the prince.

**9.28.35**  But Dhṛṣṭadyumna, seeing me, laughed and said to Sātyaki: "Why take this man captive? There is no use in his living."

**9.28.36**  Hearing Dhṛṣṭadyumna's words, Śini's grandson, that mighty warrior, raised his sharp sword and made ready to slay me then.

**9.28.37**  But the deeply wise Kṛṣṇa Dvaipāyana came up and said: "Let Saṃjaya be released alive; he must by no means be slain."

**9.28.38**  Hearing Dvaipāyana's words, Śini's grandson joined his palms, then released me and said: "Fare well, Saṃjaya; go your way."

**9.28.39**  Dismissed by him, I laid aside my armor and weapons and set out toward the city at evening, drenched in blood.

**9.28.40**  Having withdrawn about a krośa, I saw, O king, Duryodhana standing alone, mace in hand, grievously wounded.

**9.28.41**  His eyes brimming with tears, he could not gaze upon me; yet, seeing me standing there wretched, he looked toward me.

**9.28.42**  And I too, seeing him grieving and alone upon the field, was so overwhelmed with sorrow that for a moment I could say nothing.

**9.28.43**  Then I told him all of it—how I had been taken captive, and how by the grace of Dvaipāyana I had been released alive from the battle.

**9.28.44**  After brooding for a moment and recovering his senses, he then asked me about his brothers and all his armies.

**9.28.45**  To him I, who had seen it with my own eyes, told everything: that all his brothers were slain and his army cut down.

**9.28.46**  "Three warriors of your host are left, it is said, O lord of men"—so Kṛṣṇa Dvaipāyana had told me at the time of my departure.

**9.28.47**  Sighing as if from the depths, and gazing about him again and again, your son touched me on the shoulder with his hand and spoke.

**9.28.48**  "Save you, Saṃjaya, no one else here lives through this battle; I see no second man—while the Pāṇḍavas have their allies still."

**9.28.49**  "Tell the king, Saṃjaya—the lord whose eye is wisdom—that your son Duryodhana has entered the lake."

**9.28.50**  "Bereft of such friends, of sons and of brothers, and with the kingdom seized by the Pāṇḍavas, what man like me would go on living?"

**9.28.51**  "Tell him all of this—and that I, escaped from the great battle, lie sleeping alive in this lake of water, sorely wounded."

**9.28.52**  Having spoken thus, great king, the lord entered that lake, and by his magic power the king of men froze its waters firm.

**9.28.53**  When he had entered the lake, I, alone, saw three warriors with their teams worn out coming together to that place—

**9.28.54**  Kṛpa, the hero of Śaradvat's line, and Droṇa's son, best of warriors, and Kṛtavarman of the Bhojas, together, all torn by arrows.

**9.28.55**  All of them, catching sight of me, swiftly urged on their horses, and drawing near they said: "By good fortune you live, Saṃjaya!"

**9.28.56**  And they all asked me about your son, the lord of men: "Does our king Duryodhana yet live, Saṃjaya?"

**9.28.57**  I told them then that the king was unharmed, and I recounted all that Duryodhana had said to me, and I pointed out the lake into which the king of men had entered.

**9.28.58**  But Aśvatthāman, O king, hearing my words and gazing upon that broad lake, lamented piteously.

**9.28.59**  "Alas, shame! The king does not know that we still live—for together with him we are enough to fight the foe."

**9.28.60**  Those great warriors, foremost of charioteers, lamented there a long while, then fled away, having seen Pāṇḍu's sons upon the field.

**9.28.61**  Mounting me on Kṛpa's well-furnished chariot, those three warriors, the survivors of the slaughter, came to the army's encampment.

**9.28.62**  There the terror-stricken guard-posts, as the sun went down, all cried aloud when they heard of the destruction of your sons.

**9.28.63**  Then the aged men who guarded the women, great king, taking up the royal consorts, set out toward the city.

**9.28.64**  There arose on every side a mighty clamor of women shrieking and weeping, when they heard of that destruction of the army.

**9.28.65**  Then those women, O king, wailing again and again, made the earth resound with their cries like ospreys.

**9.28.66**  They beat their heads with their nails and with their hands, and tore their hair, crying out here and there.

**9.28.67**  Wailing their cries of woe and beating their breasts, they shrieked and wept there, lamenting, O lord of peoples.

**9.28.68**  Then Duryodhana's counselors, their throats choked with tears and sorely stricken, taking up the royal women, set out toward the city.

**9.28.69**  And the gatekeepers, O lord of peoples, with worn canes in their hands, the guardians of the women, gathered up the gleaming couches with their costly coverlets and went swiftly toward the city.

**9.28.70**  Other men, mounting chariots yoked with she-mules, taking up each his own womenfolk, set out toward the city.

**9.28.71**  Those women whom even the sun had never before beheld within their chambers, men now saw, great king, as they went toward the city.

**9.28.72**  Those tender, delicate women, O best of the Bhāratas, their own kinsmen and relations slain, hastened toward the city.

**9.28.73**  Down to the very cowherds and shepherds, the people fled in confusion toward the city, tormented by fear of Bhīmasena.

**9.28.74**  And a fierce, most dreadful fear of the Pārthas was upon them; gazing at one another, they ran toward the city.

**9.28.75**  While that most terrible rout was taking place, Yuyutsu, bewildered with grief, pondered what the hour demanded.

**9.28.76**  "Duryodhana, lord of eleven armies, is vanquished in battle by the Pāṇḍavas of fearsome valor; his brothers are slain, and all the Kurus, with Bhīṣma and Droṇa at their head, are dead."

**9.28.77**  "I alone have escaped, by the working of fortune and by chance, while all the camps are scattered on every side."

**9.28.78**  "Whatever of Duryodhana's ministers are left, they have taken up the royal women and are running toward the city."

**9.28.79**  "I think the hour has come for me to enter the city along with them, O lord—once I have taken leave of Yudhiṣṭhira and of Bhīmasena as well."

**9.28.80**  The mighty-armed one set this matter before the two of them; the king, ever moved by compassion, was pleased with him, and the strong-armed one, embracing the son of the vaiśya woman, gave him leave to go.

**9.28.81**  Then, mounting his chariot, he swiftly urged on his horses, and, overtaking the royal women unlooked-for, set off toward the city.

**9.28.82**  Together with them he swiftly entered Hāstinapura as the sun was setting, his throat choked with tears and his eyes streaming.

**9.28.83**  He saw the deeply wise Vidura, his eyes too wet with tears, coming out from the king's presence, his mind stricken with grief.

**9.28.84**  To him, who stood bowing before him, the steadfast-in-truth one said: "Now that this destruction of the Kurus has come to pass, by good fortune, my son, you are alive."

**9.28.85**  "Yet without the king's own entry, how is it that you have come here? Tell me the whole reason of this in full."

**9.28.86**  Yuyutsu said: "When Śakuni was slain, dear sir, with his kinsmen, sons, and relations, then King Duryodhana, with but the remnant of his retinue left, abandoned his own horse and, turning eastward, fled in fear."

**9.28.87**  "When the king had withdrawn from the encampment of the host, all the people, distraught with fear, fled toward the city."

**9.28.88**  "Then the keepers of the women, mounting upon the carriages the consorts of the king and of all his brothers, fled in fear."

**9.28.89**  "Then I, having taken leave of the king together with Keśava, entered Hāstinapura, to guard against the world's reproach."

**9.28.90**  Hearing these words spoken by the son of the vaiśya woman, and knowing them timely, Vidura, knower of all that is right, of immeasurable soul, honored the eloquent Yuyutsu.

**9.28.91**  "All this of yours is timely amid the ruin of the Bhāratas; today rest here, and tomorrow you shall go to Yudhiṣṭhira."

**9.28.92**  Having spoken these words, Vidura, knower of all that is right, dismissed Yuyutsu and entered the king's dwelling; and Yuyutsu too passed that night in his own house.

**9.29.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: When all my armies had been slain by Pāṇḍu's sons on the field of battle, what did those survivors of my host do, Saṃjaya?

**9.29.2**  Kṛtavarman and Kṛpa, and the valiant son of Droṇa—and King Duryodhana of feeble mind—what did he do then?

**9.29.3**  Saṃjaya said: When the women of the great-souled kṣatriyas had fled away and the camp lay empty and abandoned, the three warriors, deeply distraught,

**9.29.4**  hearing the shouts of the victorious sons of Pāṇḍu and seeing the camp deserted at evening, longing for their king, found that place no longer to their liking, and so they went toward the lake.

**9.29.5**  Yudhiṣṭhira too, the righteous-souled, together with his brothers, ranged exultant over the field, O king, eager to slay Duryodhana.

**9.29.6**  Enraged and bent on victory, they searched for your son, seeking him out with all their effort, yet nowhere did they see the lord of men.

**9.29.7**  For he, mace in hand, had withdrawn with swift speed and had entered that lake, holding back its waters by his own magic.

**9.29.8**  When at last all the Pāṇḍavas found their teams quite spent, they returned to their own camp and halted there with their soldiers.

**9.29.9**  Then Kṛpa and Droṇa's son and Kṛtavarman the Sātvata, while the Pārthas were encamped, set out slowly toward that lake.

**9.29.10**  Reaching the lake where the lord of men lay, they addressed the unassailable king as he slept within the waters.

**9.29.11**  "Rise, O king, and fight beside us! Conquer Yudhiṣṭhira and enjoy the earth, or, slain, attain to heaven."

**9.29.12**  "Their whole force too, Duryodhana, has been struck down by you, and most of those soldiers who remain there are sorely beset."

**9.29.13**  They will not be able to withstand your onslaught, O lord of peoples, when you are shielded by us. Therefore rise, O Bhārata.

**9.29.14**  Duryodhana said: By good fortune I see you delivered from such a slaughter of men, surviving still, O bulls among men, from the deadly clash of Pāṇḍava and Kaurava.

**9.29.15**  We shall conquer them all once we are rested and our weariness has passed. But you are exhausted, and I am gravely wounded, while their force is at its height; for that reason I do not favor battle now.

**9.29.16**  It is no marvel, O heroes, that your spirit is so great and your devotion to us so supreme; yet this is not the hour for valor.

**9.29.17**  Having rested this one night, I shall tomorrow give battle to the enemy at your side; of this I have no doubt.

**9.29.18**  Saṃjaya said: Thus addressed, the son of Droṇa spoke to the king, frenzied for battle: Rise, O king—good fortune to you—we shall conquer the foe in war.

**9.29.19**  By my sacrifices and pious works, by charity, by truth, and by my prayers I swear, O king, that this very day I shall slay the Somakas.

**9.29.20**  May I never attain the joy fitting for the virtuous that belongs to those who have performed sacrifices, if, when this night has passed, I do not slay the enemy in battle.

**9.29.21**  Until I have slain all the Pāñcālas I shall not loosen my armor, O lord; this I declare as truth—hear it from me, O ruler of men.

**9.29.22**  While they were thus conversing, certain hunters came by chance to that place, weary beneath their loads of meat, seeking water.

**9.29.23**  For these hunters, O great king, were ever in Bhīmasena's service, bringing him their loads of meat with the deepest devotion, O lord.

**9.29.24**  Halting there, gathered together among themselves, they overheard in secret all that the warriors said, and Duryodhana's words as well.

**9.29.25**  And all those mighty bowmen, longing for battle, pressed the Kaurava with the utmost insistence, though he had no wish to fight.

**9.29.26**  Watching thus those great chariot-warriors of the Kauravas, and the king who lingered in the waters with no mind for battle—

**9.29.27**  having heard their conversation and the words of the king who lay within the water, the hunters came to know, O lord of kings, that Suyodhana was hidden in the lake.

**9.29.28**  These men had earlier been questioned by the son of Pāṇḍu about your son; coming there by chance, they had been seeking the king.

**9.29.29**  Then, recalling what the son of Pāṇḍu had said, the deer-hunters spoke softly to one another, O king, in this wise:

**9.29.30**  "Let us reveal Duryodhana; the Pāṇḍava will give us wealth. Plainly enough, King Duryodhana has been disclosed to us in the lake."

**9.29.31**  "Therefore let us all go to where King Yudhiṣṭhira is, to tell him that the wrathful Duryodhana lies asleep in the water."

**9.29.32**  "Let us all tell the wise Bhīmasena, the wielder of the bow, that the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra lies hidden in the water."

**9.29.33**  "Well pleased, he will give us abundant riches. What use to us is this dry, withered, shriveled meat?"

**9.29.34**  Having spoken thus, the hunters, greatly delighted and eager for wealth, took up their loads of meat and set out toward the camp.

**9.29.35**  And the Pāṇḍavas, O great king, sure-aimed and hard-striking warriors, not seeing Duryodhana standing on the field of battle,

**9.29.36**  and longing to reach the end of that wicked deceiver's trickery, sent out scouts in every direction across the field of war.

**9.29.37**  Returning, all the soldiers together reported to the Dharma-king that King Duryodhana had vanished.

**9.29.38**  Hearing those words of the scouts, O bull of the Bhāratas, the king fell into bitter brooding and drew a heavy sigh.

**9.29.39**  Then, O bull of the Bhāratas, while the Pāṇḍavas stood there dejected, the hunters, hastening away from that place, O lord,

**9.29.40**  came joyfully to the camp, having seen King Duryodhana; and though held back, they pressed in within sight of Bhīmasena.

**9.29.41**  Coming before the Pāṇḍava Bhīmasena of mighty strength, they told him all that had happened and all that they had heard.

**9.29.42**  Then Vṛkodara, O king, the scorcher of foes, having given them abundant wealth, related the whole matter to the Dharma-king.

**9.29.43**  "That Duryodhana, O king, has been discovered for me by the hunters; he for whose sake you grieve lies asleep, having stilled the waters about him."

**9.29.44**  Hearing those welcome words of Bhīmasena, O lord of peoples, Ajātaśatru, the son of Kuntī, rejoiced together with his brothers.

**9.29.45**  And learning that the great bowman had entered the watery lake, he set out at once, placing Janārdana at the fore.

**9.29.46**  Then there arose a tumult of glad shouting, O lord of peoples, from the exultant Pāṇḍavas and Pāñcālas on every side.

**9.29.47**  They raised lion-roars and battle-cries, O bull of the Bhāratas; and the warriors hastened, O king, to the lake of Dvaipāyana.

**9.29.48**  "The wicked son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra is found, he is seen!"—again and again in the fray the Somakas cried out there on all sides, their faces glad.
