**9.29.49**  From their swift-moving, speeding chariots there arose a tumultuous din that touched the heavens, O lord of the earth.

**9.29.50**  Eager to lay hold of Duryodhana, they followed King Yudhiṣṭhira here and there in haste, their mounts worn out.

**9.29.51**  Arjuna and Bhīmasena, and the two Pāṇḍava sons of Mādrī, and Dhṛṣṭadyumna the Pāñcāla, and the unvanquished Śikhaṇḍin,

**9.29.52**  Uttamaujas, Yudhāmanyu, and the unconquered Sātyaki, and those Pāñcālas who remained, and the sons of Draupadī, O Bhārata, and all the horses and elephants, and foot-soldiers by the hundred—

**9.29.53**  then, O great king, the son of Dharma, Yudhiṣṭhira, reached the famed lake of Dvaipāyana, where Duryodhana was.

**9.29.54**  Its waters cool and clear and pleasing, like a second ocean—there your son had taken his stand, having stilled the waters by his magic.

**9.29.55**  By a most wondrous device, through the working of fate, O Bhārata, he lay sunk within the water, hard for any man to behold, O lord—the ruler of men, club in hand, O sovereign of men.

**9.29.56**  Then King Duryodhana, dwelling sunk within the water, heard the tumultuous din, loud as a thundercloud's roar.

**9.29.57**  And Yudhiṣṭhira, O lord of kings, came to that lake with his brothers, O great king, to slay your son.

**9.29.58**  With the great blast of conch-shells and the rumble of chariot-wheels, raising up a vast cloud of dust and making the earth tremble,

**9.29.59**  the great chariot-warriors Kṛtavarman, Kṛpa, and the son of Droṇa, hearing the din of Yudhiṣṭhira's army, spoke thus to the king:

**9.29.60**  "Here come the Pāṇḍavas, exultant and flushed with victory. We shall withdraw for now—give us leave, O lord."

**9.29.61**  Duryodhana, hearing this from those glorious men, said "So be it," and by his magic stilled that lake, O lord.

**9.29.62**  Having taken leave of the king, deeply sunk in grief, those chariot-warriors led by Kṛpa went far off, O great king.

**9.29.63**  Having traveled a long way, they spied a banyan tree, O venerable one, and sat down, sorely wearied, brooding upon the king.

**9.29.64**  The mighty son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra slept, having stilled the water; and the Pāṇḍavas too had reached that place, eager for battle.

**9.29.65**  "How then will the battle come about? What will become of the king? How, O king, will the Pāṇḍavas deal with the Kaurava?"

**9.29.66**  Brooding thus, those chariot-warriors led by Kṛpa unyoked their horses from the chariots and took their rest there, O king.

**9.30.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then, when those three chariot-warriors had departed, the Pāṇḍavas drew near to the lake where Duryodhana was.

**9.30.2**  Reaching the lake of Dvaipāyana, O best of the Kurus, and seeing that body of water held fast by the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, the delight of the Kurus spoke these words to Vāsudeva:

**9.30.3**  "Behold this magic the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra has worked upon the waters! Having stilled the water, he lies there; he has no fear of any man."

**9.30.4**  "Having wrought this divine illusion, he has sunk himself within the water; yet that knower of trickery shall not escape me alive through his deceit."

**9.30.5**  "Even if the wielder of the thunderbolt himself should lend him aid in battle, still the world shall see him slain in war, O Mādhava."

**9.30.6**  The illustrious Vāsudeva said: Destroy this illusion of the illusionist by illusion, O Bhārata. The trickster must be slain by trickery—this is the truth, Yudhiṣṭhira.

**9.30.7**  By many ways and means working an illusion upon the waters, slay the wicked-souled Suyodhana, O best of the Bhāratas.

**9.30.8**  By ways and means Indra slew the Daityas and Dānavas; by manifold ways and means the great-souled one bound Bali.

**9.30.9**  By ways and means of old the great Asura Hiraṇyākṣa, and Hiraṇyakaśipu too, were destroyed by stratagem; and Vṛtra was slain, O king, by stratagem alone—of this there is no doubt.

**9.30.10**  So too the rākṣasa named Rāvaṇa, son of Pulastya's line, was slain by Rāma, O king, together with his kin and his followers, through resort to a contrived design; therefore do you also show your prowess thus.

**9.30.11**  By ways and means, O king, in olden time were slain by me the great Daitya Tāraka and the valorous Vipracitti.

**9.30.12**  Vātāpi and Ilvala, and Triśiras as well, O lord, and the asuras Sunda and Upasunda—all were destroyed by stratagem alone.

**9.30.13**  By ways and means does Indra enjoy the threefold heaven, O lord. Stratagem is mighty, O king; there is nothing else like it, Yudhiṣṭhira.

**9.30.14**  Daityas and Dānavas, rākṣasas and kings alike, have been slain by ways and means; therefore put a stratagem into practice.

**9.30.15**  Saṃjaya said: Thus addressed by Vāsudeva, the Pāṇḍava of firm vows, the son of Kuntī, O great king, spoke to your mighty son who stood within the water, smiling as it were, O Bhārata:

**9.30.16**  "Suyodhana, to what end is this resort to the waters made by you, after you have brought to ruin all the warrior-folk and your own kin as well, O lord of peoples?"

**9.30.17**  "Today you have entered the water, craving your own life. Rise, O king, and do battle with us, Suyodhana!"

**9.30.18**  "Where, O best of men, has that pride of yours gone, and where that arrogance—you who, having stilled the water, stand there in fear, O king?"

**9.30.19**  "All men proclaim you a hero in the assembly; but I deem that valor of yours, who lie hidden in the water, to be in vain."

**9.30.20**  "Rise, O king, and fight! You are a warrior, born of noble line, a Kaurava above all—remember your birth in that house."

**9.30.21**  "How is it that you, vaunting your birth in the Kaurava line, now in terror of battle have entered the water and there take your stand?"

**9.30.22**  "To refuse battle and to abandon one's post—this is not the eternal law. Flight from the field, O king, is the practice of the ignoble and bars the way to heaven."

**9.30.23**  "How, without having reached the far shore of battle, can you wish to live, having seen these fallen—sons and brothers and fathers alike?"

**9.30.24**  "Having brought to death your kinsmen and your comrades, your uncles and your relations, how, my friend, do you now linger in this lake?"

**9.30.25**  "You fancy yourself a hero, but a hero you are not. Falsely you speak, O Bhārata—'I am a hero!'—you fool, in the hearing of all the world."

**9.30.26**  "For heroes never flee, in any wise, at the sight of foes. Tell us, then, O hero, by what resolve you forsake the fray."

**9.30.27**  So rise up, you, and fight, casting off the fear within you—having destroyed your whole army and your brothers too, O Suyodhana.

**9.30.28**  It is no time now to set your mind on life out of a wish to act righteously, O Suyodhana; rather, one of your kind should hold fast to the warrior's duty.

**9.30.29**  Leaning upon Karṇa and upon Śakuni the son of Subala, you understood not your own self, deluded as though you were immortal.

**9.30.30**  Having wrought that exceedingly great evil, fight back, O Bhārata; for how should one of your kind, out of folly, find flight pleasing?

**9.30.31**  Where has that manhood of yours gone, and where your pride, O Suyodhana? Where has your valor gone, and where your great thundering boast?

**9.30.32**  Where has your skill in arms gone, and why do you lie down in this pool of water? Rise up, you, and fight by the warrior's duty, O Bhārata.

**9.30.33**  Either conquer us and rule this earth, or, slain by us, you shall sleep upon the ground, O Bhārata.

**9.30.34**  This is your foremost duty, ordained by the great-souled Creator; fulfill it truly and become a king, O mighty chariot-warrior.

**9.30.35**  Duryodhana said: It is no wonder, great king, that fear should enter a living being; yet it is not from fear for my life, O Bhārata, that I withdrew in dread.

**9.30.36**  Without a chariot and without a quiver, my flanking driver slain, alone and without a host in the battle, I chose to take a moment's rest.

**9.30.37**  Not for the sake of my life, nor from fear, nor from despair, O lord of peoples, have I entered these waters; this I have done out of exhaustion.

**9.30.38**  Take rest yourself, O son of Kuntī, and those who follow you; I shall rise and fight you all in the battle.

**9.30.39**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: We are all well rested indeed, and long have we been seeking you; so rise up now and fight here, O Suyodhana.

**9.30.40**  Either slay the sons of Pṛthā in battle and win a flourishing kingdom, or, slain by us in the fight, you shall attain the world of heroes.

**9.30.41**  Duryodhana said: Those for whose sake I desired the kingdom of the Kurus, O delight of the Kurus—they, all my brothers, lie slain, O lord of men.

**9.30.42**  This earth, stripped of her jewels, her bull-like warriors slain, I have no heart to enjoy—as one would not a widowed woman.

**9.30.43**  Yet even now I hope to vanquish you, Yudhiṣṭhira, breaking the spirit of the Pāñcālas and the Pāṇḍavas, O bull of the Bhāratas.

**9.30.44**  But now I think there is no longer any purpose at all in fighting, with Droṇa and Karṇa stilled in death and the grandsire slain.

**9.30.45**  Let this whole earth be yours alone now, O king; for what king, bereft of helpers, would wish to rule a kingdom?

**9.30.46**  Having lost such friends, sons, brothers, and fathers, with the kingdom seized by you, who like me would care to live?

**9.30.47**  I shall go to the forest, clothed in deerskins; for I, whose side has been slain, take no joy in a kingdom, O Bhārata.

**9.30.48**  Her kinsmen for the most part slain, her horses slain, her elephants slain—this earth is yours, O king; enjoy her, your fever gone.

**9.30.49**  To the forest I shall go, clad in two deerskins; for I, vanquished, have no longing for life this day, O mighty one.

**9.30.50**  Go, you, and enjoy the earth at your ease, O lord of kings—her rulers slain, her warriors slain, her jewels lost, her ramparts crumbled.

**9.30.51**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: Do not, dear sir, standing there in the water, utter the wailings of one in distress; this prattle of yours, like Śakuni's, finds no place in my mind, O king.

**9.30.52**  And even were you able to make a gift of it, O Suyodhana, I would not wish to rule an earth bestowed by you.

**9.30.53**  By unrighteousness I would not take this earth given by you; for the acceptance of gifts is not held to be the duty of a warrior, O king.

**9.30.54**  I would not desire the whole earth given by you; rather, having vanquished you in battle, I shall enjoy this realm.

**9.30.55**  And being no longer her lord, how do you wish to give the earth away? Why, O king, did you not give this earth even then?

**9.30.56**  When we sought it rightfully, for the sake of peace within our line, you first rebuffed the mighty scion of the Vṛṣṇis, O king.

**9.30.57**  What now do you give? What madness has seized your mind? For what king, once assailed, would wish to give away the earth?

**9.30.58**  You have no power today to give the earth, O delight of the Kurus, nor to wrest her away by force, O king; so how do you wish to give? Vanquish me in battle, then rule this earth.

**9.30.59**  Even so much of the land as could be held on the tip of a needle—not even that much would you give us before, O Bhārata.

**9.30.60**  How then do you give away this whole earth, O lord of peoples? You who once would not yield a needle's point, how do you now relinquish the land?

**9.30.61**  Having attained such lordship and ruled this earth, what fool would resolve to give the realm to his enemy?

**9.30.62**  But you, deluded by sheer folly, do not understand: though you wish to give the earth, this day you shall be parted from your life.

**9.30.63**  Either conquer us and rule this earth, or, slain by us, go to the most exalted worlds.

**9.30.64**  While the two of us yet live, O king—both I and you—a doubt about the victory of either will surely remain among all beings.

**9.30.65**  Your life, O evil-minded one, rests now in my hands; I could, if I wished, let you live—but you are not fit to live.

**9.30.66**  For you made every effort to burn us, above all, and with venomous serpents, with poisons, and with drownings in water; you wronged us, O king, by the seizure of our kingdom too.

**9.30.67**  For this reason, O wicked one, there is no life left for you; rise up, rise up and fight—that shall be the better course for you.

**9.30.68**  Saṃjaya said: Thus did those heroes proclaim, again and again, manifold words ringing with victory, here and there, O lord of men.

**9.31.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Thus reviled, my son, the lord of the earth, a hero wrathful by nature, a scorcher of foes—how did he bear it?

**9.31.2**  For never before had he heard such reviling, and through his kingly state he had been honored by all the world.

**9.31.3**  And this entire earth, thronged with barbarians and forest dwellers, was upheld by his grace, as you yourself witnessed, Saṃjaya.

**9.31.4**  He, thus reviled in particular by the sons of Pāṇḍu, bereft of his own servants and wholly hemmed in a desolate place—

**9.31.5**  hearing those bitter words ringing with victory again and again, what did he say to the Pāṇḍavas? Tell me that, Saṃjaya.

**9.31.6**  Saṃjaya said: Reviled then, O king, your son standing in the water—reviled by Yudhiṣṭhira together with his brothers, O lord of kings—

**9.31.7**  hearing those bitter words, the lord of men in his dire plight sighed long and hot, again and again, as he stood there in the water.

**9.31.8**  Submerged within the water, the king, wringing his hands again and again, set his mind upon battle and addressed the king.

**9.31.9**  You, the sons of Pṛthā, are all with your friends, all with your chariots and mounts; I am but one, worn down, chariotless, my mounts slain.

**9.31.10**  Surrounded by many who bear their weapons and ride their chariots—how, being one man on foot and unarmed, can I dare to fight?

**9.31.11**  But let each of you fight me one by one, O Yudhiṣṭhira; for it is not just that one man should be made to fight against many heroes in battle.

**9.31.12**  all the more so when I am without armor, exhausted, taking refuge in the water, my limbs sorely wounded, my mounts and men worn out.

**9.31.13**  I have no fear of you, O king, nor of Pārtha, nor of Vṛkodara, nor of Phalguna, nor of Vāsudeva, nor again of the Pāñcālas;

**9.31.14**  nor of the twins, nor of Yuyudhāna, nor of whatever other warriors you have; alone and enraged, I could well dare to fight them all here.

**9.31.15**  The fame of the good among men is rooted in righteousness, O lord of men; guarding both righteousness and renown here, I declare it.

**9.31.16**  I shall rise and fight you all in the battle, meeting each in turn as the year meets all the seasons that come round one after another.

**9.31.17**  Today, though unarmed and chariotless, I shall destroy you with your chariots and steeds by my splendor, as the sun at the ebbing of night destroys all the stars; stand firm, O Pāṇḍavas.

**9.31.18**  Today I shall discharge my debt to the illustrious warriors—to Bāhlika, Droṇa, and Bhīṣma, and to the great-souled Karṇa,

**9.31.19**  to brave Jayadratha and to Bhagadatta, to both of them, to Śalya the king of the Madras, and to Bhūriśravas as well,

**9.31.20**  to my sons, O best of the Bhāratas, and to Śakuni the son of Subala, to my allies and friends and kinsmen likewise.

**9.31.21**  Today I shall discharge my debt by slaying you together with your brothers. Having spoken this much, the lord of men fell silent.

**9.31.22**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: By good fortune you too know the warrior's duty, O Suyodhana; by good fortune your mind turns toward battle alone, O mighty-armed one.

**9.31.23**  By good fortune you are a hero, O Kaurava; by good fortune you know warfare, you who, alone, wish to fight us all in the battle.

**9.31.24**  Meeting one against one, whatever weapon is approved by you, take that up and fight; we will stand as your onlookers.

**9.31.25**  And this, your cherished wish, O hero, I grant you further: slay any one of us and the kingdom is yours, or, slain, attain heaven.

**9.31.26**  Duryodhana said: If the choice is granted me this day to fight one in single combat, then of weapons this mace is the one I choose, with your consent.

**9.31.27**  Whichever one of your brothers thinks he can withstand me, let him fight me in battle on foot with the mace.

**9.31.28**  Chariot-battles, wondrous at every step, have run their course; let this one great and marvelous mace-duel come to pass today.

**9.31.29**  Even with their foods do men wish to make a change in turn; let there be a change of battles too, with your assent.

**9.31.30**  With the mace, O mighty-armed one, I shall vanquish you together with your younger brothers, the Pāñcālas and the Sṛñjayas, and whatever other warriors are yours.

**9.31.31**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: Rise up, rise up, O son of Gāndhārī, and do battle with me, O Suyodhana, meeting one against one in the fight, mace to mace, you mighty one.

**9.31.32**  Be a man, O son of Gāndhārī, and fight with full resolve; this day there is no life left for you, even were you swift as thought.

**9.31.33**  Saṃjaya said: This your son, that tiger among men, could not endure, submerged within the water and hissing like a great serpent in its hole.

**9.31.34**  Thus goaded again and again by the lash of those words, the wise one could not brook that speech, as a noble steed cannot brook the whip.

**9.31.35**  Churning the water with force, the valorous one took up his mace—heavy, wrought of mountain-iron, adorned with golden bands—and rose up from beneath the water, hissing like the lord of serpents.

**9.31.36**  Cleaving the spellbound water and laying the iron mace upon his shoulder, your son rose up, blazing like the radiant sun.

**9.31.37**  Then the wise and mighty son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra grasped the heavy mace of tempered iron, embellished with gold.

**9.31.38**  Beholding him mace in hand, like a peaked mountain, standing there like the trident-bearer enraged against his creatures, the Bhārata with his mace shone, blazing like the sun.

**9.31.39**  As that mighty-armed subduer of foes rose up from the water, mace in hand, all beings beheld him as Death himself, the rod of doom in his grasp.

**9.31.40**  Like Śakra with the thunderbolt in his hand, like Hara with the trident in his, all the Pāñcālas, O lord of men, gazed upon your son.

**9.31.41**  But seeing him risen from the water, the Pāñcālas and the sons of Pāṇḍu thrilled with joy on every side and struck their palms against one another.

**9.31.42**  But taking that for mockery, your son Duryodhana, enraged, rolled his eyes wide as though he would burn the Pāṇḍavas to ash.

**9.31.43**  Knitting his brow into a threefold furrow and biting his lip, he then made answer to the Pāṇḍavas and Keśava among them.

**9.31.44**  "This mockery of yours I shall answer this very day, O Pāṇḍavas. Slain on the instant, you and the Pāñcālas with you shall go to the abode of Yama."

**9.31.45**  Risen from those waters, your son Duryodhana stood firm, mace in hand, drenched and bespattered with blood.

**9.31.46**  His body, smeared with gore and sprinkled with water, shone then like a mountain streaming with red ochre.

**9.31.47**  Beholding that hero with mace upraised, the Pāṇḍavas there took him for the wrathful son of Vivasvat, his attendants standing with weapons lifted.

**9.31.48**  Roaring like a thundercloud, bellowing in his joy like a mighty bull, that valiant one then summoned the Pārthas to battle with his mace.

**9.31.49**  Duryodhana said: Come against me one by one, O Yudhiṣṭhira; for it is not right, O hero, that one should be made to fight against many in battle.

**9.31.50**  And the more so when one has laid aside his armour, is weary and drenched in the waters, his limbs sorely gashed, his mounts and soldiers slain.

**9.31.51**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: Where was this wisdom of yours, Suyodhana, when many great chariot-warriors together slew Abhimanyu in the fight?

**9.31.52**  Put on your armour, O hero, and bind up your hair; and whatever else you lack, take that too, O Bhārata. This one wish besides, O hero, I now grant you.

**9.31.53**  Of the five sons of Pāṇḍu, choose whomever you wish to fight here; having slain him you shall be king, or being slain attain heaven. What, O hero, save your life itself, can we do to please you in this combat?

**9.31.54**  Saṃjaya said: Then your son, O king, took up his golden armour and a wondrous helmet adorned with the gold of the Jambū river.

**9.31.55**  With his helmet fastened on and wearing his fair golden mail, your son shone, O king, like a golden mountain-king.

**9.31.56**  Armed and mace in hand, made ready at the forefront of battle, your son Duryodhana spoke, O king, to all the Pāṇḍavas:

**9.31.57**  "Let one of you brothers fight with me, mace to mace. I will fight with Sahadeva, or with Bhīma, or with Nakula,

**9.31.58**  or with Phalguna this day, or with you yourself, O bull of the Bhāratas. Joining battle, I shall conquer on the field of war.

**9.31.59**  This day, O tiger among men, I shall reach the far and hardly-won end of this enmity, with my mace bound about with bands of gold.

**9.31.60**  Be sure of this: in mace-combat there is none my equal. With my mace I shall slay you all, even should you come together. Let him who will fight with me this day take up his mace."

**9.32.1**  Saṃjaya said: As Duryodhana roared thus again and again, O king, Vāsudeva, wroth with Yudhiṣṭhira, spoke these words:

**9.32.2**  "If indeed, O Yudhiṣṭhira, he should choose for combat you, or Arjuna, or Nakula, or even Sahadeva—

**9.32.3**  what is this rashness, O king, that you have uttered, saying, 'Slay but one of us in the fight and be king among the Kurus'?

**9.32.4**  For thirteen years has he practised here, O king, upon a man of iron, in his longing to slay Bhīmasena.

**9.32.5**  How then shall our purpose be accomplished, O bull of the Bhāratas? Out of compassion, O best of kings, you have done a rash thing.

**9.32.6**  I see no other fit to meet him in battle save Vṛkodara, son of Pṛthā—and even he is not greatly practised.

**9.32.7**  So this gambling-match is begun again as of old, the same crooked game of Śakuni's and of yours, O lord of peoples.

**9.32.8**  Bhīma is strong and able, but King Suyodhana is skilled. Between strength and skill, O king, the skilled man prevails.

**9.32.9**  Thus, O king, you have set the enemy upon even ground and staked yourself upon a perilous one; you have brought us into sore distress.

**9.32.10**  What man, having vanquished all his foes, would choose to gamble it all on a single throw with one remaining adversary, and so take pleasure in such a fight?

**9.32.11**  For I see not in the world that best of men, mace in hand, who could fight Duryodhana in battle and by his skill surpass him—

**9.32.12**  neither Phalguna, nor yourself, nor the two sons of Mādrī do I deem a match for that mace-wielder in the fray.

**9.32.13**  How then, O Bhārata, do you tell the foe, 'Fight with the mace, and having slain but one of us in battle be king'?

**9.32.14**  Even when Vṛkodara meets him, our victory is in doubt should they fight by the rules; for this man is skilled and of great might."

**9.32.15**  Bhīma said: O slayer of Madhu, give way to no despair, O joy of the Yadus. This day I shall reach the farther shore of this enmity, so hard to cross.

**9.32.16**  I shall slay Suyodhana in battle, of that there is no doubt. Victory for the King of Righteousness, O Kṛṣṇa, is seen for certain.

**9.32.17**  This mace of mine is heavier by half again than that of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son. Be not troubled, O Mādhava.

**9.32.18**  The three worlds with the gods themselves, bearing weapons of every kind, would I gladly fight in battle—how much more, this day, Suyodhana!

**9.32.19**  Saṃjaya said: As Vṛkodara spoke thus, Vāsudeva, rejoicing, honoured him and spoke these words:

**9.32.20**  "Leaning upon you, O mighty-armed one, the King of Righteousness Yudhiṣṭhira, his foes slain, shall surely regain his own resplendent fortune, of that there is no doubt.

**9.32.21**  By you have all the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra been laid low in battle, and kings and princes and elephants struck down.

**9.32.22**  The Kaliṅgas, the Māgadhas, the Easterners, the Gāndhāras, and the Kurus, meeting you in the great battle, were slain, O joy of Pāṇḍu.

**9.32.23**  Having slain Duryodhana too, O son of Kuntī, give over the sea-girt earth to the King of Righteousness, as Viṣṇu gave it to the lord of Śacī.

**9.32.24**  Meeting you in battle, the wicked son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra shall perish; breaking both his thighs, you shall fulfil your vow.

**9.32.25**  Yet the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra must ever be fought with care, O Pārtha; he is skilled and strong, and at all times a connoisseur of battle."

**9.32.26**  Then Sātyaki, O king, honoured the son of Pāṇḍu, and with words of many kinds Mādhava too honoured him.

**9.32.27**  The Pāñcālas and the sons of Pāṇḍu, with the King of Righteousness at their head, all alike applauded those words of Bhīmasena.

**9.32.28**  Then Bhīma of terrible strength spoke to Yudhiṣṭhira, who stood amid the Sṛñjayas, blazing like the sun:

**9.32.29**  "Joining with him, I am eager to fight in this combat. This basest of men is not able to vanquish me in battle.

**9.32.30**  This day I shall loose upon Suyodhana, son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, the wrath long pent and brooding in my heart, as Arjuna loosed the fire upon the Khāṇḍava forest.

**9.32.31**  This day I shall draw forth the dart that lies in your heart, O son of Pāṇḍu. Having slain the wicked one with my mace, be happy this day, O king.

**9.32.32**  This day I shall set upon you a garland woven of glory, O sinless one; this day Suyodhana shall let go of his life, his fortune, and his kingdom.

**9.32.33**  And King Dhṛtarāṣṭra, hearing this day that his son is slain by me, shall remember that evil deed which was born of Śakuni's counsel."

**9.32.34**  Having spoken thus, that best and most valiant of the Bhāratas raised his mace and rose up for the fight, as Śakra rose to challenge Vṛtra.

**9.32.35**  Coming upon the mighty son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, who stood alone like an elephant strayed from its herd, the Pāṇḍavas thrilled with joy.

**9.32.36**  Seeing him with mace upraised, peaked like Kailāsa, Bhīmasena then, O king, spoke to Duryodhana:

**9.32.37**  "Recall that evil deed which King Dhṛtarāṣṭra and you wrought against us—what came to pass at Vāraṇāvata.

**9.32.38**  Recall how Draupadī, in her courses, was tormented in the midst of the assembly, when the king was won at dice by the settled cunning of Śakuni.

**9.32.39**  And whatever other sins, O evil-souled one, you have wrought against the blameless sons of Pṛthā—behold now the great fruit thereof.

**9.32.40**  For your sake the son of Gaṅgā, the glorious best of the Bhāratas, the grandsire of us all, lies slain upon a bed of arrows.

**9.32.41**  Slain is Droṇa, and Karṇa, and slain is the mighty Śalya; and Śakuni, the first author of this enmity, has been slain in battle.

**9.32.42**  Slain are your valiant brothers, and your sons together with their soldiers; slain too are the brave kings who never turned back in battle.

**9.32.43**  These and many other bulls among the kṣatriyas have been slain; and the wicked Prātikāmin too, who wrought Draupadī's affliction, has been slain.

**9.32.44**  You alone are left, destroyer of your line, basest of men; and you too this day shall I slay with my mace—herein is no doubt.

**9.32.45**  This day, O king, in battle I shall destroy all your pride, and your vast hope of kingdom, and the wrong you did the Pāṇḍavas."

**9.32.46**  Duryodhana said: What use this boasting in so many ways? Fight with me now. This day, O Vṛkodara, I shall take from you all appetite for battle.

**9.32.47**  Do you not see me, O sinner, standing ready for the mace-fight, gripping a mighty mace shaped like a Himālayan peak?

**9.32.48**  What foe, O sinner, dares this day hope to vanquish me, mace in hand and fighting by the rules—not even Purandara among the gods?

**9.32.49**  Roar not in vain, O son of Kuntī, like a rainless autumn cloud. Show in battle what strength is yours this day.

**9.32.50**  Hearing those words of his, the Pāñcālas together with the Sṛñjayas, all longing for victory, applauded that speech.

**9.32.51**  As one rouses a maddened elephant, the men, O king, with the clapping of their hands urged King Duryodhana on yet more.

**9.32.52**  There the elephants trumpeted, the horses neighed again and again, and the weapons of the victory-seeking Pāṇḍavas blazed forth.

**9.33.1**  Saṃjaya said: When that most terrible combat had begun, O great king, and all the high-souled Pāṇḍavas had taken their seats,

**9.33.2**  then Rāma of the palm-tree banner, the bearer of the plough, hearing that combat between his two pupils was at hand, came near, O king.

**9.33.3**  Seeing him, the kings, well pleased, did him honour and said, "Behold, O Rāma, the skill of your two pupils in battle."

**9.33.4**  And then Rāma, beholding Kṛṣṇa and the son of Pāṇḍu, and Duryodhana of the Kuru line standing with mace in hand, spoke:

**9.33.5**  "Forty days and two are gone this day since I set forth; I departed under the asterism Puṣya and am returned under Śravaṇa. I long, O Mādhava, to see the mace-combat of my two pupils."

**9.33.6**  Then King Yudhiṣṭhira, embracing the bearer of the plough, bade him welcome and duly enquired after his well-being.

**9.33.7**  The two Kṛṣṇas as well, those mighty archers, saluted Halāyudha; greatly delighted and full of affection, the two glorious ones embraced him.

**9.33.8**  The two valiant sons of Mādrī, and the five sons of Draupadī, having saluted the mighty son of Rohiṇī, stood waiting, O king.

**9.33.9**  Then the powerful Bhīmasena, and your son too, O lord of men, both with maces upraised, paid honour to the strong one.

**9.33.10**  Honouring him there again and again with words of welcome, the kings said to Rāma, "Behold the combat, O mighty-armed one!" — thus did the rulers of men address the great-souled son of Rohiṇī.

**9.33.11**  Then Rāma, embracing the Pāṇḍavas and the Sṛñjayas as well, asked after the welfare of all those Pāṇḍavas of measureless might; and they in turn, approaching him, inquired after his good health.

**9.33.12**  The plough-bearer, great of mind, returned the honour to all the kṣatriyas, exchanging cordial greetings of welfare each according to his age.

**9.33.13**  Janārdana and Sātyaki he embraced with love, and kissing the two upon the head, he inquired after their welfare.

**9.33.14**  And those two, O king, honoured him in due form as their elder, as Indra and Upendra, filled with joy, would honour Brahmā, the lord of the gods.

**9.33.15**  Then the son of Dharma said to the son of Rohiṇī, that tamer of foes, "Behold this great combat of the two brothers, O Rāma!" — so he spoke, O Bhārata.

**9.33.16**  In their midst the mighty-armed and illustrious elder brother of Keśava took his seat, supremely pleased, honoured by the great chariot-warriors.

**9.33.17**  Seated amid the kings, clad in dark blue and of fair complexion, he shone like the moon in the sky, ringed about by the hosts of stars.

**9.33.18**  Then the clash of those two grew tumultuous and hair-raising, O king — a clash that would put an end to the enmity of your sons.

**9.34.1**  Janamejaya said: Earlier, when that war was at hand, the lord Rāma, having taken leave of Keśava, departed together with the Vṛṣṇis.

**9.34.2**  "I shall give no aid to the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, O Keśava, nor to the sons of Pāṇḍu; I shall go my way as I came."

**9.34.3**  Having said this, Rāma, the crusher of foes, departed then. Of his return now, O brāhmaṇa, you should tell me once more.

**9.34.4**  Recount to me in full how Rāma came to be present, and how he beheld the combat; for you are skilled, O most excellent one.

**9.34.5**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: While the great-souled Pāṇḍavas were encamped at Upaplavya, the slayer of Madhu was sent to the presence of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, in the cause of peace, O mighty-armed one, for the good of all living beings.

**9.34.6**  Having gone to Hāstinapura and met with Dhṛtarāṣṭra, he spoke words that were true and especially salutary; but the king did not act upon them, just as has been told you before.

**9.34.7**  Failing to win peace there, Kṛṣṇa, that best of men, the mighty-armed one, came back to Upaplavya, O lord of men.

**9.34.8**  Then Kṛṣṇa, having returned, dismissed by the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra with nothing accomplished, O tiger among men, said this to the Pāṇḍavas:

**9.34.9**  "The Kurus, driven on by fate, will not heed my word. March forth, O Pāṇḍavas, together with me, under the constellation Puṣya."

**9.34.10**  Then, as the forces were being marshalled, the best of the mighty, the great-souled son of Rohiṇī, spoke to his brother Kṛṣṇa:

**9.34.11**  "To those others too, O mighty-armed slayer of Madhu, let aid be given"; but Kṛṣṇa did not then act upon that word of his.

**9.34.12**  Then, his heart overcome with displeasure, the delight of the Yadus, the plough-bearer of great renown, set out on a pilgrimage to the sacred fords along the Sarasvatī, under the conjunction of the Maitra constellation, accompanied by all the Yādavas.

**9.34.13**  The Bhoja, that tamer of foes, gave his allegiance to Duryodhana, while Vāsudeva, together with Yuyudhāna, supported the Pāṇḍavas.

**9.34.14**  When the valiant son of Rohiṇī had departed, the slayer of Madhu, under the star Puṣya, placing the Pāṇḍavas at the fore, marched forth to face the Kurus.

**9.34.15**  As he journeyed, while still upon the road, Rāma said to his attendants: "Bring from Dvārakā the provisions for the pilgrimage and all the requisites — the sacred fires and the officiating priests as well."

**9.34.16**  "Gold and silver, milch cows, garments, horses, elephants and chariots, mules and camels and beasts of burden — let all the equipage for the sake of the pilgrimage be brought quickly."

**9.34.17**  "Travel swiftly, upstream along the Sarasvatī; and bring the officiating priests and bulls among the twice-born by the hundred."

**9.34.18**  Having thus instructed his attendants, the mighty Baladeva set forth on the pilgrimage, O king, during the slaughter of the Kurus, journeying upstream along the Sarasvatī from the sea.

**9.34.19**  Surrounded by officiating priests and friends and other most excellent of the twice-born, by chariots, elephants and horses and attendants, O bull of the Bharatas, and by many conveyances drawn by oxen, mules and camels.

**9.34.20**  For the weary and worn of body, for children and for the long-lived, those conveyances waited in their several places, O Bhārata; and food was made ready on every side for the sake of the hungry.

**9.34.21**  Whatever twice-born one wished, wherever, to take his food, to that very one, just there, they brought it then, O king.

**9.34.22**  The men stationed there, O king, by the command of the son of Rohiṇī, made heaps of food and drink everywhere about.

**9.34.23**  And costly garments, couches and spreadings were arranged there for the honouring of the brāhmaṇas who sought comfort.

**9.34.24**  Wherever a brāhmaṇa, or a kṣatriya too, lay down to sleep, O Bhārata, there for that very one was everything seen ready prepared.

**9.34.25**  All the folk then rested or journeyed at their ease — conveyances for one who wished to travel, and drink for one who thirsted,

**9.34.26**  and savoury foods for one who hungered, O bull of the Bhāratas; there the men brought garments and ornaments besides.

**9.34.27**  That road shone forth, O king, bringing comfort to every one; it was then like heaven, O hero, for the men who travelled there.

**9.34.28**  Filled with unending joy, abounding in delicacies, attended by auspicious things, thronged with hundreds of diverse folk trading their wares in shops and markets, set about with many trees and creepers, and adorned with jewels of every kind.

**9.34.29**  Then the great-souled one, his self set firm in vows, at the holy fords, O king, gave wealth to the twice-born and the sacrificial fees — that foremost hero of the Yadus, the renowned plough-bearer.

**9.34.30**  Milk-giving cows by the thousand, draped in fine cloth, their horns bound with gold, and horses born in lands of many kinds, conveyances and serving-women — these he gave to the twice-born.

**9.34.31**  Jewels — pearls, gems and coral — and horned gold and bright silver, and vessels of iron and of copper, Rāma bestowed upon the foremost of the twice-born.

**9.34.32**  Thus did the great-souled one bestow abundant wealth at the excellent fords of the Sarasvatī; then he of incomparable might and noble conduct came by stages to Kurukṣetra.

**9.34.33**  Janamejaya said: Tell me of the origin of the virtues of the fords along the Sarasvatī, and their fruit, O best of two-footed beings, and the accomplishment of rites there.

**9.34.34**  And in due order, O blessed one, of the fords one after another, O brāhmaṇa, O best of the knowers of brahman; for great is my curiosity.

**9.34.35**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Hear, O king, the full account of the fords and the origin of their virtues in every part, the holy tale told by me, O lord of kings, in its entirety.

**9.34.36**  First, O great king, that foremost hero of the Yadus, together with hosts of officiating priests, friends and brāhmaṇas, came to the holy Prabhāsa — where the lord of the stars was being afflicted by consumption.

**9.34.37**  There, freed from the curse and regaining his splendour, he lights up the whole world, O lord of men. Thus this foremost ford upon the earth, from his shining forth, is therefore called Prabhāsa.

**9.34.38**  Janamejaya said: For what reason was the blessed Soma seized by consumption? And how did the moon plunge himself into that foremost of fords?

**9.34.39**  And how, having bathed therein, was the hare-marked moon replenished once more? Tell me all of this in full, O great sage.

**9.34.40**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Those who were the daughters of Dakṣa came into being, O lord of peoples; and Dakṣa gave twenty-seven maidens to Soma.

**9.34.41**  Devoted to the conjunctions of the constellations and serving for the reckoning of time, O Bhārata, they were the wives of that Soma, O lord of kings, marked with auspicious signs.

**9.34.42**  All of them were wide-eyed and unequalled in beauty upon the earth; yet among them Rohiṇī surpassed the rest in the fullness of her beauty.

**9.34.43**  Therefore the blessed maker of the night set his love upon her; she became dear to his heart, and so he ever resorted to her.

**9.34.44**  For in olden time, O lord of kings, Soma dwelt long with Rohiṇī; and at this the constellations, wives of the great-souled one, grew wroth.

**9.34.45**  They went to their father, the Lord of Creatures, and without delay declared: "Soma dwells not with us; he resorts ever to Rohiṇī."

**9.34.46**  "We all, gathered together, will dwell in your presence, O lord of creatures, with restricted food, intent upon the practice of austerities."

**9.34.47**  Having heard their words, Dakṣa then said to Soma: "Behave equally toward your wives, lest a great unrighteousness touch you."

**9.34.48**  And to all of them Dakṣa said: "Go to Soma's side; by my command the moon shall dwell equally with all of you."

**9.34.49**  Dismissed, they then went to the dwelling of the cool-rayed one; yet even so the blessed Soma, O lord of the earth, dwelt still with Rohiṇī, delighting in her again and again.

**9.34.50**  Then all of them together spoke again to their father: "Engaged in serving you, we shall dwell in your hermitage; Soma dwells not with us, he has not done your bidding."

**9.34.51**  Having heard those words of theirs, Dakṣa then said to Soma: "Behave equally toward your wives, lest I curse you, O shining one."

**9.34.52**  But disregarding that word of Dakṣa, the blessed hare-marked one dwelt with Rohiṇī; then once more those wives grew wroth.

**9.34.53**  And going to their father, bowing then with their heads, they said: "Soma dwells not with us; therefore be you our refuge."

**9.34.54**  "With Rohiṇī alone, O blessed one, the moon dwells ever; therefore protect us all, that Soma may come to us."

**9.34.55**  Hearing this, the blessed one, wrathful, O lord of the earth, in his anger let loose consumption against Soma; and it entered the lord of the stars.

**9.34.56**  His being overcome by the consumption, the hare-marked one waned day by day; and he made effort, O king, to be freed of that consumption.

**9.34.57**  Though the maker of the night performed sacrifices of many kinds, O great king, he was not released from the curse, and indeed came to wasting away.

**9.34.58**  Then, as Soma wasted away, the herbs no longer grew; all of them were robbed of savour and wholly stripped of their potency.

**9.34.59**  When the wasting of the herbs had come about, there was wasting of living beings too; and all creatures grew gaunt as the maker of the night waned away.

**9.34.60**  Then the gods, having assembled, said to Soma, O lord of the earth: "What is this? Your form does not shine forth as it once did."

**9.34.61**  "Tell us all the cause, whereby this great peril has come upon you; and having heard the word from you, we shall then provide a remedy."

**9.34.62**  Thus addressed, the hare-marked one answered all of them — the curse, and its cause, and the consumption that had come upon himself.

**9.34.63**  The gods, hearing his words, went to Dakṣa and said: "Be gracious, O blessed one, toward Soma, and let this curse be turned back."

**9.34.64**  "For yonder moon is wasted, and but a little of him is seen remaining; and through his wasting, O lord of the gods, the creatures too have come to ruin."

**9.34.65**  "The plants and herbs and the seeds of many kinds, and we ourselves, O teacher of the world — you should show us grace."

**9.34.66**  Thus addressed, the Lord of Creatures pondered and spoke this word: "This word of mine cannot be turned otherwise; yet, O illustrious ones, by some means it shall be reversed."

**9.34.67**  "Let the hare-marked one behave equally toward all his wives evermore; and emerging at the excellent ford of the Sarasvatī, the hare-marked one shall grow full again, O gods — that word of mine is true."

**9.34.68**  "For half a month Soma shall ever pass to waning, and for half a month always to waxing — this word of mine is true."

**9.34.69**  Then, by the seer's command, Soma went to the Sarasvatī; he came indeed to Prabhāsa, the supreme tīrtha upon the Sarasvatī.

**9.34.70**  On the new-moon day the mighty-splendored one, rising up there in great brilliance, illumined the worlds and regained his cool-rayed nature.

**9.34.71**  And all the gods, O lord of kings, having reached abundant Prabhāsa and joined together with Soma, stood before Dakṣa.

**9.34.72**  Then the Lord of Creatures dismissed all the deities, and the blessed one, pleased, spoke once more to Soma these words:

**9.34.73**  "Despise not women, my son, nor ever the brāhmaṇas. Go now, and being ever diligent, carry out my command."

**9.34.74**  Dismissed thus, O great king, he went to his own abode, and the creatures grew glad, partaking of nourishment as before.

**9.34.75**  All this I have told you—how the night-maker was cursed, and how Prabhāsa came to be the foremost among tīrthas.

**9.34.76**  On every new-moon day, O great king, the hare-marked one, the glorious Moon, bathing at the supreme tīrtha of Prabhāsa, is replenished.

**9.34.77**  Therefore men know it as Prabhāsa, O lord of the earth; for the Moon, rising up there, attained his supreme radiance (prabhā).

**9.34.78**  Then the unfallen, mighty one went on to Camasodbheda—that which men speak of as Camasodbheda.

**9.34.79**  There Halāyudha, having bestowed choice gifts, stayed a single night and bathed according to the prescribed rite.

**9.34.80**  Then the elder brother of Keśava went swiftly to Udapāna, gaining there too the foremost benediction and great reward.

**9.34.81**  For by the moistness of the herbs and of the earth, O Janamejaya, the perfected ones, O lord of kings, discern the Sarasvatī even where she has vanished.

**9.35.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: From there Halāyudha went on, O great king, to the Udapāna—the river-sunk well of the illustrious Trita.

**9.35.2**  There the wielder of the pestle, well pleased, bestowed much wealth and honored the brāhmaṇas, and touched the water in that very place.

**9.35.3**  There it was that Trita dwelt, devoted to dharma, of mighty austerity; and that great soul, while abiding in the well, drank the soma.

**9.35.4**  And there, abandoning him, his two brothers went home; whereupon Trita, that best of brāhmaṇas, laid a curse upon them both.

**9.35.5**  Janamejaya said: How came the well to be, O brāhmaṇa, and how did that one of great austerity, that best of the twice-born, fall in, and why was he forsaken by his brothers?

**9.35.6**  And how, leaving him in the well, did the two brothers go home? Tell me this, O brāhmaṇa, if you think it fit to be heard.

**9.35.7**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: In a former age, O king, there were three brother-sages, Ekata, Dvita, and Trita, resembling the sun.

**9.35.8**  All were like Prajāpati, blessed with offspring, all winners of the world of Brahman by austerity, all expounders of the Veda.

**9.35.9**  Their father Gautama, ever devoted to dharma, was always pleased by their austerity, their discipline, and their self-restraint.

**9.35.10**  And in the course of long time, having gained their devotion, the blessed one departed to the abode befitting himself.

**9.35.11**  Those kings who before had been the patrons of that great soul, when he had gone to heaven, all honored his sons.

**9.35.12**  By his works and by his study, O king, Trita attained pre-eminence, even as his father had done.

**9.35.13**  All the blessed sages of holy mark honored that illustrious one for his learning as well.

**9.35.14**  Now once, O king, the two brothers Ekata and Dvita set their minds upon a sacrifice, and especially upon gaining wealth thereby.

**9.35.15**  This thought arose in the two, O scorcher of foes: to take Trita with them, to gather all their patrons, and to receive cattle from them.

**9.35.16**  "Joyfully we shall drink the soma, having gained a sacrifice of great fruit." And so, O great king, the three brothers performed it.

**9.35.17**  Going about thus among all their patrons for the sake of cattle, they performed sacrifices for them, and so obtained from those patrons very many beasts.

**9.35.18**  Having received their due by that priestly office according to rule, those great-souled seers came toward the eastern quarter.

**9.35.19**  Trita walked before them, O great king, gladly, while Ekata and Dvita came behind, driving the cattle.

**9.35.20**  Seeing the great herd of cattle, a thought arose in the two: "How might these cows be ours, the two of us, without Trita?"

**9.35.21**  Conferring with one another, Ekata and Dvita—the two sinners—spoke together; hear what they said, O lord of men.

**9.35.22**  "Trita is skilled in sacrifices, Trita is grounded in the Vedas; Trita will obtain still other and far more numerous cows."

**9.35.23**  "Therefore let the two of us, joined together, drive off the cows and go; and let Trita too go where he will, parted from us."

**9.35.24**  As they came on by night, a wolf appeared in their path; and not far off, on the bank of the Sarasvatī, there was a great well.

**9.35.25**  Then Trita, seeing the wolf standing before him in the path, recoiled in fear of it and fell into that well—bottomless, most terrible, dreadful to all beings.

**9.35.26**  Then the illustrious Trita, that best of sages, fallen within the well, raised a cry of distress, and the two sages heard it.

**9.35.27**  Knowing him fallen into the well, the brothers Ekata and Dvita, out of fear of the wolf and out of greed, abandoned him and went on.

**9.35.28**  Forsaken by his two brothers, greedy for cattle, that one of great austerity lay, O great king, in the well, waterless and choked with dust.

**9.35.29**  Trita beheld himself sunk in the well overgrown with creepers and grass, O best of Bhāratas, as a sinner sunk in hell.

**9.35.30**  Wise as he was, fearing death and yet undrinker of soma, he reckoned in his mind: "How might the soma be drunk by me, while I bide here?"

**9.35.31**  Pondering thus in that well, the one of great austerity saw a creeper hanging there by chance.

**9.35.32**  Then in that dust-clogged well the sage, imagining the water, conceived the sacrificial fires, and himself as the hotṛ-priest.

**9.35.33**  Then, conceiving that creeper to be the soma, the sage of mighty austerity, calling to mind in thought the Ṛcs, the Yajuses, and the Sāmans, and making the pebbles into the pressing-stones, performed the pressing, O king.

**9.35.34**  He made the water into clarified butter and the portions for the dwellers of the threefold heaven; and having pressed the soma, he raised a tumultuous sound.

**9.35.35**  And that trained, well-pitched chant of Trita pierced heaven, O king; and he accomplished that sacrifice as it is prescribed by the expounders of the Veda.

**9.35.36**  As the sacrifice of the great-souled Trita went forward, all the threefold heaven was thrown into agitation, and none could fathom the cause.

**9.35.37**  Then Bṛhaspati heard that most tumultuous sound, and hearing it, the chaplain of the gods spoke to all the deities:

**9.35.38**  "The sacrifice of Trita is in progress; let us go thither, O gods. For that one of great austerity, if angered, might create other gods as well."

**9.35.39**  Hearing that word of his, all the deities together set forth to the place where that sacrifice of Trita was being performed.

**9.35.40**  The gods, having gone there to the well where Trita was, beheld that great soul consecrated for the sacrificial rites.

**9.35.41**  And seeing that great soul endowed with surpassing splendor, they said to the illustrious one: "We have come, seeking our portions."

**9.35.42**  Then the seer said to the gods: "Behold me, O dwellers of heaven, sunk in this fearful well and well-nigh out of my senses."

**9.35.43**  Then Trita, O great king, gave them their portions according to rule, accompanied by the sacred formulas; and they were well pleased thereat.

**9.35.44**  Then the dwellers of heaven, having received the portions duly obtained, glad at heart, granted him the boons that he desired in his mind.

**9.35.45**  And he chose this boon: "You ought to deliver me from here; and whoever touches the water in this well, let him attain the state of the soma-drinkers."

**9.35.46**  Thereupon, O king, the Sarasvatī surged up there full of waves, and Trita, lifted by her, stood honoring the dwellers of heaven.

**9.35.47**  Saying "So be it," the gods went, O king, as they had come; and Trita too, well pleased, then went to his own abode.

**9.35.48**  But that one of great austerity, having reached his two brother-sages, then in anger spoke harsh words to them and cursed them:

**9.35.49**  "Since the two of you, greedy for cattle, forsook me and ran away, therefore in the form of fanged beasts you shall roam about,

**9.35.50**  cursed by me for this evil deed; and your offspring shall be langurs, bears, and monkeys."

**9.35.51**  And when this was spoken by him, that very instant, O lord of peoples, the two were seen so transformed by the word of the truth-speaker.

**9.35.52**  There too Halāyudha of boundless valor touched the water, bestowed gifts of many kinds, and honored the brāhmaṇas.

**9.35.53**  And having seen that river-sunk well and praised it again and again, the undaunted one then reached Vinaśana.

**9.36.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Then, O king, Halāyudha came to Vinaśana, where the Sarasvatī vanished out of loathing for the Śūdras and the Ābhīras.

**9.36.2**  Because she, the Sarasvatī, O best of Bhāratas, vanished out of loathing, therefore the seers ever call that place Vinaśana.

**9.36.3**  And there too Bala of mighty strength touched the waters of the Sarasvatī, and then went on to Subhūmika upon the fair bank of the Sarasvatī.

**9.36.4**  There the radiant apsarases, ever unwearied, of unblemished countenance, sport at all times with pure and lovely games.

**9.36.5**  There, O lord of men, the gods together with the gandharvas come month after month to that holy tīrtha frequented by brāhmaṇas.

**9.36.6**  There the gandharvas and likewise the throngs of apsarases were seen, gathered and joined together, O king, each as it chanced and at their ease.

**9.36.7**  There the gods and the ancestors rejoice amid the herbs, ever showered again and again with holy, heavenly blossoms.

**9.36.8**  That fair playground of those apsarases, O king, is renowned as Subhūmika, upon the lovely bank of the Sarasvatī.

**9.36.9**  There the Mādhava, having bathed and bestowed wealth upon the brāhmaṇas, and having heard that heavenly song and the sound of the instruments—

**9.36.10**  and having seen the broad shadows of the gods, gandharvas, and rākṣasas—the son of Rohiṇī then came to the tīrtha of the gandharvas.

**9.36.11**  There the gandharvas led by Viśvāvasu, endowed with austerity, make most delightful dance, music, and song.

**9.36.12**  There the plough-bearer bestowed upon the brāhmaṇas manifold wealth—goats and sheep, cattle, asses and camels, gold and silver as well—

**9.36.13**  and having fed the twice-born with all they desired and gratified them with great riches, the Mādhava went onward in the company of the brāhmaṇas, being praised by them.

**9.36.14**  And from that gandharva-tīrtha the mighty-armed subduer of foes, wearing a single earring, came to the great tīrtha of Gargasrotas.

**9.36.15**  There it was that the aged Garga, his soul refined by austerity, attained the science of the reckoning of time and of the courses and transits of the heavenly bodies.

**9.36.16**  There too, O Janamejaya, the dread and the auspicious portents were determined by that great soul at the holy ford of the Sarasvatī; and after his name that ford is remembered as Gargasrotas.

**9.36.17**  There, O king, the sages of firm vows constantly waited upon the illustrious Garga, O lord, to learn from him the science of time.

**9.36.18**  Having gone there, O great king, Bala, anointed with white sandal paste, duly bestowed wealth upon the sages whose souls were refined.

**9.36.19**  And having given to the brāhmaṇas foods of every kind, high and humble, that hero of great renown, clad in dark blue, then proceeded to the Śaṅkha ford.

**9.36.20**  There the mighty Bala, the palmyra-bannered, beheld a great conch-tree, lofty as Mount Meru, resembling a white mountain, frequented by hosts of sages, a tree grown upon the bank of the Sarasvatī.

**9.36.21**  There were yakṣas and vidyādharas and rākṣasas of boundless energy, and piśācas of measureless strength, and siddhas by the thousand.

**9.36.22**  All of them, forsaking other food, would from time to time eat only the fruit of that lord of the forest, observing their vows and restraints.

**9.36.23**  Keeping each his own appointed observances, they roamed about apart, one and all, and ranged there unseen by men, O bull among men.

**9.36.24**  Thus famed in this world, O ruler of men, was that lord of the forest; and there upon the Sarasvatī is a purifying ford renowned throughout the world.

**9.36.25**  At that ford the tiger of the Yadus bestowed upon the renowned sages vessels of copper and of iron and garments of every kind.

**9.36.26**  Having honored the brāhmaṇas and being himself honored by those rich in austerity, Halāyudha came, O king, to the holy Dvaitavana.

**9.36.27**  Having gone there and beheld the sages wearing garbs of many kinds, Bala bathed in the water and paid honor to the brāhmaṇas.

**9.36.28**  And having likewise given the brāhmaṇas ample objects of enjoyment, Bala then set forth, O king, along the southern bank of the Sarasvatī.

**9.36.29**  And that mighty-armed one of great renown, righteous of soul and unfailing, having gone no great distance, came to the ford named Nāgadhanvan.

**9.36.30**  There, O great king, was the abode of Vāsuki, king of serpents, of mighty splendor, surrounded by many serpents; and there dwelt fourteen thousand sages who had attained perfection.

**9.36.31**  There the gods, having assembled, duly consecrated Vāsuki, foremost of serpents, as king over all the serpents; and there, O Kaurava, no fear of serpents is to be found.

**9.36.32**  There too, having duly bestowed heaps of jewels upon the brāhmaṇas, he proceeded toward the east, O king, blazing with his own splendor.

**9.36.33**  Bathing again and again with delight in those fords, the plough-bearer gave wealth to the twice-born and passed on, surpassingly given to austerity.

**9.36.34**  Having saluted the hosts of sages who abode there, Halāyudha—Rāma—then went to a great ford frequented by sages.

**9.36.35**  There the Sarasvatī once again turned back and flowed eastward, for the sake of the great-souled sages of the Naimiṣa forest.

**9.36.36**  Beholding that best of rivers there turned back upon her course, Bala the plough-bearer, anointed with white sandal, was filled with wonder, O king.

**9.36.37**  Janamejaya said: Why, O brāhmaṇa, did the Sarasvatī turn back and flow eastward? I wish to hear all this explained, O best of priests.

**9.36.38**  And for what cause was the joy of the Yadus filled with wonder there, and how did that best of rivers turn back? Tell me this, O most excellent of the twice-born.

**9.36.39**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Of old, in the Kṛta age, O king, the ascetics of the Naimiṣa forest were holding a vast sacrificial session lasting twelve years; and many sages, O king, assembled there together.

**9.36.40**  And when those illustrious ones had dwelt at that session according to the ordinance, and the twelve-year session of the Naimiṣa sages had come to its close, many sages came thither for the sake of the holy fords.

**9.36.41**  And by reason of the multitude of sages, O lord of peoples, the fords along the southern bank of the Sarasvatī then took on the likeness of cities.

**9.36.42**  As far as Samantapañcaka, O tiger among men, those most excellent of the twice-born, longing for the holy fords, took their stand along the river's shore.

**9.36.43**  As those sages of refined souls offered their oblations, the quarters of the sky were filled with the mighty sound of their study of the sacred texts.

**9.36.44**  Then, with the fire-oblations of those great souls being offered and blazing on every side, the best of rivers shone resplendent.

**9.36.45**  There were the Vālakhilyas, O great king, and ascetics who ground their food with stones, and others who used their teeth for a mortar, and still others who washed clean all they ate.

**9.36.46**  And ascetics who fed on air, who took only water, who ate but leaves, who kept observances of many kinds, and who lay upon the bare ground.

**9.36.47**  Such sages dwelt there beside the Sarasvatī, gracing that best of rivers as the dwellers of heaven grace the Gaṅgā.

**9.36.48**  Thereafter came other sages, performers of sacrificial sessions; but those keepers of mighty vows found no open room for them in Kurukṣetra.

**9.36.49**  Then with their sacred threads they measured out a ford for themselves, offered their fire-oblations, and performed rites of many kinds.

**9.36.50**  Then, O lord of kings, the Sarasvatī showed herself for their sake to that company of sages who were dejected and sunk in anxious thought.

**9.36.51**  Then, out of compassion for the sages of holy austerities, the best of rivers turned back her course, forming many bowers, O Janamejaya.

**9.36.52**  Then, having turned back for their sake, O lord of kings, the Sarasvatī, best of rivers, flowed once more toward the west.

**9.36.53**  "Not in vain shall my coming be: for their sake I flow back again,"—and so, O king, the great river wrought a great marvel.

**9.36.54**  Thus, O lord of kings, that bower in Kurukṣetra is remembered as Naimiṣeya; there, O best of the Kurus, perform great rites.

**9.36.55**  Beholding there the many bowers and that river turned back upon her course, wonder then came over the great-souled Rāma.

**9.36.56**  There too the joy of the Yadus touched the water according to the rite, and bestowed gifts upon the twice-born—vessels of many kinds and food and drink of every sort he supplied to the brāhmaṇas.

**9.36.57**  Then Bala set forth, O king, honored by the twice-born, to an excellent ford of the Sarasvatī thronged with hosts of birds of many kinds.

**9.36.58**  It was adorned with jujube and iṅguda, kāśmarya and plakṣa, fig-tree and vibhītaka, with breadfruit and palāśa, with karīra and pīlu trees.

**9.36.59**  And with trees grown on the Sarasvatī's bank—the bandhana and the syandana, with groves of parūṣaka, and with bilva and āmrātaka trees.

**9.36.60**  It was graced with thickets of atimuktaka and with pārijāta trees, abounding most in groves of plantain—a place beloved, lovely, and delightful to the heart.

**9.36.61**  It was peopled by many forest sages who fed on air, on water, on fruit, and on leaves, who used their teeth for a mortar, and who ground their food with stones.

**9.36.62**  It resounded with the chant of sacred study, was thronged with hundreds of herds of deer, and was much frequented by men who did no harm and held righteousness supreme.

**9.36.63**  To the ford named Saptasārasvata Halāyudha came, where the great sage Maṅkaṇaka, who had won perfection, had performed his austerities.

**9.37.1**  Janamejaya said: Why is it called Saptasārasvata, and who was the sage Maṅkaṇaka? How did the venerable one win perfection, and what was the observance he kept?

**9.37.2**  In whose lineage was he born, and what did he learn, O most excellent of the twice-born? All this I wish to hear in due order, O best of brāhmaṇas.

**9.37.3**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: O king, there are seven Sarasvatīs, by whom this world is pervaded; for, summoned by the mighty here and there, the Sarasvatī came.

**9.37.4**  They are Suprabhā and Kāñcanākṣī, Viśālā and Mānasahradā, the Sarasvatī known as Oghavatī, Suveṇu, and Vimalodakā.

**9.37.5**  While a sacrifice of the mighty Grandsire was in progress upon the earth, the sacrificial enclosure spread wide and the twice-born assembled there—

**9.37.6**  with the pure proclamations of the holy day and the resounding of the Vedas, while the gods themselves were intent upon that sacrificial rite—

**9.37.7**  there, O great king, when the great-grandsire had been consecrated and was offering a session that fulfilled every desire,

**9.37.8**  whatever things the twice-born, skilled in righteousness and worldly gain, conceived in their minds, those things, O lord of kings, came to them there of their own accord.

**9.37.9**  There the gandharvas sang, and the troops of apsarases danced, and divine instruments of music were straightway sounded.

**9.37.10**  At the perfection of that sacrifice even the gods were delighted and fell into the utmost wonder—how much more, then, those of mortal birth?

**9.37.11**  As the sacrifice went forward thus, with the Grandsire abiding at Puṣkara, the sages said, O king: "This sacrifice cannot bear great fruit, since the best of rivers, the Sarasvatī, is nowhere seen here."

**9.37.12**  Hearing this, the venerable one was pleased and called the Sarasvatī to mind; and being summoned by the sacrificing Grandsire, the Sarasvatī came to Puṣkara, there bearing the name Suprabhā, O lord of kings.

**9.37.13**  Beholding the swift-flowing Sarasvatī, who came to do honor to the Grandsire, the sages were gladdened and held that sacrifice in high esteem.

**9.37.14**  Thus this best of rivers, the Sarasvatī, came into being at Puṣkara for the Grandsire's sake and for the delight of the wise.

**9.37.15**  At the Naimiṣa forest, O king, the sages assembled and sat together; and there, O lord of peoples, wondrous discourses arose concerning the Veda.

**9.37.16**  There were sages versed in the recitation of various branches of sacred lore; and those sages, having gathered, called the Sarasvatī to mind.

**9.37.17**  Thus meditated upon, O great king, by the sages who were performing the session, the illustrious and holy Sarasvatī came thither, O lord of kings, to aid those great souls who had assembled.

**9.37.18**  At the Naimiṣa forest the best of rivers came as Kāñcanākṣī, and there, O Bhārata, she was worshipped by the sages who performed the session.

**9.37.19**  When Gaya was offering a great sacrifice at Gayā, the best of rivers, the Sarasvatī, was summoned to the sacrifice of Gaya.

**9.37.20**  At Gayā the sages of strict vows call her Viśālā—that swift-flowing river, born from the side of Himavat.

**9.37.21**  And at the sacrifice of Auddālaka, when he was offering it, O Bhārata, with a great circle of sages assembled there from every side,

**9.37.22**  in the northern region of Kosala, O king, the holy place of that great soul, the Sarasvatī was first called to mind by the sacrificing Auddālaka.

**9.37.23**  The best of rivers came to that country for the sages' sake, honored by hosts of sages clad in bark and deerskin; and because she was drawn thither by their minds, she became famed as Mānasahradā.

**9.37.24**  As Suveṇu the illustrious best of rivers, the Sarasvatī, came to the holy isle of Ṛṣabha, frequented by royal sages, and to Kurukṣetra when the great-souled Kuru was sacrificing there.

**9.37.25**  As Oghavatī too, O lord of kings, the Sarasvatī of divine waters was summoned at Kurukṣetra by the great soul Vasiṣṭha.

**9.37.26**  And summoned by Dakṣa when he sacrificed at Gaṅgādvāra, the divine Sarasvatī came as Vimalodā; and again, summoned by Brahmā at his sacrifice, she went thither to the holy mountain of Himavat.

**9.37.27**  Then those seven, gathered together at that ford, became one; and thus that ford became renowned upon the earth as Saptasārasvata.

**9.37.28**  Thus the seven Sarasvatīs have been proclaimed by name, and thus too the holy ford of Saptasārasvata is remembered.

**9.37.29**  Hear now of the great sport of Maṅkaṇaka also, who had kept the vow of celibacy from his boyhood, when he had plunged into the river, O king.

**9.37.30**  Beholding by chance there in the water, O Bhārata, a woman bathing—fair of glance, clad only in the quarters of the sky, and blameless—his seed, O great king, fell into the waters of the Sarasvatī.

**9.37.31**  That seed the great ascetic gathered into a jar; and there within the jar it split into seven parts, and from it were born seven sages, the hosts of the Maruts.

**9.37.32**  Vāyuvega and Vāyubala, Vāyuha and Vāyumaṇḍala, Vāyujvāla, Vāyuretas, and the valiant Vāyucakra — thus were these born, the begetters of the hosts of the Maruts.

**9.37.33**  And hear now, O lord of kings, another wonder more marvellous still upon this earth — such a deed of that great seer as is renowned throughout the three worlds.

**9.37.34**  Of old, Maṅkaṇaka the perfected one — so we have heard — wounded his hand upon the tip of a kuśa-blade, O king, and from it flowed a sap like vegetable juice; and beholding that sap, he was seized with joy and fell to dancing.

**9.37.35**  Then as he danced, all that was unmoving and all that moves — both alike, O hero — fell to dancing, bewildered by the force of his power.

**9.37.36**  Then the gods led by Brahmā, O king, and the seers rich in penance besought Mahādeva on the seer's behalf, O lord of men: 'Bring it to pass, O god, that this one dance no longer.'

**9.37.37**  Then the god, seeing the sage utterly transported with joy, Mahādeva, desiring the welfare of the gods, addressed him thus:

**9.37.38**  'Ho there, Brāhmaṇa, knower of dharma, why do you dance on so? What cause for joy is this of yours, O best of sages — for one given to penance and set upon the path of dharma, O best of the twice-born?'

**9.37.39**  The seer said: 'Do you not see, O brahman, the sap that has flowed from my hand? Beholding it I fell to dancing in great joy, O mighty one.'

**9.37.40**  Laughing, the god said to the sage thus bewildered by passion: 'I, O brahman, am not moved to wonder by such a thing — look upon me.'

**9.37.41**  Having so spoken to the best of sages, the wise Mahādeva, O lord of kings, struck his own thumb with the tip of his finger.

**9.37.42**  Then from the cut, O king, came forth ash white as snow; and seeing it, O king, the sage was abashed and fell at his feet.

**9.37.43**  The seer said: 'I deem none greater, none mightier, than the god Rudra; you, wielder of the trident, are the refuge of the world of gods and demons alike.'

**9.37.44**  'By you was this universe created, so the wise declare here; and into you alone does all enter again at the ending of the age.'

**9.37.45**  'Not even by the gods can you be fully known — how then by me? In you are all the gods beheld, beginning with Brahmā, O sinless one.'

**9.37.46**  'You are all things; you are of the gods the doer and the cause of doing; by your grace all the gods rejoice here, freed from every fear.'

**9.37.47**  Having thus praised Mahādeva, the seer bowed low and said: 'O Lord, by your grace may my penance never wane.'

**9.37.48**  Then the god, well pleased in heart, spoke again to that seer: 'May your penance increase a thousandfold by my grace, O brahman; and here in this hermitage I shall ever dwell together with you.'

**9.37.49**  'And whatever man shall worship me here at this Seven-Sarasvatī, nothing shall be hard for him to win, in this world or the next; and they shall go to the Sārasvata world, of this there is no doubt.'

**9.37.50**  Such was the deed of Maṅkaṇaka of abundant splendour; for he was a son begotten upon Sajanyā by Mātariśvan, the Wind.

**9.38.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Having dwelt there and honoured the dwellers of the hermitage, Rāma the plough-wielder showed too his gracious favour toward Maṅkaṇaka.

**9.38.2**  Having given gifts to the twice-born and passed that night in fasting, honoured by the throngs of sages, the plough-bearer rose at dawn.

**9.38.3**  Having taken leave of all the sages and touched water, O Bhārata, mighty Rāma set forth in haste for the sake of the sacred fords.

**9.38.4**  Then the plough-wielder came to the ford of Uśanas, called Kapālamocana, the Skull-Release, where the great sage was set free.

**9.38.5**  There, O king, Mahodara had had his leg gripped by a great head — the head of a rākṣasa hurled of old by Rāma, O mighty monarch.

**9.38.6**  There of old the great-souled Kāvya practised penance, and there the whole of his statecraft came forth for that high-souled one; abiding there he pondered the warfare of the Daityas and Dānavas.

**9.38.7**  And having reached that most excellent of fords, O king, Bala duly bestowed wealth upon the great-souled Brāhmaṇas.

**9.38.8**  Janamejaya said: How, O brahman, came it to be the Skull-Release, where the great sage was freed? How did the head cleave to him, and for what cause?

**9.38.9**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Of old, in the Daṇḍaka forest, by the great-souled Rāghava dwelling there, O tiger among kings, were the rākṣasas slain.

**9.38.10**  At Janasthāna the head of an evil-souled rākṣasa was severed with a keen-edged shaft, and it fell within the great forest.

**9.38.11**  That head, by chance, fastened upon the shin of Mahodara as he wandered in the forest, O king, piercing the bone and there it stuck fast.

**9.38.12**  With that thing clinging to him the twice-born one, wise though he was, could no longer make his way to the fords and holy places.

**9.38.13**  Tormented with pain by the foul stuff that oozed from it, the great sage went, so we have heard, to all the sacred fords upon the earth.

**9.38.14**  Having gone to all the rivers and to the seas, that great ascetic told all this to the seers of purified souls.

**9.38.15**  Bathing in all the fords, he yet won no release; but that lord of brahmans heard a weighty word of the sages.

**9.38.16**  That on the Sarasvatī there was a renowned excellent ford of Uśanas, dispeller of all sins, an unsurpassed field of the perfected ones.

**9.38.17**  And the twice-born one, going thither to the ford of Uśanas, as he touched the water at the ford of Uśanas, that head let go his foot and fell down into the depths of the water.

**9.38.18**  Then Mahodara, freed of pain, O king, his soul made pure and his stain gone, came back glad to his hermitage, his purpose fulfilled.

**9.38.19**  And the great ascetic, set free and come to the holy hermitage, told all this to the seers of purified souls.

**9.38.20**  Hearing his words, O giver of honour, the assembled sages gave to that ford the name Kapālamocana, the Skull-Release.

**9.38.21**  There Mādhava, the foremost of the Vṛṣṇis, having given many gifts and honoured the brahmans, went then to the hermitage of Ruṣaṅgu.

**9.38.22**  Where Ārṣṭiṣeṇa had practised dread penance, O Bhārata, and where the great sage Viśvāmitra had won the estate of a brāhmaṇa.

**9.38.23**  Thence the glorious plough-bearer, surrounded by brahmans, went, O lord of kings, to the place where Ruṣaṅgu had laid down his body.

**9.38.24**  Ruṣaṅgu, an aged brāhmaṇa ever devoted to penance, O Bhārata, set his mind upon laying down his body, having pondered the matter long and in many ways.

**9.38.25**  Then the great ascetic Ruṣaṅgu, gathering all his sons, said to them: 'Lead me to Pṛthūdaka.'

**9.38.26**  Knowing Ruṣaṅgu, rich in penance, to be far advanced in years, those ascetics led him to that ford of the Sarasvatī.

**9.38.27**  Brought then by his sons, that wise one came to the Sarasvatī, holy, endowed with a hundred fords, frequented by throngs of brahmans.

**9.38.28**  There, O king, having bathed according to rite, that great ascetic, knowing the virtues of the ford, the best of seers, well pleased, O tiger among men, spoke thus to all his sons who attended upon him:

**9.38.29**  'Whoever on the northern bank of the Sarasvatī, at Pṛthūdaka, intent upon prayer, lays down his body — him the morrow's death shall not afflict.'

**9.38.30**  There the righteous plough-bearer bathed and touched the water, and gave many gifts, fond as he was of the brahmans.

**9.38.31**  Where the Lord, the Grandsire of the worlds, brought forth the worlds; where, O Kaurava, Ārṣṭiṣeṇa of firm vows, the best of seers, attained brāhmaṇa-hood through mighty penance, O king.

**9.38.32**  And where the royal seer Sindhudvīpa, and Devāpi of great penance, and the great sage Viśvāmitra — that mighty ascetic, the lord of fierce splendour and great penance — won brāhmaṇa-hood.

**9.38.33**  Thither came the mighty and majestic Balabhadra.

**9.39.1**  Janamejaya said: How did the lord Ārṣṭiṣeṇa practise his vast penance, and how too did Sindhudvīpa then win brāhmaṇa-hood?

**9.39.2**  And how Devāpi, O brahman, and how Viśvāmitra, O best of men? Tell me this, O lord, for great is my curiosity.

**9.39.3**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Of old, in the Kṛta age, O king, Ārṣṭiṣeṇa, best of the twice-born, dwelling ever in his teacher's house, was ever devoted to study.

**9.39.4**  Yet for him, O king, dwelling ever in his teacher's house, his learning came to no completion, nor did the Vedas, O lord of peoples.

**9.39.5**  Then, grown weary, O king, that great ascetic practised penance; and by that penance he won the supreme Vedas.

**9.39.6**  Learned and endowed with the Vedas and perfected, the best of seers, that great ascetic bestowed three boons there at the ford.

**9.39.7**  'At this ford of the great river, from this day forth, the man who bathes shall win the abundant fruit of the horse-sacrifice.'

**9.39.8**  'From this day forth there shall be here no fear from beasts of prey, and even with little effort one shall win abundant fruit.'

**9.39.9**  Having so spoken, the sage of great splendour went to the third heaven; thus did the majestic lord Ārṣṭiṣeṇa attain perfection.

**9.39.10**  At that very ford then the majestic Sindhudvīpa, and Devāpi, O mighty king, attained the high estate of brāhmaṇa-hood.

**9.39.11**  And likewise Kauśika, my son, ever devoted to penance, his senses subdued, attained brāhmaṇa-hood through well-practised penance.

**9.39.12**  There was a great kṣatriya named Gādhi, renowned upon the earth; his son, O king, was the majestic Viśvāmitra.

**9.39.13**  That king Kauśika, my son, became a great yogin, it is said; and the great ascetic, having consecrated his son Viśvāmitra,

**9.39.14**  set his mind upon laying down his body; but his subjects, bowing low, said to him: 'You must not go, O wise one; save us from this great peril.'

**9.39.15**  Thus addressed, Gādhi then answered his subjects: 'My son shall be the protector of the whole world.'

**9.39.16**  Having so spoken, Gādhi, having established Viśvāmitra in his place, went to the third heaven, O king; and Viśvāmitra became king, yet for all his effort he could not protect the earth.

**9.39.17**  Then that king heard of a great peril from the rākṣasas, and went forth from the city attended by his fourfold army.

**9.39.18**  Having gone a long road, he came upon the hermitage of Vasiṣṭha; and his soldiers, O king, wrought there many wrongs.

**9.39.19**  Then the lordly brahman Vasiṣṭha came to his hermitage and saw the whole great forest being broken to pieces.

**9.39.20**  Wrathful at this, O mighty king, Vasiṣṭha, the best of sages, said to his cow: 'Bring forth dread Śabaras.'

**9.39.21**  Thus bidden, the cow brought forth men of terrible aspect; and they, falling upon that army, scattered it in every direction.

**9.39.22**  Seeing his army put to flight, Viśvāmitra the son of Gādhi, deeming penance the higher power, set his mind upon penance alone.

**9.39.23**  At this excellent ford of the Sarasvatī, O king, with mind composed, he wasted his own body with vows and fasts.

**9.39.24**  He became one who fed on water, who fed on air, who fed on leaves; one who lay upon the bare ground, and observed whatever other separate vows there are.

**9.39.25**  Again and again the gods set obstacles to his vow, yet the resolve of that great soul would not turn aside from his observance.

**9.39.26**  Then, having practised manifold penance with the utmost effort, the son of Gādhi grew in splendour like unto the sun.

**9.39.27**  Viśvāmitra being thus endowed with penance, the Grandsire of mighty splendour, the giver of boons, deemed him worthy of that boon.

**9.39.28**  And he chose his boon, O king, saying: 'May I become a brāhmaṇa'; and Brahmā, the Grandsire of all the worlds, said, 'So be it.'

**9.39.29**  Having won brahminhood by fierce austerity, that man of great renown, his desire fulfilled and god-like in his splendour, roamed across the whole earth.

**9.39.30**  At that excellent sacred ford Rāma bestowed wealth of many kinds — milk-giving cows, draught animals, and couches —

**9.39.31**  and garments too, and ornaments, and fine food and drink. Gladly, O king, he gave these gifts, having honoured the foremost of the twice-born.
