**9.39.32**  Then, O king, Rāma went on from there to the hermitage of Baka close by, where, as tradition tells, Baka the son of Dalbha had performed fierce austerity.

**9.40.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: The delight of the Yadus went to that place thronged with men of brahmin lineage, where, O king, Baka the son of Dalbha, of mighty austerity, for the sake of cattle once offered up into the fire the kingdom of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, son of Vicitravīrya.

**9.40.2**  Wasting his own body with austerity of dreadful aspect, that righteous and mighty sage was seized by a great anger.

**9.40.3**  For long ago, when the twelve-year sacrificial session of the seers of Naimiṣa had been completed, at the close of the Viśvajit rite the sages went to the Pāñcālas.

**9.40.4**  There those wise men asked the lord of that land for a sacrificial fee: twenty-one strong young calves, free of disease.

**9.40.5**  To them aged Baka said: 'Divide these cattle among yourselves; for my part, leaving these beasts aside, I shall beg of the best of kings.'

**9.40.6**  Having spoken thus to all the seers, O king, that mighty and foremost of brahmins went to the dwelling of Dhṛtarāṣṭra.

**9.40.7**  Drawing near to Dhṛtarāṣṭra, lord of men, the son of Dalbha begged him for cattle; but that king answered him in anger:

**9.40.8**  'Look on these cows that have died of their own accord, O best of kings' — so the king said — 'take these beasts at once, you wretched excuse for a brahmin, if you wish.'

**9.40.9**  The seer, knower of dharma, hearing these words, fell to thinking: 'Alas, cruel indeed is the speech with which I have been addressed in the assembly!'

**9.40.10**  Having pondered a while, that foremost of the twice-born, seized by wrath, set his mind upon the destruction of King Dhṛtarāṣṭra.

**9.40.11**  That best of the twice-born, cutting the flesh from the dead cattle, then offered up into the fire the kingdom of King Dhṛtarāṣṭra.

**9.40.12**  At a secluded ford of the Sarasvatī, having kindled the sacred fire, Baka the son of Dalbha, O great king, took up a most rigorous vow; and that sage of mighty austerity offered up his kingdom with those very pieces of flesh.

**9.40.13**  When that most terrible sacrificial session had been duly begun, the kingdom of Dhṛtarāṣṭra began to waste away, O king.

**9.40.14**  Like a boundless forest hewn down by the axe, O lord, that kingdom became ruined, scattered, and bereft of sense.

**9.40.15**  Seeing his kingdom so laid waste, that lord of men grew downcast, O king, and the sovereign fell to brooding.

**9.40.16**  He strove together with his brahmins to win release from it; yet that king remained distressed, O king, and so too were those learned men.

**9.40.17**  And when he could not free his kingdom, O king, he then questioned the readers of omens there, O Janamejaya.

**9.40.18**  Then the readers of omens declared: 'The sage Baka, slighted by you in the matter of cattle, is offering up your kingdom with flesh as oblation.'

**9.40.19**  'By his oblation this great ruin has come upon your kingdom; this is the working of his austerity, by which this great calamity has fallen upon you. In a watery thicket of the Sarasvatī, O king, win his favour.'

**9.40.20**  Then the king went to the Sarasvatī and, prostrating with his head to the ground, his palms joined, said to Baka, O bull of the Bhāratas:

**9.40.21**  'I beseech your favour, O holy one; forgive my offence — wretched, greedy, and bereft of sense through my own folly that I am. You are my refuge, you are my protector; deign to show me grace.'

**9.40.22**  Seeing him lamenting thus, his mind overwhelmed with grief, the sage was moved to pity, and he set that kingdom free.

**9.40.23**  The seer became gracious toward him and, laying aside his fury, offered up another oblation for the release of that kingdom.

**9.40.24**  Having freed the kingdom and received many head of cattle, with a glad heart he went back once more to the Naimiṣa forest.

**9.40.25**  And the righteous, high-minded king Dhṛtarāṣṭra, now easy in mind, returned to his own most prosperous city.

**9.40.26**  At that ford, O great king, Bṛhaspati of noble intellect — for the undoing of the Asuras and the flourishing of the dwellers in heaven —

**9.40.27**  performed a sacrifice with offerings of flesh as well; and thereupon the Asuras began to waste away, shattered too in battle by the gods, who shone with the look of victors.

**9.40.28**  There too that man of great renown duly gave to the brahmins horses and elephants and chariots yoked with mules,

**9.40.29**  and gems of great worth, and wealth and abundant grain; then that mighty-armed one went to the ford of Yayāti, O lord of the earth.

**9.40.30**  There, O great king, at the sacrifice of Yayāti, the great-souled son of Nahuṣa, the Sarasvatī flowed forth with ghee and milk.

**9.40.31**  Having there performed sacrifice, Yayāti, tiger among men and lord of the earth, ascended on high rejoicing, and won abundant worlds.

**9.40.32**  Where, O king, for Yayāti as he sacrificed, the Sarasvatī streamed forth and granted the desires of the great-souled brahmins —

**9.40.33**  for wherever any brahmin wished for whatever he desired, there and there that best of rivers poured forth manifold flavours.

**9.40.34**  There the gods along with the Gandharvas were delighted at the splendour of the sacrifice, and the mortals too were amazed to behold that perfection of the rite.

**9.40.35**  Then he of the palm-tree banner, a great bulwark of dharma, high-souled and perfected in spirit, ever devoted to lavish giving, steadfast and self-mastered, came to the place of Vasiṣṭha's carrying-off, where the river had run with mighty and terrible force.

**9.41.1**  Janamejaya said: How came that carrying-off of Vasiṣṭha, of fearful swiftness, to be? And why did that best of rivers bear the seer away?

**9.41.2**  And with whom did he have this enmity, and what was its cause, O lord? Being asked, O most wise one, tell it; for I am not sated — let it be told.

**9.41.3**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Between the seer Viśvāmitra and Vasiṣṭha, O Bhārata, there arose a fierce and mighty enmity, O king, born of their rivalry in austerity.

**9.41.4**  The great hermitage of Vasiṣṭha stood at the ford of Sthāṇu on the eastern side, while that of the wise Viśvāmitra lay to the west.

**9.41.5**  There, O great king, Sthāṇu performed exceedingly great austerity, and there, as the wise relate, he wrought that dreadful deed.

**9.41.6**  Where the lord Sthāṇu, having sacrificed and worshipped the Sarasvatī, established that ford, which is therefore called Sthāṇutīrtha, O lord.

**9.41.7**  There, O ruler of men, all the gods consecrated Skanda, slayer of the foes of the gods, with the great office of commander of their host.

**9.41.8**  At that ford of the Sarasvatī, the great sage Viśvāmitra unsettled Vasiṣṭha by fierce austerity — hear how it was.

**9.41.9**  Day after day, O Bhārata, those two — Viśvāmitra and Vasiṣṭha, both rich in austerity — carried on their fierce contest born of penance.

**9.41.10**  There the great sage Viśvāmitra, burning all the more, seeing the splendour of Vasiṣṭha, fell into deep thought; and this was the resolve that came to that ever-righteous one, O Bhārata:

**9.41.11**  'This Sarasvatī shall swiftly bring near to me, by the force of her current, Vasiṣṭha rich in austerity, that best of those who chant the sacred words; and when that foremost of the twice-born is brought hither, I shall slay him — of this there is no doubt.'

**9.41.12**  Having so resolved, the lord Viśvāmitra, that great sage, his eyes red with anger, called to mind the best of rivers.

**9.41.13**  Summoned by the sage's thought, she fell into agitation, for the fair one knew him to be of great might and great wrath.

**9.41.14**  Then, trembling and pale, her palms joined, the Sarasvatī came and stood before that best of sages, Viśvāmitra.

**9.41.15**  Like a woman whose husband has been slain, she was sorely grieved, and she said to that best of sages: 'Tell me — what shall I do?'

**9.41.16**  The angry sage said to her: 'Bring Vasiṣṭha quickly, that I may slay him this very day.' Hearing this, the river was stricken with dread.

**9.41.17**  Then she, whose eyes were like lotus-petals, joining her palms, trembled like a well-grown creeper shaken by the wind.

**9.41.18**  Seeing her come thus, trembling, with palms joined, Viśvāmitra in anger said: 'Bring Vasiṣṭha quickly!'

**9.41.19**  Then that best of rivers, afraid, fell to thinking, O Bhārata, dreading the curse of both: 'How shall this come about?'

**9.41.20**  And going to Vasiṣṭha, she set forth this matter — all that the best of rivers had been told by the wise Viśvāmitra —

**9.41.21**  trembling again and again in fear of the curse of both, brooding upon the dread curse, sorely terrified by the seers.

**9.41.22**  Seeing her wasted and pale and filled with anxiety, the righteous Vasiṣṭha, best of men, O king, said to her:

**9.41.23**  'Save yourself, O best of rivers; swift-flowing one, carry me away — for else Viśvāmitra will curse you. Do not hesitate.'

**9.41.24**  Hearing that word of the compassionate sage, the river pondered, O Kaurava: 'What course, if taken, would be well done?'

**9.41.25**  This thought arose in her: 'Vasiṣṭha has ever shown me great kindness; surely his good must be wrought by me.'

**9.41.26**  Then, O king, beholding upon her own bank the son of Kuśika, that best of seers, as he chanted and offered oblation, the Sarasvatī reflected:

**9.41.27**  'This is the moment.' And so that best of rivers tore away her own bank with the force of her current.

**9.41.28**  By that carrying-away of the bank the son of Mitra and Varuṇa was borne off; and as he was borne along, O king, he then praised the Sarasvatī:

**9.41.29**  'From the lake of the Grandsire have you sprung forth, O Sarasvatī, and all this world is pervaded by your most excellent waters.'

**9.41.30**  'You, O goddess, moving through the sky, release your waters in the clouds; you alone are all the waters, and from you do we draw our sustenance.'

**9.41.31**  'You are nourishment, radiance, and renown, attainment, increase, and Umā; you alone are Speech, you are Svāhā; upon you this world depends, and you dwell here in all beings in your fourfold form.'

**9.41.32**  Thus praised by the great seer, O king, the Sarasvatī bore that brahmin swiftly toward the hermitage of Viśvāmitra, and again and again she presented the sage to Viśvāmitra.

**9.41.33**  Seeing him brought by the Sarasvatī, filled with rage, Viśvāmitra then sought out a weapon to make an end of Vasiṣṭha.

**9.41.34**  But seeing him enraged, the river, from fear of the slaying of a brahmin, carried Vasiṣṭha off untiringly toward the eastern quarter — thus obeying the word of both, yet outwitting the son of Gādhi.

**9.41.35**  Then, seeing Vasiṣṭha, that best of seers, borne away, the wrathful and unforgiving Viśvāmitra said:

**9.41.36**  'Since you, O best of rivers, having deceived me, have slipped away again — flow with blood, fair one, the kind that demon-chiefs delight in!'

**9.41.37**  Then the Sarasvatī, cursed by the wise Viśvāmitra, carried water mingled with blood for a whole year thereafter.

**9.41.38**  And the seers and the gods, the Gandharvas and the Apsarases, seeing the Sarasvatī in such a state, were sorely grieved.

**9.41.39**  Thus came the carrying-off of Vasiṣṭha to be famed in the world, O ruler of men; and that best of rivers came again to her own course.

**9.42.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: She, cursed by the wrathful and wise Viśvāmitra, carried blood at that bright and excellent ford.

**9.42.2**  Then, O king, the Rākṣasas came thither, O Bhārata, and there they all dwelt at ease, drinking the blood.

**9.42.3**  Sated to the full by that blood, contented and freed of fever, they danced and laughed like those who had conquered heaven.

**9.42.4**  After some time, O lord of earth, the sages rich in austerity came together on a pilgrimage to the sacred fords along the Sarasvatī.

**9.42.5**  Bathing at all those fords, those bulls among sages—learned men eager for further penance—won the highest joy; and then, O king, they proceeded onward to that very ford.

**9.42.6**  Then, O best of kings, when those greatly blessed ones reached that dreadful ford, they beheld the water of the Sarasvatī flooded with blood and being drunk by a host of rākṣasas.

**9.42.7**  Seeing those rākṣasas, O king, the sages of firm vows made the utmost effort to rescue the Sarasvatī.

**9.42.8**  Those greatly blessed ones, men of mighty vows, all gathered together, and summoning the best of rivers, they spoke these words.

**9.42.9**  "Tell us the cause, O blessed one: why has this pool of yours fallen into such a troubled state? When we have heard it, we shall set it right."

**9.42.10**  Then, trembling, she told them all that had come to pass; and beholding her in her grief, those sages rich in penance spoke.

**9.42.11**  "We have heard the cause, O sinless one, and we have heard of the curse as well; now all these sages rich in penance shall do what is needful."

**9.42.12**  Having spoken thus to the best of rivers, they said one to another, "Let us all release this Sarasvatī from the curse."

**9.42.13**  At their word alone the Sarasvatī was restored to her natural state, her waters grown clear as before; and freed, that best of rivers shone forth as she had of old.

**9.42.14**  Beholding the water of the Sarasvatī thus made pure by those sages, the rākṣasas, tormented by hunger, O king, with hands joined in reverence, spoke again and again to all those merciful sages.

**9.42.15**  "We are famished, and fallen away from the eternal law; yet this is no doing of our own desire, that we have become workers of evil."

**9.42.16**  "It is through your displeasure and through evil deeds that our number swells; for from such causes do we become brahma-rākṣasas."

**9.42.17**  "For thus it is: among vaiśyas and śūdras, and among kṣatriyas likewise, those who hate the brāhmaṇas become rākṣasas here."

**9.42.18**  "Those creatures who slight the teacher, the officiating priest, the elder, and the aged become rākṣasas here; and the breed grows too through the taint in the wombs of sinful women."

**9.42.19**  "Therefore show us your compassion here, O best of the twice-born; you have the power to deliver even all the worlds."

**9.42.20**  Hearing their plea, those sages praised that great river; and for the deliverance of those rākṣasas they spoke, their minds intent.

**9.42.21**  "Food into which an insect has fallen, or that is left as another's leavings, into which a hair has dropped, or that is shaken about, or sickened, or touched by dogs—that is the portion of the rākṣasas here."

**9.42.22**  "Therefore the wise man, knowing this, should ever shun such food; for whoever eats food of that kind eats the food of rākṣasas."

**9.42.23**  Then, having purified that ford, those sages rich in penance urged the river onward for the deliverance of the rākṣasas.

**9.42.24**  Knowing the will of the great sages, that best of rivers, O bull among men, brought forth her own stream as the Aruṇā.

**9.42.25**  Bathing in her, those rākṣasas cast off their bodies and went to heaven; for the Aruṇā, O great king, washes away the slaying of a brāhmaṇa.

**9.42.26**  Knowing this virtue of it, the king of the gods, Lord of a Hundred Sacrifices, bathed at that excellent ford and was freed, indeed, from his sin.

**9.42.27**  Janamejaya said: For what cause did the blessed Śakra incur the guilt of slaying a brāhmaṇa, and how, by bathing at this ford, did he become free of stain?

**9.42.28**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Hear this tale as it came to pass, O lord of men—how of old Vāsava broke his compact with Namuci.

**9.42.29**  Namuci, in dread of Vāsava, entered into a ray of the sun; with him Indra made friendship, and spoke this compact.

**9.42.30**  "Not with anything wet nor dry, not by night nor yet by day shall I slay you, O best of asuras; I swear it to you, my friend, in truth."

**9.42.31**  Having made this compact, the Lord conjured up a mist, and with the foam of the waters, O king, Vāsava severed his head.

**9.42.32**  That severed head of Namuci followed close behind Śakra, crying out hard upon him, "O slayer of a friend! O sinner!"

**9.42.33**  Thus driven on again and again by that head, sorely tormented, he laid the matter before the Grandsire.

**9.42.34**  The Teacher of the worlds said to him: "At the Aruṇā, O lord of gods, offer sacrifice according to rule and touch its waters; for it washes away the slaying of a brāhmaṇa."

**9.42.35**  Thus addressed, O Janamejaya, the Slayer of Bala, in a bower of the Sarasvatī, sacrificed as ordained and touched the waters of the Aruṇā.

**9.42.36**  Freed of that sin wrought by the slaying of a brāhmaṇa, the lord of the thirty gods went to heaven with gladdened heart.

**9.42.37**  And that head of Namuci too, bathing there, O Bhārata, attained the worlds that grant every wish and never wane, O best of kings.

**9.42.38**  There too the great-souled Bala touched the waters, and bestowing gifts of many kinds, that doer of noble deeds won the highest merit; then he went on to the great ford of Soma.

**9.42.39**  There, O lord of kings, Soma himself had of old performed the Rājasūya sacrifice duly; and at that foremost of rites the wise Atri, chief of brāhmaṇas, that great soul, served as the invoking priest.

**9.42.40**  At whose close there arose a tremendous battle—the gods against the dānavas, the daityas, and the rākṣasas—the fierce war called Tāraka's, wherein Skanda slew the asura named Tāraka.

**9.42.41**  There Mahāsena, slayer of the daityas, won the command of the armies of the gods; and there in person dwelt Kārttikeya—where the youth Kumāra abides forever, that lord of the Plakṣa king.

**9.43.1**  Janamejaya said: This glory of the Sarasvatī you have told, O best of the twice-born; but now, O brāhmaṇa, you ought to relate the consecration of Kumāra.

**9.43.2**  At what time and in what place, and in what manner, O best of speakers, and by whom and by what rite the blessed lord was consecrated—

**9.43.3**  and how Skanda wrought the great slaughter of the daityas—tell me all of it, for my curiosity is very great.

**9.43.4**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: This curiosity of yours is worthy of the line of Kuru; this word of yours gives me joy, O Janamejaya.

**9.43.5**  Come then, I shall tell you, as you listen, O ruler of men, the consecration of Kumāra and the glory of that great soul.

**9.43.6**  Of old, the seed of Maheśvara, spilled and fallen into the fire—that imperishable splendour the blessed All-devourer could not consume.

**9.43.7**  By it the bearer of oblations grew exceedingly bright and blazing; yet he could not hold that embryo made of splendour.

**9.43.8**  Then the lord, at the command of Brahmā, drew near to Gaṅgā and placed in her the divine embryo, splendid as the sun.

**9.43.9**  But Gaṅgā too, unable to bear the burden of that embryo, cast it off upon the lovely mountain Himavat, honoured by the immortals.

**9.43.10**  There the son of the Fire grew, filling the worlds; and then the Kṛttikās beheld that embryo in the likeness of flame.

**9.43.11**  That great soul, the son of Fire, the lord, lying in a thicket of reeds—they all approached him, each longing for a son, crying, "He is mine!"

**9.43.12**  Knowing that longing of those mothers, the blessed lord then drank with six mouths the milk that flowed from them.

**9.43.13**  Beholding that power of the child, the Kṛttikās, goddesses bearing forms divine, fell into the utmost amazement.

**9.43.14**  There, on the mountain's crest, where the blessed one was cast off by Gaṅgā, that whole peak turned to gold and shone, O best of the Kurus.

**9.43.15**  By that growing embryo the earth itself was made radiant, and thus all the mountains became mines of gold.

**9.43.16**  And Kumāra of mighty valour came to be known as Kārttikeya; first he was Gāṅgeya, the son of Gaṅgā, endowed with the great power of yoga.

**9.43.17**  That god, possessed of penance and of valour, grew exceedingly, O lord of kings, fair to look upon as the moon.

**9.43.18**  There in that golden, divine thicket of reeds, wrapped in splendour, he lay while gandharvas and sages sang his praises.

**9.43.19**  And so before him danced the daughters of the gods by the thousand—skilled in heavenly music and dance, lovely to behold, hymning his praise.

**9.43.20**  The river Gaṅgā, best of streams, waited upon the god, and the earth, taking on her fairest form, bore him up.

**9.43.21**  Bṛhaspati performed for him the birth-rite and the rest of the ceremonies; and the Veda in fourfold form attended him with hands joined in reverence.

**9.43.22**  The science of archery in its four divisions, the whole array of weapons with their lore, and Speech herself, all alone, in person stood there to serve him.

**9.43.23**  He beheld the god of mighty valour, the god of gods, the lord of Umā, seated together with the Daughter of the Mountain and surrounded by hundreds of throngs of spirits.

**9.43.24**  The hosts of those spirit-throngs were most wondrous to look upon—misshapen, of distorted form, with distorted ornaments and banners.

**9.43.25**  Some had the faces of tigers, lions, and bears; some the visages of cats and crocodiles; others bore the muzzles of bulls and rats, and yet others the faces of elephants and camels.

**9.43.26**  Some had the faces of owls, some the look of vultures and jackals, and some with faces like the krauñca and the dove, or like the antelope.

**9.43.27**  Others, here and there, bore bodies resembling those of porcupines, hedgehogs, and monitor lizards, of asses, rams, and cattle.

**9.43.28**  Some were like mountains or rain-clouds, armed with discus, firebrand, and mace; some shone like heaps of collyrium, and some gleamed like a white mountain.

**9.43.29**  The bands of the Seven Mothers too came together, O lord of peoples, and the Sādhyas, the Viśvedevas, the Maruts, the Vasus, and the fathers as well.

**9.43.30**  The Rudras and Ādityas, the siddhas, serpents, dānavas, and birds; and the blessed self-born Brahmā with his sons, together with Viṣṇu.

**9.43.31**  Śakra too came to look upon that noblest and imperishable Kumāra, as did the best of the divine gandharvas, led by Nārada.

**9.43.32**  The divine sages and the siddhas, led by Bṛhaspati, and the boon-giving ones called Ṛbhus, gods even of the gods—they too gathered there, the Yāmas and the Dhāmas, all of them.

**9.43.33**  And the blessed one, though but a child, endowed with the great power of yoga, drew near to the lord of gods, the trident-bearer, the wielder of the Pināka.

**9.43.34**  As they marked him drawing near, a thought arose at one and the same moment in the minds of Śiva, of the Daughter of the Mountain, of Gaṅgā, and of the Fire.

**9.43.35**  "To which of us first will this child come out of reverence—will it be to me?"—such was the thought in the mind of each of them.

**9.43.36**  Perceiving this intent of those four, he at once resorted to yoga and put forth bodies of various kinds.

**9.43.37**  Then in an instant the blessed lord became fourfold in form: Skanda, Śākha, and Viśākha, and Naigameṣa behind them.

**9.43.38**  Thus having made himself fourfold, the blessed lord—as Skanda, wondrous to behold—went to where Rudra was.

**9.43.39**  Viśākha went to where the goddess, daughter of the best of mountains, was; and the blessed Śākha, in the form of the Wind, went to the Fire; while Naigameṣa, the youth bright as flame, went to Gaṅgā.

**9.43.40**  All four of them, with shining bodies and alike in form, drew near them unhurried; and this was as a wonder to behold.

**9.43.41**  A great cry of wonder arose from the gods, dānavas, and rākṣasas as they beheld that mighty marvel, wondrous and hair-raising.

**9.43.42**  Then Rudra, and the Goddess, and Pāvaka, together with Gaṅgā—all of them bowed down before the Grandsire, the Lord of the world.

**9.43.43**  Having made their obeisance in due form, O bull among kings, they then spoke these words, O king, in their wish to secure a boon for Kārttikeya:

**9.43.44**  "For this boy, O Lord, for our gratification, O Lord of the gods, it is fitting that you grant a sovereignty befitting him, such as he may desire."

**9.43.45**  Then that wise and blessed Grandsire of all the worlds pondered in his mind: "What should be bestowed upon him?"

**9.43.46**  For all the lordships of the gods, the gandharvas and the rākṣasas, of the spirits, the yakṣas, and the birds, and of the serpents in their entirety,

**9.43.47**  he had already ordained among the hosts of the great-souled ones; yet that one of vast wisdom deemed the boy fit for sovereignty.

**9.43.48**  Then, having reflected for a moment, intent upon the welfare of the gods, he bestowed upon him the office of commander over all beings, O Bhārata.

**9.43.49**  All those who were renowned as kings among the several hosts of the gods—the Grandsire of all beings appointed them, every one, to his command.

**9.43.50**  Then the gods, led by Brahmā, taking the prince with them, came together to the king of mountains for the consecration.

**9.43.51**  They came to the holy goddess sprung from Himavat, Sarasvatī, the best of rivers, who at Samantapañcaka is renowned throughout the three worlds.

**9.43.52**  There upon the holy bank of the Sarasvatī, endowed with every excellence, the gods and gandharvas all took their seats, their hearts filled with contentment.

**9.44.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Then, having gathered together all the materials of the consecration according to scripture, Bṛhaspati poured clarified butter into the kindled fire as the rite ordained.

**9.44.2**  Then, seated upon a divine throne supreme, given by Himavat, gleaming with the choicest gems and studded with celestial jewels,

**9.44.3**  the hosts of the gods, taking up the materials of the consecration with all auspicious appurtenances, attended by the proper rites and sacred formulas,

**9.44.4**  Indra and Viṣṇu of mighty valour, the Sun and the Moon, Dhātṛ and Vidhātṛ likewise, and Anila and Anala,

**9.44.5**  with Pūṣan, Bhaga, and Aryaman, with Aṃśa and Vivasvat, and the wise Rudra together with Mitra and Varuṇa—

**9.44.6**  the Lord surrounded by the Rudras, the Vasus, the Ādityas, and the two Aśvins, by the Viśvedevas and the Maruts, and by the Sādhyas together with the Fathers,

**9.44.7**  by gandharvas and apsarases, by yakṣas, rākṣasas, and serpents, by countless divine sages and by the foremost brahmin sages,

**9.44.8**  by the Vaikhānasas and the Vālakhilyas, by those who feed on air and those who drink the rays of the sun, by the Bhṛgus and the Aṅgirases and the great-souled ascetics, and surrounded by all the holy vidyādharas perfected in yoga,

**9.44.9**  the Grandsire, and Pulastya, and Pulaha of great austerity, Aṅgiras, Kaśyapa, and Atri, Marīci and Bhṛgu,

**9.44.10**  Kratu, Hara, and Pracetas, Manu and Dakṣa likewise, and the seasons, the planets, and the heavenly luminaries, O lord of peoples,

**9.44.11**  the rivers in embodied form, and the eternal Vedas, the seas and the lakes and the various sacred fords, the Earth, the Sky, and the quarters, and the trees, O ruler of men,

**9.44.12**  Aditi the mother of the gods, and Hrī, Śrī, Svāhā, and Sarasvatī, Umā, Śacī, Sinīvālī, and Anumati, Kuhū, and Rākā, and Dhiṣaṇā, and the other wives of the dwellers of heaven,

**9.44.13**  Himavat, and Vindhya, and Meru of many peaks, Airāvata with his followers, the moments and the divisions of time, the months and half-months, the seasons, and Night and Day, O king,

**9.44.14**  Uccaiḥśravas, the best of horses, and Vāmana, the king of elephants, Aruṇa and Garuḍa, and the trees together with the healing herbs,

**9.44.15**  and the divine Lord Dharma—all these assembled and came together, and Time, and Yama, and Death, and those who are Yama's followers.

**9.44.16**  And the manifold hosts of deities not named here on account of their multitude came together from this place and that for the prince's consecration.

**9.44.17**  Then, O king, all those dwellers of heaven took up the vessels of consecration and every auspicious object.

**9.44.18**  With golden jars filled with divine appurtenances, O king, and with the holy waters of the Sarasvatī, waters indeed celestial,

**9.44.19**  the dwellers of heaven, greatly rejoicing, consecrated the prince, the great-souled commander, the terror of the asuras.

**9.44.20**  As of old, O great king, Varuṇa the lord of waters was consecrated, even so did the blessed Brahmā, the Grandsire of the world, consecrate him, and Kaśyapa of great splendour, and the others not here recounted.

**9.44.21**  To him Brahmā, well pleased, the Lord gave mighty attendants swift as the wind, perfected beings who bore valour at will, great companions of his retinue:

**9.44.22**  Nandiṣeṇa, Lohitākṣa, and the esteemed Ghaṇṭākarṇa, and as his fourth follower, the renowned Kumudamālin.

**9.44.23**  Then, O lord of kings, Sthāṇu gave to Skanda the great companion Kratu of mighty force, and Kāma, wielder of a hundred illusions, endowed with strength and valour at will, the destroyer of the foes of the gods.

**9.44.24**  For he, in the war of the gods and the asuras, in his wrath slew with his two arms fourteen million of the daityas of dreadful deeds.

**9.44.25**  Likewise the gods gave him an army teeming with rākṣasas, that wrought the destruction of the gods' enemies, unconquerable and of every shape.

**9.44.26**  Then all the gods together with Vāsava raised the cry of victory, and the gandharvas, yakṣas, and rākṣasas, the sages, and the Fathers as well.

**9.44.27**  Yama gave two followers, both like Yama and Time themselves, Unmātha and Pramātha, of mighty valour and great splendour.

**9.44.28**  Subhrāja and Bhāskara, the two who attend upon the Sun—those the glorious Sūrya, well pleased, gave to Kārttikeya.

**9.44.29**  Resembling the peak of Kailāsa, garlanded and anointed with white, Soma too gave two followers, Maṇi and Sumaṇi.

**9.44.30**  Jvālājihva and Jyoti—these the Fire-god gave to his own son as followers, two heroes who crushed the armies of the foe.

**9.44.31**  Parigha and Vaṭa, and the mighty Bhīma, and Dahati and Dahana, fierce and esteemed for valour—these five followers Aṃśa too gave to the wise Skanda.

**9.44.32**  Utkrośa and Paṅkaja, both bearing the thunderbolt and the rod, Vāsava the slayer of enemy heroes gave to the son of Agni; for these two had slain in battle many of great Indra's foes.

**9.44.33**  Cakra and Vikramaka, and the mighty Saṃkrama—these three followers Viṣṇu of great renown gave to Skanda.

**9.44.34**  Vardhana and Nandana, both skilled in all the sciences, the two Aśvins, well pleased, gave to Skanda, O bull of the Bhāratas.

**9.44.35**  Kundana and Kusuma and Kumuda, and Ḍambara and Āḍambara—these the illustrious Dhātṛ gave to the great-souled one.

**9.44.36**  Vakra and Anuvakra, two mighty ones with ram-faces, swollen with strength and full of great illusion—Tvaṣṭṛ gave these excellent followers to Skanda.

**9.44.37**  Suvrata and Satyasaṃdha, two great-souled bearers of austerity and sacred knowledge—the Lord Mitra gave to the great-souled prince.

**9.44.38**  Fair to look upon, granters of boons, renowned throughout the three worlds—the great-souled Suprabhā and Śubhakarman, famed in the worlds, Vidhātṛ bestowed upon Kārttikeya.

**9.44.39**  Pālitaka and Kālika, both masters of great illusion—these two attendants Pūṣan gave to Kārttikeya, O Bhārata.

**9.44.40**  Bala and Atibala, both great of mouth and great of strength, Vāyu gave to Kārttikeya, O best of the Bhāratas.

**9.44.41**  Ghasa and Atighasa, two of mighty strength with the faces of sea-monsters, Varuṇa, true to his pledge, gave to Kārttikeya.

**9.44.42**  The great-souled Suvarcas, and likewise Ativarcas, Himavat gave, O king, to the son of the Fire-god.

**9.44.43**  The great-souled Kāñcana and Meghamālin—these two followers Meru gave to the son of Agni, O Bhārata.

**9.44.44**  Sthira and Atisthira—two more Meru gave likewise to the great-souled son of Agni, both of great strength and prowess.

**9.44.45**  Ucchrita and Atiśṛṅga, two warriors who fought with great boulders, Vindhya gave to the son of Agni as his attendants.

**9.44.46**  Saṃgraha and Vigraha, both bearers of the mace, the Ocean too gave to the son of Agni, two great companions of his retinue.

**9.44.47**  Unmāda, Puṣpadanta, and Śaṅkukarṇa likewise—these Pārvatī, fair of aspect, gave to the son of Agni.

**9.44.48**  Jaya and Mahājaya, two serpents, O tiger among men, Vāsuki the lord of serpents gave to the son of Fire.

**9.44.49**  Thus the Sādhyas and the Rudras, the Vasus and the Fathers, the seas and the rivers, and the mighty mountains,

**9.44.50**  gave him captains over the hosts of his army, bearers of the lance and the spear, furnished with divine weapons and adorned in manifold guise.

**9.44.51**  Hear now the names of the others, those who were Skanda's soldiers, furnished with manifold weapons and bright ornaments and armour:

**9.44.52**  Śaṅkukarṇa and Nikumbha, Padma and Kumuda, Ananta of twelve arms, and Kṛṣṇa and Upakṛṣṇaka,

**9.44.53**  Droṇaśravas, Kapiskandha, Kāñcanākṣa, Jalaṃdhama, Akṣasaṃtarjana, O king, Kunadīka, and Tamobhrakṛt,

**9.44.54**  Ekākṣa and Dvādaśākṣa, and Ekajaṭa the lordly, Sahasrabāhu, Vikaṭa, Vyāghrākṣa, and Kṣitikampana,

**9.44.55**  Puṇyanāman and Sunāman, Suvaktra and Priyadarśana, Pariśruta, Kokanada, and Priyamālyānulepana,

**9.44.56**  Ajodara, Gajaśiras, Skandhākṣa, Śatalocana, Jvālājihva, Karāla, Sitakeśa, Jaṭin, and Hari,

**9.44.57**  Caturdaṃṣṭra and Aṣṭajihva, Meghanāda, Pṛthuśravas, Vidyudakṣa, Dhanurvaktra, Jaṭhara, and Mārutāśana,

**9.44.58**  Udarākṣa and Jhaṣākṣa, Vajranābha, Vasuprabha, Samudravega, O lord of kings, and Śailakampin likewise,

**9.44.59**  Putrameṣa and Pravāha, and Nanda and Upanandaka, Dhūmra, Śveta, and Kaliṅga, Siddhārtha, and Varada,

**9.44.60**  Priyaka and Nanda, and the valorous Gonanda, Ānanda and Pramoda, Svastika, and Dhruvaka likewise,

**9.44.61**  Kṣemavāpa and Sujāta, and Siddhayātra, O Bhārata, Govraja, Kanakāpīḍa, the great lord of the retinue,

**9.44.62**  Gāyana and Hasana, Bāṇa and the valiant Khaḍga, Vaitālin and Atitālin, and Katika and Vātika,

**9.44.63**  Haṃsaja, Paṅkadigdhāṅga, and Samudronmādana, Raṇotkaṭa and Prahāsa, Śvetaśīrṣa, and Nandaka,

**9.44.64**  Kālakaṇṭha and Prabhāsa, and the other Kumbhāṇḍaka, Kālakākṣa and Sītā, and Bhūtalonmathana likewise,

**9.44.65**  Yajñavāha and Pravāha, Devayājin and Somapa, the mighty Sajāla, and Krātha and Krātha, O Bhārata,

**9.44.66**  Tuhana and Tuhāna, and the valiant Citradeva, Madhura and Suprasāda, and the mighty Kirīṭin,

**9.44.67**  Vasana and Madhuvarṇa, and Kalaśodara, Dhamanta, Manmathakara, and the valiant Sūcīvaktra,

**9.44.68**  Śvetavaktra and Suvaktra, and Cāruvaktra, Pāṇḍura, Daṇḍabāhu and Subāhu, Rajas, and Kokilaka likewise,

**9.44.69**  Acala and Kanakākṣa, and the lordly protector of children, Saṃcāraka, Kokanada, Gṛdhravaktra, and Jambuka.

**9.44.70**  Iron-horse-faced Jaṭhara, and Kuṇḍaka with a face like a pot, and Madgugrīva the diver-necked, and Kṛṣṇaujas of dark might, and goose-faced Candrabhās of moonlike sheen.

**9.44.71**  And Śambūka with hands like a tortoise, and Śikṣaka the five-faced, and Jambūka with the face of a blue jay, and Kuṇḍaka of the herb-leaf countenance.

**9.44.72**  Yoked in yoga, great of soul, ever dear to the brāhmaṇas, descended from the Grandsire, lofty-spirited, mighty attendants — youths in their prime, and children, and the aged too, O Janamejaya.

**9.44.73**  By the thousand the attendants drew near to wait upon the Prince; and those who bore faces of every kind — hear of them now, O Janamejaya.

**9.44.74**  With faces of tortoise and cock, and others with the muzzles of hare and owl, and some with the faces of ass and camel, and others with the snouts of boars.

**9.44.75**  With the faces of men and rams, and others with the muzzles of jackals, and terrible ones with the maws of sea-monsters, and faces like the river-dolphin's.

**9.44.76**  With the faces of cat and hare, and long-faced ones, O Bhārata, with the faces of mongoose and owl, and yet others dog-faced.

**9.44.77**  With the faces of mouse and ichneumon, and the countenances of peacocks, and others fish-faced and ram-faced, and some bearing the faces of goat, sheep, and buffalo.

**9.44.78**  With the faces of bear and tiger, and the countenances of leopard and lion, and terrible ones with the faces of elephants, and others with the maws of crocodiles.

**9.44.79**  With the faces of the eagle and the rhinoceros, and the faces of wolf and crow, and others with the faces of ox, ass, and camel, and some with the muzzles of the wildcat.

**9.44.80**  With huge bellies, feet, and limbs, and eyes like stars, O Bhārata, and others with the faces of doves, and yet others with the faces of bulls.

**9.44.81**  And others with the faces of cuckoos, and countenances of hawk and partridge, and some with the faces of chameleons, clad in spotless garments.

**9.44.82**  Serpent-faced, with faces like spear-points, fierce-faced, hundred-faced, venomous as fanged snakes, clad in bark, and shrouded like the bull-nosed cobra.

**9.44.83**  With swollen bellies yet slender limbs, and bulky limbs yet lean bellies, short-necked, large-eared, adorned with serpents of every kind.

**9.44.84**  Robed in the hides of lordly elephants, and clad in black antelope-skins, some with faces set in their shoulders, O great king, and some with faces set in their bellies.

**9.44.85**  Some with faces in their backs, some with faces upon their jaws, and some with faces upon their shanks, and many with faces in their flanks, with faces set in every region of the body.

**9.44.86**  And there were troop-lords with mouths like those of worms and winged insects, and others with the faces of sundry beasts of prey, bearing many arms and heads.

**9.44.87**  Some with arms like the branches of various trees, others with heads set at their hips, with faces like the coils of serpents, dwelling among the many thickets.

**9.44.88**  With bodies wrapped in bark, and clothed in plates of wood, wearing garb of every fashion, and some clad only in hides.

**9.44.89**  Wearing turbans and diadems, with necks ringed like the conch, of radiant splendor, crowned, some with five locks of hair, and others with stiff, bristling hair.

**9.44.90**  With three locks, with two locks, and others with seven locks, some with crests, some crowned, some shaven bald, and others with matted hair.

**9.44.91**  Some bearing wreaths of many hues, and some with hairy faces, wearing divine garlands and raiment, ever pleasing in their forms.

**9.44.92**  Dark of hue, with fleshless faces, long-backed and belly-less, some thick-backed, some short-backed, with dangling bellies and pendulous parts.

**9.44.93**  Great-armed and short-armed, with stunted bodies, dwarfish, hunchbacked, with long shanks, and bearing the ears and heads of elephants.

**9.44.94**  With the noses of elephants, of tortoises, and others with the noses of wolves, with long lips and long tongues, ghastly, and downward-faced.

**9.44.95**  With great tusks and small tusks, and others with four tusks, some like lordly elephants, terrible, O king, by the thousand.

**9.44.96**  With shapely, well-proportioned bodies, blazing, finely adorned, tawny-eyed, with pointed ears and crooked noses, O Bhārata.

**9.44.97**  With broad fangs and great tusks, thick-lipped, with tawny lion-hair, having feet, lips, and tusks of every kind, and hands and heads of every kind, sheathed in armor of every sort, and speaking tongues of every kind, O Bhārata.

**9.44.98**  Skilled in the speech of every land, these lordly ones chattered to one another; and so the mighty attendants leapt about in their delight.

**9.44.99**  Long-necked, long-nailed, with long feet, heads, and arms, tawny-eyed and blue-throated, and with drooping ears, O Bhārata.

**9.44.100**  Some with bellies like wolves', some dark as collyrium, with white bodies and crimson necks, others tawny-eyed, and many of mottled and motley hue, O king, of dappled colors, O Bhārata.

**9.44.101**  Resembling clusters of yak-tail plumes, streaked with white and crimson, of varied hues and of single hue, with a luster like the peacock's.

**9.44.102**  Hear now once more as I tell of their weapons; for the remaining attendants had taken up arms as their own.

**9.44.103**  Some with hands raised aloft bearing nooses, with gaping maws and ass-faces, broad-eyed and blue-throated, and with arms like iron clubs.

**9.44.104**  Bearing in their hands the śataghnī and the discus, and grasping pestles, holding spears and swords, of huge frame and mighty strength.

**9.44.105**  Bearing maces and the bhuśuṇḍi in their hands, and grasping lances, holding swords and mallets, and wielding staves, O Bhārata.

**9.44.106**  With dread weapons of every kind, lofty of spirit, swift of pace, mighty in strength and impetus — such were these great attendants.

**9.44.107**  Beholding the consecration of the Prince, those lovers of battle rejoiced, and with their limbs bound about with nets of bells, those puissant ones fell to dancing.

**9.44.108**  These and many other mighty attendants, O king, drew near to wait upon the great-souled and glorious Kārttikeya.

**9.44.109**  Heavenly and aerial and earthly, swift as the wind, those heroes, appointed by the gods, became the followers of Skanda.

**9.44.110**  Of such as these, by the thousands, the millions, and the hundred-millions, they surrounded the consecrated great-souled one and stood in attendance upon him.

**9.45.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Hear now, O king, of these troops of mothers, the followers of the Prince, whom I shall celebrate, O hero — destroyers of the hosts of his foes.

**9.45.2**  Hear the names of the glorious mothers, O Bhārata, by whom — auspicious ones — the three worlds, with all that moves and is unmoving, are pervaded.

**9.45.3**  Prabhāvatī, Viśālākṣī, Palitā, and Gonasī, Śrīmatī, and Bahulā, and likewise Bahuputrikā.

**9.45.4**  And Apsujātā and Gopālī, and Bṛhadambālikā, Jayāvatī, Mālatikā, Dhruvaratnā, Bhayaṃkarī.

**9.45.5**  Vasudāmā and Sudāmā, Viśokā, and Nandinī, Ekacūḍā, Mahācūḍā, and Cakranemi, O Bhārata.

**9.45.6**  Uttejanī, Jayatsenā, then Kamalākṣī and Śobhanā, and Śatruṃjaya likewise, Krodhanā, Śalabhī, Kharī.

**9.45.7**  Mādhavī and Śubhavaktrā, and Tīrthanemi, O Bhārata, Gītapriyā and Kalyāṇī, Kadrulā, and Amitāśanā.

**9.45.8**  Meghasvanā, Bhogavatī, and Subhrū, and Kanakāvatī, Alātākṣī, Vīryavatī, and Vidyujjihvā, O Bhārata.

**9.45.9**  Padmāvatī, Sunakṣatrā, Kaṇḍarā, Bahuyojanā, and Saṃtānikā, O Kaurava, and Kamalā the mighty.

**9.45.10**  Sudāmā and Bahudāmā, and Suprabhā the glorious, and Nṛtyapriyā, O lord of kings, and Śatolūkhalamekhalā, girt with a hundred mortars.

**9.45.11**  Śataghaṇṭā, Śatānandā, and Bhaganandā the comely, Vapuṣmatī, Candraśītā, and Bhadrakālī, O Bhārata.

**9.45.12**  Saṃkārikā, Niṣkuṭikā, Brahmā, Catvaravāsinī, Sumaṅgalā, Svastimatī, Vṛddhikāmā, Jayapriyā.

**9.45.13**  Dhanadā and Suprasādā, and Bhavadā, and Jaleśvarī, Eḍī, Bheḍī, and Sameḍī, and Vetālajananī, mother of the ghoul, and Kaṇḍūti, and Kālikā, and Devamitrā, O Bhārata.

**9.45.14**  Lambasī and Ketakī, and Citrasenā, and Balā, Kukkuṭikā, Śaṅkhanikā, and Jarjarikā, O king.

**9.45.15**  Kuṇḍārikā, Kokalikā, and Kaṇḍarā, and Śatodarī, Utkrāthinī, and Jareṇā, and Mahāvegā, and Kaṅkaṇā.

**9.45.16**  Manojavā, Kaṇṭakinī, Praghasā, and Pūtanā, Khaśayā, Curvyuṭi, Vāmā, Krośanā, and Taḍitprabhā, bright as the lightning.

**9.45.17**  Mandodarī and Tuṇḍā, and Koṭarā, Meghavāsinī, Subhagā, Lambinī, Lambā, Vasucūḍā, Vikatthanī.

**9.45.18**  And Ūrdhvaveṇīdharā, who wears her braid upturned, tawny-eyed Lohamekhalā girt with iron, broad-faced Pṛthuvaktrā, Madhurikā, and Madhukumbhā likewise.

**9.45.19**  Pakṣālikā, Manthanikā, Jarāyu, Jarjarānanā, and she renowned as Dahadahā, and likewise Dhamadhamā, O king.

**9.45.20**  And Khaṇḍakhaṇḍā, O lord of kings, Pūṣaṇā, Maṇikuṇḍalā, and Amocā, O Kaurava, and Lambapayodharā with pendulous breasts.

**9.45.21**  And Veṇuvīṇādharā, bearing flute and lute, tawny-eyed Lohamekhalā girt with iron, hare-and-owl-faced, dark of hue, with ass's shanks, and very swift.

**9.45.22**  Śiśumāramukhī with the face of a river-dolphin, Śvetā the white, Lohitākṣī the red-eyed, Vibhīṣaṇā the terrible, Jaṭālikā, Kāmacarī who roves at will, Dīrghajihvā the long-tongued, and Balotkaṭā of surpassing strength.

**9.45.23**  Kāleḍikā, Vāmanikā, and Mukuṭā, O Bhārata, Lohitākṣī the red-eyed, Mahākāyā of huge frame, and Haripiṇḍī, O lord of the earth.

**9.45.24**  Ekākṣarā, Sukusumā, and Kṛṣṇakarṇī the dark-eared, O Bhārata, Kṣurakarṇī the razor-eared, Catuṣkarṇī the four-eared, and Karṇaprāvaraṇā who is mantled in her ears.

**9.45.25**  Catuṣpathaniketā who dwells at the crossroads, Gokarṇī the cow-eared, Mahiṣānanā the buffalo-faced, Kharakarṇī the ass-eared, Mahākarṇī the great-eared, and she whose voice resounds like the kettledrum.

**9.45.26**  And she whose voice is like conch and pot, Bhaṅgadā the mighty, Gaṇā and Sugaṇā, and Abhīti the fearless, and Kāmadā the granter of desires.

**9.45.27**  And Catuṣpatharatā who delights in the crossways, Bhūtitīrthā, Anyagocarā, Paśudā giver of cattle, and Vittadā giver of wealth, and Sukhadā of great fame, giver of joy, Payodā giver of milk, Gomahiṣadā giver of cow and buffalo, and Suviṣāṇā the fair-horned, O Bhārata.

**9.45.28**  Pratiṣṭhā and Supratiṣṭhā, Rocamāna, Surocanā, Gokarṇī and Sukarṇī, Sasirā, and Stherikā likewise, Ekacakrā, Megharavā whose roar is the thundercloud, Meghamālā garlanded with clouds, and Virocanā.

**9.45.29**  These and many other mothers, O bull of the Bhāratas, followers of Kārttikeya, of every form, by the thousand.

**9.45.30**  Long-nailed, long-toothed, and long-snouted, O Bhārata, yet upright and sweet, in the bloom of youth and finely adorned.

**9.45.31**  Endowed with grandeur of soul, able to take any shape at will, some with fleshless bodies and white of hue, and others gleaming like gold.

**9.45.32**  Others like the black thundercloud, and some smoke-gray, O bull of the Bhāratas, some glowing like the dawn, the highly blessed, long-haired and white-robed.

**9.45.33**  And some wearing their braids upturned, tawny-eyed, with pendant girdles, with hanging bellies, with drooping ears, and likewise with pendulous breasts.

**9.45.34**  With copper-red eyes and copper hue, and others tawny-eyed, granters of boons, roving at will, and ever joyous.

**9.45.35**  Some belonging to Yama, some to Rudra, and some to Soma, those of Kubera, and the mighty, those of Varuṇa, and those of great Indra, and those of Agni, O scorcher of foes.

**9.45.36**  And those of Vāyu, and those of Kumāra, and those of Brahmā, O bull of the Bhāratas, equal to the apsarases in beauty, and like the wind in swiftness.

**9.45.37**  In speech like the cuckoo, and in abundance like Kubera the lord of wealth, in valor the equals of Śakra, and in radiance like the fire.

**9.45.38**  Dwelling among trees and at the crossroads, making their abodes at the meeting of four ways, dwelling in caverns and burning-grounds, and lodging by mountains and waterfalls.

**9.45.39**  Wearing ornaments of every kind, garlanded and robed in every fashion, in garb of manifold and wondrous sorts, and speaking tongues of every kind as well.

**9.45.40**  These and many other hosts, terror to their foes, followed the great-souled one, with the consent of Indra, lord of the thirty gods.

**9.45.41**  Then the blessed Pākaśāsana bestowed the spear-weapon upon Guha, O tiger among kings, for the destruction of the enemies of the gods.

**9.45.42**  And a banner, loud-resounding, hung with a great bell, blazing, of white radiance and the hue of the young sun, O bull of the Bhāratas—

**9.45.43**  Paśupati gave him that, and a mighty army of all the spirits, fierce, bearing weapons of every kind, endowed with the power of austerity, valor, and strength.

**9.45.44**  Viṣṇu gave the Vaijayantī garland that swells one's strength; Umā gave two spotless garments, gleaming bright as the sun.

**9.45.45**  Gaṅgā with love gave the prince a divine, supreme water-pot born of the nectar, and Bṛhaspati gave a staff.

**9.45.46**  Garuḍa gave his beloved son the peacock with its dappled plumage, and Aruṇa gave the red-crested cock, that creature whose weapons are its feet.

**9.45.47**  King Varuṇa gave a noose endowed with strength and valor; and Brahmā the Lord, maker of the worlds, gave a black antelope skin to the devout one, and bestowed victory in battles as well.

**9.45.48**  Having attained command over the host of the gods, Skanda shone forth, blazing and flaming like a second fire, surrounded then by his attendants and by the Mothers.

**9.45.49**  That dreadful army of spirits, with bells and uplifted banners, with kettledrums, conches, and tabors, with weapons and standards, shone like the autumn sky adorned with its stars.

**9.45.50**  Then those companies of the gods and the throngs of the spirit-army, unhurried, sounded their drums and abundant conches,

**9.45.51**  war-drums and rattles, saws and ox-horns, kettledrums and cow-faced trumpets, and tabors of mighty sound.

**9.45.52**  All the gods with Vāsava praised the prince, the celestial gandharvas sang, and the troops of apsarases danced.

**9.45.53**  Then the great-armied one, well pleased, granted a boon to the thirty gods: "I shall slay in battle those foes who seek your destruction."

**9.45.54**  Receiving the boon from that best of the wise, the great-souled gods, glad at heart, counted their foes as already slain.

**9.45.55**  When the boon was granted by the great-souled one, a roar of joy arose from all the assembled spirits and filled the three worlds.

**9.45.56**  Then the great-armied one set forth, encircled by his vast army, to slay the daityas in battle and to protect the dwellers of heaven.

**9.45.57**  Resolve, Victory, Righteousness, Success, Fortune, Constancy, and Memory marched before the armies of the great-armied one, O lord of men.

**9.45.58**  With that terrible host—weapons of pike and mace, of club and pestle, of iron shaft, spear, and lance in its hands—roaring like a proud lion, the god Guha bellowed and went forth.

**9.45.59**  Seeing him, all the daiteyas, rākṣasas, and dānavas fled in every direction, agitated with fear on every side; and the gods, weapons of every kind in hand, rushed upon them.

**9.45.60**  And the blessed Skanda, beholding this, was enraged; endowed with splendor and strength, he hurled the dreadful spear-weapon again and again, kindling his own brilliance like a fire fed with oblation.

**9.45.61**  As the spear-weapon was being hurled by Skanda of measureless brilliance, blazing meteors fell, O great king, upon the surface of the earth.

**9.45.62**  And crashing thunderbolts fell upon the ground, as fearsome as those at the hour of the world's end, O king.

**9.45.63**  A single spear was hurled by the dread son of fire, and from it sprang forth tens of millions of spears, O bull of the Bhāratas.

**9.45.64**  With that spear-weapon the blessed Lord slew in battle the daitya-king named Tāraka, of mighty strength and prowess, surrounded by a hundred thousand valiant daityas and by ten mighty warriors, O king.

**9.45.65**  He smote down Mahiṣa, encircled in battle by eight hundred billion, and slew Tripāda, surrounded by ten times a million times a hundred thousand.

**9.45.66**  And the Lord slew Hradodara, encircled by ten hundred billion, together with his followers bearing weapons of every kind.

**9.45.67**  There, as the foes were being slain, the prince's followers raised a mighty roar, O king, filling the ten directions.

**9.45.68**  By the flames of the spear-weapon on every side, O lord of kings, the daityas were burned by the thousand, and others by the war-cries of Skanda.

**9.45.69**  Some enemies of the gods, struck down by the lashing of the banner, were slain; some, terrified by the clang of the bell, fell upon the earth; and some, cleft by weapons, dropped down lifeless.

**9.45.70**  Thus the heroic Kārttikeya, mighty and strong, slew in battle many a powerful and aggressive foe of the gods.

**9.45.71**  Now a daiteya named Bāṇa, the son of Bali, of mighty strength, took to Mount Krauñca and harassed the hosts of the gods.

**9.45.72**  The great-armied one, lofty of mind, advanced upon that enemy of the gods; and out of fear of Kārttikeya he took refuge in Krauñca.

**9.45.73**  Then the blessed Kārttikeya, full of great wrath, clove Krauñca—resounding with the cry of the krauñca-bird—with the spear given by Agni.

**9.45.74**  With its sāl trunks and pines, with elephants of the forest in panic, with birds startled from its riverbanks and serpents tumbling forth,

**9.45.75**  echoing with troops of fleeing langurs and bears, loud with the rush of running deer, thronged with bewildered wild oxen,

**9.45.76**  with śarabhas and lions suddenly bursting forth and fleeing—though fallen into a pitiable state, that mountain still shone in splendor.

**9.45.77**  The vidyādharas who dwelt upon its peaks flew up, and the kiṃnaras too, terrified and startled by the roar of the falling spear.

**9.45.78**  Then the daityas, in their hundreds and thousands, decked in gleaming ornaments and garlands, rushed forth from that blazing best of mountains.

**9.45.79**  The prince's followers overtook and slew them in the fray, and the son of fire, slayer of hostile heroes, clove Krauñca with his spear.

**9.45.80**  Making itself now manifold, now single, the spear hurled in battle by the great-souled one returned to his hand again and again.

**9.45.81**  Of such power was the blessed son of fire, and greater still: by him Krauñca was riven asunder and the daityas were slain by the hundred.

**9.45.82**  Then the blessed god, having slain the enemies of the gods, honored by the gods, attained the highest joy.

**9.45.83**  Then the kettledrums sounded, O king, and the conches, O Bhārata, and the women of the gods let fall an incomparable rain of flowers.

**9.45.84**  A holy breeze blew, bearing a celestial fragrance, and the gandharvas praised him, as did the sacrificing great seers.

**9.45.85**  Some declare him to be the Lord, the son of the Grandsire, Sanatkumāra, the first-born of all, sprung from Brahman;

**9.45.86**  some the son of Maheśvara, some the son of Vibhāvasu; and they speak of him also as the son of Umā, of the Kṛttikās, and of Gaṅgā.

**9.45.87**  The mighty one, lord of yogins, that god—one and twofold and fourfold, and in his hundreds and thousands of forms.

**9.45.88**  Thus has the consecration of Kārttikeya been told to you, O king; now hear the holiness of the lineage of fords of the Sarasvatī.

**9.45.89**  When the enemies of the gods had been slain by the prince, there arose a most excellent ford, O great king, like a second heaven.

**9.45.90**  And dwelling there, the Lord, the son of fire, then bestowed dominions, each in turn, upon the chief spirits throughout the three worlds.

**9.45.91**  Thus was the blessed one, the ender of the daitya race, consecrated at that ford by the gods as commander of the host of heaven, O great king.

**9.45.92**  That ford is named Aujasa, where of old the lord of waters, Varuṇa, was consecrated by the hosts of the gods, O bull of the Bhāratas.

**9.45.93**  Having bathed at that most excellent ford and worshipped Skanda, the Plough-bearer gave gold, garments, and ornaments to the brāhmaṇas.

**9.45.94**  Having passed the night there, Mādhava, slayer of hostile heroes, the Plough-bearer, worshipped that excellent ford and touched its waters; and that best of the Mādhavas became glad and joyful at heart.

**9.45.95**  All this has been told to you, that which you asked of me—how the blessed Skanda was consecrated by the assembled gods.

**9.46.1**  Janamejaya said: Most wondrous is this, O brāhmaṇa; I have heard truly and in full detail the consecration of the prince, just as it was ordained.

**9.46.2**  Hearing it, I know my very self to be purified, O treasury of austerity; the hairs of my body stand thrilled, and my mind is serene.

**9.46.3**  At hearing the consecration of the prince and the slaying of the daityas, my joy is supreme—and my curiosity is greater still.

**9.46.4**  How was the lord of waters consecrated here by gods and asuras? Tell me that, O greatly wise one, for you are skilled, O best of men.

**9.46.5**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Hear, O king, this marvel just as it was in a former age. Of old, while that first age, the Kṛtayuga, ran its course in due order, all the deities came together and spoke thus to Varuṇa:

**9.46.6**  "As Śakra, king of the gods, ever guards us from dangers, so do you too become the lord of all the rivers.

**9.46.7**  And your dwelling shall ever be, O god, in the ocean, abode of the sea-monsters; this sea, lord of streams, shall be under your sway.

**9.46.8**  And with Soma your waning and waxing shall come to pass." "So be it," said Varuṇa in answer to those gods.

**9.46.9**  Then all of them, coming together, made Varuṇa, dweller in the ocean, the lord of waters, by rites prescribed in the ordinance.

**9.46.10**  Having consecrated Varuṇa as lord of the water-creatures and honored the lord of waters, the gods then went each to his own abode.

**9.46.11**  Consecrated by the gods, the greatly glorious Varuṇa governed the rivers and seas, the streams and the lakes, by rule, even as the Lord of a Hundred Sacrifices governs the gods.

**9.46.12**  Then, having touched the water there too and given wealth of many kinds, the greatly wise slayer of Pralamba went to the Agni-tīrtha, where the oblation-eater, hidden in the heart of the śamī tree, had vanished and could not be seen.

**9.46.13**  And when the destruction of light and darkness throughout the worlds had come to pass, O sinless one, the gods drew near the great-souled Grandsire of all the worlds.

**9.46.14**  "The blessed fire has vanished, and we do not know the cause; let there be no destruction of all the worlds—restore the fire to us."

**9.46.15**  Janamejaya said: Why did the blessed fire, sustainer of the worlds, vanish, and how was it found out by the gods? Tell me that truly.

**9.46.16**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Sorely afraid by reason of the curse of Bhṛgu, the mighty Jātavedas, entering the heart of the śamī tree, the blessed one thereupon vanished.

**9.46.17**  When the fire had thus vanished, all the gods with Vāsava, sorely grieved, then searched for the lost flame.

**9.46.18**  Then, reaching the Agni-tīrtha, they beheld the flame there dwelling, after its own manner, within the heart of the śamī tree.

**9.46.19**  All the gods, O tiger among men, with Bṛhaspati at their head and with Vāsava, drew near that flame and were gladdened; they returned each as they had come, and that fire became the devourer of all.

**9.46.20**  By the curse of Bhṛgu, O guardian of the earth, that which was spoken by the knower of Brahman came to pass; and having bathed there too, the wise one went to the source of Brahman.

**9.46.21**  There, where the blessed Grandsire of all the worlds had brought forth creation, having bathed, the Lord Brahmā with the gods of old created the foods of the deities, each in due order.

**9.46.22**  Having bathed there and bestowed wealth of many kinds, he went on to the tīrtha of Kubera, where, having performed great austerity, the Lord, son of Ilabilā, attained the sovereignty over riches, O king.

**9.46.23**  Even as he abode there, O king, riches and treasures waited upon him, O best of men; and the Plough-bearer, having gone to that tīrtha and bathed according to rule, gave wealth to the brāhmaṇas.

**9.46.24**  There in Kubera's domain, in that loveliest of forests, he beheld that place where of old great austerity had been performed by the exalted one.

**9.46.25**  There it was that King Kubera obtained abundant boons, and from Rudra of measureless splendour he won lordship over wealth and friendship besides.

**9.46.26**  There, O mighty-armed one, the lord of riches readily gained godhood, the office of world-guardian, and a son, Nalakūbara.

**9.46.27**  There too he was consecrated, having assembled with the hosts of the Maruts; and to him was given that lovely conveyance yoked with swans, the celestial Puṣpaka chariot, and the sovereignty over the Nairṛtas as well.

**9.46.28**  Having bathed there, O king, and bestowed lavish gifts, Bala — Rāma, smeared with white unguents — went on in haste to the next holy ford.

**9.46.29**  It was frequented by all living beings, Badarapācana by name, graced with groves of every season, ever blossoming and fruitful and fair.

**9.47.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Then Rāma proceeded to the excellent ford named Badarapācana, hallowed by the deeds of ascetics and adepts, where a maiden, firm in her vows, once dwelt.

**9.47.2**  She was the daughter of Bharadvāja, peerless in beauty upon the earth, O lord, a maiden named Srucāvatī, who lived in chastity.

**9.47.3**  She practised the fiercest austerity through many observances, O king, that lovely woman, having resolved within herself, 'May the king of the gods become my husband.'

**9.47.4**  Many years passed over her, O upholder of the Kuru line, as she kept those several vows, severe and most hard for women to fulfil.

**9.47.5**  By that conduct of hers, by her austerity, O lord of peoples, and by her supreme devotion, the divine Punisher of Pāka was well pleased.

**9.47.6**  The lord and sovereign of the thirty gods came to her hermitage, having assumed the form of the brahmin sage, the great-souled Vasiṣṭha.

**9.47.7**  Seeing him of dread austerity, Vasiṣṭha, foremost among those who do penance, she honoured him with the rites prescribed by the sages, O Bhārata.

**9.47.8**  And that auspicious maiden, versed in observances and sweet of speech, said: 'Reverend one, tiger among sages, what do you command, O lord?'

**9.47.9**  'All that lies in my power I will give you this day, O firm in vows; yet out of my devotion to Śakra I will in no wise give you my hand.'

**9.47.10**  'By vows and observances and by austerity, O wealth of penance, it is Śakra, lord of the three worlds, whom I must satisfy.'

**9.47.11**  Thus addressed, the divine lord, gazing upon her as though with a smile, spoke to her who knew the observances, soothing her as it were, O Bhārata.

**9.47.12**  'Fierce is the austerity you practise; well do I know you, O firm in vows. The purpose for which this endeavour lies in your heart, O auspicious one —'

**9.47.13**  '— all that shall come to pass even as it is, O fair-faced one. By austerity all things are won; all things rest upon austerity.'

**9.47.14**  'Those celestial realms that belong to the gods, O fair of face, are to be attained by austerity; great bliss is rooted in austerity.'

**9.47.15**  'Having performed dread austerity here and laid aside the body, mortals attain godhood, O auspicious one. Now hear this word of mine.'

**9.47.16**  'Cook these jujubes, O fortunate one, O fair in vows — cook them,' said that divine slayer of Bala, and departed.

**9.47.17**  Having taken leave of that auspicious maiden, he then murmured his sacred recitation not far from that hermitage, at the excellent ford, the Indratīrtha, O great king, renowned throughout the three worlds.

**9.47.18**  To put her to the test, the divine Punisher of Pāka, lord of the gods, caused the jujubes not to cook.

**9.47.19**  Then, O king, with self restrained, speech curbed, and weariness gone, intent upon her task and clad in spotless raiment, she set them on the fire; she of mighty vows cooked the jujubes, O tiger among kings.

**9.47.20**  As she cooked, a very long while passed, O bull among men, yet the fruits would not soften, and the day drew toward its close.

**9.47.21**  When the fire had consumed her store of firewood, seeing the flame bereft of fuel, she then set her own body to burn.

**9.47.22**  Casting first her two feet into the flame, that fair maiden, the blameless one, turned her feet back ever again into it as they burned and burned.

**9.47.23**  Though her feet were being consumed, the irreproachable one, lotus-petal-eyed, gave no thought to her pain, longing only to please the great sage.

**9.47.24**  Then, beholding this deed of hers, the lord of the three worlds was well pleased, and thereupon revealed to the maiden his own true form.

**9.47.25**  And the foremost of the gods said to that maiden of unbending vows: 'I am pleased with you, O fair one, by your devotion, your austerity, and your observance.'

**9.47.26**  'Therefore whatever desire is dear to you shall be fulfilled, O fair one; and laying aside your body, O greatly blessed, you shall dwell with me in heaven.'

**9.47.27**  'And this excellent ford of yours shall stand abiding in the world, washing away all sins, O fair-browed one — Badarapācana by name, renowned throughout the three worlds, frequented by brahmin sages.'

**9.47.28**  'For at this excellent and auspicious ford, O greatly blessed one, the Seven Sages of old once departed and went to the Himālaya, leaving Arundhatī behind.'

**9.47.29**  'Then those greatly blessed and well-disciplined sages, having gone thither, set forth, it is said, to gather fruits and roots for their sustenance.'

**9.47.30**  'As they dwelt there seeking their livelihood in the Himālayan forest, a drought set in then that lasted twelve years.'

**9.47.31**  'Those ascetics, having made a hermitage there, took up their abode; and the auspicious Arundhatī too was at that time ever steadfast in austerity.'

**9.47.32**  'Then beholding Arundhatī engaged in fierce observance, the three-eyed god, bestower of boons, came to her, greatly pleased.'

**9.47.33**  'Then Mahādeva of great renown, having assumed the form of a brahmin, approached her and said: "I wish for alms, O fair one."'

**9.47.34**  'Then that fair maiden answered the brahmin: "Our store of food is spent, O brahmin; eat these jujubes here." Thereupon Mahādeva said: "Cook these, O firm in vows."'

**9.47.35**  'Thus addressed, that illustrious woman, wishing to please the brahmin, set the jujubes upon the kindled fire and cooked them.'

**9.47.36**  'Meanwhile she listened to divine, delightful, and holy tales; and so passed away that dreadful drought of twelve years.'

**9.47.37**  'As she fasted and cooked and hearkened to those auspicious tales, that most grievous span of time passed for her as though it were a single day.'

**9.47.38**  'Then those sages arrived, bringing fruits from the mountain; and thereupon the pleased lord spoke to Arundhatī.'

**9.47.39**  '"Draw near, O knower of duty, to these sages as before. I am pleased with you, O knower of duty, by your austerity and your observance."'

**9.47.40**  'Then the lord Hara revealed his own true form, and thereupon recounted to them that great deed of hers.'

**9.47.41**  '"The austerity that you gained upon the slopes of the Himālaya, O brahmins, and the austerity of this woman — these I deem not equal."'

**9.47.42**  '"For by this ascetic woman austerity most hard to perform was practised; fasting and cooking, she has carried through twelve full years."'

**9.47.43**  'Then the lord again spoke to that same Arundhatī: "Choose a boon, O auspicious one, whatever you desire in your heart."'

**9.47.44**  'She of wide coppery eyes said to the god in the assembly of the Seven Sages: "If, O lord, you are pleased with me, may this become an excellent ford, dear to the Siddhas, gods, and sages, Badarapācana by name."'

**9.47.45**  '"And, O lord of the lords of gods, may one who dwells here in purity for three nights gain by his fasting the fruit of twelve years." Saying to her, "So be it," Hara then departed to heaven.'

**9.47.46**  'The sages were filled with wonder, beholding Arundhatī unwearied and unfaded, the chaste one who had borne hunger and thirst.'

**9.47.47**  'Thus did the pure Arundhatī attain supreme perfection — even as you, O greatly blessed one of unbending vows, have done for my sake.'

**9.47.48**  'For a still greater devotion, O gentle one, have you given to this vow; and so this day, well pleased by your observance, I grant you this.'

**9.47.49**  'A special boon I grant you, O auspicious and excellent one, beyond that boon which the great-souled god bestowed upon Arundhatī.'

**9.47.50**  'And by his grace and by your own splendour, O auspicious one, I shall now declare here yet another boon in due form.'

**9.47.51**  'Whoever shall dwell at this ford for a single night with mind well composed, having bathed, shall upon the laying aside of his body attain worlds hard to win.'

**9.47.52**  Having so spoken to the holy Srucāvatī, the mighty thousand-eyed god then returned again to heaven.

**9.47.53**  When the wielder of the thunderbolt had gone, O king, a rain of celestial flowers fell there, sweet with celestial fragrance, O best of the Bhāratas.

**9.47.54**  Kettledrums of mighty sound resounded on every side, and a fragrant, gentle breeze blew fittingly, O lord of peoples.

**9.47.55**  And casting off her fair body, she passed to the state of Indra's consort, having won it by fierce austerity, and rejoiced together with that imperishable lord.

**9.47.56**  Janamejaya said: Who, O reverend one, was her mother, and where was that lovely maiden reared? I wish to hear, O brahmin, for great is my curiosity.

**9.47.57**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: When the great-souled brahmin sage Bharadvāja beheld the wide-eyed apsaras Ghṛtācī approaching, his seed was shed.

**9.47.58**  That foremost of those who murmur prayers caught the seed in his hand and cast it into a cup of leaves; and therein that fair maiden came into being.

**9.47.59**  Having performed for her all the birth-rites and the rest, the great sage Bharadvāja, that wealth of penance, bestowed upon her a name.

**9.47.60**  'Srucāvatī' — so the righteous one named her in the assembly of the sages; and leaving her in the hermitage, he went to the Himālayan forest.

**9.47.61**  There too the high-souled foremost of the Vṛṣṇis, having touched the waters and given wealth to the great brahmins, went with well-composed soul to the ford of Śakra at that time.

**9.48.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Having then gone to the Indratīrtha, the mighty foremost of the Yadus, after bathing in due form, gave wealth and jewels to the brahmins.

**9.48.2**  For there that king of the immortals worshipped with a hundred sacrifices, and the lord of the gods bestowed abundant wealth upon Bṛhaspati.

**9.48.3**  Unhindered rites, attended by the jārūthya offerings, all with manifold sacrificial fees, he performed there as prescribed, with priests deeply versed in the Vedas.

**9.48.4**  Those sacrifices, O best of the Bhāratas, the one of great splendour duly accomplished a hundred times over, and thence became renowned as Śatakratu, 'lord of a hundred rites.'

**9.48.5**  And by his name that ford, blessed, holy, and eternal, became famed as the Indratīrtha, the deliverer from all sins.

**9.48.6**  Having there too touched the waters in due form, the wielder of the pestle honoured the brahmins with drink, raiment, and food, and from that excellent and auspicious ford went on to Rāmatīrtha.

**9.48.7**  There the greatly blessed Rāma, scion of Bhṛgu, of mighty austerity, again and again rid the whole earth of its foremost warriors of the Kṣatriya race.

**9.48.8**  Setting at the fore his preceptor Kaśyapa, best of sages, he worshipped with the Vājapeya rite and with a hundred horse-sacrifices, and gave away as the sacrificial fee the whole earth with its seas.

**9.48.9**  Rāma, having given wealth there to the brahmins, O Janamejaya, touched the waters according to rule and likewise honoured the twice-born,

**9.48.10**  and at that holy ford, in that auspicious place, the fair-faced one bestowed wealth and saluted the sages, and then came to the Yamunātīrtha.

**9.48.11**  There it was that the greatly blessed son of Aditi, Varuṇa of white radiance, once conducted the Rājasūya sacrifice, O lord of the earth.

**9.48.12**  There, having vanquished in battle both men and gods, Varuṇa, slayer of hostile heroes, performed that excellent sacrifice.

**9.48.13**  When that excellent sacrifice was concluded, a war arose between the gods and the dānavas, bringing destruction upon the three worlds.

**9.48.14**  When the Rājasūya, that foremost of sacrifices, is brought to its close, O Janamejaya, a most terrible war arises that falls upon the Kṣatriyas.

**9.48.15**  Then Rāma, the plough-armed, the Mādhava, having bathed at that finest of fords and bestowed wealth upon the twice-born,

**9.48.16**  the lotus-eyed one, garlanded with woodland flowers, gladdened then and praised by the twice-born, went on from there to the Āditya ford.

**9.48.17**  There, O best of kings, the radiant Lord Bhāskara, the maker of light, by sacrificing won lordship over the luminaries and his splendor.

**9.48.18**  On the bank of that river, all the gods with Vāsava, the Viśvedevas together with the Maruts, the Gandharvas and Apsarases,

**9.48.19**  Dvaipāyana and Śuka too, and Kṛṣṇa the slayer of Madhu, the Yakṣas and the Rākṣasas and the Piśācas, O lord of peoples,

**9.48.20**  these and many others, perfected in yoga by the thousands, abode at that holy and auspicious ford of the Sarasvatī, O scorcher of foes.

**9.48.21**  There, O best of the Bhāratas, Viṣṇu of old, having slain the Asuras Madhu and Kaiṭabha, bathed at that highest and most excellent of fords.

**9.48.22**  And the righteous-souled Dvaipāyana, O Bhārata, having bathed there, attained the supreme yoga and reached the highest perfection.

**9.48.23**  And Asita Devala too, of great austerities, taking his stand there in supreme yoga, that sage attained the yoga.

**9.49.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: There indeed of old dwelt the righteous-souled Asita Devala, rich in austerities, holding to the householder's duty.

**9.49.2**  Ever devoted to dharma, pure, self-controlled, one who had laid down the rod of punishment, of great austerities, even toward all creatures in deed, in mind, and in word;

**9.49.3**  free of anger, O great king, regarding alike blame and what is dear and not dear, of great austerities, looking with even eye upon gold and upon a clod of earth;

**9.49.4**  ever worshipping the gods and his guests together with the twice-born, ever delighting in chastity, always devoted to dharma.

**9.49.5**  Then, O great king, having drawn near and taken his stand in yoga, the mendicant, the wise sage Jaigīṣavya, came composed to that ford.

**9.49.6**  In the hermitage of Devala, O king, that one of great splendor dwelt, ever steadfast in yoga, O great king, who had attained perfection through his great austerities.

**9.49.7**  Yet Devala, though he beheld the great sage Jaigīṣavya dwelling there, did not address him in the manner that righteousness required.

**9.49.8**  Thus, O great king, a long time passed for the two of them, and Devala did not see the sage Jaigīṣavya.

**9.49.9**  At mealtime, O Janamejaya, the wise wandering ascetic, knower of dharma, would present himself before Devala at the hour of begging.

**9.49.10**  Seeing the great sage come there in the guise of a beggar, Devala showed him the highest reverence and great affection.

**9.49.11**  And Devala, O Bhārata, honored him to the best of his power according to the rite as seen by the seers, attentively, for many years.

**9.49.12**  Once, O king, a very great wonder arose in the great-souled Devala as he beheld that sage of great splendor.

**9.49.13**  "Many years have passed while I have honored him, yet this listless mendicant has never spoken a word to me."

**9.49.14**  Pondering thus, the illustrious Devala, a traveler through the sky, took up his water-jar and set out for the great ocean.

**9.49.15**  As that righteous-souled one was going toward the ocean, lord of rivers, he then saw Jaigīṣavya, who had already arrived there before him, O Bhārata.

**9.49.16**  Then the powerful Asita, filled with amazement, fell to wondering: "How has this mendicant arrived at the ocean and already bathed?"

**9.49.17**  Thus did the great seer Asita ponder; and then, having bathed in the ocean according to rule and being pure, he murmured his prayer.

**9.49.18**  His muttered prayer and daily rites performed, the illustrious one went back to his hermitage, taking up his jar filled with water, O Janamejaya.

**9.49.19**  Then, as he was entering his own hermitage-ground, the sage saw Jaigīṣavya seated there within the hermitage.

**9.49.20**  And Jaigīṣavya did not speak to him in any way at all; like a block of wood that one of great austerities dwelt upon the hermitage-ground.

**9.49.21**  He had seen him bathed in the waters of the ocean—that one like the ocean itself—and now he saw him already entered into the hermitage before him.

**9.49.22**  Asita Devala, O king, the wise one, fell to pondering, having seen the power of Jaigīṣavya's austerity born of yoga.

**9.49.23**  That best of sages pondered, O lord of kings: "How is it that I have seen this one both at the ocean and in the hermitage?"

**9.49.24**  Pondering thus, that sage who had mastered the sacred formulas rose up from the hermitage into the sky, O lord of peoples—Devala then, to learn the truth about the mendicant Jaigīṣavya.

**9.49.25**  He beheld the Siddhas who traverse the sky, composed in meditation, and he saw Jaigīṣavya being honored by those Siddhas.

**9.49.26**  Then Asita Devala, deeply intent, resolute, firm in his vows, saw Jaigīṣavya passing on into heaven.

**9.49.27**  And from there he saw him going to the world of the ancestors; and from the world of the ancestors he saw him passing on to the world of Yama.

**9.49.28**  And rising up even from there, he watched the great sage Jaigīṣavya going on to the much-praised world of Soma.

**9.49.29**  He watched him rise to the blessed worlds of those who sacrifice in solitary devotion, and thence to the worlds of those who keep the fire-oblation, and from those too he rose.

**9.49.30**  The wise one beheld him pass beyond the worlds of those rich in austerity who offer the new-moon and full-moon sacrifices, and beyond the worlds of those who sacrifice with animals; and he saw him going to a spotless world honored by the gods.

**9.49.31**  He saw him passing likewise to the station of those rich in austerity who sacrifice with the manifold four-monthly rites, and so to that of those who offer the Agniṣṭoma.

**9.49.32**  And Devala watched him reach the station of those rich in austerity who sacrifice with the Agniṣṭut as well.

**9.49.33**  He saw him in the worlds of those very wise ones who perform the Vājapeya, the finest of rites, abounding in gold.

**9.49.34**  And Devala saw Jaigīṣavya in the worlds of those who sacrifice with the Puṇḍarīka and with the Rājasūya.

**9.49.35**  He saw him in the worlds of those best of men who perform the Aśvamedha, the finest of rites, and the Naramedha as well.

**9.49.36**  And Devala saw Jaigīṣavya in the worlds of those who perform the hard-to-attain Sarvamedha and the Sautrāmaṇī.
