**9.49.37**  And in the worlds of those who sacrifice with the various twelve-day sacrificial sessions, O king, Devala saw Jaigīṣavya.

**9.49.38**  Then Asita saw him having attained shared dwelling in the worlds of Mitra and Varuṇa, and so too of the Ādityas.

**9.49.39**  And then Asita saw him having passed beyond the station of the Rudras and of the Vasus, and that of Bṛhaspati—all of them.

**9.49.40**  And having ascended the world of cattle, Asita then saw Jaigīṣavya going on to the worlds of those who keep the Brahmā-session.

**9.49.41**  He watched that brāhmaṇa rise by his own splendor to three further worlds, and pass on to the worlds of women faithful to their husbands.

**9.49.42**  Then Asita no longer saw that best of sages, Jaigīṣavya, fixed in yoga and vanished from sight, O subduer of foes.

**9.49.43**  The greatly fortunate Devala reflected upon the power of Jaigīṣavya, his excellence in vows, and the matchless perfection of his yoga.

**9.49.44**  Then Asita, steadfast and self-restrained, with palms joined, asked the most excellent Siddhas in those worlds, those keepers of the Brahmā-session:

**9.49.45**  "I do not see Jaigīṣavya; tell me of that mighty one. This I wish to hear, for my curiosity is very great."

**9.49.46**  The Siddhas said: "Hear, O Devala, firm in your vows, the truth of the matter as we tell it. Jaigīṣavya has gone to the eternal, imperishable world of Brahmā."

**9.49.47**  Hearing the words of those Siddhas, keepers of the Brahmā-session, Asita Devala swiftly soared upward—and then fell back down.

**9.49.48**  Then those Siddhas spoke again to Devala: "You have no power to go there, O Devala, rich in austerity—to reach, O brāhmaṇa, the abode of Brahmā that Jaigīṣavya has attained."

**9.49.49**  Hearing those words of the Siddhas, Devala descended again, in due order, through all those worlds.

**9.49.50**  He came to his own holy hermitage-ground like a bird alighting, and as he entered Devala saw Jaigīṣavya there.

**9.49.51**  Then Devala reckoned with a mind grounded in righteousness, having beheld the power of Jaigīṣavya's austerity, born of yoga.

**9.49.52**  Then Devala, O king, bowed in humility, drew near the great sage and said to the great-souled Jaigīṣavya: "I would wish, O blessed one, to take up the dharma of liberation."

**9.49.53**  Hearing those words of his, the other gave instruction—the supreme method of yoga, and what should and should not be done according to scripture.

**9.49.54**  Then, seeing that one whose mind was set upon renunciation, the great ascetic performed all his rites for him with the rite as ordained.

**9.49.55**  Seeing him with his mind set upon renunciation, the beings together with the ancestors wept aloud: "Who now will share his portion with us?"

**9.49.56**  But Devala, hearing the piteous words of the beings as they cried out through the ten directions, set his mind upon abandoning liberation.

**9.49.57**  And then, O Bhārata, the fruits and roots, the sacred grasses, the flowers and the herbs cried out aloud by the thousands:

**9.49.58**  "Surely the base, ill-minded Devala will cut us down again—he who, having granted safety to all beings, takes no heed of it!"

**9.49.59**  Then once more that best of sages reckoned with his own mind: "In liberation or in the householder's dharma—which would bring the greater good?"

**9.49.60**  Having so resolved in mind, O best of kings, Devala abandoned the householder's dharma and chose the dharma of liberation.

**9.49.61**  Reflecting upon these things and the like, Devala by his resolve then attained the supreme perfection and the highest yoga, O Bhārata.

**9.49.62**  Then the gods, the ascetics led by Bṛhaspati, came together and praised Jaigīṣavya and his austerity.

**9.49.63**  Then the best of seers, Nārada, said to the gods: "There is no austerity in Jaigīṣavya, who only astonishes Asita."

**9.49.64**  To that steadfast one who spoke thus the dwellers of heaven replied, "Not so!"—and they praised the great sage Jaigīṣavya.

**9.49.65**  There too the great-souled plough-bearer touched the water, and having bestowed wealth upon the twice-born and gained the highest merit, that doer of noble deeds went on to the great ford of Soma.

**9.50.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Where Soma, lord of the stars, sacrificed of old with the Rājasūya, O Bhārata—when that was done, there arose the great battle called Tārakāmaya.

**9.50.2**  There too Bala touched the water, and the self-possessed, righteous-souled one, having given gifts, went on to the ford of the sage Sārasvata.

**9.50.3**  There, in a twelve-year drought of old, the sage Sārasvata taught the Vedas to the best of the twice-born.

**9.50.4**  Janamejaya said: How was it that in a twelve-year drought of old the ascetic sage Sārasvata taught the Vedas?

**9.50.5**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: There was of old, O great king, a wise sage of great austerities, renowned as Dadhīca, a celibate who had conquered his senses.

**9.50.6**  Of him and his surpassing austerity Śakra was ever afraid, O lord; nor could he be tempted by rewards of every kind.

**9.50.7**  Then, to tempt him, the Chastiser of Pāka sent forth a celestial apsaras, holy and fair to behold, named Alambusā.

**9.50.8**  As that great-souled one was offering libations to the gods upon the Sarasvatī, O great king, the lovely woman drew near and stood close beside him.

**9.50.9**  Beholding her of celestial form, the seed of that contemplative sage spilled forth into the Sarasvatī, and the river took it up.

**9.50.10**  Seeing that seed, O bull among men, she received it into her womb; and that great river bore the embryo for the sake of a son.

**9.50.11**  In due time that best of rivers gave birth to a son, and taking the child she went to that sage, O lord.

**9.50.12**  Seeing that most excellent of sages in the assembly of seers, the river, O lord of kings, spoke thus as she handed over his son: 'O brahmā-seer, this is your son, borne by me out of devotion to you.'

**9.50.13**  'When at the sight of the apsaras Alambusā your seed was once spilled, that, O brahmā-seer, I held within my womb out of devotion to you.'

**9.50.14**  'Resolved that this radiance of yours should not perish, I bore it; receive now your own blameless son, given to you by me.'

**9.50.15**  Thus addressed, that best of twice-born accepted him and felt the highest delight; with mantras and with love he kissed the boy upon the head.

**9.50.16**  Embracing him a long while, O best of the Bhāratas, the great sage, well pleased, then granted a boon to the Sarasvatī.

**9.50.17**  'The Viśvedevas together with the fathers, and the hosts of gandharvas and apsarases, shall attain satisfaction, O fortunate one, when honored with libations of your waters.'

**9.50.18**  Having spoken thus, pleased and supremely joyful in heart, he praised the great river with these words; hear them duly, O king.

**9.50.19**  'O greatly blessed one, you flowed forth of old from the lake of Brahmā; the sages of strict vows know you, O best of rivers.'

**9.50.20**  'And you have ever done me kindness, O lovely to behold; therefore great shall be your son Sārasvata, O fair-complexioned one.'

**9.50.21**  'Renowned by your very name, your son, a benefactor of the worlds, shall become famed as Sārasvata, great in austerity.'

**9.50.22**  'During a drought of twelve years, O greatly blessed one, this Sārasvata shall teach the Vedas to the bulls among the twice-born.'

**9.50.23**  'And of all holy rivers you shall ever be the most holy, O auspicious one, O greatly blessed Sarasvatī, by my grace.'

**9.50.24**  Thus praised by him, the great river, having received her boon, took her son and went away rejoicing, O bull of the Bhāratas.

**9.50.25**  At that very time, in the strife between the gods and the dānavas, Śakra, seeking a weapon, ranged through the three worlds.

**9.50.26**  Yet the lord Śakra found then no weapon that would be fit to slay those foes of the gods.

**9.50.27**  Then Śakra said to the gods: 'These mighty asuras, foes of the thirty, cannot be slain by me save with the bones of Dadhīca.'

**9.50.28**  'Therefore go, O best of gods, and let that foremost of sages be entreated: "Give the bones of Dadhīca"—with them we shall slay our enemies.'

**9.50.29**  Earnestly entreated by the gods to give up his bones—to lay down his very life—that best of sages did so without a moment's hesitation; and having done the gods this kindness, he won then the imperishable worlds.

**9.50.30**  From his bones, then, Śakra, with greatly delighted heart, had fashioned divine weapons of many kinds—thunderbolts, discuses, maces, and ample heavy staves.

**9.50.31**  For he, the benefactor of the worlds, had been begotten through fierce austerity by the supreme sage Bhṛgu, son of Prajāpati.

**9.50.32**  Vast of body, full of splendor, fashioned from the essence of the worlds, he was born—heavy as a mountain, towering, renowned for his might, a lord; ever did the Chastiser of Pāka tremble at his radiance.

**9.50.33**  With that thunderbolt, charged with mantras, O Bhārata, hurled in furious wrath and sprung from the power of brahman, the lord slew nine times ninety of the daitya and dānava heroes.

**9.50.34**  Then, when a long and most terrible span of time had passed, a drought of twelve years came on, O king.

**9.50.35**  In that twelve-year drought the great seers, afflicted by hunger, fled in every direction in search of livelihood, O king.

**9.50.36**  Seeing them scattered to the quarters, the sage Sārasvata then made up his mind to depart; but the Sarasvatī spoke to him.

**9.50.37**  'You must not go from here, my son; I shall always give you food—the choicest of fishes; dwell here, O Bhārata.'

**9.50.38**  Thus addressed, he made offering to the fathers and to the gods, took his food daily, and so preserved both his life and the Vedas.

**9.50.39**  Then, when that drought had passed, the great seers questioned one another concerning the recitation of their sacred study once more.

**9.50.40**  For all of them, gripped by hunger and wandering about, the Vedas had been lost, O lord of kings; not one of them had any recollection left.

**9.50.41**  Then a certain one of the seers came upon Sārasvata, that most excellent and disciplined of sages, engaged in his sacred recitation.

**9.50.42**  Going back, he told them of the brilliant Sārasvata, godlike in his luster, performing his sacred recitation in a solitary place.

**9.50.43**  Then all the great seers gathered there, O king, and, assembled before Sārasvata, that best of sages, said this:

**9.50.44**  'Teach us,' they said; and the sage replied to them: 'Become my pupils duly, sirs, according to the rule.'

**9.50.45**  Then the company of seers said: 'You are but a boy, dear child.' He answered the sages again: 'So that my dharma not be lost.'

**9.50.46**  'He who teaches against right, or who receives teaching against right—let both of them swiftly perish, or both become foes to each other.'

**9.50.47**  'Not by years, nor by grey hairs, nor by wealth, nor by kinsmen did the seers determine eminence in dharma: he who is learned in the sacred lore is great among us.'

**9.50.48**  Hearing these words of his, those sages duly received the Vedas from him and re-established their dharma anew.

**9.50.49**  Sixty thousand sages became the pupils of the brahmā-seer Sārasvata for the sake of recovering the recitation of the Vedas.

**9.50.50**  Then each of them brought handful upon handful of darbha grass for the seat of that brahmā-seer; though he was but a boy, they remained obedient to him.

**9.50.51**  There too the son of Rohiṇī, the mighty elder brother of Keśava, having bestowed wealth, then went on rejoicing, in due course, to that great and famed sacred ford where the Aged Maiden once dwelt.

**9.51.1**  Janamejaya said: How, O reverend one, did that maiden of old come to be devoted to austerity? For what purpose did she practice penance, and what was her vow?

**9.51.2**  Most arduous and unsurpassed is this that I have heard from you, O brāhmaṇa; tell me the whole truth, how she stood firm in austerity.

**9.51.3**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: There was a sage of great vigor and great fame, Kuṇi of the line of Garga; having performed vast austerity, O king, that best of ascetics, the mighty one, brought forth from his mind a fair-browed daughter.

**9.51.4**  And beholding her, Kuṇi of Garga's line, of great fame, was exceedingly pleased; then, O king, casting off his body here, he went to heaven.

**9.51.5**  That fair-browed and lovely maiden, blameless, with eyes like lotus-petals, having built a hermitage, gave herself to fierce and mighty austerity.

**9.51.6**  Honoring with fasts the fathers and the gods of old, she passed a great span of time in fierce austerity, O king.

**9.51.7**  Though offered by her father to a husband, that blameless one would not consent, for she saw no husband worthy of herself.

**9.51.8**  Then, mortifying her own body with fierce austerity, she remained intent upon the worship of the fathers and the gods in a solitary forest.

**9.51.9**  Though she deemed herself to have fulfilled her purpose, she grew worn with toil, O lord of kings, wasted by old age and by penance alike.

**9.51.10**  When she could no longer walk even a single step by herself, she then set her mind upon passing to the other world.

**9.51.11**  But seeing her wishing to release her body, Nārada said: 'Whence are there worlds for you, O sinless maiden, when you are unconsecrated by marriage?'

**9.51.12**  'For thus have we heard in the world of the gods, O woman of great vows: supreme austerity you have attained, but the worlds you have not won.'

**9.51.13**  Hearing those words of Nārada, she said in the assembly of seers: 'I will give half of my austerity to him who takes my hand, O most excellent ones.'

**9.51.14**  When she had thus spoken, a sage sprung from Gālava, named Śṛṅgavān, took her hand and laid down this condition:

**9.51.15**  'On this condition shall I touch your hand today, O fair one: that you must dwell with me for a single night.'

**9.51.16**  'So be it,' she promised, and gave her hand to him then; and the son of Gālava took her hand and wedded her.

**9.51.17**  In the night she became, O king, a young woman of godlike complexion, adorned with celestial ornaments and raiment, with celestial garlands and unguents.

**9.51.18**  Beholding her, who seemed to shine with her own radiance, the son of Gālava was delighted, and dwelt with her one night; at dawn she said to him:

**9.51.19**  'The condition you made with me, O brāhmaṇa, best of ascetics—by it I have dwelt with you; blessings upon you, may all be well with you; now I depart.'

**9.51.20**  Granted leave, she spoke once more: 'Whoever, intent in spirit, shall dwell one night at this ford, having made offering to the dwellers of heaven—'

**9.51.21**  '—he shall obtain the fruit of one who duly observes chastity for eight-and-forty and for two-and-eight years.' Having spoken thus, the virtuous woman then cast off her body and went to heaven.

**9.51.22**  The sage too grew downcast, brooding on her beauty; and by the terms of the pact he had with difficulty received half her austerity.

**9.51.23**  Then, having perfected himself, he followed in her course, grief-stricken, O best of the Bhāratas, overpowered by her beauty. This great tale of the Aged Maiden has now been told to you.

**9.51.24**  While he abode there, the Plough-armed one heard that Śalya had been slain; and there too, having bestowed gifts upon the twice-born, O scorcher of foes, he grieved for Śalya, then slain in battle by the Pāṇḍavas.

**9.51.25**  Then, passing out from the gate of Samantapañcaka, Rāma the Mādhava asked the company of seers what was the fruit of Kurukṣetra.

**9.51.26**  Questioned by the lion of the Yadus about the fruit of Kurukṣetra, O lord, those great-souled ones recounted it all to him exactly as it was.

**9.52.1**  The seers said: Samantapañcaka is called, O Rāma, the eternal northern altar of Prajāpati, where of old the gods of heaven, the great bestowers of boons, worshipped with an excellent sacrifice.

**9.52.2**  And of old this field was ploughed through many years by the wise and best of royal seers, the great-souled Kuru of measureless splendor; thence it became famed in the world as Kurukṣetra.

**9.52.3**  Rāma said: For what purpose was this field ploughed by the great-souled Kuru? This I wish to hear told, O you whose wealth is austerity.

**9.52.4**  The seers said: Of old, O Rāma, as Kuru was ploughing, ever unwearied, Śakra came down from heaven, approached him, and asked the reason.

**9.52.5**  'What is this you are about, O king, with such utmost effort? O royal seer, what is your aim, that you plough this earth?'

**9.52.6**  Kuru said: 'Those men who die upon this field, O Hundred-Sacrificer, shall go to the worlds of the virtuous, free from all sin.'

**9.52.7**  Then, laughing in derision, the lord Śakra went back to heaven; but the royal seer, undaunted, kept on ploughing the earth.

**9.52.8**  Coming again and again, and mocking him again and again, the Hundred-Sacrificer, questioning him over and over, would each time depart, while Kuru remained undaunted.

**9.52.9**  But when the king ploughed the earth with fierce austerity, then Śakra told the gods what the royal seer was minded to do.

**9.52.10**  Hearing this, the gods spoke these words to the thousand-eyed one: "Let the royal sage be appeased with a boon, O Śakra, if it can be done."

**9.52.11**  "For if men who die in this place go straight to heaven without honoring us through sacrifices, then no share of the offerings will be left for us."

**9.52.12**  So Śakra came and spoke then to the royal sage: "Enough of your toil! Let my word be carried out."

**9.52.13**  "Those men who, forgoing food, untiringly cast off the body, or who are duly slain in battle, even those gone to the state of beasts, O king—"

**9.52.14**  "—let them become partakers of heaven, O lord of kings, O great-minded one." "So be it," said King Kuru then to Śakra in reply.

**9.52.15**  Then, having taken his leave, the slayer of Bala, his inmost soul rejoicing, swiftly returned once more to the realm of heaven.

**9.52.16**  Thus it was, O best of the Yadus, that this field was plowed of old by the royal sage, and Śakra too granted that it should be holy for those who release their breath here.

**9.52.17**  And here Śakra himself, the lord of the gods, sang a verse composed about Kurukṣetra; hear it, O wielder of the plow.

**9.52.18**  "Even the dust-grains from Kurukṣetra, stirred up by the wind, lead even the doer of evil deeds to the supreme goal."

**9.52.19**  Bulls among the gods and the most excellent of brāhmaṇas, and Nṛga and other chiefs among god-like kings, O lion among men, having worshiped with costly sacrifices and laid down their bodies, attained the blessed goal.

**9.52.20**  That which lies between Tarantuka and Arantuka, and between the lakes of Rāma and Macakruka—this Samantapañcaka of Kurukṣetra is called the upper altar of Prajāpati.

**9.52.21**  Auspicious, great, and holy is this place, honored by the dwellers of heaven, endowed with the virtues of the celestial realm; and therefore all the lords of the earth, when slain here, shall go to the goal of great souls.

**9.53.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Having then beheld Kurukṣetra and bestowed gifts, the Sātvata went on to a very great and divine hermitage, O Janamejaya.

**9.53.2**  It was furnished with groves of madhūka and mango, thick with plakṣa and banyan trees, holy, set about with ciribilva, and crowded with panasa and arjuna trees.

**9.53.3**  Beholding it, foremost in holy marks, the best of the Yādavas asked all those sages: "Whose excellent hermitage is this?"

**9.53.4**  And they all said to the great-souled wielder of the plow, O king: "Hear in full, O Rāma, whose hermitage this was of old."

**9.53.5**  "Here in days past the god Viṣṇu performed supreme austerity; here all his eternal sacrifices were duly carried out."

**9.53.6**  "Here too a brāhmaṇa woman, perfected, chaste from girlhood, joined to yoga and accomplished in austerity, the ascetic went to heaven."

**9.53.7**  "She was the illustrious daughter of the great-souled Śāṇḍilya, O king—firm in her vows, virtuous, disciplined, and chaste."

**9.53.8**  "Having attained the highest yoga, she went to the supreme heaven; the blessed one, having enjoyed in that hermitage the fruit of the horse-sacrifice among those who reap reward, the highly fortunate one went to heaven, honored by the self-controlled."

**9.53.9**  Having approached and beheld the holy hermitage, the bull of the Yadus, that unfailing one, then saluting those sages, sent back all his encampments and ascended the mountain on the slope of the Himavat.

**9.53.10**  Going not far thence up the mountain, the mighty one whose banner bears the palm beheld a holy and excellent ford, and was filled with utmost wonder.

**9.53.11**  And Bala reached the source of the Sarasvatī, the spring of the plakṣa tree, and Kārapacana, the foremost and most excellent of fords.

**9.53.12**  There too the mighty wielder of the plow bestowed gifts, bathed in the cold water, and thence, greatly pleased, went on to the hermitage of Mitra and Varuṇa.

**9.53.13**  To that region where Indra, Agni, and Aryaman of old found their delight, on the Yamunā, he went from Kārapacana.

**9.53.14**  Bathing there too, the righteous one attained supreme contentment; and the mighty bull of the Yadus, seated together with sages and perfected ones, listened to glorious tales.

**9.53.15**  And while they were tarrying thus, the blessed sage Nārada came to that region where Rāma was stationed.

**9.53.16**  Wrapped in a coil of matted locks, clad in golden bark, of great austerity, bearing a golden staff, O king, and carrying a water-pot as well,

**9.53.17**  holding that lovely lute named Kacchapī of sweet sound, skilled in dance and song, honored by gods and brāhmaṇas,

**9.53.18**  a stirrer-up of quarrels and ever fond of strife, he came to that region where the illustrious Rāma was stationed.

**9.53.19**  Rising up, they all paid honor to that one of restrained vows, and questioned the divine sage about what had befallen the Kurus.

**9.53.20**  Then, O king, Nārada, knower of all dharmas, recounted to him all just as it had happened—the past destruction of the Kurus.

**9.53.21**  Then the son of Rohiṇī said to Nārada in a sorrowful voice: "In what state now is that warrior caste, and the kings who were there?"

**9.53.22**  "All this I have heard before, O wealth of austerity, yet a great eagerness has arisen in me to hear it in full."

**9.53.23**  Nārada said: Bhīṣma was slain at the very first, and Droṇa, and the lord of the Sindhus likewise; Karṇa, son of Vikartana, was slain, and his sons, the great chariot-warriors.

**9.53.24**  Bhūriśravas, O son of Rohiṇī, and the valiant king of the Madras—these and many other mighty ones, here and there,

**9.53.25**  kings and princes who never turned back in battles, laid down their own dear lives for the sake of the Kaurava's pleasure.

**9.53.26**  But hear from me, O mighty-armed Mādhava, who there are not slain: those remaining in the host of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son—Kṛpa, the valiant Bhoja, and the mighty Aśvatthāman—their army broken, have scattered to the directions.

**9.53.27**  His army slain, and Kṛpa and the others fled, Duryodhana, sorely grieved, entered the lake named Dvaipāyana.

**9.53.28**  As the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra lay there, the waters frozen still, the Pāṇḍavas, together with Kṛṣṇa, tormented him with fierce words.

**9.53.29**  Goaded on every side by their words, O Rāma, the mighty hero rose up at last from the lake, seizing his great mace.

**9.53.30**  And even now he has come to combat with Bhīma; and forthwith, O Rāma, a most terrible fight between the two shall take place.

**9.53.31**  If you have any curiosity, go, O Mādhava, without delay; behold the most dreadful fight of your two pupils, if you are so inclined.

**9.53.32**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Having heard Nārada's words and honored those bulls among brāhmaṇas, he dismissed all who had come there with him, and commanded his followers, "Let us go to Dvārakā."

**9.53.33**  Descending from the best of mountains, from the auspicious spring of the plakṣa tree, then Rāma, glad of heart, having heard of the great fruit of the ford, sang in the presence of the brāhmaṇas this verse, that unfailing one:

**9.53.34**  "Where is delight to equal dwelling by the Sarasvatī? Where are virtues to equal dwelling by the Sarasvatī? Men who have gained the Sarasvatī and gone to heaven shall ever remember the river Sarasvatī."

**9.53.35**  "The Sarasvatī is holy among all rivers; the Sarasvatī ever brings happiness to the world. Men, even doers of grievous deeds, having gained the Sarasvatī, never grieve, in the world beyond or here."

**9.53.36**  Then, gazing again and again with affection upon the Sarasvatī, the scorcher of foes mounted his gleaming chariot yoked with horses.

**9.53.37**  With that swift-going chariot the bull of the Yadus, eager to see, arrived where the combat of his pupils was at hand.

**9.54.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Thus did that tumultuous combat come about, O Janamejaya, concerning which the grief-stricken King Dhṛtarāṣṭra spoke these words.

**9.54.2**  "When the mace-combat was at hand and Rāma seen present, how did my son fight back against Bhīma, O Saṃjaya?"

**9.54.3**  Saṃjaya said: Coming into Rāma's presence, your son Duryodhana, mighty-armed and valiant, eager for combat, rejoiced greatly.

**9.54.4**  Beholding the plow-bearer, the king rose up, O descendant of Bharata, and, filled with utmost joy, then said to Yudhiṣṭhira:

**9.54.5**  "Let us go swiftly from here to Samantapañcaka, O lord of peoples—that renowned upper altar of Prajāpati in the world of the gods."

**9.54.6**  "In that most holy and eternal place of the three worlds, one who meets his death in battle shall surely attain heaven."

**9.54.7**  Saying "So be it," O great king, the heroic lord Yudhiṣṭhira, son of Kuntī, set out facing toward Samantapañcaka.

**9.54.8**  Then King Duryodhana, the resplendent one, seizing his great mace, went on foot in his wrath along with the Pāṇḍavas.

**9.54.9**  As he went thus, mace in hand and clad in armor, the gods who move in the sky honored him crying "Well done! Well done!"; and what men of the air there were, beholding him, were filled with joy.

**9.54.10**  Surrounded by the Pāṇḍavas, the Kuru king, your son, went forward, assuming the gait of a rutting lordly elephant.

**9.54.11**  Then with the blare of conches, the great din of kettledrums, and the lion-roars of the heroes, all the directions were filled.

**9.54.12**  Having gone to the westward-facing region as designated by your son, it was encircled by them on every side, in all directions.

**9.54.13**  To the south of the Sarasvatī lay an excellent ford named Samanta; in that region, on ground free of barren salt, they resolved to do battle.

**9.54.14**  Then Bhīma, clad in armor, taking up his mace of vast bulk, bore a form, O great king, like unto Garutmat.

**9.54.15**  His helm bound on, wearing golden armor in the fray, your son shone forth, O king, like a golden king of mountains.

**9.54.16**  Both heroes, Bhīma and Duryodhana, encased in their armor, shone in the combat like two enraged elephants.

**9.54.17**  Standing in the midst of the battle-circle, those two bulls among men, the kinsmen, shone, O great king, like the risen moon and sun.

**9.54.18**  They glared at each other like two angry mighty elephants, burning each other with their eyes, O king, each seeking the other's death.

**9.54.19**  With exultant heart, O king, the Kaurava took up his mace, licking the corners of his mouth, his eyes red with wrath, breathing hard.

**9.54.20**  Then the valiant King Duryodhana, taking up his mace and looking toward Bhīmasena, challenged him as one elephant challenges another.

**9.54.21**  Likewise the valiant Bhīma, taking up his mace forged of the essence of the mountains, challenged the king as one lion challenges another in the forest.

**9.54.22**  With maces raised in their hands, Duryodhana and Wolf-belly shone in the fray like two mountains crowned with peaks.

**9.54.23**  Both were exceedingly enraged, both of terrible prowess, both pupils in mace-combat of the wise son of Rohiṇī.

**9.54.24**  Both were alike in their deeds, like Yama and Vāsava, and likewise the two mighty ones matched the deeds of Varuṇa.

**9.54.25**  Those two, O great king, were the equals of Vāsudeva, of Rāma, and of Vaiśravaṇa, and of Madhu and Kaiṭabha in combat.

**9.54.26**  Both were alike in their deeds in battle to Sunda and Upasunda, and the two scorchers of foes were equal as well to Time and to Death.

**9.54.27**  They rushed upon each other like two great elephants in rut, frenzied as in autumn, proud at the meeting over a she-elephant.

**9.54.28**  Those two bulls of the Bhāratas, eager for victory like two maddened elephants, spat forth the blazing venom of their wrath like two serpents.

**9.54.29**  Furious at each other, those two tamers of foes glared, both tigers among the Bhāratas, endowed with valor.

**9.54.30**  Unassailable as two lions, those scorchers of foes in mace-combat, the heroes were hard to withstand as two tigers armed with claws and fangs.

**9.54.31**  They were like two oceans hard to cross, churned at the destruction of all creatures; and the two great chariot-warriors blazed in their fury like two angry red-bodied planets of Mars.

**9.54.32**  Radiant and great-souled, blazing and of mighty strength, the two foremost of the Kurus were beheld, like twin suns of doom risen at once.

**9.54.33**  Furious as two tigers, roaring like rain-clouds, the mighty-armed pair bristled with delight like a pair of maned lions.

**9.54.34**  Enraged like two elephants, ablaze like two fires, those great-souled ones were beheld like two peaked mountains.

**9.54.35**  Their lips quivering with wrath, gazing upon each other, the two great-souled best of men came together, maces in hand.

**9.54.36**  Both supremely exultant, both held in highest honor, neighing like two fine stallions, trumpeting like two elephants—

**9.54.37**  bellowing like two bulls, Duryodhana and Vṛkodara, drunk with strength like two Daityas, those best of men shone forth.

**9.54.38**  Then, O king, Duryodhana spoke these words to Yudhiṣṭhira, who stood among the Sṛñjayas blazing like the sun:

**9.54.39**  "This combat between us two, between Bhīma and myself, is resolved upon. Be seated, O best of kings, and watch the clash."

**9.54.40**  Then that vast circle of kings, seated all around and resplendent, appeared like the orb of the sun shining in the sky.

**9.54.41**  In their midst, O great king, the mighty-armed and glorious elder brother of Keśava sat, honored on every side.

**9.54.42**  Clad in dark blue and of fair radiance, set amid the kings, he shone like the full moon ringed by the stars at night.

**9.54.43**  And so those two, O great king, maces in hand and hard to assail, stood facing each other, hewing one another with fierce words.

**9.54.44**  Having spoken bitter taunts to each other, those two bulls of the Kurus stood glaring, like Vṛtra and Śakra upon the field of battle.

**9.55.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Then arose a tumultuous war of words, O Janamejaya, whereupon the grief-stricken king Dhṛtarāṣṭra spoke thus:

**9.55.2**  "Fie indeed upon mortal life, whose end is such as this—where my son, master of eleven armies, was the overlord!"

**9.55.3**  "Having commanded all the kings and enjoyed this earth, taking up his mace he now sets out on foot, in haste, to battle."

**9.55.4**  "Having been lord of the world, my son now goes forth like one bereft of a protector, mace raised aloft—what is this but the working of fate?"

**9.55.5**  "Ah, great is the sorrow my son has come to, Saṃjaya!" Having spoken thus, the lord of men, stricken with grief, fell silent.

**9.55.6**  Saṃjaya said: With a voice like thunder, roaring in his joy like a great bull, the valiant one then summoned Pārtha to combat in the fray.

**9.55.7**  As the great-souled king of the Kurus called forth Bhīma, manifold and most fearful portents appeared.

**9.55.8**  Winds blew with crashing thunderbolts, a rain of dust fell down, and all the quarters became veiled in darkness.

**9.55.9**  Loud-roaring, thunderous, tumultuous, and hair-raising, meteors then fell by the hundreds, splitting open the vault of heaven.

**9.55.10**  And Rāhu swallowed the sun though it was no eclipse-time, O lord of peoples, and the earth with its forests and trees shook with a mighty trembling.

**9.55.11**  Harsh winds blew low, raining down gravel, and the very peaks of mountains crashed down upon the surface of the earth.

**9.55.12**  Beasts of manifold forms came rushing from the ten directions, and blazing jackals of dreadful aspect, most terrible, set up their howling.

**9.55.13**  Most fearful, hair-raising thunder-crashes resounded, and in the blazing quarter, O lord of kings, beasts cried out their ill-boding cries.

**9.55.14**  The waters in the wells swelled up on every side, and great bodiless voices were heard then, O king.

**9.55.15**  Having beheld these and like portents, Vṛkodara spoke to his eldest brother, Yudhiṣṭhira the King of Righteousness:

**9.55.16**  "This dull-witted Suyodhana cannot vanquish me in battle. Today I shall loose upon Suyodhana, lord of the Kauravas, the wrath long hidden in my heart—as the fire upon the Khāṇḍava forest."

**9.55.17**  "This day, O Pāṇḍava, I shall draw out the dart lodged in your heart, slaying with my mace this wicked one, the basest of the Kuru line."

**9.55.18**  "This day I shall fasten a garland woven of glory upon you, when I have slain this doer of evil deeds with my mace at the forefront of battle."

**9.55.19**  "This day I shall shatter his body into a hundred pieces with this mace; never again shall he enter the city called Vāraṇasāhvaya."

**9.55.20**  "For the loosing of serpents upon my bed, for the giving of poison in my food, for the hurling down at Pramāṇakoṭi, for the burning in the house of lac;"

**9.55.21**  "for the mockery in the assembly-hall, for the seizing of all our wealth, for the year spent in hidden exile and the years dwelt in the forest, O sinless one—"

**9.55.22**  "this day, O best of the Bhāratas, I shall reach the end of all these sorrows; slaying him in a single day, I shall be free of my debt to myself."

**9.55.23**  "This day, O best of the Bhāratas, the life-span of the evil-minded, unrestrained son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra is at an end—and the sight of his mother and father with it."

**9.55.24**  "This day this disgrace of the line of the Kuru king Śaṃtanu, abandoning life and fortune and kingdom, shall lie stretched upon the ground."

**9.55.25**  "And King Dhṛtarāṣṭra, hearing this day that his son has been slain by me, will recall that inauspicious deed born of Śakuni's counsel."

**9.55.26**  Having spoken thus, O tiger among kings, the valiant one took up his mace and stood ready for battle, like Śakra challenging Vṛtra.

**9.55.27**  Seeing him with mace upraised, peaked like Mount Kailāsa, Bhīmasena, wrathful once more, spoke thus to Duryodhana:

**9.55.28**  "Call to mind, then, that evil deed of King Dhṛtarāṣṭra's, and of yourself as well—what was done at Vāraṇāvata."

**9.55.29**  "And how Draupadī was tormented in the assembly-hall, though she was in her season; and how the king was cheated at dice by you and by the son of Subala."

**9.55.30**  "And the great grief we suffered in the forest through your doing, and in the city of Virāṭa, as though we had entered the wombs of others—for all of that I take requital this day. By good fortune you are now in my sight, O evil-minded one!"

**9.55.31**  "Through your doing the majestic son of Gaṅgā, foremost of chariot-warriors, lies slain upon a bed of arrows, struck down by the son of Yajñasena."

**9.55.32**  "Droṇa is slain, and Karṇa, and the majestic Śalya too; and Śakuni, son of Subala, the kindler of the fire of this enmity, lies slain."

**9.55.33**  "The wicked herald who wrought Draupadī's torment is slain; and all your brothers, heroes and valiant fighters, are slain."

**9.55.34**  "These and many other kings have been slain because of you; and you too I shall slay this day with my mace—of this there is no doubt."

**9.55.35**  As Vṛkodara spoke thus aloud, O lord of kings, your son of unfailing prowess answered him without fear, O king:

**9.55.36**  "What use is all this boasting? Fight, Vṛkodara! This day I shall drive out of you your appetite for battle, O disgrace of your line!"

**9.55.37**  "Duryodhana, O base one, is not to be frightened with words by any of your sort, as some common man might be."

**9.55.38**  "By good fortune the gods have brought to pass this thing long desired and set in my heart—this mace-combat with you."

**9.55.39**  "What use is much talk and boasting, O evil-minded one? Let this speech of yours be made good by deeds—do not delay any longer."

**9.55.40**  Hearing those words of his, all the kings and the Somakas who had gathered and were present there acclaimed him.

**9.55.41**  Then, honored by all, his body-hairs bristling with delight, the delight of the Kurus once more set his mind, resolute, upon battle.

**9.55.42**  With clapping of palms the lords of men spurred on once more that wrathful Duryodhana, like a maddened elephant.

**9.55.43**  Then the great-souled Pāṇḍava Vṛkodara, raising his mace, rushed with great speed upon the great-souled son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra.

**9.55.44**  There the elephants trumpeted and the horses neighed again and again, and the weapons of the Pāṇḍavas, eager for victory, blazed forth.

**9.56.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then Duryodhana, seeing Bhīmasena come on in this wise, undismayed in spirit, rushed to meet him with great speed, roaring aloud.

**9.56.2**  Bellowing, the two closed upon each other like a pair of horned bulls, and their clash arose with the din of a great thunderbolt.

**9.56.3**  And there arose between them a tumultuous, hair-raising combat, each seeking to vanquish the other in battle, like Indra and Prahrāda of old.

**9.56.4**  Their every limb drenched in blood, maces in hand, those resolute and great-souled ones appeared like two kiṃśuka trees in full bloom.

**9.56.5**  As that great and most terrible combat went on, the field shone lovely to behold, as though the sky were thronged with fireflies.

**9.56.6**  As that fierce and tumultuous melee raged on, both of those tamers of foes, fighting hard, grew weary.

**9.56.7**  Having rested for a moment, those two scorchers of foes, gripping fast their gleaming maces, once again fell to striking each other there.

**9.56.8**  Beholding those two bulls among men of mighty valor, rested and rejoined, like two powerful elephants frenzied with rut for the sake of a cow-elephant—

**9.56.9**  gazing upon those two of boundless valor, both with maces upraised, the gods, Gandharvas, and Dānavas fell into utmost wonder.

**9.56.10**  Seeing Duryodhana and Vṛkodara with maces upraised, doubt arose in all beings as to which should win.

**9.56.11**  Closing once more, those two kinsmen, the foremost of the strong, each seeking an opening in the other, maneuvered for an opening against the other.

**9.56.12**  The onlookers beheld, O king, the heavy mace, like the staff of Yama, raised on high like the thunderbolt of Indra—fierce and death-dealing.

**9.56.13**  As Bhīmasena whirled his mace in the encounter, a most tumultuous and dreadful sound arose for a moment.

**9.56.14**  Watching the Pāṇḍava as he whirled that mace of no slight force, the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra was filled with wonder.

**9.56.15**  And weaving through manifold movements and circling figures, O Bhārata, the hero Vṛkodara then shone forth all the more.

**9.56.16**  Closing with one another, each warding off the other, those two hacked away again and again, like two cats fighting over a morsel.

**9.56.17**  And Bhīmasena ranged through movements of many kinds, and varied circlings, and stances of every sort.

**9.56.18**  the zigzag steps, the varied passes, the advance and retreat, the parrying of blows, the evasion, and the circling run;

**9.56.19**  the charge, the feint, the firm stand with its grapple, the wheeling and the closing, the leap aside and the leap forward, the laying on of the mace and the drawing of it back—so skilled were the two in mace-combat.

**9.56.20**  Thus ranging about, those two best of the Kurus struck at each other, and feinting again, the two moved on.

**9.56.21**  Disporting themselves, the two mighty ones wheeled through their circling figures; then those two strong ones, maces in hand, took their stand in the circle.

**9.56.22**  To the rightward circle, O king, the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra moved, while Bhīmasena there moved to the leftward circle.

**9.56.23**  And as Bhīma moved thus at the forefront of battle, Duryodhana, O great king, struck him upon the region of his flank.

**9.56.24**  Struck thus by your son, O Bhārata, Bhīma, heedless of the blow, whirled his heavy mace aloft.

**9.56.25**  Terrible as Indra's thunderbolt, lifted like the rod of Yama, that mace of Bhīmasena, O great king, the onlookers beheld.

**9.56.26**  Seeing Bhīmasena whirling his mace, your son, that tamer of foes, raised his own dreadful mace and whirled it back in answer.

**9.56.27**  From the wind-swift sweep of your son's mace, O Bhārata, a tremendous roar arose, and a blaze of splendor was kindled.

**9.56.28**  Ranging through manifold paths and tracing his circling figures one by one, the radiant Suyodhana shone forth even more brilliantly than Bhīma.

**9.56.29**  Whirled with all his might by Bhīma, that great and fearsome mace, loud-roaring, shot forth fire wreathed in smoke and flame.

**9.56.30**  Seeing the mace brandished by Bhīmasena, Suyodhana, whirling his own heavy mace hard as the heart of a mountain, shone resplendent.

**9.56.31**  Beholding the wind-swift speed of that great soul's mace, fear entered all the Pāṇḍavas together with the Somakas.

**9.56.32**  Displaying on every side their battle-sport upon the field, those two tamers of foes struck at each other all at once with their maces.

**9.56.33**  Closing upon each other like two tuskers clashing with their tusks, the pair, O great king, drenched in blood, shone splendidly.

**9.56.34**  Thus did that battle rage, dreadful of aspect and unrestrained, savage as the day declined, like the war of Vṛtra and Vāsava.

**9.56.35**  Seeing Bhīma standing firm, your mighty son, ranging through ever more wondrous paths, rushed upon the son of Kuntī.

**9.56.36**  As that furious one charged, the enraged Bhīma struck down his mace, swift-driven and adorned with gold.

**9.56.37**  From the clash of the two there arose a roar shot through with sparks, O great king, as of two thunderbolts hurled together.

**9.56.38**  As that swift mace, loosed by Bhīmasena, came hurtling down, O great king, the earth itself trembled.

**9.56.39**  The Kaurava brooked not the parrying of his mace in battle, enraged like a maddened elephant at the sight of a rival bull.

**9.56.40**  Wheeling to the left, O king, his purpose set, he smote the son of Kuntī upon the head with his fearsomely swift mace.

**9.56.41**  Yet struck by it, the Pāṇḍava Bhīma, smitten by your son, did not waver, O great king — a thing well-nigh marvelous.

**9.56.42**  And all the armies, O king, applauded that wonder — that Bhīma, struck by the mace, did not stir a single step.

**9.56.43**  Then Bhīma of terrible prowess hurled at Duryodhana his blazing, gold-wrought mace, heavier still.

**9.56.44**  Unflustered, the mighty Duryodhana by his nimbleness made that blow miss its mark, and great was the wonder thereat.

**9.56.45**  That mace, O king, gone wide as it fell, driven by Bhīma, shook the earth with a sound like a mighty thunderclap.

**9.56.46**  Taking to the Kauśika maneuvers, leaping up again and again, and foreseeing the descent of the mace, he eluded Bhīmasena.

**9.56.47**  Having thus baffled Bhīma, that best of the Kurus, mighty and enraged, struck him with his mace upon the chest.

**9.56.48**  Struck by the mace and reeling in that great battle, Bhīma, smitten by your son, could not for the moment think what next to do.

**9.56.49**  As things so stood, O king, the Somakas and the Pāṇḍavas, their hopes sorely shaken, grew downcast at heart.

**9.56.50**  But he, goaded by that blow like a maddened elephant, rushed elephant-like upon your son, himself like an elephant.

**9.56.51**  Then the impetuous Bhīma, with his mace, charged your son at full speed, as a lion charges a wild forest elephant.

**9.56.52**  Closing in upon the king, skilled in the casting of the mace, he whirled it, O king, aiming straight at your son.

**9.56.53**  Bhīmasena struck Duryodhana then upon the flank, and he, staggered by the blow, sank to the earth upon his knees.

**9.56.54**  When that best of the Bhāratas had sunk to the ground upon his knees, a great shout went up from the Sṛñjayas, O lord of the world.

**9.56.55**  Hearing that roar of the Sṛñjayas, that bull among men, your son, O best of the Bhāratas, blazed up in fury.

**9.56.56**  Rising up, the mighty-armed one, enraged and hissing like a serpent, gazed upon Bhīmasena as though to burn him with his eyes.

**9.56.57**  Then that best of the Bhāratas, mace in hand, rushed forward as if to crush Bhīmasena's head in the fray.

**9.56.58**  That great soul, of terrible prowess, struck the great-souled Bhīma upon the temple, yet, mountain-like, Bhīma only swayed.

**9.56.59**  Struck by the mace in battle, the son of Pṛthā shone all the more, O king, his blood gushing forth, like a rutting elephant whose temples flow.

**9.56.60**  Then, seizing his hero-slaying mace of iron, roaring like the thunderbolt, the elder brother of Dhanaṃjaya, grinder of foes, sprang forth with all his might and smote his enemy.

**9.56.61**  Struck by Bhīmasena, your son fell, the very knitting of his frame shaken loose, like a great flowering sāla tree in a vast forest, reeling under the force of the storm-wind.

**9.56.62**  Then the Pāṇḍavas roared and rejoiced, beholding your son fallen to the earth; but your son, regaining his senses, sprang up again like an elephant rising from a lake.

**9.56.63**  That king, ever brooking no defeat, the great chariot-warrior, wheeling about with the grace of one well-schooled, struck the Pāṇḍava who stood before him; and Bhīma, his limbs faltering, touched the ground.

**9.56.64**  The Kaurava raised a lion's roar, having felled Bhīma to the ground in battle by his might, and with a mace-blow blazing like the thunderbolt he split asunder Bhīma's body-armor.

**9.56.65**  Then in the sky a great clamor arose from the dwellers of heaven and the apsarases as they cried out, and from on high, sent down by the immortals, there fell a glorious rain, a shower of heaped and wondrous blossoms.

**9.56.66**  Then the utmost fear seized the foe, beholding that best of men fallen to the ground, and the Kaurava in no wise diminished in his strength, and hearing how the stout armor had been cloven.

**9.56.67**  Then in a moment Vṛkodara recovered his senses, wiped his own face wet with blood, gathered his resolve, and, his eyes rolling, steadied himself by main strength and stood firm.

**9.57.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then, beholding the battle of the two foremost Kurus swelling to its height, Arjuna spoke to the illustrious Vāsudeva.

**9.57.2**  "Of these two heroes in combat, which do you deem the greater? And in which is the larger merit? Tell me this, Janārdana."

**9.57.3**  Vāsudeva said: "The training of the two is equal, yet Bhīma is the stronger; but this son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra is more practiced in his art than Vṛkodara."

**9.57.4**  "Bhīmasena, fighting by the rules, will not prevail; but fighting by unfair means, he may yet slay Suyodhana."

**9.57.5**  "By guile the gods overcame the asuras — so have we heard; and Virocana too was overcome by Śakra through guile, my friend. By guile, moreover, the slayer of Bala wrested away the energy of Vṛtra."

**9.57.6**  "And Bhīma vowed at the time of the dicing, O Dhanaṃjaya, saying to Suyodhana: 'In battle I will shatter your thighs with the mace.'"

**9.57.7**  "Let this grinder of foes, then, make good that vow, and cut down by guile this guileful king."

**9.57.8**  "For if, relying on his strength, he strikes by fair means, then King Yudhiṣṭhira will be left in dire straits."

**9.57.9**  "And once more I tell you, O Pāṇḍava — mark this from me: through the fault of the Dharma-king, peril has come upon us again."

**9.57.10**  "Having wrought a most mighty deed, having slain the Kurus led by Bhīṣma, you have won victory and foremost fame, and the feud is requited; yet now that very victory, once gained, has been cast again into doubt."

**9.57.11**  "This was a great folly of the Dharma-king, O Pāṇḍava — that he staked the whole on a single combat of this kind; for Suyodhana is skilled and valiant, a man with nothing to lose, brought to his last resort."

**9.57.12**  "Moreover, there is heard an ancient verse sung by Uśanas, freighted with the truth of things — hear it as I recite it to you."

**9.57.13**  "Of broken foes who turn back again, clinging to life, the last remnant must be feared, for they are come to their final stand."

**9.57.14**  "This Suyodhana — broken, his army slain, gone to take refuge in the lake, defeated, longing for the forest, despairing of regaining his kingdom —"

**9.57.15**  "what man of sense would summon him afresh to single combat in battle? Might not Suyodhana yet snatch back from you the kingdom already won?"

**9.57.16**  "For thirteen years has he labored with the mace, and he ranges high and crosswise in his eagerness to slay Bhīmasena."

**9.57.17**  "And so, if the mighty-armed one slay him not by unfair means, this Kaurava, the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, will yet become your king."

**9.57.18**  And Dhanaṃjaya, hearing this from the great-souled Keśava, struck his own thigh with his hand within Bhīmasena's sight.

**9.57.19**  Taking the sign, Bhīma then ranged the battlefield with his mace, tracing wondrous circling figures, both paired and single.

**9.57.20**  The rightward circle, the leftward, and the zigzag course as well, the Pāṇḍava traced, O king, as though to bewilder his foe.

**9.57.21**  In like manner your son too, skilled in the ways of the mace, ranged nimbly and intricately in his eagerness to slay Bhīmasena.

**9.57.22**  Brandishing their dreadful maces, smeared with sandal and aloe, seeking to make an end of their enmity, the two were in battle like Death himself enraged.

**9.57.23**  Seeking each the other's death, those two foremost heroes, bulls among men, fought like two Garuḍas striving after the flesh of serpents.

**9.57.24**  As the king and Bhīma traced their wondrous circling figures, there sprang up from the clashing of their maces tongues of fire.

**9.57.25**  As the two valiant and mighty ones struck alike in the fray, they were, O king, like two oceans churned up by the wind.

**9.57.26**  As the two struck evenly, like a pair of rutting elephants, there arose a crashing thunder of mace-strokes from their blows.

**9.57.27**  In that fierce and tumultuous combat, both those tamers of foes, fighting on, grew sorely spent.

**9.57.28**  Resting a moment, those two scorchers of foes, enraged once more, gripped their great maces and fell upon each other again.

**9.57.29**  There arose between them a battle dreadful of aspect and unrestrained, O lord of kings, as with falling maces they hewed at one another.

**9.57.30**  Hurtling forward in their exertion, those two swift and bull-eyed heroes smote at each other like buffaloes mired in the mud.

**9.57.31**  Their every limb battered and bruised, drenched all over in blood, the two appeared like a pair of kiṃśuka trees in bloom upon the Himālaya.

**9.57.32**  When Duryodhana had laid open an opening, the son of Pṛthā, smiling a little, all at once sprang forward.

**9.57.33**  Seeing him come within reach in the battle, the wise and mighty Vṛkodara hurled his mace down at him with tremendous force.

**9.57.34**  But seeing that descending stroke, your son, O lord of peoples, slipped aside from his place, and the mace fell wide upon the ground.

**9.57.35**  Having made that blow miss in his swift recovery, your son, that best of the Kurus, struck Bhīmasena in turn with his mace.

**9.57.36**  With the blood streaming from him and from the heavy fall of that blow, a swoon, as it were, came over the immeasurably mighty Bhīma.

**9.57.37**  Duryodhana knew that the Pāṇḍava had been wounded in the battle; yet Bhīma too held up his sorely battered body.

**9.57.38**  Your son thought him to be standing ready and about to strike in the fray, and for that reason he did not smite him again.

**9.57.39**  Then, after recovering for a moment, O king, the valiant Bhīmasena rushed with great speed upon Duryodhana, who stood his ground.

**9.57.40**  Seeing him of measureless might charging in fury, O bull of the Bhāratas, your son wished to make that blow of his fall in vain.

**9.57.41**  Setting his mind firmly in his stance, your high-souled son resolved to leap upward, O king, meaning to outwit Vṛkodara.

**9.57.42**  Bhīmasena perceived the king's intent, and so, rushing upon him and bounding forward like a lion,

**9.57.43**  as the king deceived him by his footwork and was again about to spring up, the Pāṇḍava hurled his mace with great force at his thighs, O king.

**9.57.44**  Cast by him of dreadful deeds, that mace, like the crash of a thunderbolt, shattered both the comely thighs of Duryodhana.

**9.57.45**  That tiger among men fell, making the earth resound, his thighs broken by Bhīmasena—your son, O lord of the earth.

**9.57.46**  Winds blew with thunderclaps, a rain of dust fell, and the earth trembled with its trees, shrubs, and mountains.

**9.57.47**  When that hero fell, the lord of all earthly kings, a great blazing meteor, loud-roaring, attended by thunderclaps and terrible to behold, fell as the master of the earth went down.

**9.57.48**  So too, O Bhārata, Maghavan rained down a rain of blood and a rain of dust there, when your son was struck down.

**9.57.49**  A great roar of Yakṣas, of Rākṣasas, and of Piśācas as well was heard in the sky, O bull of the Bhāratas.

**9.57.50**  At that dreadful sound there arose from many beasts and birds a still more dreadful cry on every side.

**9.57.51**  Those horses and elephants that remained there, together with the men, let loose a mighty clamor when your son fell.

**9.57.52**  And a great sound of kettledrums, conches, and tabors arose, even from beneath the earth, when your son was struck down.

**9.57.53**  The quarters were then filled, O king, with headless trunks—many-footed, many-armed, fearful to behold—that danced about and spread terror.

**9.57.54**  Those bearing standards, those bearing missiles, and those bearing weapons all began to tremble then, O king, when your son was struck down.

**9.57.55**  Lakes and wells vomited forth blood, O best of kings, and rivers of mighty current began to flow backward against their streams.

**9.57.56**  Women took on, as it were, the marks of men, and men the marks of women, when your son Duryodhana fell, O king.

**9.57.57**  Beholding those wondrous portents, the Pāñcālas together with the Pāṇḍavas all became troubled in mind, O bull of the Bhāratas.

**9.57.58**  The gods departed at their pleasure, and the Gandharvas and Apsarases likewise, recounting the wondrous combat of your sons, O Bhārata.

**9.57.59**  So too the Siddhas, O king of kings, and the wandering Cāraṇas, praising those two lions among men, departed each the way he had come.

**9.58.1**  Saṃjaya said: Seeing him laid low, like a towering sāla tree felled, all the Pāṇḍavas there became glad at heart.

**9.58.2**  Like a maddened elephant struck down by a lion they beheld him; and all those Somakas too, their hair bristling with joy, looked on.

**9.58.3**  Then, having struck down Duryodhana, the valiant Bhīmasena drew near to the fallen lord of the Kauravas and spoke these words:

**9.58.4**  "'Cow! Cow!'—O fool, when of old in the assembly you laughed and mocked us and Draupadī clad in a single garment, O wicked-minded one: of that mockery receive this day the fruit!"

**9.58.5**  Having spoken thus, with his left foot he touched the diadem, and with that foot he spurned the head of the lion among kings.

**9.58.6**  And then, red with wrath, Bhīma the crusher of hostile hosts spoke once more these words; hear them, O ruler of men.

**9.58.7**  "Those who once danced before us crying again and again 'Cow! Cow!'—those we now dance against, crying once more 'Cow! Cow!'"

**9.58.8**  "In us there is no treachery, no fire of arson, no dicing, no deceit; relying on the strength of our own arms, we crush our foes."

**9.58.9**  Having reached the far shore of his supreme enmity, Vṛkodara, laughing softly, spoke to Yudhiṣṭhira, to Keśava and the Sṛñjayas, to Dhanaṃjaya, and to the two sons of Mādrī:

**9.58.10**  "Those who dragged Draupadī in while she was in her courses, and those who in the assembly stripped her of her garment—behold those sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, slain in battle by the Pāṇḍavas through the ascetic merit of the daughter of Yajñasena!"

**9.58.11**  "Those cruel sons of King Dhṛtarāṣṭra who once called us barren sesame seeds—they are slain by us with their followers and kin; now let us go, if it must be, to heaven or to hell."

**9.58.12**  And again, as the king lay fallen on the ground, he—gazing at the mace resting on his shoulder, and crushing the head with his left foot—called Duryodhana a worker of deceit.

**9.58.13**  But when, O king, they saw the foot set upon the head of the Kuru king by the exultant, base-minded Bhīmasena, the righteous chiefs of the Somakas did not rejoice.

**9.58.14**  As Vṛkodara, having thus slain your son, boasted and danced about again and again, the Dharma King spoke these words:

**9.58.15**  "Crush not his head with your foot, lest your great righteousness pass away. He is a king and a kinsman, and now slain; this is not seemly for you, O blameless one."

**9.58.16**  "He is undone, his counselors slain, his brothers slain, his offspring slain, his line's oblations cut off—and he is our brother; this you have done is not seemly."

**9.58.17**  "'This Bhīmasena is a righteous man,' so people once spoke of you. Why then, Bhīmasena, do you set your foot upon a king?"

**9.58.18**  Seeing Duryodhana brought to such a state, King Yudhiṣṭhira, son of Kuntī, with eyes brimming with tears spoke these words:

**9.58.19**  "Surely this was ordained by the mighty, high-souled Ordainer—that we should seek to slay you, and you us, O best of the Kurus."

**9.58.20**  "For through your own fault you have met with so great a calamity, born of greed, of pride, and of folly, O Bhārata."

**9.58.21**  "Having caused the death of your companions, your brothers, your fathers, your sons, your grandsons, and your teachers, you have come at last to your own destruction."

**9.58.22**  "Through your fault we have slain your brothers, those great chariot-warriors, and your other kinsmen too; I deem destiny hard to overcome."

**9.58.23**  "The daughters-in-law and granddaughters-in-law of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, distraught and widowed, wasted with grief, will surely reproach us."

**9.58.24**  Having spoken thus, that king, sore afflicted with grief, sighed deeply; and Yudhiṣṭhira, the son of Dharma, lamented long.

**9.59.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Seeing the king slain by unrighteous means, what then, O bard, did the foremost of the Mādhavas, the mighty Baladeva, say?

**9.59.2**  He who knew the finer points of mace-combat, skilled in mace-combat—the son of Rohiṇī—what he did, that recount to me, Saṃjaya.

**9.59.3**  Saṃjaya said: Seeing your son struck on the head by Bhīmasena, Rāma, the strongest of strikers and mighty himself, blazed up in great wrath.

**9.59.4**  Then in the midst of the kings, the plough-wielder, raising his arms aloft and uttering a fierce cry of anguish, said: "Shame, shame upon you, Bhīma!"

**9.59.5**  "Alas, shame, that a blow was struck below the navel in a contest of pure valor! Never has it been seen in mace-combat, this which Vṛkodara has done."

**9.59.6**  "'One must not strike below the navel'—such is the ruling of the treatises; but this fool, ignorant of the treatises, acts after his own willful pleasure."

**9.59.7**  As he spoke thus again and again, a great fury arose in him; then, raising his plough, the mighty one rushed upon Bhīma.

**9.59.8**  With his arms raised aloft, the form of that high-souled one was like a great white mountain, gleaming with manifold mineral hues.

**9.59.9**  As he sprang forward, Keśava, bowed in humility yet powerful and strong, caught hold of him with effort in his round, brawny arms.

**9.59.10**  The two best of the Yādavas, the fair one and the dark, shone forth then exceedingly, O king, like the moon and the sun risen together in the sky at the day's end.

**9.59.11**  And Keśava, as if to calm that infuriated one, said to him: "One's own increase, the increase of one's friend, and the rising of one's friend's friend—and the reverse of these among one's foes: such is the sixfold increase of one's own self."

**9.59.12**  "When in oneself and in one's friends the contrary should come to pass, one should recognize the decline of one's purpose and quickly become a maker of peace."

**9.59.13**  "The Pāṇḍavas, of pure manhood, are our natural friends; they are our own, the sons of our father's sister, and they have been grievously wronged by their foes."

**9.59.14**  "The fulfillment of a vow is the duty of a Kṣatriya—this you know. 'I shall shatter Suyodhana's thighs with my mace in the great battle'—this was vowed of old by Bhīma upon the floor of the assembly."

**9.59.15**  "And he was cursed long before by the great seer Maitreya: 'Bhīma shall break your thighs with his mace,' O scorcher of foes. Therefore I see no fault here; be not wroth, O slayer of Pralamba."

**9.59.16**  "By ties of marriage and of the heart we are bound here to the Pāṇḍavas; in their prosperity is our own prosperity. Be not wroth, O bull among men."

**9.59.17**  Rāma said: "Righteousness, well practiced by the good, keeps in check the other two—wealth in the case of one too greedy for gain, and pleasure in the case of one too much addicted to it."

**9.59.18**  "He who pursues righteousness and wealth, righteousness and pleasure, pleasure and wealth—nay, righteousness, wealth, and pleasure together—without letting one injure another, attains supreme happiness."

**9.59.19**  "But all this has been thrown into disorder by the injury done to righteousness by Bhīmasena, O Govinda—however much you may speak to me as you do."

**9.59.20**  Vāsudeva said: "Free from anger, righteous of soul, ever cherishing righteousness—so are you renowned in the world. Therefore grow calm; be not wroth."

**9.59.21**  "Know that the Kali age has come, and remember the Pāṇḍava's vow; let the Pāṇḍava be quit of his debt of enmity and of his vow alike."

**9.59.22**  Saṃjaya said: Hearing even this quibble upon righteousness from Keśava, O lord of peoples, Rāma, his mind not at all pleased, spoke these words in the assembly:

**9.59.23**  "Having slain by unrighteousness the king Suyodhana, a man of righteous soul, the Pāṇḍava will gain renown in this world as a crooked fighter."

**9.59.24**  "Duryodhana too, a righteous soul, will attain the everlasting goal—a king who fought straight, slain though he was, the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, ruler of men."

**9.59.25**  "Having entered upon the consecration of battle, having spread out the sacrifice of war, and having offered himself as oblation in the fire of his foes, he has won the glory of the final ablution."

**9.59.26**  Having spoken thus, the valiant son of Rohiṇī, in form like the peak of a white cloud, mounted his chariot and set out toward Dvārakā.

**9.59.27**  And the Pāñcālas with the Vārṣṇeyas, and the Pāṇḍavas, O lord of peoples, when Rāma had departed for Dvāravatī, were not greatly pleased at heart.

**9.59.28**  Then Vāsudeva spoke these words to Yudhiṣṭhira, who was downcast, sunk in thought, his face bent low, his resolve stricken by grief:

**9.59.29**  "O Dharma King, why do you countenance this unrighteousness—done to this man whose kin are slain, who lies fallen and bereft of sense?"

**9.59.30**  "Why do you look on, knowing righteousness as you do, O ruler of men, while Duryodhana's head is crushed by Bhīma's foot?"

**9.59.31**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: "This is not pleasing to me, Kṛṣṇa, that Vṛkodara in his wrath touched a king's head with his foot; nor do I rejoice in the ruin of our house."

**9.59.32**  "By deceit were we ever deceived by the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra; with many a harsh word spoken, we were sent away into the forest."

**9.59.33**  "That grief of Bhīmasena's dwells exceedingly in his heart; reflecting upon this, O Vārṣṇeya, I overlooked this deed."

**9.59.34**  Therefore, now that this man of feeble wits, greedy and a slave to his desires, has been slain, let the Pāṇḍava have his heart's wish—whether what was done was righteous or unrighteous.

**9.59.35**  Saṃjaya said: When the King of Righteousness had spoken thus, Vāsudeva, the upholder of the Yadu line, said only with difficulty, "So be it; let it be as you wish."

**9.59.36**  Thus addressed by Vāsudeva, who sought Bhīma's welfare and delight, Yudhiṣṭhira approved of all that Bhīma had done in the battle.

**9.59.37**  And Bhīmasena, the wrathful one, having slain your son in battle, stood before Yudhiṣṭhira, saluted him, and, filled with joy, folded his palms in reverence.

**9.59.38**  O lord of peoples, that hero of mighty splendour, his eyes wide with gladness and wearing the look of a victor, spoke to Yudhiṣṭhira, the King of Righteousness.

**9.59.39**  "Today the earth is yours, O king, made secure and freed of every thorn. Govern it, O great king, upholding your own sacred duty."

**9.59.40**  "He who was the author of this enmity, the lover of deceit who delighted in treachery—he now lies slain upon the earth, O lord of the earth."

**9.59.41**  "Duḥśāsana and all the rest, those harsh of speech, Rādheya too, and Śakuni—all your foes have been struck down."

**9.59.42**  "This earth, strewn with jewels, with its forests and its mountains, has this day returned to you, O great king, now that your enemies are slain."

**9.59.43**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: The end of the feud has come; King Suyodhana is slain. Following the counsel of Kṛṣṇa, this earth has been won.

**9.59.44**  By good fortune you have discharged your debt both to our mother and to her wrath; by good fortune you are victorious, O unassailable one; by good fortune the enemy has been cast down.

**9.60.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: When they beheld Duryodhana slain by Bhīmasena in the combat, what did the Pāṇḍavas and the Sṛñjayas do, O Saṃjaya?

**9.60.2**  Saṃjaya said: When they beheld Duryodhana slain by Bhīmasena in the combat, O great king, as a lion slays a rutting wild elephant in the forest—

**9.60.3**  there, with their hearts rejoicing, the Pāṇḍavas together with Kṛṣṇa, and the Pāñcālas and the Sṛñjayas, when the joy of the Kurus had been slain—

**9.60.4**  whirled their upper garments and roared their lion-roars; so filled with joy were they that the earth itself could scarcely bear them.

**9.60.5**  Some brandished their bows, and others twanged their bowstrings; some blew their great conches, and others beat their kettledrums.

**9.60.6**  Still others sported and laughed, those foes of yours, and again and again the heroes spoke these words to Bhīmasena:

**9.60.7**  "A hard deed, a very great deed, have you accomplished in battle this day, slaying with your mace the lord of the Kauravas, worn out as he was by his exertions."

**9.60.8**  As Indra slew Vṛtra in the supreme combat, so the people deemed this slaying of the foe to have been wrought by you.

**9.60.9**  "This brave Duryodhana, who ranged through manifold paths and wheeled in every circling movement—who other than Vṛkodara could have slain him?"

**9.60.10**  "You have reached the far shore of this enmity, a shore beyond the reach of others here; such a feat as this no other could have brought to pass."

**9.60.11**  "As by a rutting tusker, O hero, at the head of the fray, by good fortune Duryodhana's head has been crushed by your foot."

**9.60.12**  "As a lion does with a buffalo, having waged a wondrous battle, by good fortune the blood of Duḥśāsana was drunk by you, O blameless one."

**9.60.13**  "Those who wronged King Yudhiṣṭhira, the soul of righteousness—upon their heads, by your own deeds and by good fortune, your foot has been set."

**9.60.14**  "By the overthrow of your enemies and by the slaying of Duryodhana, O Bhīma, by good fortune your vast renown is spread abroad over the earth."

**9.60.15**  "Surely, as the bards rejoiced over Śakra when Vṛtra was slain, so we rejoice over you, your foe destroyed, O descendant of Bharata."

**9.60.16**  "The hairs upon our bodies that bristled with joy at the slaying of Duryodhana—know, O descendant of Bharata, that even now they will not lie down again." So the praise-singers gathered there spoke to Bhīmasena.

**9.60.17**  To those exultant tigers among men, the Pāñcālas together with the Pāṇḍavas, who spoke words befitting the moment, Madhusūdana then said:

**9.60.18**  "It is not fitting, O lords of men, to slay again and again an enemy already slain. This dullard has indeed already been struck down by your harsh words."

**9.60.19**  "This wicked man was slain at that very moment when, shameless, greedy, the companion of the wicked, he transgressed the counsel of his friends."

**9.60.20**  "Though urged again and again by Vidura, Droṇa, Kṛpa, the son of Gaṅgā, and the Sṛñjayas, he would not grant the Pāṇḍavas their ancestral share."

**9.60.21**  "This basest of men is no longer fit to be reckoned friend or foe. What use is it to keep reviling with words one who has become as a senseless log of wood?"

**9.60.22**  "Mount your chariots swiftly; let us depart, O lords of the earth. By good fortune this evil-souled one is slain, together with his counsellors, his kinsmen, and his allies."

**9.60.23**  But hearing this reproach from Kṛṣṇa, King Duryodhana, overcome with indignation, raised himself up, O lord of peoples.

**9.60.24**  Seated upon his haunches, propping himself against the earth with both arms, he knit his brows into a frown and cast his gaze upon Vāsudeva.

**9.60.25**  The form of the king, his body half upraised, was then like that of an angered venomous serpent whose tail had been severed, O descendant of Bharata.

**9.60.26**  Heedless of the dreadful, life-ending agony, Duryodhana assailed Vāsudeva with savage words:

**9.60.27**  "O heir of the slave of Kaṃsa, have you no shame for this, that by unrighteous means I have been brought down in the mace-duel?"

**9.60.28**  "Do you think I did not perceive how, treacherously prompting Bhīma's memory, you said to Arjuna, 'Smash his thighs'?"

**9.60.29**  "Having caused thousands of kings who fought fairly to be slain by many crooked devices—have you no shame, no remorse?"

**9.60.30**  "Day after day, as he wrought great slaughter among heroes, your grandsire was made to be slain by you, with Śikhaṇḍin set in the forefront."

**9.60.31**  "By slaying the elephant that bore the same name as Aśvatthāman, O most wicked one, the preceptor was made to lay down his weapons—do you think this is unknown to me?"

**9.60.32**  "And when that valiant one was being struck down by this cruel Dhṛṣṭadyumna, you looked on and did not restrain him."

**9.60.33**  "The very spear that was begged for, meant for the slaying of the son of Pāṇḍu, you caused to be wasted upon Ghaṭotkaca. Who is a greater doer of evil than you?"

**9.60.34**  "His arm severed, set upon the vow of fasting unto death, the mighty Bhūriśravas was slain at your bidding by the vile descendant of Śini."

**9.60.35**  "And Karṇa, performing a deed of the highest valour in his longing to vanquish Pārtha, was thwarted through the wasting of the arrow that was Aśvasena, son of the lord of serpents—"

**9.60.36**  "and again, when his chariot wheel sank down, that foremost of men, Karṇa, struck by calamity and overcome, distracted with his wheel, was cast down in the combat."

**9.60.37**  "Had you fought fairly against me and against Karṇa, against Bhīṣma and Droṇa in battle, victory would surely never have been yours."

**9.60.38**  "But by you, ignoble one, through crooked ways were we and other kings, who kept to our own sacred duty, brought to destruction."

**9.60.39**  Vāsudeva said: O son of Gāndhārī, you have been slain together with your brothers, your sons, and your kinsmen, your followers and your friends, because you trod the path of wickedness.

**9.60.40**  It was by your own misdeeds that the heroes Bhīṣma and Droṇa were laid low, and Karṇa too, who followed your ways, was slain in the encounter.

**9.60.41**  Though entreated by me, O fool, you would not give the Pāṇḍavas their ancestral share, the half of their own kingdom, out of greed and at Śakuni's resolve.

**9.60.42**  You administered poison to Bhīmasena, and all the Pāṇḍavas, together with their mother, you sought to burn in the house of lac, O most wicked one.

**9.60.43**  And the daughter of Yajñasena, in her courses, was dragged into the assembly hall at the dicing—even then, O evil-souled and shameless one, you deserved to be killed.

**9.60.44**  Because, with the dice-cunning son of Subala, you by treachery defeated one who knew righteousness but knew nothing of the dice—for that you have been slain in battle.

**9.60.45**  Because, when they had gone hunting, Kṛṣṇā was tormented in the forest by the wicked Jayadratha at the hermitage of Tṛṇabindu—

**9.60.46**  and because Abhimanyu, a mere boy, alone amid many in the fray, was slain through your misdeeds, O wicked one—for that you have been slain in battle.

**9.60.47**  Duryodhana said: I have studied as the rule prescribes, I have given gifts duly, I have ruled the earth with its girdling seas, I have stood upon the heads of my enemies—who then has come to a happier end than I?

**9.60.48**  That very death which is the wish of warriors who keep their own sacred duty in view—that death I have now attained. Who then has come to a happier end than I?

**9.60.49**  Pleasures worthy of the gods, such mortal joys as kings can scarcely win, I have enjoyed; sovereignty supreme I have attained. Who then has come to a happier end than I?

**9.60.50**  With my friends and my followers I shall go to heaven, O Acyuta; while you, your purposes shattered, shall live on in grief.

**9.60.51**  Saṃjaya said: At the close of this speech of the king of the Kurus, O descendant of Bharata, a great shower of sweet-scented flowers fell down.

**9.60.52**  The Gandharvas made music and the throngs of Apsarases sang, and the Siddhas uttered their voices, crying "Well done! Well done!", O descendant of Bharata.

**9.60.53**  And a fragrant breeze blew, holy-scented, gentle and pleasant, and the spotless sky shone forth, gleaming like a sapphire.

**9.60.54**  Beholding these wondrous portents, and seeing the honour shown to Duryodhana, those led by Vāsudeva were overcome with shame.

**9.60.55**  And hearing that they had been slain unrighteously, they grieved, stricken with sorrow, for Bhīṣma and Droṇa, for Karṇa, and for Bhūriśravas.

**9.60.56**  But seeing the Pāṇḍavas sunk in thought and downcast in spirit, Kṛṣṇa, whose voice was as the thunder of the clouds, spoke these words:

**9.60.57**  "This swift-handed warrior, and all those great chariot-heroes, valiant as they were, could not have been slain by you in fair fight upon the field."

**9.60.58**  "These stratagems were therefore devised by me, O lords of men; otherwise the sons of Pāṇḍu would never anywhere have won the victory."

**9.60.59**  "For those four great-souled warriors, mightiest of charioteers upon the earth, could not by righteous means have been slain even by the guardians of the worlds themselves."

**9.60.60**  "And likewise this son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, mace in hand and unwearied, could not by righteous means have been slain even by Death himself, the wielder of the rod."

**9.60.61**  "Nor should you take it to heart that this king was struck down. When the enemies are many and overpowering, they must be slain by stratagem and craft."

**9.60.62**  "This is the path followed of old by the gods who slew the Asuras; the road that the good have trodden is the road that all men follow."

**9.60.63**  "Our task is accomplished; let us settle upon our lodging now in the evening. Let us all rest, O lords of men, with our horses, elephants, and chariots."

**9.60.64**  Hearing the words of Vāsudeva, the Pāñcālas together with the Pāṇḍavas, exceedingly gladdened, then roared like a pride of lions.

**9.60.65**  Then those bulls among men, rejoicing to see Duryodhana slain, blew their conches, and Mādhava blew the Pāñcajanya.

**9.61.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then all those rulers of the earth set forth toward their lodging, blowing their conches in their joy, their arms like iron bludgeons.

**9.61.2**  And as the Pāṇḍavas were going to our camp, O lord of peoples, the great archer Yuyutsu followed behind them, and Sātyaki as well.

**9.61.3**  Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Śikhaṇḍin, and the sons of Draupadī, one and all, and all the other great archers, went each to his own encampment.

**9.61.4**  Then the sons of Pṛthā entered the camp of Duryodhana, its splendour quenched and its lord slain, like a theatre when the people have dispersed.

**9.61.5**  It was like a city whose festival had ended, like a pool from which the elephant had been carried off—thronged for the most part with women, and watched over by aged ministers.

**9.61.6**  There, O king, those who had once gone before Duryodhana waited upon them, their palms joined in supplication, clad in soiled ochre garments.

**9.61.7**  Having reached the camp of the Kuru king, the Pāṇḍavas, those foremost of chariot-warriors, descended from their chariots, O great king.

**9.61.8**  Then Keśava, ever devoted to his welfare and his good, addressed the wielder of the Gāṇḍīva, O bull of the Bhāratas.

**9.61.9**  "Lay down the Gāṇḍīva and the two inexhaustible great quivers; afterward I too shall descend, O best of the Bhāratas."

**9.61.10**  "And do you yourself dismount; this is for your good, O blameless one." And so the hero Dhanaṃjaya, son of Pāṇḍu, did just that.

**9.61.11**  Then afterward the wise Kṛṣṇa, releasing the reins of the horses, descended from the chariot of the wielder of the Gāṇḍīva.

**9.61.12**  And when the lord of beings, that great-souled one, had stepped down, the divine ape-banner of the wielder of the Gāṇḍīva vanished from sight.

**9.61.13**  That mighty chariot, scorched by Droṇa and Karṇa with their divine weapons, then, kindled by fire, blazed up at once, O lord of the earth.
