**9.61.14**  With its quivers, its reins, its horses, its yoke and chariot-pole, the chariot of the wielder of the Gāṇḍīva, reduced to ashes, fell to the ground.

**9.61.15**  Seeing it thus turned to ashes, the sons of Pāṇḍu were filled with wonder, O lord; and Arjuna, O king, spoke these words.

**9.61.16**  With joined palms, affectionately bowing down and saluting him: "Govinda, blessed lord, why was this chariot consumed by fire?"

**9.61.17**  "What is this great marvel that has come to pass, O delight of the Yadus? Tell me of it, mighty-armed one, if you deem it fit for me to hear."

**9.61.18**  Vāsudeva said: This chariot had already been burned by weapons of many kinds, O Arjuna; yet because I occupied it, it did not break apart in battle, O scorcher of foes.

**9.61.19**  But now, scorched by the fiery power of the Brahmā-weapon, it has crumbled, for I have released it today, O son of Kuntī, now that your task is accomplished.

**9.61.20**  Saṃjaya said: And the blessed Keśava, slayer of foes, smiling faintly, embraced King Yudhiṣṭhira and spoke to him.

**9.61.21**  "By good fortune you are victorious, O son of Kuntī; by good fortune your foes are conquered; by good fortune the wielder of the Gāṇḍīva and Bhīmasena the Pāṇḍava are well."

**9.61.22**  "And you too are safe, O king, and the two Pāṇḍava sons of Mādrī; delivered from this slaughter of heroes, your enemies slain, swiftly perform the rites that now remain to be done, O Bhārata."

**9.61.23**  "When I came to Upaplavya together with the wielder of the Gāṇḍīva, and you brought me the honey-offering, what you then said to me—"

**9.61.24**  "'This Dhanaṃjaya is your brother and your friend, O Kṛṣṇa; he is to be guarded, mighty-armed one, in every peril, O lord'—and to you who spoke thus I answered, 'So be it.'"

**9.61.25**  "That ambidextrous archer has been guarded for you and is victorious, O lord of men; together with his brothers, O king of kings, that valiant one of truthful prowess is delivered from this slaughter of heroes, from this hair-raising battle."

**9.61.26**  Thus addressed by Kṛṣṇa, the Dharma-king Yudhiṣṭhira, the hair of his body bristling with joy, O great king, replied to Janārdana.

**9.61.27**  "The Brahmā-weapon let loose by Droṇa and by Karṇa, O crusher of foes—who other than you could withstand it, even Purandara the wielder of the thunderbolt in person?"

**9.61.28**  "By your grace many have been conquered in battle, and Pārtha, plunged into the great fray, never turned his face away."

**9.61.29**  "And likewise, mighty-armed one, through many turns of fortune I have seen the unbroken succession of deeds and the auspicious course of your splendor."

**9.61.30**  "At Upaplavya the great sage Kṛṣṇa Dvaipāyana said to me: 'Where there is righteousness, there is Kṛṣṇa; where Kṛṣṇa is, there is victory.'"

**9.61.31**  When this had been said, those heroes, having entered your camp, O Bhārata, took possession of the stores of treasure, jewels, and riches.

**9.61.32**  Silver and gold, gems and pearls, the finest ornaments, woolen blankets and deerskins, slave-women and slaves beyond counting, and the appurtenances of kingship.

**9.61.33**  Having obtained your inexhaustible wealth, O bull of the Bhāratas, those great archers, their foes conquered, raised a shout of triumph, O lord of men.

**9.61.34**  Then those heroes, having taken rest and unyoked their mounts, halted there awhile—all the Pāṇḍavas, and Sātyaki likewise.

**9.61.35**  Then, O great king, the illustrious Vāsudeva said: "For the sake of auspiciousness we must lodge outside the camp."

**9.61.36**  Saying "So be it," all the Pāṇḍavas, and Sātyaki as well, together with Vāsudeva, went outside for the sake of auspiciousness.

**9.61.37**  Reaching the holy river Oghavatī, O king, the Pāṇḍavas, their enemies slain, passed that night there.

**9.61.38**  Then they dispatched the Yādava to the city named after the elephant; and the mighty Vāsudeva, mounting Dāruka upon his chariot, set out swiftly with all speed toward where the king, son of Ambikā, was.

**9.61.39**  As he was about to set forth, his steeds Sainya and Sugrīva yoked, they said to him: "Console Gāndhārī, the illustrious lady whose sons are slain."

**9.61.40**  Bidden by the Pāṇḍavas, that best of the Sātvatas set out for that city, eager to reach without delay Gāndhārī, whose sons had been slain.

**9.62.1**  Janamejaya said: For what reason did that tiger among kings, the Dharma-king Yudhiṣṭhira, send Vāsudeva, scorcher of foes, to Gāndhārī?

**9.62.2**  When Kṛṣṇa had earlier gone for the sake of peace to the Kauravas and had not won that desire, then this war came to pass.

**9.62.3**  But now that the warriors were slain and Duryodhana likewise killed, and the earth made rivalless for the son of Pāṇḍu by the war,

**9.62.4**  the camp scattered and emptied, and the highest glory attained—what then was the reason, O brāhmaṇa, for which Kṛṣṇa went forth once more?

**9.62.5**  And this reason does not seem to me a small one, O brāhmaṇa, since the immeasurable Janārdana himself went there in person.

**9.62.6**  Recount it all to me truly, O best of officiating priests—the reason, O brāhmaṇa, that determined this undertaking.

**9.62.7**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: This further question that you ask of me is worthy of you, O prince; I shall declare it to you duly, O bull of the Bhāratas.

**9.62.8**  Seeing Duryodhana, the mighty son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, slain by Bhīmasena in the duel in transgression of the agreed rule, O king—

**9.62.9**  seeing him slain unfairly in the mace-combat, O Bhārata, a great fear then entered Yudhiṣṭhira, O great king.

**9.62.10**  For he thought of the illustrious Gāndhārī, endowed with austerity—armed with dreadful penance, she could burn even the three worlds.

**9.62.11**  As he pondered, this thought then arose in him: the wrath of Gāndhārī, blazing with anger, must first be pacified.

**9.62.12**  "For she, hearing of such a slaying of her sons as we have wrought, will in her wrath reduce us to ashes with the fire of her mind."

**9.62.13**  "How will Gāndhārī endure this fierce grief, when she hears that her son, who fought without guile, was killed by a trick?"

**9.62.14**  Having pondered thus in many ways, filled with fear and grief, the Dharma-king addressed these words to Vāsudeva.

**9.62.15**  "By your grace, Govinda, the kingdom, its thorns destroyed—a thing unattainable even in thought—has here been won by us, O imperishable one."

**9.62.16**  "Before my own eyes, mighty-armed one, in that hair-raising battle, a most mighty struggle was undertaken by you, O delight of the Yādavas."

**9.62.17**  "As of old in the war of the gods and demons you lent your aid for the slaying of the foes of the immortals, and the enemies of the gods were destroyed,"

**9.62.18**  "so too, mighty-armed and imperishable one, you lent your aid to us; and by your charioteering, O Vārṣṇeya, we were upheld."

**9.62.19**  "Had you not been Phalguna's protector in the great battle, how could this ocean of armies have been conquered in the fray?"

**9.62.20**  "Vast blows of maces, and battering with iron bludgeons, with lances, slings, javelins, and battle-axes—"

**9.62.21**  "and harsh words too were endured by you who sought our good; and all these have borne fruit for you, now that Duryodhana is slain, O imperishable one."

**9.62.22**  "Be mindful, mighty-armed Mādhava, of the wrath of Gāndhārī; for that illustrious lady is ever wasted thin by fierce austerity."

**9.62.23**  "Hearing of the slaughter of her sons and grandsons, she will surely burn us up; her appeasement, O hero, is in my judgment now timely."

**9.62.24**  "And what man, save you, O best of men, is able even to look upon her, her eyes blazing with anger, worn thin by the calamity of her sons?"

**9.62.25**  "To me it seems fitting that you go there, O Mādhava, if it please you, to pacify Gāndhārī, who is ablaze with wrath, O subduer of foes."

**9.62.26**  "For you are the maker and unmaker of the worlds, their origin and dissolution; with words joined to motive and cause and fitted to the occasion,"

**9.62.27**  "swiftly, O greatly wise one, will you calm Gāndhārī; and the venerable grandfather Kṛṣṇa Dvaipāyana too will be there."

**9.62.28**  "By every means, mighty-armed best of the Sātvatas, the quelling of Gāndhārī's wrath must be accomplished by you who seek the welfare of the Pāṇḍavas."

**9.62.29**  Hearing the words of the Dharma-king, the upholder of the Yadu line summoned Dāruka and said: "Let the chariot be made ready."

**9.62.30**  Hearing the words of Keśava, Dāruka then in haste reported to the great-souled Keśava that the chariot was ready.

**9.62.31**  Mounting that chariot, the best of the Yādavas, the scorcher of foes, the all-pervading Keśava, set out in haste for Hāstinapura.

**9.62.32**  Then, O great king, the blessed Mādhava, that chariot-warrior, set forth; and reaching the city named after the elephant, the valiant one entered it.

**9.62.33**  Entering the city, the hero, making it resound with the thunder of his chariot, and announced to Dhṛtarāṣṭra, descended from his splendid chariot.

**9.62.34**  With undejected spirit he approached the dwelling of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, and there he saw the best of sages, who had come before.

**9.62.35**  Clasping the feet of Kṛṣṇa Dvaipāyana and of the king as well, Janārdana, unperturbed, paid his respects; and Keśava saluted Gāndhārī too.

**9.62.36**  Then the best of the Yādavas, Adhokṣaja, taking hold of the king Dhṛtarāṣṭra's hand, wept aloud.

**9.62.37**  After shedding for a moment the tears born of grief, washing his eyes with water and sipping water as enjoined, the subduer of foes spoke humble words to Dhṛtarāṣṭra.

**9.62.38**  "There is nothing of past or future unknown to you, O Bhārata; and how the working of time has come to pass is well known to you, O lord."

**9.62.39**  "This was the very thing all the Pāṇḍavas, deferring to your wishes, strove for—that there might be no destruction of the lineage, nor of the warrior class, O Bhārata."

**9.62.40**  "Making a compact with his brothers, that lover of righteousness forbore; defeated by the trickery of the dice though they were able men, he submitted to dwelling in the forest."

**9.62.41**  "And the practice of living in concealment, cloaked in various disguises, and many other hardships besides, were borne ever, as though by men without power."

**9.62.42**  "And by me, coming in person when the hour of war was at hand, in the presence of all the world, you were asked for five villages."

**9.62.43**  "By you, smitten by time, out of greed they were not granted; and through your fault, O king, the whole warrior race has gone to destruction."

**9.62.44**  "By Bhīṣma, by Somadatta, by Bāhlika and Kṛpa, by Droṇa together with his son, and by the wise Vidura, you were ever entreated for peace; yet you did not make it."

**9.62.45**  For every man whose mind is overthrown by Time grows bewildered, O Bhārata, even as you yourself, deluded before, rose up in this very matter.

**9.62.46**  What else is there but the working of Time? Destiny alone is the final refuge. Lay not the blame, O great king, upon the Pāṇḍavas.

**9.62.47**  Not the slightest transgression is there in the great-souled Pāṇḍavas, neither in righteousness, nor in justice, nor in affection, O scorcher of foes.

**9.62.48**  Knowing all this to be the fruit wrought by your own fault, you ought not to harbor resentment against the sons of Pāṇḍu.

**9.62.49**  Your lineage, your line of descent, the funeral offering, and the fruit that a son procures — for both you and Gāndhārī all of these now rest upon the Pāṇḍavas.

**9.62.50**  Reflecting upon all this, and upon your own offense, turn your thoughts toward the Pāṇḍavas with goodwill. Homage to you, O bull of the Bhāratas.

**9.62.51**  You know full well, O mighty-armed one, O tiger of the Bhāratas, what devotion the Dharmarāja bears toward you, and what natural affection too.

**9.62.52**  Even having wrought this slaughter of the offending foe, he burns with grief day and night, and finds no peace at all.

**9.62.53**  Grieving for you, O tiger among men, and for the renowned Gāndhārī, O best of the Bhāratas, he finds no calm.

**9.62.54**  Overcome by the deepest shame, he dares not approach you — you who are consumed by sorrow for your sons, your senses confounded in mind.

**9.62.55**  Having spoken thus to Dhṛtarāṣṭra, O great king, the foremost of the Yadus addressed these supreme words to Gāndhārī, wasted with grief.

**9.62.56**  O daughter of Subala, hear what I shall say to you, O woman of noble vows. There is no woman equal to you this day in all this world, O auspicious one.

**9.62.57**  I know, O queen, how in the assembly, in my presence, you spoke a word fraught with righteousness and gain, beneficial to both parties, O gracious lady — yet it was not heeded by your sons.

**9.62.58**  To Duryodhana, hungry for victory, you spoke a stern word: 'Hear my word, O fool — where righteousness is, there is victory.'

**9.62.59**  That word of yours, O daughter of a king, has now come wholly true. Knowing this, O gracious one, set not your mind upon grief, and let your thought never turn to the destruction of the Pāṇḍavas.

**9.62.60**  For you are able, O greatly blessed one, by the power of your austerities, to burn up the earth with all that moves and is unmoving, with an eye kindled by wrath.

**9.62.61**  Hearing the words of Vāsudeva, Gāndhārī spoke: 'It is even so, O mighty-armed one, as you say, O Keśava.'

**9.62.62**  My mind, scorched by anguish, had been set trembling; but now, hearing your word, O Janārdana, it has grown steadfast.

**9.62.63**  For this old, blind king, bereft of his sons, O Keśava, you are now the refuge, together with those heroes, the Pāṇḍavas, O best of men.

**9.62.64**  Having spoken only this much, covering her face with her garment, Gāndhārī, consumed with sorrow for her sons, broke into loud weeping.

**9.62.65**  Then the mighty-armed lord Keśava consoled her, wasted with grief, with words fraught with reason and argument.

**9.62.66**  And having comforted Gāndhārī and Dhṛtarāṣṭra, Mādhava, Keśava, perceived the resolve that the son of Droṇa had conceived.

**9.62.67**  Then, rising in haste and bowing his head to the feet of Dvaipāyana, O lord of kings, he spoke thus to the Kaurava:

**9.62.68**  'I take my leave of you, O best of the Kurus; do not set your mind on grief. The son of Droṇa harbors an evil purpose, and for that reason have I risen up in haste; this night his mind has shown itself bent on the slaughter of the Pāṇḍavas.'

**9.62.69**  Hearing this word, the mighty-armed Dhṛtarāṣṭra, together with Gāndhārī, spoke to Keśava, the slayer of Keśin:

**9.62.70**  'Go swiftly, O mighty-armed one, and guard the Pāṇḍavas; I shall meet with you again right soon, O Janārdana.' Then Acyuta set forth in haste, together with Dāruka.

**9.62.71**  When Vāsudeva had departed, O king, Vyāsa of measureless soul, revered by all the worlds, consoled Dhṛtarāṣṭra, the lord of men.

**9.62.72**  And Vāsudeva too, the righteous-souled, his purpose accomplished, set out from Hāstinapura toward the camp, desiring to behold the Pāṇḍavas, O king.

**9.62.73**  Reaching the camp by night, he came to the Pāṇḍavas, and having reported all to them, he settled down in their company.

**9.63.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: My son, his thighs shattered, fallen to the earth, his head trodden under foot, proud of his might — what words did he utter, O Saṃjaya?

**9.63.2**  That king, exceedingly prone to wrath and bearing inveterate enmity against the sons of Pāṇḍu, having met with utter ruin in that supreme combat — what did he say?

**9.63.3**  Saṃjaya said: Hear, O king; I shall relate it as it befell, O ruler of men — what was spoken by the broken king when that calamity had come upon him.

**9.63.4**  With his thighs shattered, the king, O monarch, was covered over with dust; gathering up his hair, and gazing about at the ten directions,

**9.63.5**  binding up his hair with effort, hissing like a serpent, with eyes brimming with tears of fury he looked upon me,

**9.63.6**  and pounding his arms upon the ground again and again, like an elephant in rut, shaking his scattered locks, grinding tooth against tooth, reviling the eldest of the Pāṇḍavas, he sighed and then spoke thus:

**9.63.7**  'With Bhīṣma, son of Śaṃtanu, as my protector, with Karṇa, foremost of those who bear arms, with Gautama and Śakuni too, and with Droṇa, foremost of those who bear arms —

**9.63.8**  with Aśvatthāman likewise, and Śalya, and the valiant Kṛtavarman — yet to this state have I come; for Time is hard indeed to overpass.'

**9.63.9**  'I, the lord of eleven armies, have come to this plight! When Time comes upon him, O mighty-armed one, no one prevails against it.'

**9.63.10**  'It must be told to those of my own who yet live in this battle — how I was slain by Bhīmasena in violation of the rules of fair fight.'

**9.63.11**  'Many most cruel deeds have indeed been done by the Pāṇḍavas — against Bhūriśravas, against Karṇa, against Bhīṣma, and against the illustrious Droṇa.'

**9.63.12**  'And this infamous deed too has been wrought by the cruel Pāṇḍavas, whereby, among the good, they shall come to be despised — such is my conviction.'

**9.63.13**  'What joy can there be for a man of true worth in a victory won by trickery? And what wise man would deign to commend the breaker of compacts?'

**9.63.14**  'What learned man would rejoice at a victory gained by unrighteousness, as that wicked son of Pāṇḍu, Vṛkodara, rejoices?'

**9.63.15**  'And what wonder is there in this — that today, my thighs being broken, my head was crushed with his foot by the enraged Bhīmasena?'

**9.63.16**  'Against one resplendent, blessed with fortune, and moving honorably among his kin — the man who would act thus, O Saṃjaya, is he then to be esteemed?'

**9.63.17**  'My mother and my father both know well the duty of the warrior caste; those two, O Saṃjaya, stricken with grief, are to be informed in my words.'

**9.63.18**  'Sacrifices have I performed, my servants well maintained, the sea-girt earth duly ruled; while my foes yet lived, I stood upon their heads, O Saṃjaya.'

**9.63.19**  'Gifts have I bestowed according to my power, and kindness done to friends; all my enemies have been crushed — who then has come to a better end than I?'

**9.63.20**  'The realms of others have I overrun, and kings I have made to serve me like slaves; good have I done to those dear to me — who then has come to a better end than I?'

**9.63.21**  'All my kinsmen have I honored, every worthy person duly revered; the threefold ends of life have all been pursued — who then has come to a better end than I?'

**9.63.22**  'My command has held sway over the chief among kings, and honor most hard to win was mine; borne I was by steeds of noble breed — who then has come to a better end than I?'

**9.63.23**  'The Veda I have studied, gifts duly bestowed, a span of life free from disease attained; by my own duty I have won the higher worlds — who then has come to a better end than I?'

**9.63.24**  'By good fortune I was not vanquished in battle so as to depend upon foes like a menial; by good fortune my ample royal splendor has passed, at my death, to another, O lord.'

**9.63.25**  'That death which is the desire of warriors who hold fast to their own duty — that very death I have attained; who then has come to a better end than I?'

**9.63.26**  'By good fortune I never turned back from enmity to be vanquished like a common man; by good fortune I was overthrown without ever stooping to any baseness.'

**9.63.27**  'As one might slay a man asleep, or off his guard, or by poison — even so, by one who had cast off righteousness, was I slain through the breaking of the compact.'

**9.63.28**  'The greatly blessed Aśvatthāman, and Kṛtavarman of the Sātvata clan, and Kṛpa, son of Śaradvat — these are to be told in my words:'

**9.63.29**  'In the Pāṇḍavas, who have so many times acted by unrighteousness and broken their compacts, you must place no trust.'

**9.63.30**  And the king, your son of truthful valor, spoke also to the messengers: 'Tell them how I was slain in battle by Bhīmasena through unrighteousness.'

**9.63.31**  'I shall follow after Droṇa, gone to heaven, and both Śalya and Karṇa likewise, and the mighty Vṛṣasena, and Śakuni too, the son of Subala,'

**9.63.32**  'Jalasaṃdha of great valor, and king Bhagadatta, the son of Somadatta, mighty archer, and Jayadratha the lord of the Sindhus,'

**9.63.33**  'and my brothers, with Duḥśāsana at their head, equal to my very self, and the valiant son of Duḥśāsana, and Lakṣmaṇa — my own two sons —'

**9.63.34**  'these and very many others of mine by the thousand I shall follow from behind, like a traveler bereft of his caravan.'

**9.63.35**  'How, on hearing of her slain brothers and her slain husband, will my sister Duḥśalā fare, wailing aloud, stricken with grief?'

**9.63.36**  'And my aged father the king, together with Gāndhārī, with his daughters-in-law and their daughters-in-law, lamenting — what refuge shall he find?'

**9.63.37**  'Surely Lakṣmaṇa's mother too, her son slain and her lord slain, the gracious, large-eyed lady, will swiftly go to her destruction.'

**9.63.38**  'If Cārvāka the wandering ascetic, that master of speech, comes to know of this, the greatly blessed one will surely exact requital for me.'

**9.63.39**  'At holy Samantapañcaka, renowned throughout the three worlds, having met my death, I shall attain the everlasting worlds.'

**9.63.40**  Then thousands of people, their eyes brimming with tears, O honored one, hearing the lament of the king, fled away in all ten directions.

**9.63.41**  The dread earth, with its seas and forests, with all that moves and is unmoving, then trembled with a roar, and the quarters of the sky grew turbid.

**9.63.42**  They, coming to the son of Droṇa, reported all as it had befallen — the manner of the mace-duel and the slaying of the king.

**9.63.43**  Having related this to the son of Droṇa, O Bhārata, they all, brooding for a long while, departed grief-stricken by the way they had come.

**9.64.1**  Saṃjaya said: Having heard from the messengers that Duryodhana was slain, the great chariot-warriors of the Kauravas who survived the slaughter, O king —

**9.64.2**  pierced through by sharp arrows, by maces, javelins, and spears — Aśvatthāman, and Kṛpa, and Kṛtavarman of the Sātvatas — came in haste upon swift horses to the field of battle.

**9.64.3**  There they beheld the great-souled son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra struck down, broken as a mighty sāla tree in the forest is broken by the force of the wind.

**9.64.4**  writhing upon the ground, drenched with blood, like a great elephant in the wild brought down by a hunter,

**9.64.5**  rolling about again and again, awash in a flood of blood, fallen as if by chance — like the disk of the sun cast down from the sky,

**9.64.6**  like an ocean dried up by a mighty gale, like the full moon in the heavens with its orb veiled in mist,

**9.64.7**  begrimed with dust, long-armed, of prowess equal to an elephant's, that best of kings encircled on every side by dreadful hosts of spirits and flesh-eating beasts, as though by servants greedy to seize his wealth,

**9.64.8**  his brows knit at the corners of his face, his eyes rolled upward in wrath — that tiger among men still full of fury, like a tiger struck down.

**9.64.9**  But when those great archers saw the king fallen to the earth, all the chariot-warriors, Kṛpa and the rest, were overcome with a swoon.

**9.64.10**  Descending from their chariots, they ran to where the king lay, and gazing upon Duryodhana, they all sank down upon the ground.

**9.64.11**  Then, O great king, the son of Droṇa, his eyes brimming with tears and breathing hard, spoke to that foremost of the Bhāratas, the lord of all the lords of the world.

**9.64.12**  Surely there is nothing unendurable in the lot of mortals, when you, O tiger among men, lie here smeared with dust.

**9.64.13**  Having once been a king, having ruled over the whole earth, how is it, O lord of kings, that today you lie alone in a desolate wood?

**9.64.14**  I do not see Duḥśāsana, nor Karṇa the mighty charioteer, nor all those dear friends—what does this mean, O bull of the Bhāratas?

**9.64.15**  Hard indeed it is, by any means, to know the course of fate, when you, the lord of the worlds, lie here smeared with dust.

**9.64.16**  This scorcher of foes, who marched at the head of the anointed kings, now swallows dust along with grass—behold the reversal wrought by time!

**9.64.17**  Where is that spotless parasol of yours, where the fan, O prince? Where has that mighty army of yours gone, O best of kings?

**9.64.18**  Hard to fathom, surely, is the course of events when the cause is otherwise than it seems, for you, who were once the teacher of the world, have come to this pass.

**9.64.19**  Among all mortals fortune is shown to be unstable indeed, now that we behold the grievous calamity of you who once rivalled Indra himself.

**9.64.20**  Hearing that speech of the deeply grieving man, O king, your son spoke these words befitting the hour.

**9.64.21**  Wiping his eyes with both hands and shedding the tears born of sorrow, that ruler of men then addressed all those heroes, Kṛpa and the rest:

**9.64.22**  "Such, men say, is this law of mortals ordained by the Creator: the destruction of all beings, brought about by the turning of time.

**9.64.23**  This destruction has now overtaken me before your very eyes—I who once protected the earth have come to this end.

**9.64.24**  By good fortune I never turned my back in battle in any peril; by good fortune I have been slain by the wicked, and that especially through treachery.

**9.64.25**  By good fortune I, ever eager to fight, kept my resolve unflagging; by good fortune I am slain in battle, my kinsmen and friends already fallen.

**9.64.26**  And by good fortune I see you delivered from this slaughter of men, safe and sound and whole—that is to me a joy beyond all others.

**9.64.27**  Let none of you grieve out of affection at my death; if the Vedas are an authority for you, then the imperishable worlds have been won by me.

**9.64.28**  Though I knew well the might of Kṛṣṇa of boundless splendour, not even by him was I made to fall away from the warrior's law well observed.

**9.64.29**  That end has been attained by me, and in no way am I to be mourned. You have acted in a manner worthy of yourselves and true to your nature; you strove ever for victory—but fate is hard to overcome."

**9.64.30**  Having spoken thus far, his eyes troubled with tears, he fell silent, O lord of kings, overwhelmed grievously by his pain.

**9.64.31**  But seeing the king thus filled with tears and grief, the son of Droṇa blazed up with wrath, like the fire at the dissolution of the world.

**9.64.32**  And he, possessed by anger, wringing one hand within the other, spoke to the king in a voice broken by tears:

**9.64.33**  "My father was slain by base men through a most cruel deed; yet I do not grieve so much for that, O king, as I grieve this day for you.

**9.64.34**  Hear now this word of mine, O lord, as I speak in truth—by my sacrifices and works of merit, by my gifts, by my righteousness, and by my good deeds:

**9.64.35**  This very day, before the eyes of Vāsudeva himself, I will by every means lead all the Pāñcālas to the dwelling-place of the king of the dead. But you, O great king, must grant me your leave."

**9.64.36**  Hearing this speech of Droṇa's son, which gladdened his heart, the Kaurava spoke this word to Kṛpa: "Teacher, bring quickly a pitcher filled with water."

**9.64.37**  Understanding that command of the king, the best of brāhmaṇas took up a brimming pitcher and drew near to the king.

**9.64.38**  Then your son spoke to him, O great king, O lord of peoples: "By my command, O foremost of the twice-born, let the son of Droṇa be consecrated to the office of commander-in-chief—blessings upon you—if you wish to do what pleases me."

**9.64.39**  At the king's command a brāhmaṇa too must fight, and especially one who walks in the law of the warriors—so the knowers of dharma have declared.

**9.64.40**  Hearing then the king's word, Kṛpa, the son of Śaradvat, by the king's command consecrated the son of Droṇa to the command of the army.

**9.64.41**  Once consecrated, O great king, he embraced that best of kings and set forth, making all the quarters resound with his lion's roar.

**9.64.42**  And Duryodhana too, O lord of kings, drenched in a flood of blood, then passed into that night which struck terror into all beings.

**9.64.43**  Then, withdrawing swiftly from that field of battle, O king, their minds shaken with grief, they sank into the helplessness of brooding thought.
